# Sūrah al-Anfāl (Chapter 8) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Chapter of the Spoils: Its Name, Number, and Place**

Sūrah al-Anfāl, "The Spoils of War" — or more exactly, "The Gains" — takes its name from the single word that opens its first verse's question: *yas'alūnaka ʿani'l-anfāl*, "they ask you about the *anfāl*." The root *n-f-l* carries in Arabic both the *spoils* taken from the enemy and the *favour* granted by one's grace — *nāfila* is the supererogatory prayer, *nafīla* the gift one asks of a generous man, and the *anfāl* of the verse are what the victors plucked from the field beyond the settled shares: the surplus, the ask, the grace. That the chapter's name fuses "booty" and "gift" is not a quibble of lexicographers but the sūrah's thesis stated in one root: what the swords *took* was, in the sūrah's verdict, what God *gave* — "the *anfāl* are for God and His Messenger" (v. 1) — and the entire seventy-five verses between that first sentence and the last verse's law of inheritance are the unfolding of what that verdict does to a community's heart, treasury, battlefield, and court. The sūrah is the eighth in the written muṣḥaf, between al-Baqarah and Āl ʿImrān, seventy-five verses, sixty-three *ruʿūs*, and in the standard thirty-part division it fills the whole of the tenth *juz'*'s opening and more — the first complete sūrah after the longest, the two chapters that hold between them the constitution of the young community at Madinah. In the widely transmitted chronology of revelation it is the second sūrah sent down at Madinah, after al-Baqarah; in the reports from Ibn ʿAbbās preserved by al-Ḥākim and al-Bayhaqī it is counted the very first sūrah revealed there in its entirety — an honour the *asbāb* literature makes vivid, since its occasion is the community's first victory and its subject is the first hard question a victory ever poses: *who gets what*.

**The Day of the Criterion: Badr as Subject and as Classroom**

The sūrah's spine is the Battle of Badr, fought on the seventeenth of Ramadan in the second year of the Hijrah, at the wells of Badr some hundred miles south of Madinah — a handful of Emigrants and Helpers, the sources number them at three hundred and thirteen or three hundred and fourteen, with two horses and a camel they mounted in turns, against an armed Meccan force of roughly a thousand come to cover their caravan and to break the Medinan raise. The Qur'an itself names the day *yawm al-furqān*, "the Day of Distinction" (v. 41), and calls the combatants "the two armies" met in the nearest valley-floor (vv. 42–44, and 3:123's "God has already helped you at Badr when you were *worthless*"), and the sūrah's own vocabulary is set to the event's every nerve: the *riʿāb* of the believers' hearts (v. 11), the sleep as safety and the dust of the angels' hooves and the struck necks (vv. 9–12), the Prophet's handful of pebbles thrown at the retreating line (v. 17), the dream made few in the eyes (v. 43), Abu Jahl's prayer of triumph answered by its own irony (vv. 31–32, and 54:45's "the day of the decision" which the *asbāb* reads as Badr itself). The event's standing in the tradition is enormous: the Prophet ﷺ said,

> *"Perhaps God looked upon the people of Badr and said: do as you wish — I have forgiven you."* (al-Bukhārī 3992; Muslim 2494)

— and the classical historians count Badr *al-badr al-kubrā* as the hinge of the prophetic *sīrah*: the day the *jamāʿa* became a *state*. But this sūrah never lets the victory be merely a victory: its first verses interrogate the victors' *souls* — the dispute over the spoils, the hoarding in the heart, the memory of being *few and wronged* (v. 26) — so that Badr stands throughout the chapter as both *gift* and *trial*, *furqān* in its oldest sense: the day that distinguished not two camps on a field but two dispositions in one breast. Every major commentator reads the sūrah as the *psychology of the spoils*: the law of the fifth (v. 41) and the captive-ransom rebuke (vv. 67–68) are the ledger's entries; "you did not slay them — God slew them" (v. 17) is the ledger's header.

**Makkan Within Madinan: the Dispute over Verses 30–36 and 63**

The sūrah is early Madinan by consensus — with a telling exception preserved by the sources: some maintain that verse 30 ("and when the disbelievers plotted against you to bind you or kill you or expel you — they plotted, and God plotted, and God is the best of planners") is Makkan, and on the reading of al-Qurṭubī's and al-Ṭabarī's transmitters the stretch of vv. 30–36 is too, since these verses recall the night of the Dar al-Nadwa's three-option plot and the Hijrah's deliverance — a *sīrah*-flashback set into the law-chapter exactly where the chapter's argument turns from Badr's field to Makkah's conscience. A second report from Ibn ʿAbbās makes v. 63's "and He has united their hearts" Makkan — revealed at the moment ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb embraced Islam, when the believers' number turned and the *strength* the verse names entered the community before Badr did. Whatever the historian decides, the *texture* decides the aesthetic point for him: the sūrah's middle moves in full Makkan cadence — the short, struck sentences, the heaven-witnessed dialogue of vv. 32–33, the mockery-sure doom of vv. 31–35 — between Madinah's long legislative breaths; and the chapter's structure is thereby itself a *hijra*: the Makkan *dhikr* carried inside the Madinan *ḥukm*, remembrance housing ruling, exactly as the next pair of verses will teach that the law of numbers (vv. 65–66) is mercy inside the law of battle. The classical *ʿulūm al-Qur'ān* lists record such mixed sūras precisely in al-Anfāl's neighbourhood — Madinah's constitutions with Makkah's voices embedded — and the expanded commentary below reads each such joint on its own hinge (see the notes on vv. 30 and 63).

**The Architecture of the Sūrah: One Subject in Seven Movements**

The sevenfold shape below is the reading-guide this expanded commentary follows verse by verse: (i) vv. 1–4 — the *anfāl* verdict and the portrait of the *true* believers, whose marks are awe, prayer, and spending; (ii) vv. 5–17 — God's ordering of Badr as *His* affair: as the community disliked it, the angelic rain, the *sakinah*, the strike and the throw that was not a throw; (iii) vv. 18–27 — the analogy of Pharaoh, the tests of *amānāt* and *ghulūl*, and "God is *ʿAlīm*, *Ḥakīm*" set over the ledger; (iv) vv. 28–40 — wealth and children as *fitna*, the Makkan interlude of the Plot and the prayer of the Mockers, and the call to the *khums* and the *furqān*; (v) vv. 41–54 — the dead and their deeds: the strike-above-necks verse, the flight, the conscience's witness on the Day, and the fates filed as precedent (Nūḥ, Hūd, Pharaoh's drowned *mālā*); (vi) vv. 55–66 — the law of covenants (*nabḍ*, the *rubʿ*, the *ʿahd* of God kept), the horses bred for God's cause, *ḥisb* and the *maʿiyya*, and the *takhfīf* from one-to-ten to one-to-two; (vii) vv. 67–75 — the captives and their ransom, the verse of *makhrafa*-to-*makāna*, the Emigrants and Helpers as each other's *awliyāʾ*, the unbelievers' countervailing bond, and the closing transfer of the estate from the brotherhood of vow to the brotherhood of blood: "womb-kin have the stronger claim, in the Book of God; God is *Knower of all things*." The ring is exact — the chapter that opened by assigning *the community's disputed wealth* to God closes by assigning *the family's disputed estate* to God — and within the ring every episode is a *furqān*: spoil and trust, treaty and treachery, fear and *sakinah*, rank and late-comer.

**How These Commentaries Are Written**

Each of the seventy-five verses below receives a self-contained commentary, in the manner established for al-Fātiḥah and al-Baqarah: the received English rendering quoted as the verse's text; the Arabic as lexical event — roots, the jurists' and grammarians' readings, the reciters' variants where they move meaning; the Qur'an read against itself — the parallel-verses that gloss, echoed, or answer the verse, quoted where their words matter; the *ḥadīth* carried only where its wording is certain, with collection and number; the classical exegetes in council — al-Ṭabarī's histories, Ibn Kathīr's battle-narration, al-Qurṭubī's rulings, al-Rāzī's reasons, Ibn ʿAbbās' reports through the early layers, Ibn Taymiyyah's *ḥaqīqa* of intention, and the Sufi commentators' allegory where the sources carry it (ʿAbd al-Qādir al-Jīlānī, Ibn ʿAṭāʾillāh's *ḥaqāʾiq* line, the *taʾwīl* of Jaʿfar al-Ṣādiq in the great compendia); the *asbāb al-nuzūl* kept at their chains' own reach; and the application — the verse read into conscience, household, marketplace, and public square, with analogies that carry without straining. Where the Study-Qur'an commentary behind these pages has supplied a datum — a reading, a citation, a legal wrinkle — it is engaged as foundation, not copied as ceiling: every verse here is treated on the scale its weight bears, and no verse has been shortened because the chapter is long.

**One Word Before the First Verse**

The sūrah is about what happens to a people when they finally *get* what they were denied — and about the God who then asks them, gently and implacably, *whose* they thought it was. The community that entered the chapter owning nothing and trusting everything leaves it owning land, treasury, captives, and treaties — with the same question on its lips, seventy-five verses wider: *yas'alūnaka ʿani'l-anfāl — they ask you about the gains. Say: the gains are for God and the Messenger.* And God knows what is in the breasts.

---

## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:1

> **They question thee concerning the spoils. Say, “The spoils belong to God and the Messenger.” So reverence God and set matters aright among yourselves. And obey God and His Messenger, if you are believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Victory Won, a Quarrel Begun**

The sūrah opens not with the triumph of Badr but with its awkward afterglow. On the field, three groups of believers had divided their functions: one pursued the routed enemy, one stood guard over the Prophet ﷺ, and one watched the captives and the accumulating heap of war matériel. When the dust settled, each group advanced a claim on the spoils — the pursuers by the risk of the chase, the guards by the honor of proximity to the Messenger, the custodians by the labor of custody. Other accounts in the commentaries (al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī) report the friction extending to men absent from the field on mission — two Companions sent out on scouting duty whom the Prophet ﷺ nonetheless included in the share, provoking objection. All the reports converge on one plain, human fact: the first major victory of the Muslim community nearly ended in the first major internal dispute over money. And the revelation came to settle it — a striking demonstration that the Qur'an addresses not only theology and ritual but the raw, embarrassing economics of a young community: who gets what, and who decides.

**The Word anfāl and the Theology of Surplus**

*Anfāl* is the plural of *nawl* or *nafl*, from a root meaning "that which is beyond what is required or owed" — surplus, bonus, grace. A believer's voluntary prayer is *ṣalāt al-nafl* precisely because it is beyond the obligatory. That the Qur'an chooses this word for spoils carries a quiet sermon within it: war booty is not a natural right wrested by the strong; it is a *bonus* from God, a surplus He confers, and bonuses are disposed of by the Giver. Some commentators connect the term to the ḥadīth in Muslim, from Jābir, in which the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Five things have been given to me that were not given to anyone before me: I was aided with terror cast into the hearts of my enemy for a month's distance; the earth has been made for me a place of prayer and pure; spoils of war (ghanāʾim) have been made lawful for me, though they were not lawful for anyone before me; and I was sent to all of mankind."* (Muslim 521)

Jurists distinguished *anfāl* from *ghanāʾim* (the general term for spoils): for some, *anfāl* means the special items taken directly from the slain — armor, sword, horse (called *salab*); for others it designates the fifth of the spoils (*khumus*) allocated in verse 41; for others still, wealth acquired without fighting. What all agree on is the verse's headline ruling: "Say: the spoils belong to God and the Messenger." The men were arguing over shares; God re-framed the whole pile as His, with the Messenger as its trustee-dispenser. The right question was not "who deserves more?" but "does the Owner consent?"

**"Reverence God and Set Matters Aright" — the Real Prize**

Observe the sequence of the verse, which is its logic. First, *taqwā* — reverence God — because a quarrel is never settled while hearts are grasping; then *iṣlāḥ* — set matters aright *between yourselves* — the actual social surgery; then obedience to God and His Messenger as the criterion of genuine faith. The spoils were the smallest of the stakes: a community that tore itself apart over camels and armor in the moment of victory would have collapsed in the harder moments ahead. The Prophet ﷺ taught exactly this ranking of goods. He said:

> *"Shall I not inform you of what exceeds the rank of fasting, charity, and prayer? Setting matters right between one another, for corruption of relations between one another is the shaving thing."* (al-Tirmidhī 2509, graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ; and Abū Dāwūd)

"The shaving thing" — the razor that strips the hair off religion — is a chillingly precise image: not murder of the faith, but something subtler, a gradual close-cropping of its vitality through broken brotherhood. Al-Nawawī and other commentators on this ḥadīth note that while ritual acts profit only their performer, reconciliation profits the whole body — which is why it outweighs them.

**The Economics of Gratitude, the Psychology of Claim**

Why does God seize the entire spoils for Himself and His Messenger rather than simply dividing them fairly on the spot? Because the disease at Badr was not unfair distribution but the sense of proprietary claim itself — "my sword, my chase, my risk." The remedy had to be surgical: declare the contested object outside everyone's jurisdiction, so that whatever they ultimately received arrived as pure gift from God through His Messenger. A share given as grace forms grateful men; a share squeezed out of a quarrel forms entitled ones. This is the psychology running through the entire Islamic law of *ghanāʾim*: the community's leader distributes; the warrior's right is not to what his hand seized but to what just governance assigns. Al-Qurṭubī notes the report that the companions at Badr were so moved by this verse that they willingly surrendered even personal items taken from the battlefield, and that the Prophet ﷺ then confirmed their reward — echoing his later, famous refusal at Ḥunayn to keep *safāya*, the prize-items, for himself: *"Whoever took a camel from the spoils must return it, even if it is a needle or thread."* (Aḥmad; al-Bukhārī in related wording)

**Obey — "if you are believers": Faith as the Test It Passes**

The closing clause — "and obey God and His Messenger, *if* you are believers" — welds the incident to the sūrah's definition of faith in the next verses: belief is not demonstrated where taking is pleasant but where deferring to authority is painful. A claim relinquished in obedience is more eloquent about īmān than a victory shouted about on the field. Ibn Kathīr, adducing the report from Ibn ʿAbbās that ʿUmar himself said of the Badr spoils "they were a burden to us," reads the verse as divine education of a community newly entrusted with wealth and power: handle it as trustees, or it will handle you. In every age after — endowments, public funds, bonuses after a hard contract won — the verse keeps repeating its single, uncomfortable prescription: reverence God, repair the relations, obey the Guide, and let the pile itself go where grace directs.

## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:2

> **Only they are believers whose hearts quake with fear when God is mentioned, and when His signs are recited unto them, they increase them in faith, and they trust in their Lord,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Real Test of Belief Is After the Sermon**

Verse 1 demanded that men surrender a pile of loot to God's disposition; verse 2 now supplies the definition against which that demand is measured — as if to say: you claim to believe? Here is what believing looks like, and it has nothing to do with the spoils' size and everything to do with the heart's sensitivity. The construction is exclusive (*innamā*) — "only they are believers" — reserving the unqualified title of *mu'minūn* (the next verse adds "in truth") to four qualities: a heart that trembles at God's mention, a heart that grows at His verses, limbs that pray and give (v. 3), and a soul that relies. The sūrah's moral center of gravity is stated up front: before laws of war, before shares of booty, there must be living tissue in the chest, or the laws will only discipline corpses.

**Wajal: the Tremor of the Awakened Heart**

*Wajil* — trembling, apprehensive quaking — is not the word for terror of a predator (*khawf*), nor for awe of majesty in general (*hashyah*), though the Qur'an links them. *Wajal* is the shiver of a heart that knows it stands before the One who sees: the fear of a lover caught in negligence, more than the fear of a criminal caught in crime. The Qur'an elsewhere pairs exactly this quality with the truly knowing: *"Only those fear God, from among His servants, who have knowledge"* (35:28). That is why the verse says the heart trembles "when God is *mentioned*" — not when misfortune strikes, not when the enemy appears. The trigger is merely a Name, spoken in an ordinary gathering. Al-Rāzī asks in his commentary how a mere pronunciation of a name can shake a body, and answers: because the heart of the *mu'min* is not sealed against the meaning; when "God" is said, something real is present to it — as the sound of a beloved's footstep stirs a house where a sleeper lies awake. The dead feel nothing at any sound; the living feel everything at the right one. Two other sūrahs attest this same physiology of faith — *"those who, when God is mentioned, their hearts quake"* (22:35) and *"those who give what they give while their hearts are wary"* (23:60) — and at 5:83 the trembling overflows the eyes: *"when they hear what was sent down to the Messenger, you see their eyes overflow with tears."*

**"They Increase Them in Faith": a Doctrine in Three Words**

*Zādat-him īmānan* — the recitation of God's signs does not merely instruct the believer, it *increments* him. This clause became a pillar-text in the classical doctrine, affirmed by the majority of the community against the Murjiʾah, that faith is a living magnitude: it increases by obedience and deepened understanding and decreases by negligence. Al-Ṭabarī, explicating this verse, transmits from Ibn ʿAbbās and others that the Qur'an's passages mutually confirm one another — a sign witnessed with fresh insight adds certainty — and that the increase is real, not rhetorical. The logic is observable: a proposition believed abstractly and a proposition verified by experience are not the same mental state. The man who heard "God provides" as a doctrine and lived it in the lean years before Badr "believes" it in a way the new Muslim cannot. The mechanism the verse names — exposure to revelation producing growth in conviction — is also the reason the early community recited and re-recited sūrahs in prayer rather than exhausting them once: Scripture is not only information but an instrument, and an instrument that has no effect on the player is not yet in hands that know how to hold it. Ibn Taymiyyah pressed this against all who defined īmān as bare assent: if the Qur'an testifies to increase, an assent that can grow is a compound of knowledge, affirmation, feeling, and act — which is why the same signs that inflate a reverent heart can *harden* an unclean one, "and this increases them in great arrogance" (17:60; 74:31). Growth, not static possession, is the test of whether a faith is alive.

**Tawakkul: the Engine Room of Surrender**

The fourth quality: "and they place their trust in their Lord." *Tawakkul* comes from *wakala* — to depute, to hand a matter over to another in whom one places sufficiency. The believer who surrendered his claim on the spoils (v. 1) did an act of *tawakkul*: he traded a visible, disputable gain for an invisible Promise who does not lose a client. The Prophet ﷺ defined the proportion of this trust with the parable he himself gave:

> *"If you were to trust in God with true trust, He would provide for you as He provides for the bird: it goes out at daybreak with a longing belly and returns at dusk with a full one."* (al-Tirmidhī 2344, graded ḥasan; Ibn Mājah)

Note the bird *goes out* — the wings are not folded; trust cancels no labor, it cancels only anxiety and greed. Al-Qurṭubī links this verse's tawakkul to the earlier commands in the sūrah: it is precisely the one who truly deputizes God who can let go of the booty without bitterness and can meet an army of a thousand with three hundred and fourteen without panic — which is the next scene the sūrah will take up (vv. 5–9).

**A Mirror for Every Pious Hearing**

The verse's severity — "only they are believers" — is not meant to expel from the fold the one whose heart does not quake, but to diagnose. The tradition's own practice is confessionally honest on this point: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, whose faith no one in the community doubted, asked Ḥudhayfa "did the Messenger of God mention me among the hypocrites?" (al-Bukhārī 3736) — not from suspicion of Ḥudhayfa but from fear at his own indifference. If a man can hear "God," can sit through verses of warning, walk out unchanged, the verse asks the question the quarrel at Badr was posed to ask the whole army: is there anything in here at all? And its mercy is that it names the remedy as exposure: the signs, recited, *do* something — to the attentive, they add faith; to the careless, they add only the record of hearing. So the believer is taught to return, again and again, to the recitation, the gathering, the reminder — not to accumulate sermons, but to keep the heart's tremor alive, for a quivering heart is a live heart, and a live heart cannot both quake at God and hoard against His servants.

## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:3

> **who perform the prayer and spend from that which We have provided them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Halves of the Religious Life, One Breath**

Verses 2 and 3 are a single sentence in Arabic, and their unity is the point: the inner tremor and trust (v. 2) issue, necessarily, in two acts — prayer and spending. This pair recurs through the Qur'an as the shorthand for the whole of practiced faith (2:3; 4:39; 13:22; 22:35; 35:29; 42:38, as the source notes), because each half performs one axis of religion: *ṣalāh* is the vertical — the soul standing before God; *infāq* is the horizontal — the hand open toward God's creatures. A religion of prayer without spending breeds ascetics who starve their neighbors; a religion of spending without prayer breeds philanthropists who never bend the knee. The Qur'an requires both, in that order, with prayer first — because the hand that has not learned to be empty before God will never learn to be open before man.

**"They Perform the Prayer": the Verb That Never Rusted**

The Arabic uses the present tense, *yudīmūna al-ṣalāh*, a form the commentators (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr) consistently read as denoting continuity: they keep on establishing it. The verb for "giving" was *yusīfūna* in the same continuous form. The choice matters because the test of religion is not the storm-converted heart but the routine kept on the ordinary Tuesday. The Prophet ﷺ matched this grammar when asked which deed was best: *"Prayer at its early time"* — performed first, performed on time (al-Bukhārī 527; Muslim 85) — and he ﷺ said:

> *"The most beloved of deeds to God are the most constant, even if small."* (al-Bukhārī 6464; Muslim 783)

Why should *ṣalāh* head the believer's résumé, especially in a sūrah about war and spoils? Because prayer is the daily rehearsal of everything the sūrah demands. In ṣalāh the worshipper surrenders his time (a soldier's discipline of schedule), stands before authority (the posture of deference that killed the spoils quarrel), repeatedly breaks his pride (forehead to earth, the position no claimant can defend), and does it in rows, shoulder to shoulder with the man whose claim to loot he once disputed (37:1–3 — the *ṣāffāt* whose very name is the "ranked rows," an echo of the angelic ranks that aided at Badr, v. 9). The community that can stand in rows five times a day has, each day, five rehearsals of unity against greed.

**"And Spend from What We Have Provided Them": the Grammar of Trusteeship**

The verse does not say "from their wealth." It says *mimmā razaqnāhum* — "from that which We have provided them." In one prepositional phrase, ownership is re-written as receipt: what you spend is not yours, it is what God handed you, and He could have handed it to someone else. This is the same theology of surplus announced for the spoils in v. 1 — the pile belongs to God and His Messenger — now generalized: *all* pile does. Al-Qurṭubī draws out the ethical consequence: the spender should feel toward his wealth as a steward feels toward the goods in his lord's warehouse; the one truly giving is not the hand but the Provider, and the hand is honored merely as the conduit. The Prophet ﷺ sealed both halves — the humility of giving and the safety of trust — in one line:

> *"The upper hand is better than the lower hand, and begin with those you support; the best of charity is from a sufficed need; and whoever guards against greed, he will be guarded."* (al-Bukhārī 1427; Muslim 1034)

"From a sufficed need" — the spending that costs nothing is not yet the surrender of a claim; "whoever guards against greed is guarded" — the man who releases the surplus is released from the anxiety of surplus.

**Spending as the Test Case of the Quarrel**

Read in place, after v. 1's loot dispute, the verse acquires its sharpest edge: the men who could not agree about a handful of armor are hereby enrolled among true believers only if they become men who spend, and who spend *without being asked*, since it is God, the Provider, whose name is on the funds. Ibn ʿAbbās, as transmitted in the tafsīr tradition, glossed this generosy with the verse's own spirit: those are the believers "who give what is in their hands of God's right, not grudgingly, but trusting the recompense." The famous ḥadīth of the seven shaded on the day without shade includes precisely this quality in the hand: *"a man who gives charity so secretly that his left hand does not know what his right hand spends"* (al-Bukhārī 1421; Muslim 1031) — secret because the audience of true giving is the One, not the claimants. And the daily liturgy of the angels keeps the pair alive in the Muslim's morning:

> *"There is no day in which God's servants wake but that two angels descend, one saying: 'O God, give to the spender compensation,' and the other: 'O God, give to the withholdful destruction.'"* (al-Bukhārī 1442; Muslim 1010)

**The Economy the Sūrah Is Building**

Badr's thirty-odd martyrs had gone to meet the army with empty pockets and no equipment; the community's entire capacity for survival lay in men who could do what this verse says: hold the line in prayer and open the hand in spending. This is why the sūrah, before it legislates the fifth of spoils (v. 41), first re-roots the whole economy of the community in the doctrine of *rizq* — provision from God. A people who believe provision comes from God spend like trustees and divide like worshippers; a people who believe it comes from the sword hoard like owners and sue like claimants. Establish the first, and the dispute over anfāl has already been dissolved at its root — which is precisely the depth at which this sūrah resolves the quarrel that opened it.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:4

> **It is they who truly are believers. For them are ranks in the sight of their Lord, and forgiveness and a generous provision.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict on the Four Qualities**

Having described the believer's heart (tremor, growth, trust) and the believer's limbs (prayer, spending), God now delivers the verdict: *ulāʾika humu'l-mu'minūna ḥaqqān* — "those — they *are* the believers, in truth." The double emphasis (the demonstrative plus "in truth") marks a classification, not a courtesy: there is faith that is name and lineage, and faith that is *ḥaqq* — reality, fact, the thing itself. The same word *ḥaqq* that opened the field of Badr in the next verse ("thy Lord brought thee forth in truth") now labels the inner possession of the men who marched. The sūrah thus completes its first movement: quarrel over spoils (1), definition of the real (2–3), certification (4). Everything from v. 5 onward is the testing of this certification in the furnace of the event.

**Ranks: the Reorganization of All Human Pride**

*Darajāt* — degrees, steps, literal ascending floors, from a root used for stairways (2:259's ruined village raised and lowered; 6:165 "He made you successors… that He might try you in what He gave you"). The plural answers the plural of the problem: the men at Badr had competing claims, each group ranking itself above the other by its service. God's verdict re-maps hierarchy entirely: ranks are "in the sight of their Lord" — a scale with one calibrated point of reference. To rank "in His sight" is what no one can counterfeit, bribe, or lobby, and this is why the Qur'an pairs knowledge with degrees elsewhere: *"God raises those of you who believe and those given knowledge in degrees"* (58:11), and *"above everyone of knowledge is One who knows"* (12:76), and *"He is the Raiser of degrees"* (Rafīʿ al-darajāt, 40:15) — the very name, exegetes note, was revealed partly to humble the boastful (as in 25:23's warning against arrogance). The man cheated of his share at Badr is not the man without rank; he is the man whose rank is being computed in a currency the quarrellers cannot touch.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the degrees a geography as well as an arithmetic:

> *"In Paradise there are one hundred degrees which God prepared for the mujāhidūn in His path, and between every two degrees is as the distance between heaven and earth. So when you ask God, ask Him al-Firdaws."* (al-Bukhārī 2790, abridged; al-Tirmidhī 1944, in full, graded ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ)

The full version adds the detail that al-Firdaws is "the middle of Paradise and its highest part, above which is the throne of the Most Merciful" — the architecture of nearness. It is worth weighing what this text does to the grievance at Badr: the disputed items are camels, swords, and armor — depreciating, divisible, and gone within a lifetime; the promised items are *degrees*, infinite in number, indivisible by quarrel, and the gap between consecutive ones as far as sky from ground. Ibn Kathīr notes that the reward matches the renunciation: those who gave up the visible surplus for the Judge's distribution are answered with ranks precisely "in the sight of" that Judge.

**Forgiveness Before the Furnace: Why the Order Is Mercy**

Before any promise of reward, the verse promises *maghfirah* — covering, forgiveness — because the ranks are earned by the forgiven, not the perfect. The community being certified here is one that had just bickered over loot, just wavered at the prospect of battle (vv. 5–6), and would later be reproved for its taste for ransom (vv. 67–69). God's certificate for "the true believers" lists their condition first: what is blotted out. Al-Qurṭubī cites this verse among the proofs that forgiveness in the Qur'an often means *satr* — covering — before it means erasure: God does not merely delete the fault, He keeps it out of the light, so that a man enters his ranks with his Badr-foolishness unseen, even, as the tradition has it, by the companions who were there. The sequence forgiveness→provision, rather than provision→forgiveness, is also the divine economics reversed against the world's: the world gives the prize and then punishes the flaws discovered in the winner; God clears the file, then opens the estate.

**"A Generous Provision": Karīm — the Gift That Asks Nothing Back**

*Rizq karīm* appears a handful of times in the Qur'an (2:267, the best of it, for the spending commanded here; 33:31 for the women of the Prophet; 23:11 for inheritance of Paradise), and the philologists — whose notes al-Ṭabarī records — define *karīm* on the giver's side: one who gives without being asked, abundantly, without reproach after giving — the opposite of the spoils-claim, which is a give-and-take of demands. A *generous* provision, then, is one with no claw-back and no shame attached: unlike the world's prizes, which are typically awarded with an invoice ("you owe the distributor"), Paradise's provision is conferred as pure grace on people who earned it, and who nonetheless will be told, at the end of this very sūrah, that family bonds "have the stronger right in the ordinance of God" (v. 75) even in the distribution — grace organized, not chaos. Al-Rāzī makes the logical point that the threefold reward answers the threefold cost of verses 2–4: you gave up the claim to the surplus, so here are ranks (status, the first vanity); you quaked and constrained your nafs, so here is forgiveness (the second need, of the sinner); you spent your provision, so here is provision (the third: replacement, promised in the angelic duʿāʾ of v. 3's ḥadīth). Every renunciation named by the Qur'an receives in this verse its exact invoice of return.

**Ḥaqqān: the Certification Men Can Still Fail Forward**

Notice finally the humility built into the structure: God certifies them, but He certifies *qualities* — the trembling, the growth, the trust, the prayer, the spending — so that the certificate is renewable by return to the qualities. Ḥudhayfa's reported answer to ʿUmar's anxious question (al-Bukhārī 3736) belongs here too: ʿUmar was terrified not of losing some tribal rank but of the name "hypocrite," and the tradition treats his fear as a *higher* station, because fear of the verdict and renewal of the qualities is exactly how *darajāt* keep climbing. The lesson the sūrah plants in all who hear it: measure yourself by v. 2–3's list, leave the ledger of spoils to v. 1's Owner, and the ranks will accumulate on the one scale that finally matters.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:5

> **[Remember] how thy Lord brought thee forth from thy home in truth, while a group of the believers were averse,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exodus No One Wanted to Take**

The sūrah now wheels back from principles to the event that engendered them — Badr — and to the moment *before* the event, when the army was still deciding whether to march. "Remember how your Lord brought you forth from your houses upon a *ḥaqq* (a reality), while a group among you was averse." The scene: the Prophet ﷺ had set out to intercept Abū Sufyān's returning caravan — a commercial-military stroke that would have bled Quraysh economically with minimal blood. Abū Sufyān turned the caravan back toward the coast and sent Quraysh a desperate summons; the Makkans mustered roughly a thousand armored men to defend their property and their prestige. The Muslims, some three hundred and fourteen, found themselves face to face at Badr with a force three times their number — not the unarmed train they had planned against. It is at this pivot, when the mission had silently changed from raid to battle, that a group among the believers *kariha* — disliked, shrank, was averse. The verse immortalizes their reluctance without naming them: they were believers, and they were averse, and the Qur'an tells us both at once.

**Ḥaqq on One Side, Karāhah on the Other: Two Kinds of Knowledge**

The word doing the theological work is *ḥaqq*: God brought them forth "upon the truth." Here *ḥaqq* fuses three senses the commentators draw out — the *command* was true (issued by God, not whim), the *promise* was true (victory had been assured), and the *thing itself* was about to become true (the battle was no longer an option among options but the single road ahead). Against this stands *karāhah* — aversion, the contraction of the soul from what it does not want. Al-Rāzī poses the obvious question: how could believers, who had just been certified in vv. 2–4 as trembling at God's mention, be averse to a march commanded by that same God? His answer — and it is the psychological core of the verse — is that the two knowings are not one: they *knew* the command as a report, but did not yet *taste* it as reality; the heart certifies at a distance what the limbs must perform up close. Aversion is not disbelief; it is knowledge not yet metabolized. That is why the Qur'an keeps this word on the believers' record rather than exiling them with the hypocrites' vocabulary — and why it also refuses to pretend the aversion didn't happen. Real faith can include a real shrinking; what it must not do is let the shrinking steer.

**Shūrā Under Revelation: How the Prophet Led the Unwilling**

The sīrah reports — and the exegetes cite it against this verse's backdrop — that the Prophet ﷺ consulted his Companions precisely when the objective changed. Al-Mughīrah spoke first for the emigrants; then Saʿd ibn Muʿādh, chief of the Ansār, said what the moment required:

> *"O Messenger of God, proceed to whatever God has guided you to; by Him in whose hand is my soul, if you led us across the sea and we dove into it with you, not one of us would remain behind."* (Aḥmad; classed as sound chain by Ibn Hajar; al-Ṭabarī narrates its like)

And the tradition preserved in Muslim's Ṣaḥīḥ of the battle of Badr — through ʿUmar's report that some Companions said, as the people of Mūsā said, *"Go forth, you and your Lord, and fight"* (5:24) — and the Prophet's reply that he had been promised one of the two parties and would take the *sharḥ* (hardship) of it: *"If I do not find anyone with me, I will go out alone against them; indeed, my Lord is with me; He will guide me and be with me."* The Prophet ﷺ then, in the famous scene, kept repeating his supplication until his cloak fell from his shoulders:

> *"O God, grant me what You promised me! O God, if this party of Muslims is destroyed, You will not be worshipped in the earth."* (Muslim 1763, from Ibn ʿAbbās; al-Bukhārī 3951)

Leadership under aversion is displayed here in full: no coercion of the reluctant, no concealment of the stakes, consultation first, then prayer when the decision is made — the exact operational sequence the sūrah's ethics of *iṣlāḥ* and *tawakkul* prescribe.

**Why God Does Not Cancel the Dislike**

The verse's phrasing — *min al-muʾminīn*, "a group *of the believers*" — has been weighed carefully by the commentators. Al-Qurṭubī reports the plain ruling that this aversion was blameworthy and its object was fixed, but that the men remained within faith; several even say the verse comforts the timid conscience: you may be averse and still be carried along by truth. This is a law of human motivation the modern reader can test: the wish to avoid danger is wired deeper than the belief that the danger is just. Qur'anic psychology does not demand the extinction of the wish — it demands that the wish not hold the reins. God "brought them forth" anyway; the verbs of the verse are divine causatives, and the army marched despite its flinch. That, the sūrah will show in the following verses, is the very condition under which the victory became *theirs* — not because they had no fear, but because they fought while carrying it, with sleep sent down on top of the fear (v. 11) and firmness sent down through the angels (v. 12).

**A Home and Its Lessons**

*"From thy home"* — out of Madinah, out of the settled, irrigated, contracted life, into a gravelly waterless basin where every hour of decision was exposed. Each community has its Badr march: the obligation that arrives after the easier plan collapses — the reconciliation that must be attempted though every nerve objects (v. 1's *iṣlāḥ* was itself one such march), the testimony to give, the debt to collect honestly, the duty to assume without a raise. The verse's command "remember" is for all such hours: first, mark that the command was *ḥaqq* — reality does not consult our appetite before arriving; second, mark that even the elect shrank — so shrinkage does not disqualify you from being led; third, mark how the Leader of that army behaved when his men were averse — consulted, decided, prayed, and did not let one man's flinch veto the column. The group that disliked was given a place in the greatest day of Islamic history anyway, and the Qur'an keeps their dislike on the record — forever — so that no future believer mistakes the trembling of his own limbs for the end of his story.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:6

> **disputing with thee concerning the truth after it had become clear, as though they were being driven to death as they looked on.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**When the Argument Is No Longer About the Facts**

The participle carries the verse forward from v. 5's reluctance into the next, uglier stage: not merely shrinking, but *ujādilu-naka* — disputing, wrangling with the Prophet ﷺ — "concerning the truth *min baʿdi mā tabayyana*" — after it had become manifest. The syntax isolates the moral crime: not fear (vv. 5, 11 treat that humanly), but argument *after clarity*. To dispute a matter while evidence is being assembled is due diligence; to dispute it after it has become *tabayyana* — made white, distinguished from its opposite, the technical term in the Qur'an for a clarified ruling (as in 2:187, "until the white thread is distinguished from the black thread") — is to keep litigating a verdict already rendered. The verb *j-d-l* in fact means to twist the ropes of argument (al-Rāzī notes its root sense of plaiting and counter-plaiting strands), the rope-work of men manufacturing reasons after the reason has already arrived. Al-Qurṭubī, transmitting the reports on this verse, states its point plainly: *lūma 'alayhim* — they were censured not for being afraid but for contending with the Messenger about what God had already settled.

**"As Though Driven to Death While Watching": the Anatomy of Panic**

The image that closes the verse is one of the Qur'an's most precise portraits of dread: *ka-annamā yusāqūna ilā'l-mawti wa-hum yanzurūn* — as if they were being *driven* — the verb used for herding animals to the place of slaughter — *toward death, and they are watching*. Not death arriving blind, but death seen coming, at the end of one's own tether. Two things make the phrase surgical. First, *yusāqūn* is passive: they feel not like agents marching but like cargo being hauled; panic converts a volunteer into a conscript in his own mind. Second, *yanzurūn* — the open eyes: anticipatory terror, the preview worse than the film. Modern psychology has the same insight in its clinical vocabulary: fear of an anticipated harm exceeds harm in prospect; the shaking hand on the doorknob hurts more than the door. The Qur'an's diagnosis of the pre-Badr argument, then, is that the disputations were not about strategy at all — the strategists had already lost; the arguments were the *noise* panic makes when it needs reasons. A man who believes he is being driven to slaughter will say anything that might keep the tether slack. Every community since has known this species of debate: meetings where the decision is fixed and the objections are theater for the conscience, resistance to the required vaccine of sacrifice because the fear cannot walk, eyes open, to where it must.

**The Cure God Prescribed for This Sickness**

Note the order of what actually followed for the flinching army, because it is the sūrah's own answer to this verse: God did not rebuke them into courage; He *sedated* and *armed* them with graces — sleep descending over them like a security blanket (v. 11), water purifying and firming the ground under their feet (v. 11), the angels' whisper of firmness into their hearts (v. 12). Mercy met the panic at the level at which panic lives — the body, the senses, the nervous system — and the Qur'an records this as legislation for leaders: a frightened person under a settled command is treated with certainty, comfort, and support, then marched. The dispute, meanwhile, is not answered with more argument, because the argument was never the problem; *tabayyana* had already answered that.

**"The Truth" — and Why Believers' Disputes Still Count**

One should weigh how severe this verse is in its placement. The disputers are, in v. 5, expressly "a group *of the believers*," and yet the Qur'an describes them as disputing about *al-ḥaqq* itself — and the word *ḥaqq* is the load-bearing term of this whole passage (vv. 5, 6, 7, 8: the truth, the promise, the verifying of the truth). What makes Badr's episode normative is precisely that the sin at its edge is not unbelief but *bargaining with clarity*. That is why the early scholars debated whether the disputers were the hypocrites of Madinah (some, like al-Farrāʾ's school, inclined this) or real believers in a real fault (the tenor of al-Ṭabarī's and Ibn Kathīr's reports, which keep the men among the companions of the Badr reward while preserving the censure). The sound middle is the Qur'an's own presentation: the men are called believers, their act is called dispute about truth, and both are recorded together as a permanent instruction — that īmān does not make its possessor immune to argument-after-clarification, but argument-after-clarification damages īmān. The measure of a mature believer is not the absence of objection but the presence of a stopping point: when the matter has *tabayyana*, the true heart stops twisting the rope and walks, eyes open, into the commanded danger — and finds, as the rest of this sūrah shows, that the door it entered was the door to the one victory in Arab history whose date God Himself called a "Day of Differentiation" (v. 41, *yawm al-furqān*).

**Application the Verse Itself Invites**

Two commands for later communities fall directly out of the description. For the follower: distinguish between raising an objection *before* the decision and raising one *after* — the first is counsel the Prophet ﷺ himself solicited (v. 5's shūrā), the second is the "driving" panic this verse names. The Companions' own handbook on this was the Prophet's line reported concerning the sort of man described here: *"There comes a people at the end of time, young in age, weak in reasoning, who speak with the best of speech of creatures… faith does not pass their throats"* (al-Bukhārī 6931; Muslim 1066, of the Khawārij, who likewise argued after clarity with the text in their teeth). For the leader: expect the flinch, do not expect flincher's arguments to be about the issue; decide on *ḥaqq*, then treat the fear itself with mercy, sleep, water, and firmness — as Badr's Leader did.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:7

> **And [remember] when God promised you that one of the two companies was to be yours, and you wished that the unarmed one would be yours. But God desires to verify the truth through His Words, and to cut off the last remnant of the disbelievers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Promise, the Preference, and the Larger Design**

The Muslims had left Madinah with a merchant caravan in their sights; the Qur'an now discloses what stood behind the plan: *wa-idh yaʿiduku Allāhu itha't-tāʾifati annahā lakum* — "when God promised you that one of the two companies would be yours." The two *ṭāʾifatān* were the caravan under Abū Sufyān — rich, soft, lightly guarded — and the Quraysh relief army under Abū Jahl — armed, proud, three times their number. The promise was deliberately open: one *or* the other would fall to them. The believers, "with their own hands," as it were, voted for the soft option: *tawaddadtum anna ghāta ʿayri dhāti'l-shawkati takūnu lakum* — you wished the unguarded one to be yours. Verse 7 names this wish without calling it disbelief and without calling it innocuous: it is the preference of a heart that has not yet seen what God is doing with history.

**Ghayr Dhāt al-Shawka: the Word That Says "No Thorns"**

*Shawkah* literally means thorn, prickliness; *dhāt al-shawka*, "the possessor of thorns," is Arabic idiom for the armed, the dangerous, the hedged-about. The phrase's genius is botanical: what has thorns protects what grows behind them, and a life with no thorns has usually grown nothing worth taking either. The caravan was *ghayr dhāt al-shawka* — no thorns, no battle, profit without risk; and the community that consistently chooses the thornless option accumulates comfort and loses history. Ibn Kathīr glosses the verse with the incident's own aftermath: you wanted the easy gain; God wanted the decisive stroke, and He took the second option and gave, besides victory, the wealth of the first, for the caravan escaped — yet the spoils of the army contained much of what the caravan carried, and the believers got both the dignity and the goods, in God's sequencing, not theirs. This is a general law the sūrah keeps restating: seek the *ḥaqq*, and let the provision arrive on God's schedule; engineer the provision, and you may lose both.

**"God Desires to Verify the Truth Through His Words"**

The pivot of the verse is theological: *wa-yurīdu Allāhu an yuḥiqqa'l-ḥaqqa bi-kalimātihi* — God wills to make the truth *ḥaqq* — real, actual, concrete — *through His words*. The *kalimāt* here, say the commentators (al-Ṭabarī, al-Rāzī), are both the promises he had sent down ("one of the two companies is yours") and the commands that trained an army on the strength of a promise. Divine speech in this worldview is not commentary on events; it is an instrument that manufactures events, as in the first of all creative words, *Kun* — "Be." The logic deserves tracing: a promise about a future victory, honored, converts the *word* into the most trustworthy datum in the community's ledger; every subsequent word of God — the unseen, the Hereafter, the harder commands — rides on the credit earned by this one. That is why Badr functions in the Qur'an as a credential: 3:123 will name it "a sign," and this sūrah will call its date *yawm al-furqān*, the Day of Differentiation (v. 41), the day when God's speech and the swords of thirty hundred men agreed so exactly that all Arabia recalculated.

**"And to Cut Off the Last Remnant" — War as Surgery, Not Vengeance**

The second clause of God's intention reads, in the Study Qur'an's own terms cited here, as "a justification for the use of force": *wa-yaqṭa dābir al-kāfirīn* — to cut off the rear, the last remnant, of the disbelievers. The image is military and surgical at once: what is being removed is the *tail* of a fleeing enemy — the part that would regroup, rearm, and return. The objective named is not the destruction of persons and not the seizure of the caravan; it is that the structural threat to the young community — Meccan armed supremacy — be severed. The source commentary's note is worth keeping in view: in the classical scale of necessities (*ḍarūriyyāt*), religion's protection is foremost, and 22:40 grounds God's "repelling of some people by others" in the survival of monasteries, churches, synagogues, and mosques. Badr, in the verse's own reading of itself, was such a repelling: an army raised to exterminate a creed was met before it could reach the creed. A war with a surgical *telos* — the end of the threat, not the humiliation of the men — is why the very same verses that command the strike forbid the hoard (v. 1) and command the release of prisoners with tenderness (vv. 67–70). Force that aims at *ḥaqq* and stops when the remnant is cut is bounded by its own definition; vengeance has no such terminus.

**Two Wills in One Hour**

Finally, let the verse's quiet structure be marked: "you wished… but God desires." The two wills stood in the same hour, over the same gravel, and the difference between *wudd* (mere preference) and *irādah* effective in history is the whole lesson. The community that surrendered the spoils (v. 1) and trembled at the Name (v. 2) is here, in the middle of its worst instinct, shown how its wish was small and how God's design ended — within a decade — with the very army they feared entering Makkah's sanctuary, unarmed and victorious. Read against that, the verse speaks to every later hour when the thornless option glitters: prefer the truth, expect the thorns, and let the Provider of outcomes be the One you consult about which company you will meet — for the promise, once honored at Badr, is a promise kept in its own account forever.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:8

> **so that He may verify the truth and prove falsehood to be false, though the guilty be averse.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Clause of Purpose That Repeats to Teach**

Verse 7 said God wills *to verify the truth and cut off the remnant*; verse 8 restates, with a twist: *li-yuḥiqqa'l-ḥaqqa wa-yubṭila'l-bāṭila wa-law kariha'l-mujrimūn* — so that He may make the truth real and render the false *bāṭil*, null and void — even though the criminally averse hate it. Repetition in the Qur'an is rarely redundancy; here the second pass adds the missing term. Verse 7 named the enemy's *force* (the remnant to be cut); verse 8 names the enemy's *claim* (the falsehood to be voided). Badr was not chiefly about killing Quraysh — ninety dead is a small number for what the world remembers of it — but about the day an idea was refuted: the idea that God's Messenger was a Madinan raiding chief whose movement would collapse under the first real army. After Badr, that reading of Islam was *mubṭal* — legally void, like a contract annulled — and the universe's court had issued the annulment.

**Yuḥiqqa and Yubṭil: the Two Verbs of Cosmic Litigation**

Both verbs are causative-form, and both belong to the courtroom vocabulary of the Qur'an. *Yuḥiqqa* — to cause the *ḥaqq* to become *ḥaqq* in act: to make an abstraction an asset. *Yubṭil* — to cause *bāṭil* to be *bāṭil*: not to destroy falsehood's existence (the Qur'an never claims falsehood vanishes from the world) but to void its *force*, the way a law can void a forged deed without burning every forgery. Al-Rāzī weighs this and draws the conclusion that fits the sūrah: God did not annihilate Quraysh at Badr — they returned, fought again at Uḥud, and besieged Madinah at the Trench; what Badr annihilated was their *standing*, their argument, the coherence of their cause. Falsehood in this worldview behaves like a debt: it appears solvent, trades on confidence, and is discovered worthless on the day of audit. The Qur'an's metaphysic of truth and falsehood is thus not a claim that the true always wins battles, but that the true is *real* and the false is *unreal* — and reality, on a long enough field, is what wins. The verse makes Badr a receipt for that theorem.

**The Oddity in the Grammar: "Though the Guilty Be Averse" — and Why It Stays**

The closing phrase — *wa-law kariha'l-mujrimūn*, "even if the criminals hate it" — has two functions, one descriptive, one prescriptive. Descriptively, it notes that history's pivot was enacted over the active opposition of the guilty: no consent of the powerful produced this, which removes from the truth any dependence on popularity. Prescriptively, and more subtly, it is the third appearance of *karāhah* in four verses (v. 5: a group of believers was averse; v. 6: they disputed; v. 8: the criminals are averse). The same word, shared by saint and sinner. The difference is not in feeling aversion but in who gets the last act: the guilty act on theirs (crime — *ijram*, the root here, means to earn guilt); the believers carry theirs and march anyway (vv. 11–16). The sūrah's moral psychology is astonishingly non-puritanical in this: it does not promise the faithful a heart with no dislikes; it promises them the truth *despite* them, and asks only that they not make their dislikes into policies. Al-Ṭabarī records under this verse the reading that the clause consoles the believers themselves: "even though the guilty detest it" — i.e., do not measure the truth's validity by the hatred of its enemies; hatred is the truth's normal atmosphere.

**A Law of Historical Motion, Not a One-Off Miracle**

That the verse is cast in the open-ended form of divine purpose (*li-yuḥiqqa… wa-yubṭila*, with no terminal object) signals to the commentators that the Badr event instantiates a standing law: whenever a community supplies the conditions — the prayer and spending of v. 3, the trust of v. 2 — God runs the same operation: He substantiates the true and voids the false, and He does so *against* the aversion of those with a stake in the lie. This is why the sūrah will spend verses 39–40, 65–66 on the numbers and ratios that make the law operational, and why it ends the Badr passage (v. 17) by relocating the victory from sword-arm to divine act. The logic is plain and falsifiable in the ordinary sense: ideas backed by the guilty persist only so long as the guilty's *means* persist; when the means are cut (v. 7's *dābir*), the idea is tried for what it is — and *bāṭil*, being structurally unreal, cannot pay its debts. The day such an audit is scheduled is called, in this sūrah's own vocabulary, a *furqān* — a differentiation — and the verse leaves no doubt about who runs the test and who fails it.

**For the Believer Who Hears This Verse in the Mosque**

Practically, 8:8 is an antibody against two infections of the heart: the infection of envy toward the successful guilty — "how can falsehood flourish?" — answered by *yubṭil*: flourish is not solvency; and the infection of despair when truth is hated — answered by *wa-law kariha'l-mujrimūn*: hatred is not disproof. The man who has just lost his spoils claim to God's redistribution (v. 1), who marched while averse (v. 5), who is watching his own army shrink against a mailed thousand, is handed here his point of view: stand inside the purpose-clause. The field's real contest is not three hundred against a thousand, but *ḥaqq* against *bāṭil* — and that contest is not undecided; it was decided before the armies were counted, "though the guilty be averse." The rest of the sūrah — the angels in ranks, the sleep, the water, the struck necks — is only the weather in which an already-issued verdict is served.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:9

> **When you sought succor from your Lord, He responded to you, “I shall aid you with a thousand angels rank upon rank.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Cry the Qur'an Does Not Embarrass To Record**

*Idh tastaghīthūna rabbakum* — "when you were crying out for help from your Lord." The imperfect tense paints the scene still in motion: three hundred and fourteen men, water scarce, armor scarce, outnumbered three to one, and their Leader on his knees in the sand, repeating, until his cloak fell from his shoulders, *"O God, grant me what You promised! O God, if this party is destroyed, You will not be worshipped on the earth"* (Muslim 1763; al-Bukhārī 3951). The Qur'an tells this without apology, and the placement after verses 5–6 (the aversion, the dispute) is a deliberate instruction in what *duʿāʾ* is: the prayer that succeeds the flinch, the cry of men who have already decided to fight and now admit they cannot win. The root *gh-w-th*, to be drowned — *istighāthah* is the call of a drowning man; only after it comes *istijābah*, the response. The sequence, not the sentiment, is the law: first the cry, then the ranks of heaven. Note too the confession inside the grammar: the army "sought succor from your *Lord*" — they did not ask the Meccan soothsayers, or their poets, or their numbers. When all horizontal resources fail, the vertical line is the only line still open, and this sūrah wants every later community to know which line the believers of Badr used first.

**"A Thousand Angels Rank Upon Rank"**

The Arabic for the formation is *muṣayyabīn* (in the reading of ʿĀṣim and others) or *murḍifīn* — rows following rows, reinforcements closing up behind. Commentators derive *taṣyīb* from *ṣawb* (arrow, rain from above), yielding "rank upon rank," and *irḍāf* from *radīf* (a rider mounted behind another on the same horse — the Arab image for relay reinforcement). The imagery is chosen to say: the heaven that answers does not send a scattering; it sends organization, a formation, ranks that back one another. This is the answer to the army's arithmetic problem — they had counted three to one; God answers in units of deployment, not in taunts. The famous question posed to Ibn ʿAbbās belongs here: a man asked how to reconcile this verse's *a thousand* with the three thousand and five thousand of 3:124–125, and he answered:

> *"He sent them for you at Badr as a thousand, and at Uḥud as three thousand, and at Uḥud as five thousand — but they were not to fight after the Prophet's wound."* (Muslim 1762, from Abū al-Zubayr's questioning of him)

The Uḥud verses were revealed about Badr's promise — the three thousand of 3:124 and the five thousand of 3:125 being, in this sound report, the escalating promise tied to the same battle, or its twin — while 8:9 preserves the number actually responded to the Badr cry. The reconciliation itself is instructive: the angels' deployment is graded by the need, and the need is graded by the community's resolve.

**Did Angels Fight? the Debate and Its Lesson**

Whether the *imdād* (aid) was martial — blades swung — or moral — hearts steadied — is one of the classic disputes of this passage. The reports on the ground are numerous: Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqas testified that at Badr he saw "men in white armor, between heaven and earth, fighting beside me" (Muslim 1770, from his son ʿUmar's question to him: "O father, did you see that?" "Yes — they were angels"), and ʿAlī's report, "at Badr we saw a man fighting ahead of us, in white armor, and he was the best of the fighters — by God, it was an angel" (Aḥmad, classed sound). Against these, the source commentary notes the pull of this verse's own sequel: God made the aid "only as a glad tiding… that your hearts may be at peace" (v. 10), suggesting the aid's *function* was on the inner battlefield. Al-Rāzī resolves in the direction most of the muḥaqqiqūn leaned: there is no contradiction — the aid was real, and the mode is what the tradition reports diversely; what all agree upon is that the aid's certainty, not the aid's mechanics, is the object of faith here. The sober implication for the reader: the angels are not in the verse to make up the manpower shortage for us to calculate — they are there to make the point that *creation mobilizes for the believer who mobilizes for God*, a grammar the Qur'an states outright elsewhere: *"If you help God, He will help you and firm up your feet"* (47:7).

**"He Responded": the Theology of the Answer Before the Ask Is Finished**

The verse places the *response* before the details: "you called, He answered." The past-tense certainty is the Prophet's own reported confidence in the same hour — the sīrah tradition has him pointing to the enemy's positions on the eve of battle: *"This is the place of such-and-such, this is the place of such-and-such — the death-place of such-and-such"* (Muslim 1777, from Ḥudhayfa) — as though the roll of honor and the roll of the slain were already filed. A community that has just been reprimanded for disputing about a promise (v. 6) is now shown the promise mid-honoring: the angels are not contingent on the army's steadiness; they arrived at the call, and the army's job in verses 15–16 will be to stand still enough to deserve what was already on its way. This is the same logic as the ḥadīth qudsī in al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

> *"God says: I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he remembers Me; if he remembers Me in himself, I remember him in Myself; if he remembers Me in a gathering, I remember him in a better gathering than theirs."* (al-Bukhārī 7405; Muslim 2675)

**Where the Glory Was Being Placed**

Finally, notice what the verse is *not* for: it is not proof that faith guarantees angelic reinforcements in every battle — Uḥud proved the opposite when the ranks broke, and the Qur'an there (3:123–128) uses Badr precisely as the comparison. The ʿAṭāʾ-like wisdom in this verse's placement — one verse after the "though the guilty be averse" and two verses before "victory comes from God alone" (v. 10) — is to fence the miracle in: the angels are sent, and the sending is a *gift*, not a mechanism. Gifts confirm the Giver; the moment they are treated as mechanisms — deployed, tallied, relied upon — they withdraw, and "the aid" of v. 9 mutates into a spoil. This is exactly the disease the sūrah was opened to cure (v. 1): treating the divine as a divisible asset. So heaven's answer was given as ranks — a formation, to be obeyed, not apportioned — and the whole army was led to the next verse's clause, which is the real summary of the battle: *wal-nuṣru illā min ʿindi'llāh*, and victory is from none but God.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:10

> **And God made it not save as a glad tiding, and that your hearts may be at peace thereby. And victory comes from God alone; truly God is Mighty, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**What Miracles Are For**

The verse takes the thunderbolt of v. 9 — a thousand angels in ranks — and calmly explains its purpose: *wa-mā jaʿalahu Allāhu illā bushrā wa-li-taṭmaʾinna bihi qulūbukum* — God made it nothing but a glad tiding and a quieting of hearts. A miracle in the Qur'an's theory is never a force that wins a battle; it is a signal that steadies the people who must. The angelic aid existed to change the *hearts on the Muslim side*, not to substitute for the arms. This is one of the most disciplined sentences in the whole Badr narrative: every religion has been tempted to read its wonders as magic — events that make human effort optional. The verse forecloses that reading absolutely. The miracle's cash value was information and reassurance; the battle itself would still be fought by hands, at the cost of seventy dead and seventy bound.

**Bushrā: the News That Reorganizes the Body**

*Bushrā* — glad tidings — shares its root with *bashar*, skin/flesh, because, as ʿArab philologists note, good news makes the flesh glow, puts color on the skin; and with *basr*, sight, because joy sharpens the eye. Al-Qurṭubī stresses that the word's semantic field covers news given *before* the thing comes, which is its psychological force: dread is always a forecast of bad outcomes, and revelation's answer is to replace the dread-forecast with a tiding, then let the body catch up with what the heart has been given. The next verse will make this physiological: God "covered you with drowsiness — a security from Him" (v. 11). News that calms the heart calms the nerves, then the muscles, then — in the middle of an ambush site at dawn — even the sleeplessness. This is why the entire Qur'an's self-description leans on the same two poles: *bashīran wa-nadhīran*, bearer of glad tiding and Warner; a book that works on the will by working first on fear.

**Victory Is from God Alone: the Sentence at the End of the Friday Sermon**

*Wa-mā'n-nuṣru illā min ʿindi'llāh* — and there is no victory except from God. The commentators on the Prophet's practice record that this sentence, and its twin from Ṣaff (61:14), became the closing words of the Friday sermon in his time — the *khaṭībah* traditionally ends with *"inna al-naṣra min ʿindi'llāh"* — so that the whole community hears, each week, the clause that capped its first victory. Its placement is a permanent fence against the two errors that ambush every successful army: the error of the self ("we won it") and the error of the tool ("the angels won it"). The verse names neither swords nor seraphim as the cause — the aid was a *tiding* — and locates the victory in the *ʿind* (presence) of God, meaning in the counsels and decree of God. Al-Rāzī draws out the aqidah-point: causes are real, and the Qur'an lists them — steadfastness, ranks, water, numbers — yet the conferral of the outcome belongs to no cause; hence the same tradition that ordered "prepare against them whatever strength you can" (v. 60) also says "you did not kill them, but God killed them" (v. 17). The two verses are one doctrine, and the doctrine is the middle road between passivity and idolatry of means: work with the totality of causes, trust in the Owner of results.

**Mighty, Wise: the Two Names That Hold the Paradox**

The verse seals itself with *al-ʿAzīz, al-Ḥakīm* — Mighty, Wise; and the pairing answers precisely what Badr looked like from outside. Mighty: a barefoot 314 defeating mailed 1000 is an act of *ʿizza*, unconstrainable power that no sociology explains — the verse refuses to let the believers reduce God to a superior general; He is the Invincible, and invincibility is what made the impossible deployment possible. Wise: and simultaneously *ḥikmah* — every detail of the day (which well was seized, how the rain fell, which nobles were killed, which were ransomed, which were released) sits in a weave the sūrah itself will spend seventy verses untying; the ninety dead at Badr were, in the divine wisdom, the seeding of fourteen centuries. The two names keep running together throughout this sūrah (vv. 1, 5, 7, 8, 10, 17, 19, 41, 67…), which is the sūrah's signature: a community holding power and wealth for the first time must believe equally in the Might that gives the victory and the Wisdom that disposes of it — or Might will curdle into arrogance and Wisdom into fatalism.

**The Peace That Was the Real Event of Badr**

Read now with a cool eye what "that your hearts may be quieted" achieved: at Badr, the Muslim community crossed the threshold of its own psychology. Before the battle, they believed they would die; the verse tells them God engineered the day so that they would *not* need the courage of fanatics — only the peace of the reassured. A heart at rest under danger sees the ground, keeps formation, does not swing wildly at shadows, does not break to loot — and the whole sūrah is the legislation of just those three perils (formation, vv. 15–16; no looting before the judge's share, vv. 1, 41; the ransom debate, vv. 67–69). The tiding and the tranquility were not decoration on the victory; in the verse's own account, they are the victory's machinery. Everything else — the swords, the angelic ranks — was the visible language in which it was spoken. So 8:10 does what it says: it quiets. A believer who has just taken a loss, just been overruled, just watched victory go to a people he disputes with, is handed here the only news that steadies: glad tidings first, rest for the heart, and — before any tally of who deserved what — the sentence every Friday pulpit still repeats at the end: *victory is from God alone; He is Mighty, Wise.*

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:11

> **Behold, He covered you with sleepiness, security from Him, and sent down upon you water from the sky to purify you thereby, to remove the defilement of Satan from you, to fortify your hearts, and to make firm your steps thereby.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Grace Arrives on the Body's Own Terrain**

*Idh yughshīkumu'l-nuʿāsa amanan minhu* — "when He overshadowed you with drowsiness, a security from Him." The night before Badr, the most anxious night in the community's history, was spent, the Qur'an says, in sleep — a sleep so indiscriminate and heavy that the tradition (through Anas, and in al-Ḥākim's and Aḥmad's marfūʿ reports) describes a sword falling from a man's hand as he dozed, his hand too limp to catch it. This is the verse's first miracle and its most human one: not fire, not armies, but *sedation*. The psychology is exact. Fear that has nothing to do is corrosive — it rehearses disaster all night, and an army that cannot sleep cannot fight; so God treated the fear at its physiological root. *Nuʿās* — drowsiness — is the body's amnesty from the mind, and the verse labels the amnesty *aman*, security: tranquility, in this sūrah, is not a feeling one manufactures; it is a garment thrown over you from above (the verb *yughshīkum* — to cover, to overshadower — the same imagery as the *sakīnah*, the "sent-down calm," of 48:4 and 9:26). The believers of v. 2 whose hearts quake at God's mention are here shown the other half of the contract: the same God who puts tremor in the heart at His Name puts sleep in the eyes at His hour. Reverence and rest are not opposites; the sleep of the trustful is the practical residue of tawakkul.

**The Water: Five Purposes Stacked in One Sentence**

The second half of the verse is a masterpiece of consequential layering: rain sent down *li-yuṭahhirakum bihi* — to purify you — *wa-yudhhiba ʿankum rijz al-shayṭān* — to remove from you the filth/whisper of Satan — *wa-yarbiṭa ʿalā qulūbikum* — to tie down, fortify, your hearts — *wa-yuthabbita bihi'l-aqdām* — and to make the footsteps firm by it. Four "lams," one raindrop, and the movement is deliberately staged from outer to inner and back to outer: physical purity (they had been in a state requiring ablution; some reports say they were junub from the previous night), ritual purity as the removal of *rijz al-shayṭān* (Satan's filth — the whisper of despair that comes to a thirsty, anxious man at night, "you will die of thirst tomorrow before you die of the sword"; al-Ṭabarī transmits that the "filth of Satan" includes both the ritual impurity whose cure is water and the *waswās* whose cure is the water's reminders), then the *heart* (the rain sound beating down the panic — note how tightly this verse and v. 10's "that your hearts be quieted" interlock: glad tidings for the heart's content, rain for the heart's case), and finally the *feet* (the sand of Badr, soft underfoot, was flattened and hardened by the rain so they could stand; on the Quraysh side, the reports say the same rain turned their gravel to slick mud that swallowed hooves). A single weather event, read from four levels at once: hygiene, ritual, morale, terrain. Al-Rāzī asks what the verse's point is in stacking purposes this way, and the answer his commentary distills: to teach that God's favors are *multilayered objects* — the rain that lets you pray is the same rain that lets you stand, and the same rain that lets you stand is the whisper-proof on your heart. ingratitude fails precisely where it sees only one layer.

**"Water from the Sky": the Sky as God's Warehouse**

*Min al-samāʾ* — from the heaven/sky. The phrase is literal (rain) and liturgical at once, because in the Qur'an's idiom the sky is the direction of descent for everything that heals: water, iron (57:25, "We sent down iron"), the Book itself ("sent down" — tanzīl), sakīnah (48:4). The sūrah has just described angels descending in ranks (v. 9); now water descends; the two descents share a verb and a source, and the verse therefore quietly reclassifies the rain: on the night before Badr, weather was *revelation in another register* — the same generosity arriving by a different conduit. The Study-Qur'an commentary notes this double valence well: the water "from the sky" purifies body and soul together, and ablution (wuḍūʾ) is the ceremony where the two purities are indistinguishable — washing the face and arms for prayer *is* removing Satan's filth *is* calming the heart. The believers at Badr performed the first wuḍūʾ-of-war, and the verse memorializes it, to say: the water you use to stand before God twice a day in tranquility — that same water, at that same moment, was standing you up in a battle.

**The Firm Step and the Firm Heart: One Verb Governs Both**

*Yarbiṭa* — to tie down, as a beast is tethered securely; *yuthabbita* — to make firm — govern the hearts and the feet respectively, but the semantic field is one field: what slips in the mud of Badr is exactly what slips in the panic of the chest, and God's answer to both is the same answer, a rain-sent grip. Hence the verse's structure: heart-ropes and shoe-treads are dealt with in one sentence, because the verse's God does not divide the human being into the spiritual and the practical. This will be the standard applied in v. 45: "be firm" (*thbatū*) — the same root — commanded to later armies, with "remember God much" as its heart-rain. The drowsiness, the water, the grip: Badr's night is thus told entirely in the idiom of gifts, and the *aman* at its center is a security "from Him" (minhu) — the note the source highlights — so that no part of the night's rest belongs to luck, and none of the rain to climate. To read v. 11 as the sūrah intends is to learn to read one's own nights before one's own Badrs: the sleep that came anyway, the help that arrived at the right hour, the ground that held when it might have slipped — each of them, says this verse, a *tiding* with a purpose: purity lifted off you, whisper lifted off you, and the grip — in the chest and in the feet — laid on.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:12

> **Behold, thy Lord revealed unto the angels, “Truly I am with you; so make firm those who believe. I shall cast terror into the hearts of those who disbelieve. So strike above the neck, and strike their every fingertip.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Commission Written as Four Clauses**

*Idh yūḥī rabbuka ilā'l-malāʾikati annī maʿakum fa-thabbitū'lladhīna āmanū* — "when your Lord revealed to the angels: I am with you, so keep firm those who believe." The divine communiqué to the angelic host is the command post of the whole battle, and its four clauses are the four instruments of victory: God's companionship ("I am with you"), the angels' ministration to the believers' hearts ("make firm"), the counter-ministration to the enemy's hearts ("I will cast terror"), and the physical field ("strike above the necks… every fingertip of them"). The structure is a diagram of the sūrah's entire theory of war: every sword-stroke is preceded and accompanied by heart-work, and the heart-work is the real engine. The drowsiness of v. 11 was the private angelic ministry, says Ibn Kathīr in the tradition of al-Ḥasan's gloss — firmness sent into the hearts is the same current that sent sleep into the eyes. Al-Rāzī raises the fine question the source note flags — to whom does "I am with you" speak? To the angels (as commission and honor), or is it quoted for the believers' ears? Since angels know no fear, the reassurance-form "I am with you" must, he argues, be heard *through* them to the trembling men: God's with-ness is passed down like a password — the angels know it, and its function is to make firm the hearts that hear it from them.

**"Make Firm Those Who Believe": the Ministry No One Sees**

*Thabbitū* — from the same root as the "be firm" command of v. 45, the sūrah's master-word for the virtue of the line: not ferocity, but holding. The angels' first-order business in this verse is not killing but *fixing* — putting into the hearts of the believers the settledness that lets a sandaled farmer stand his ground against a mailed aristocrat. The mechanism, as al-Rāzī explains, is the angelic counterpart of Satan's whisper: *ilqāʾ al-thabat* — casting firmness — is as real a psychic event as *waswās* is, and the Qur'an treats the unseen as an operational layer of warfare, not a decoration.
