This explains the reports that the Companions woke after the sleep-vow of v. 11 feeling "as if something had tied their hearts" — a strange, new steadiness — and it is why the Qur'an can, in the same sūrah, command the *humans* to do the same job for each other: the angelic commission and the prophetic practice ("he straightened the ranks with a stick like an arrow") describe one campaign of firmness conducted on several levels simultaneously, and every Muslim commander who has steadied a line with his own voice is, in this verse's theology, doing the angels' work with the angels' verb.

**"I Shall Cast Terror": the Weapon the Qur'an Names First**

The *rubʿ* — terror — cast into the hearts of the disbelievers is the mirror-ministry, and the tradition grounds it in the Prophet's own description of his aid: *"I was aided with fear (al-ruʿb) cast into the hearts of my enemy, a month's journey ahead of me"* (al-Bukhārī 335; Muslim 521). Note its priority in this verse: God's own clause of attack is the fear in enemy hearts; the command to the angels to *strike* follows. Badr's actual combat was short — the sources' "an hour" or less before Quraysh broke — because the battle was largely decided at the level of nerves: al-Ṭabarī and the sīrah writers report Quraysh's overnight indecision, Abū Sufyān's message from the coast urging the army home, the quarrel among the clans, and, on the day, the disintegrating effect of seeing the Muslims outnumbering them — which the next verse-pair (v. 44) will reveal as deliberate: God made you *few in their eyes* until they committed, then many, at the clash. "Strike above the neck and every fingertip" (13) belongs to the same psychological warfare: an order to cut off heads (the Arab image of finality, "the neck is where the pride rides") and extremities — hands, fingers, the sinews of grasping — so that the enemy loses simultaneously the leadership that rallies (necks) and the capacity to hold on (fingertips). Ibn ʿAbbās's reading, cited by al-Ṭabarī, is that this is not only angelic but idiomatic: the angels encouraged and the believers swung; "strike the necks" is the imperative addressed to whoever does the striking, and the two armies of v. 15–16's prohibition ("whoever turns his back…") show that the striking was performed by men who had to *choose*, each minute, to keep choosing it.

**The Neck and the Fingertips: Precision Addressed to Men**

That a revealed command should specify *above the neck* (*fawqa al-aʿnāq*) and *the fingertip* (*lāmah* — a plural-only word for the tips of fingers and toes) has fed two streams of the commentary. The juristic stream (al-Qurṭubī cites the debate) takes the necks-and-extremities as an ethics directive smuggled into a battlefield order: strike where the strike is decisive (the neck's vital junction) and where it disables without butchery (the extremities) — and the sūrah's surrounding verses honor this by what they forbid: no mutilation, no pursuit of fugitives beyond necessity, ransom or release later (vv. 67–70). The mystical stream (al-Rāzī's *fa-lāma* as "the tips," the sinews of grasping) reads the order as a map of the human vice being struck: pride (the neck, the raised head) and avarice/grip (the hands) — which is, remarkably, the identical pair of faults that opened this sūrah (the spoils dispute = grip; tribal honor = neck). Badr, on this reading, was the physical enaction of the verse 1 cure: whatever hoards and swells must be cut in ranks, on the field and in the chest. The two readings are not rivals; the sūrah has been writing both at once from its first word, and v. 12 makes them rhyme: "strike" is the last clause of a verse whose first three clauses are all about the heart, and this is how this sūrah fights — interior before exterior, and the exterior in service of the interior.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:13

> **That is because they are in schism with God and His Messenger. And whosoever opposes God and His Messenger—truly God is severe in retribution.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Shiqāq: the Word That Diagnoses Before It Condemns**

*Dhālika bi-annahum shāqqu Allāha wa-Rasūlahu* — "that is because they split off from God and His Messenger." The verb *shāqqa* is Form III from *shaqq*, a cleft or fissure: the morphological sense is of two parties *making a crack between themselves*, each walking into its own side of the split. The Qur'an uses this same verb for the quarreling spouses whose case the judges pry apart (4:35), for the hypocrites who split from the community by oath (58:22's background), and here for Quraysh at Badr. The choice is the verse's entire theodicy: what happened on the field — the struck necks, the fingertip blows, the wrath — is not explained as divine temper, tribal feud, or resource competition; it is explained as the *social consequence of a fissure*. The same root yields *shiqāq* as the Qur'an's standard word for damnation itself — "and as for those who were *shaqqā*, for them the Fire" (11:103) — because damnation, in the Qur'an's moral physics, is not an added penalty but the final name for a separation that chose its own direction. The disbelievers at Badr did not merely disagree with the Message; they *cut*, and every cut cuts both ways: the verse's grammar says they are "in schism with God," as if a limb could be in schism with the body whose blood runs it.

**Why "God and His Messenger" Share One Verdict**

The pairing — schism with God *and* His Messenger — and the parallel clause "whoever opposes God and His Messenger" raise the standing question of prophetic authority, answered throughout the Qur'an: *"Whoever obeys the Messenger obeys God"* (4:80), *"the hand of God is above their hands"* (48:10). To the early exegetes, however, the dual naming here also rebuts the Quraysh's own complaint. Their grievance at Badr was precisely that they were being fought *by Muhammad*, a man of their own town — an offense against tribal kinship, not against heaven. The verse answers: the rupture you engineered is not a quarrel with a person who can be appeased by gifts or mediated by clan elders; it is a fissure in the order of being itself, because the Messenger's cause *is* God's cause — "God desires to verify the truth" (v. 7). This is why opposition to him cannot be negotiated away while the opposition stands: a man may forgive his own insult; what he cannot do is forgive for God, and the fissure is with God. Al-Qurṭubī adduces the related reports of ʿUtbah's failed negotiation (the offer of money and marriage to the Prophet ﷺ in the sīrah — refused with "I do not want what you offer"), which in the sūrah's light was the diplomacy of the cleft: the offer assumed the quarrel's stakes were the Prophet's own person.

**"God is Severe in Retribution" — ʿIqāb as Follow-through**

*Shadīd al-ʿiqāb* — the refrain of this passage (vv. 13, 25, 52, 53) — is built from *ʿ-q-b*, to come after, to follow at the heels. Retribution in the Qur'an's vocabulary is, literally, *the thing that follows*: consequence, not revenge. Severity here is the adjective of the *following-through*, the inexorability with which a chosen cleft runs to the bottom of the rock. The verse's logic is thus closer to a diagnosis than a threat: those who *shāqqu* — split against the grain of reality — will find that the split behaves like a split: it widens, it takes the weight, it fails at the weld. Badr demonstrated this on a small scale: the Quraysh's schism was the reason a trade mission became an annihilated army; their "scheme" (v. 18, and the plot of v. 30) was weakened from the inside, precisely by the internal clefts the sīrah records — Abū Sufyān's withdrawal of the caravan, the quarrels at the well, the defection of Banū Zuhrah before the march. A community at schism with God fights, in this sūrah's universe, with a severed supply line — and the supply line was cut the day they preferred their division.

**The Verse as a Warning to the Victorious, Not Only the Vanquished**

The reader to whom this verse landed, however, was not Quraysh — they were dead or captive; it was the quarreling believers of v. 1. If schism is what the vanquished at Badr died of, the sūrah's first audience must read it as the fault line under their own feet: the dispute over spoils was a *micro-shiqaq* of the very pathology that had just been avenged. That is the function of the sūrah's odd, brilliant juxtaposition: v. 1 commands *iṣlāḥ* (set matters aright), and v. 13 names *shiqaq* as the thing that iṣlāḥ prevents — the two words are the same surgery viewed from the healthy side and the necrotic side. Hence the second clause's generality — "and whoever" — is addressed to all parties including the believers: the severe retribution is not reserved for Quraysh; it names the trajectory any cleft opens when its grain runs against God and His Messenger. The classical ethicists drew the practical rule: when a dispute arises, examine which way the fissure points — toward the center of revelation and the Messenger's example, or away from it; the content of grievance matters far less than the direction of the cut. A cut that runs toward God, even in anger, is a cut that will be healed; a cut that runs away from Him, however polite, is Badr beginning again.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:14

> **Thus it is, so taste it, and [know] that the disbelievers shall have the punishment of the Fire.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Punishments in One Sentence, and One Knowledge**

*Dhālika fa-dhūqūhu wa-anna li'l-kāfirīna ʿadhāba'l-nār* — "Such it is: so taste it, and for the disbelievers there is the punishment of the Fire." The verse yokes the battlefield to the Barzakh in a single breath: the sword-stroke of Badr is the first taste; the Fire is the bill. The syntax — "so taste *this*," and then, *and* [know] that — puts them side by side as one continuum of the same consequence, and the continuum is the point. Ibn Kathīr glosses the address as directed to the slain and defeated of Quraysh on the day itself — the verse as the taunt of the field, the kind the Arabs themselves spoke in victory — while al-Ṭabarī's reports extend the sense to the living audience: taste what your schism has produced, and know its sequel. In either reading the structure of the sentence is the structure of sin in this sūrah: every fault has a near repayment and a far one, and the near one is *information* about the far one — a taste, not a token.

**Why "Taste"? the Epistemology of dhawq**

The imperative *dhūqū* — taste — draws on *dhawq*, the sense the Arabic idiom reserves for first, undeniable experience: one hears a rumor about fire; one *tastes* the ember. Throughout the Qur'an, punishment in this world is framed as tasting — *"So taste the punishment for what you have done"* appears at 8:35 for the prayer-at-the-House that was whistling and clapping, and the verb of tasting recurs on the lips of the damned as well: *"They will say: 'Taste, you the mighty, the generous!'"* (44:49 — the taunt of the Fire's keepers), while the blessed, conversely, are promised that they will *taste* no death after the first one (44:56; 15:48). The theology packed into this idiom is epistemic: doctrine can be contested, news of the unseen can be doubted, but a taste cannot be denied by the one tasting. When God says "taste," He converts an article of belief into an experienced fact — and the verse's second clause, "and know," then addresses the bystanders: let those who did not taste learn by what they saw. Badr was therefore for all Arabia the transfer of a creed into a datum: the Quraysh's families knew, from the taste of it, what their rabbis' and soothsayers' denials had kept in suspension — that the Book was true, and the schism fatal — before they ever met the Fire, which the verse names not to threaten the dead but to tell the living, calmly, where taste is leading.

**The World as Foretaste, Not Verdict**

Read with v. 10's "victory is from God alone," this "taste" blocks a dangerous inference: that Badr settled the question. In the sūrah's own chronology, Uḥud follows with the believers fleeing and seventy martyrs, and the verse's "Fire" stays future even for the slain of Badr — the day of differentiation (v. 41) *samples* the verdict without rendering it. Hence the verse's balance: the disbelievers "shall have" — a settled debt with an appointed date. The near taste proves the reality of the process; the far punishment is the completion of justice that the short field could not finish. The world's retributions are, in this view, *appetizers served with an invoice still printing* — which keeps the triumphant believer from crowning himself judge, and keeps the suffering disbeliever from believing the story over. The same two-stage structure is applied to every epoch: empires that mock the messengers taste collapse before the last word, and the last word is not collapse but standing before the Fire — a sequence the sūrah's "wont of the ancients" refrains will spell out at 8:38 and 8:52–54.

**The Sentence the Conquerors Could Not Celebrate**

And note finally the verse's chill function inside a victory ode. A chapter that has just narrated angels, sleep, rain, and a routed thousand — a narrative any people would retell for generations as their own martial glory — inserts, at the apex of the battle's account, this sentence: *so taste it, and know…* — in which the only party named as agent of the taste is God, and the only future named belongs to the Fire. The effect on the victors is the whole sūrah's ethics compressed: they cannot enjoy Badr as conquest, because the verse reframes their enjoying as *evidence* — a taste handed them by the same Judge, and a question aimed at *them* in the next verse-pair (the prohibition of turning backs, the spoils, the ransom): if this is what the schism tastes like when the other side has it, what is it becoming in your mouths? So the verse works like salt on a wound the enemy bears and the victor inhales: taste is not the end of the account; it is the beginning of the accountability — *al-nār* named flatly, as the clause the sūrah keeps repeating, so that no victory at Badr — or after it — ever dries into pride.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:15

> **O you who believe! When you meet those who disbelieve arrayed [for battle], turn not your backs to them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Line That Holds the World Together**

The address switches, abruptly and deliberately, from the dead of Quraysh to the living believers: *Yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū idhā laqītumu'lladhīna kafarū ḥādirīn fa-lā tuwallūnahunu'l-adbāb* — "O you who believe, when you meet the disbelievers advancing [to battle], do not turn to them your backs." *Ḥāḍirīn* — present, marshalled, the field actually there, not the planning of it — and *al-adbāb*, the plural of *dubur*, backsides: turn not your backsides, the most humiliating idiom Arabic has for flight, for the fugitive offers the enemy his posterior, his shame, and his neck at once. After the angels, the sleep, and the rain, the sūrah now arrives at the only human command of the battle proper, and its starkness is instructive: the divine aid of vv. 9–12 has already been given, the enemy's terror is already being cast (v. 12), the outcome is "victory from God alone" (v. 10) — and yet the entire burden of the hour is loaded onto three hundred spines that must refuse to turn. Aid without steadfastness is, in this sūrah's arithmetic, aid delivered to an empty address.

**Why Turning Is Not a Personal Option: the Physics of a Line**

The command's seriousness in the tradition is out of proportion to any single man's flight — and that disproportion is the reason. A line in battle is not a row of individuals; it is a single organism whose connective tissue is mutual sight: each fighter's courage is partly constituted by the man beside him. One back turning opens a crack — *shiqāq* again, the sūrah's root of disaster (v. 13) — and cracks propagate: the man next to the fugitive must cover the gap or flee; panic travels faster than any cavalry; the flanks roll; and what was three hundred becoming a wall becomes three hundred becoming a rout. Badr was existential — Madinah had no second redoubt; the source commentary notes that defeat there would likely have ended the community — so flight was not cowardice alone, it was attempted homicide of the whole. This is why the Prophet ﷺ classed it among the gravest crimes a Muslim can commit: *"Shall I not inform you of the most grievous of the grave sins? Associating with God, disobedience to parents — and: fleeing from the advancing army"* (al-Bukhārī 2654; Muslim 89, from Abū Hurayrah). Al-Nawawī, in his commentary on Muslim, notes the scholars' consensus that "fleeing from the zahr" — the battle-line once the fight is joined — is among the seven self-destroying sins, and why: because the sinner does not only save (the illusory) his skin; he sells, with it, the skin of every brother behind him. The verse therefore legislates a hard physical truth: moral courage, in a line, is *infrastructural*. You are not standing for your own honor; you are a load-bearing wall, and walls do not resign during earthquakes.

**"Arrayed" — Facing the Formed Enemy, Not Every Quarrel**

Note the condition: *ḥādirīn* — when the enemy comes at you *arrayed*. The verse binds its law to a formed field battle — two hosts meeting in the open, ranks visible — which the jurists used in both directions: the duty of standing applies to the marshalled confrontation, which is exactly why the exceptions of v. 16 (feint, rejoining another unit) are defined in terms of the same tactical space; and it also confines the *sin* to that space, which is why the sūrah, elsewhere, legislates withdrawal without shame when the field itself is avoidable (the emigration commands of 4:97's debate, the Trench's defensive conception, the ruses at the Ditch). The mature reading of this verse in Islamic law thus refuses two abuses: the adventurer's "never retreat, never surrender" in service of no cause but ego, and the provocateur's claim that a believer may quit any fight whenever the cost appears — the *ḥādirīn* frame both errors out: this is the battle-line, joined lawfully, where your place is decided by the community's need, not your nerves. Ibn ʿAbbās's reported gloss in al-Ṭabarī — that the verse speaks of the believers' duty in the day God had already won (v. 17's "you did not kill them…") — carries the theological sting: the promise of victory does not license dispersal; it *obliges* the formation, for the promise is deposited precisely in the standing men.

**The Back Turned in Every Other Sense**

Finally, the tradition has always read *adbāb* figuratively alongside the literal, because the image — presenting your back, facing away, going backwards at the critical front — maps onto every moral stance in which a person retreats from what he knows: *"When they saw Our might, they said: We believe in God alone and disavow what we used to associate with Him. But it did not avail them at all when they saw Our might"* (40:84–85 — the ultimate *tawlīyat al-adbāb*, the turn that arrives after the seeing); and in the believer's daily war, the sūrah's own vocabulary defines the true enemy's field: the heart's line breaks at the first turning from prayer (v. 3), from truth after clarity (v. 6), from the ranks of congregation. To keep v. 15 in Muslim memory, the Friday sermon and the battlefield alike, is to keep the entire discipline of Qur'anic manhood and community in one sentential posture: stand, face, do not turn — the aid of heaven (v. 9) is addressed to fronts, not to backs.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:16

> **And whosoever turns his back to them that day—unless it be a stratagem of battle or to withdraw to another company—shall certainly earn wrath from God. And his refuge shall be Hell. What an evil journey’s end!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Legal Exits from One Prohibition**

The verse that threatens is also the verse that thinks: *man yuwallihim yawma'idhun duburahu illā mutaharrifan li-ḥarb aw mutahayyizan ilā fiʾah* — whoever turns his back that day, *unless* one who wheeled for the battle's ruse, *or* one who betakes himself to a division (fiʾah). Two exceptions, both rational, both tactical, both instructive about the mind of this law. First, *taḥarruf li'l-ḥarb* — the wheeled movement: a feigned retreat, a deliberate withdrawal to draw the enemy out of formation, to expose his flank, to lead him into the hollow the sīrah's Badr and later campaigns would use — the Prophet's own axiom standing behind the exception: *"War is deceit"* (al-Bukhārī 3030; Muslim 1739). Second, *tahayyuz ilā fiʾah* — retiring to another company: falling back not to go home but to link up, to regroup around the reserve, to rejoin the line at a stronger seam. The verse thus contains, within its severest command, an entire small treatise on the difference between *movement* and *flight*: the same body going backward is one when it carries the battle's intent, the other when it sheds it. A man runs *from* the line in the second case; in the first he merely repositions *the line through his body*. The intention — *li-ḥarb*, *ilā fiʾah*, both clauses naming the battle's own grammar — is the whole of the law. This is a jurisprudence of courage worth pausing on, for it refuses both superstitions: it does not sanctify every standing corpse (a futile stand is not virtue, it is waste), and it does not permit every retreat (a clever-looking flight dressed in tactics remains flight).

**"I Am the Fiʾah of the Believers": the Mercy Behind the Exception**

The reports the commentaries (Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī) transmit under this verse give the exception its human texture. Some warriors at Badr, having left the immediate press of the fight and returned, came back ashamed, fearing they were written down as deserters; the Prophet ﷺ's reply — *"You are the ones who will make up the rout / I am the fiʾah (regrouping company) of every Muslim"* — is preserved in these narrations (in the Musnad and in al-Bukhārī's parallel wording of his statement at another battle: *"I am the fiʾah of every Muslim"*). And the source commentary's second report is the one to weigh: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, hearing of a fighter slain at overwhelming odds who had not run, said: *"Had he come back to me, I would have been his fiʾah"* (cited in Ibn Kathīr) — the second Caliph claiming for himself, in peacetime memory, the verse's entire function: the community is *supposed* to be the second rank; no man should face an error alone, because a man who knows there is a fiʾah to withdraw to withdraws *into duty* rather than *into panic*. This is the deepest social lesson of v. 16's exception: flight becomes rare, not by punishing flight, but by building structures the retreating man can re-enter — a company, a reserve, a leader, a family, a congregation. A society of isolated fronts produces fugitives; a society of fiʾahs produces recoveries. In every later arena where the Qur'anic ethic applies — the marriage that can be repaired, the repentance the community receives, the scholar's error that a school absorbs, the addict's relapse met with a program instead of a verdict — this verse's two exceptions are the charter of the second rank.

**The Wrath, and the Refuge — a Proportionality Worth Reading**

The threatened consequence matches the crime's real weight: *bāʾan min Allāh* — a return, a turning-back of divine wrath upon him — *wa-maʾwāhu jahannam, wa-biʾsa'l-maṣīr* — his refuge Hell, and wretched the destination. That such language sits on *fleeing*, while this same sūrah (and the whole Qur'an) will absorb the seventy who actually fled at Uḥud with *"then God turned to them"* (3:159's context, which the source commentary here explicitly cross-references), is what made the commentators argue the threat's scope: al-Ṭabarī transmits those holding it specific to Badr's existential stand; the majority of jurists (as al-Qurṭubī's discussion shows) generalized the *sin* but not the *hopeless automatism* — fleeing after the clash without exception is a *kabīrah* (al-Bukhārī and Muslim's list), a gravest sin demanding repentance, yet repentable, as 3:159 proves; and the Shāfiʿī school derived from the ratio-verses that follow (8:65–66, where the obligated ratio drops from 1:10 to 1:2 after God "lightened" the burden) the practical rule that when the enemy exceeds twice one's number, standing is no longer obligatory — a staggering piece of legal craftsmanship: *the verse-pair of wrath and the verse-pair of the ratio are read together*, and the outcome is a jurisprudence that can tell a soldier both "hold" and "the hold has limits." Wrath, in this structure, is not a tantric attribute but the moral fact of the community's exposure: the turning back is the one act whose punishment it must pronounce to survive, because it is the one act each member can perform against everyone.

**"What an Evil Journey's End" — the Eschatological Anchor**

*Wa-biʾsa'l-maṣīr* — an excellent ugliness is the destination — closes the verse as so many of this sūrah's warnings close, with the Hereafter. Its placement explains the entire law's severity at the human level: *maṣīr*, destination, is the word for where the road lands, and the verse has just described, in its exceptions, a man moving *on the road* toward his company. The contrast is the point: the fighter whose backward steps are aimed at a fiʾah is moving toward his people; the fugitive whose backward steps aim at home, safety, life, has, unbeknownst to himself, walked the last road — the road that ends where no company exists. To the modern reader this looks like excessive otherworldliness; but the sūrah's own psychology (vv. 2–4) already taught that the believer's heart is oriented by its *maṣīr*: what one holds as one's destination determines what one will hold as one's post. Courage, in this sūrah, is ultimately an eschatological competence — the ability to keep Hell in view while keeping the line in view — and the fugitive's sin is not that he feared death, but that he forgot to which meeting death was the only road, and thereby traded, for a borrowed morning, his last, permanent rank.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:17

> **You did not slay them, but God slew them, and thou threwest not when thou threwest, but God threw, that He might try the believers with a beautiful trial from Him. Truly God is Hearing, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Un-Possesses the Victors**

*Fa-lam taqtulūhum wa-lākinna Allāha qatalahum, wa-mā ramayta idh ramayta wa-lākinna Allāha ramā* — "you slew them not, but God slew them; and you threw not, when you threw, but God threw." At the very apex of the community's martial triumph, the Qur'an issues its most radical disowning of human authorship: the men killed; they did not kill. The grammar is forensic — the negation before the verb, the re-attribution after "but" — and it is aimed at the one sin this whole sūrah exists to prevent: the victor's claim to the spoils as though the sword wrote the deed. If v. 1 said the loot is not yours, v. 17 says the killing was not yours; the second is the root the first only exposed. The second clause names the famous act: the Prophet ﷺ, on the eve of battle or at its hinge, took a handful of pebbles or dust and cast it at the Quraysh lines, saying *"Shāhat al-wujūh — blighted be the faces!"* (al-Ṭabarī transmits the versions: three pebbles, right, left, center; or dust carried by the wind into every eye), ʿAlī reporting: *"The Prophet threw a handful of dust at the faces of the polytheists on the day of Badr"* (al-Bukhārī 4119). A single man's handful against a thousand armored men — and the verse says: that handful is not the cause; the God who made the dust a rout is.

**How a Throwing That Was Not a Throwing: the Doctrine in the Grammar**

The sentence is one of the Qur'an's control surfaces for the entire question of divine and human action, and the commentators worked it from every angle. The Muʿtazila read the re-attribution as moral emphasis (God is the *creator* of the effect whose *acquisition* was yours), while the Ashʿariyya took it as the proof-text of *kasb*: the servant acquires the act, God creates it — al-Rāzī, the systematizer of that school, cites this verse for the premiss that a handful of dust could not, by nature, fill a thousand eyes with flight, so a special *effectuation* came from God through the throwing; and the theologians (al-Ashʿari himself, in the reports of his school) adduced "you did not kill them" as the Qur'anic demonstration that God is the sole Maker of acts, the killing including the separating of soul from body, which no sword owns. Ibn Taymiyyah's middle way, argued at length in his theological writings, fits the verse's double clause exactly: the negation ("you threw not") denies the *independence* of the creature's act, while the affirmation ("when you threw") preserves its *reality* — the Prophet really threw, the believers really slew; they are not metaphor; but no act exists by its own authorship, and so credit cannot be hoarded where being merely *used* was everything. Neither fatalism (you did nothing) nor Pelagianism (it was all you) survives the sentence's hinge. This is also why the verse ends not with power but with attributes: *samīʿun ʿalīm* — Hearing, Knowing: the acts re-attributed to God were first heard and known as *yours* — the intention counted before the outcome — so the creature's responsibility (the trial, next clause) and the creature's nothingness (the re-attribution) are both fully affirmed, side by side, in one breath.

**"A Beautiful Trial": the Victory That Tests**

*Wa-li-yabluwa'l-muʾminīna bihi balāʾan ḥasanā* — and so that He might try the believers with a *beautiful* trial. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both record the gloss of the salaf: *balāʾan* is here the test of *naʿmāʾ* — prosperity — which the Qur'an elsewhere calls the harder assay: *"We test you with evil and with good, and to Us you are returned"* (21:35); and *"everyone is tested by how he treats the way of his companions"* is the idiom of 25:31's commentary tradition. The trial is beautiful precisely because defeat tests only the back's turning, while victory tests the heart's allegiance — whether the man who has won will, in the moment of distribution (v. 1!) and exaltation, ascribe the deed to its Owner. Sufi readers took the clause into the interior: al-Ajurrī and, in the source's note, the great allegorists read the "killing" as the slaying of the nafs, which, the verse says, is not achieved by the nafs slaying itself — the ego cannot execute the ego; hence the soul's death is *sent*, and the ḥadīth qudsī of nearness is adduced as the other side of the verse: *"My servant draws near to Me with supererogatory deeds until I love him; and when I love him, I am his hearing with which he hears, his sight with which he sees, his hand with which he grasps, and his foot with which he walks"* (al-Bukhārī 6502). The throwing that is not your throwing and the killing that is not your killing become, on that plane, the station where the servant's acts become the Beloved's — not pantheism (the sūrah's own "though the guilty be averse" forbids smoothing the moral edges) but perfect submission: you throw, honestly, with your whole will; and you own, honestly, nothing.

**"This Is from the Bounty of My Lord" — the Believer's Sentence on the Winning Moment**

The sūrah instructs its future readers how to stand inside such a victory by quoting the other great winner's line in the Qur'an: Solomon, when the throne of Bilqīs arrived before his blink, said *"This is from the bounty of my Lord, to test me whether I am grateful or ungrateful; and whoever is grateful, only for his own soul is he grateful"* (27:40) — the trial named, the bounty attributed, the profit of gratitude returned to the giver's own soul. Badr's believers are taught the same three moves in one verse: do not claim the kill (27:40's "this is from my Lord"), do not claim the throw ("God threw"), and read the whole as the assay ("that He might try you"). And behind it all, *samīʿun ʿalīm*: He heard the cry of the drowning band at Badr (v. 9: *tastaghīthūna*); He knew the hearts of those who were averse (v. 43: *ʿalīm bi-dhāti'l-ṣudūr*) — the God who re-attributes your acts to Himself is the same God who took your acts into account. To win and to say *mā ramayta idh ramayta wa-lākinna Allāha ramā* is the only posture in which the victory neither deifies the victor nor buries the gratitude — which is why, across the centuries of khutbas, this sentence, not the battle's numbers, has remained the fruit of Badr.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:18

> **Thus it is, and [know] that God makes feeble the scheming of the disbelievers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict on Every Conspiracy**

*Dhālika wa-anna Allāha muḍʿifu kayda'l-kāfirīn* — "Such it is; and God weakens the scheme of the disbelievers." The verse is short as a seal, and it seals the entire Badr account: the day had its plot, its counter-plot, and its disclosure that the counter-plot never needed to be a plot at all — only a *taḍʿīf*, a weakening, a taking-out of the load-bearing joints. *Kayd* in Arabic is scheming: the bending of means toward an end by hidden leverage; the Qur'an attributes it to Pharaoh (54:37: "Mūsā and his *kayd*"), to the brothers of Yūsuf, to the chiefs of every people who bend truth like metal. The verb here — *yuḍʿifu* — is from *ḍ-ʿ-f*, weakness, and the Form IV causative makes God the agent of the bending *down*: the scheme's own leverage is dissolved. What the Quraysh bent and bent — alliance, arms, the protection of trade, the extermination plan hatched at the Dār al-Nadwah (v. 30's plot: seize, or kill, or expel) — was made, in one sentence, "feeble": not always broken at once, not visibly, but *un-weighted*, until it could not lift what its plotters set on it. The parallel verses the source notes make this a universal declaration, not a Badr footnote: *"God is the One to Whose care every bird's forelock belongs"* (11:56); *"Do they have partners who created anything like their creation, so that the creation is confused for them? Say: All creation is God's"* (13:16); *"and the plotting of evil — it will overwhelm none but its own authors"* (35:43); *"their plot will not harm you in any way"* (3:120). The scheming, the Qur'an assures, is self-referential in the end: the trap's floor gives way under the one who dug it.

**How Badr's Plot Defeated Itself: a Case Study**

Read the verse against the field's own news, as the sīrah gives it: Quraysh marched to make the caravan *untouchable*; the march was what *lost* the caravan's wealth, because Abū Sufyān — the scheme's own agent — turned the train to the coast and saved the goods by abandoning the army, and the army, now honor-bound and leaderless in its purpose, advanced to a well-ground the enemy had reached, on sand their own hooves had never chosen, against a promised day that the soothsayers' logic had no file for. Every lever of the *kayd* — prestige, numbers, the appeal to Arab solidarity, the threat to Banū Zuhrah who were kin to Madinah — lifted something only because it was placed on the Muslim side's assumed fear; and the Qur'an says here that God's *taḍʿīf* works exactly there, at the assumption. Abū Jahl's famous ride, prancing and invoking the idols, belongs to the same weakening: a scheme that must boast is a scheme already losing the thing it boasts about. The verse's *dhālika* therefore reads as the last stroke of the battle's summary: taste it (v. 14) — and *this* is the rule you tasted: Heaven does not need to out-scheme the schemers; it removes their footing.

**"Makr" Answered by Better Makr: the Sūrah's Own Mirror**

Four verses of the sūrah later, the same divine manner appears in its most quoted form: *"they plotted (makarū), and God plotted (wa-makara Allāh), and God is the best of planners"* (8:30) — the verse whose "plot" of God sent the Prophet ﷺ safely out of the besieged house in Makkah while Ali slept in his bed and the assassins' blades fell on dust. The pairing of v. 18 with v. 30 teaches the believer's full doctrine on conspiracies: sometimes the counter-action is the *weakening* (taḍʿīf — here, the joint quietly taken out of the enemy's knee), sometimes it is the *better plot* (here, the exit no one thought to guard). Both share one property: the scheme is met *at the level where it was being built* — in the assumptions, the routes, the minds — not only where it lands. This is also why the Qur'an's moral psychology pairs "fear of God" with "trust" as twin defenses (v. 2): the two organs that catch hidden leverage are vigilance and the certainty that no leverage reaches past what God has written — *"and if God touches you with harm, there is no remover of it but He"* (6:17).

**The Strength That Needs No Scheme**

What does the verse do for the side of truth, its real audience here? It frees them from imitation: the temptation under attack is to answer *kayd* with *kayd*, and the Qur'an's law on that is stated by the end of the sūrah in full balance — "and if they incline to peace, incline to it" (v. 61), "prepare strength" (v. 60), but here first: *your job is the straight path; the weakening of the crooked is My work.* Al-Rāzī notes the clause's consoling function for the small community: a band of three hundred facing a thousand had no *kayd* at all — and none was required of them; they were required to be *ṣādiqūn* (v. 19's "desires the truth"), and the truth's adversary, by the nature of things, must eventually lean on a falsehood, and where he leans is exactly where God puts the weakness into the joint. Hence the practical, permanent reading of v. 18: do not over-engineer the defense of the just cause; do not fear the plot more than the fissure in your own line; keep the ranks (vv. 15–16), keep the criterion (v. 29) — and know, as the sūrah's own conclusion on the day, that every arrayed *kayd* of the unbelievers was, in Heaven's ledger, already being filed down to a hair before it left the drawing table. *Wa-anna Allāha muḍʿifu kayda'l-kāfirīn* — and it is so, now, and in every century since, without exception: the scheming of the unbelievers, weakened at its root, until the root gives up its claim.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:19

> **If you seek victory, then victory has come to you. And if you desist, it would be better for you. And if you return, We shall return, and your company will not avail you aught, even if they be many. And [know] that God is with the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Judgment Quraysh Asked For, and Got**

*In kuntum tastafṭiḥūna fa-qad jāʾakumu'l-futḥ* — "If you were seeking a decision, then the decision has come to you." *Istiftāḥ* — asking for *futḥ* — is the idiom of demanding judgment, and the sīrah tradition anchors it here with a reported prayer of Abū Jahl, spoken at the Rock of Makkah before the march, in which he invoked against his own people:

> *"O God, cut off the one who severed kinship ties and brought us what we know not; then judge (fāṭiḥ) between us and him decisively, and do not delay the judgment against us."* (al-Bukhārī 4803; Muslim 2801, from Abū Hurayrah, who said: "the most audacious man to God was the saying of the Lord of 'Amr ibn Hishām [Abū Jahl]…")

The Prophet ﷺ, told of it, said "Āmīn" — and the verse takes the enemy's own words in its opening clause: you wanted an adjudication between truth and falsehood in open ground, by the sword, with heaven as assessor? The verdict arrived at Badr — *fa-qad jāʾakumu'l-futḥ* — delivered on the petitioner's own head. *Futḥ* in Arabic fuses "opening," "victory," and "verdict" (hence *fātihah* — "the opening" — and the "Day of Opening" in 44:13 and 37:89), and the verse's irony is that Quraysh's prayer was literally answered: the thing that was closed — the question whether Muhammad's cause was from God — was *opened*, by their own defeat. This is the Qur'an's standing warning in miniature: the unbeliever's demand "bring the punishment if you are truthful" is not deflected; it is *granted*, in God's timing and form — as with the people of Noāḥ (54:9–15), and here.

**"If You Desist, It Is Better for You; If You Return, We Return"**

The verse's middle is the kindest ultimatum in the Qur'an's war-discourse: *wa-in tantahū fa-huwa khayrun lakum* — and if you stop, that is better for you. No demand for compensation, no conditions of humiliation on the future; simply: the spiral can end here, and ending it is the better of your options — *khayr* stated in the comparative, as a piece of prudence rather than a decree. Then the mirror: *wa-in taʿūdū naʿud* — and if you return, We return. The grammar is the theology: the believers do not have a standing army that pursues; they have a standing God that responds. The clause was verified within two years: Quraysh returned to Uḥud (where the Muslims were defeated when ranks broke), returned to besiege Madinah at the Trench (where the trench — a Madinan first, Salmān's counsel — was God's "return"), and returned no more (33:25: *"God turned back the disbelievers in their rage; they gained no advantage"*). Al-Ṭabarī notes on *naʿud*: "We shall send down again the aid, and be again with the believers" — divine repetition as the mirror-image of tribal repetition, which had no principle at all beyond honor and rage. The sentence thereby teaches the community, newly armed, the law of all its future wars: escalation is theirs to begin, never to control; de-escalation is theirs to choose, and its choice is called *better* even for the enemy — a line the sūrah will repeat verbatim in spirit at v. 38 (about the disbelievers) and v. 61 (about treaties).

**Numbers Dissolved: "Your Band Will Not Profit You, Were They Many"**

*Wa-lan tughniya ʿankum fiʾatukum shayʾan wa-in kathurat* — your party, your *fiʾah* (the sūrah's own word for a battle-company, v. 16!) will avail you nothing, even if multiplied. Addressed at surface level to Quraysh's thousand, the clause was read by the believers as a permanent law about the physics of armies, and the sūrah has just demonstrated its two halves: the small band held because God was with it (vv. 9–12), and the great host fell because its *kayd* was weakened (v. 18). That "many" does not profit is stated as a negative; the verse's final clause completes it as a positive: *inna Allāha maʿa'l-muʾminīn* — God is with the believers. The two together form the entire Qur'anic theory of power: force multiplies only across the axis of divine with-ness; add numbers to a people God is not with, and the sum remains zero — *"How many a small company has overcome a great company by God's leave"* (2:249). And the with-ness is conditional in the sūrah's own economy (the believers of vv. 2–4 with the trembling heart, the line of v. 15 that does not turn, the obedience of v. 20 that does not slip away while hearing); so the clause consoles the few who stand and terrifies the many who only count themselves. Abū Sufyān after Badr, licking his loss into a threat ("the day for the day; war is even"), was answering exactly this: numbers will come; and the verse had already spoken to that future — your *kathrah* will not be the variable.

**Whom the Verse Reprimands: the Third Reading**

The source commentary records the three classical placements of the address: to the disbelievers (Abū Jahl's reading, above); to the believers, as a reproof that their spoils-dispute (v. 1!) was a *return* to the disease the day was meant to cure — "if you desist from the dispute, it is better for you; if you return to it, We return (to the taking of the spoils from you)" in the sense of v. 41's khums, which some jurists read as exactly such a *muʿāqa* (penalty by redistribution) for the Badr contention; and the composite reading, first clause to the disbelievers, remainder to the believers or vice versa. That the verse tolerates all three is itself its deepest message: the same sentence reads the enemy's pride and the community's weakness simultaneously, because in this sūrah both are one disease — *fiʾah* trusting itself instead of the Fiʾah of every believer (v. 16, the Prophet's word for himself). To ask *futḥ* by sword or by lawsuit, to multiply the party and forget the Protector — the verse is thus equally a mirror to Badr's two camps, and it closes with the only line either camp could live or die by: *God is with the believers* — with the ones who believe, not with the ones who are believed.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:20

> **O you who believe! Obey God and His Messenger, and turn not away from him, even as you hear [him].**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Pulls Its Lesson Out of the Battle into the Ummah**

The Badr narration has reached its end — the decision pronounced (v. 19); and the sūrah now harvests the field into a law for the ear: *Yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū aṭīʿū Allāha wa-Rasūlahu wa-lā tawallū ʿanhu wa-antum tasmaʿūn* — obey, and do not turn away *from him* while you are hearing. The command's placement is precise: the men who flinched at the march (v. 5), argued after clarity (v. 6), and quarreled over the spoils (v. 1) are told what their survival has actually consisted of — a chain of hearing and obeying. Obedience to God and His Messenger is named together, as it is at 3:32 (*"Say: Obey God and obey the Messenger; but if you turn away — then God loves not the disbelievers"*), 4:59 (*"obey God, obey the Messenger, and the holders of authority among you"*), 4:80 (*"whoever obeys the Messenger obeys God"*), 5:92, 24:54, 47:33, 64:12 — the sūrah's own cross-references which the source commentary cites to fix the Prophet's "normative character": his teachings and actions carry, in matters of the community's walk, the weight of the divine command itself, because the Qur'an itself has assigned them that seat. Ibn Kathīr notes the exegetical principle: whenever the Qur'an pairs obedience to God with obedience to the Messenger, the Messenger's obedience is *independently binding* — hence the Sunnah's authority as the second root of law, and hence also its limits (he commands nothing against the Book, for his authority *is* the Book's extension — "what I forbid is as what the Qur'an forbids," in the ahl al-Sunnah's maxim drawn from Abū Dāwūd and Ibn Mājah's ḥadīths of the Sunnah's binding force).

**"While You Are Hearing" — the Sin of the Present Tense**

The clause *wa-antum tasmaʿūn* — "while you hear" — is the verse's edge, and the commentators feel it exactly. To turn away *after* a call you did not receive is ignorance, curable; to turn away *while the words are still falling from the caller's mouth* is the sin of the present tense — an act performed in the same breath as the command, the heel turning while the ear is open. Al-Ṭabarī relates it to the Companions' conduct in the field: do not leave your post while the order stands. But the whole sequence of vv. 20–23 shows its wider design: the Qur'an's diagnosis of the late-arriving heart is not refusal but *hearing-without-listening* — a body present at the call, a will absent. The Prophet ﷺ put the same law in the most quoted sentence on compliance: *"Whoever obeys me enters Paradise, and whoever disobeys me has refused [to enter]."* (al-Bukhārī 7137, from Abū Hurayrah.) The structure is binary — obedience or refusal — with no middle compartment for "I heard it, I like it, I'll think about it": the *thinking about it* that outlives the hearing is itself the refusal.

**Turning Away in the Direction of One's Own Voice**

Tawlī — turning the back (the same verb as v. 15's battlefield flight!) — binds these verses to each other: the deserter of the line and the deserter of the command are one type. This is the sūrah's boldest equation, and it is intentional: Badr proved that a man's real position is where he stands when he is ordered to stand, and the sūrah now generalizes that the *hearing* is the new line. When the muezzin calls, when the khātib delivers, when the āyah is recited that indicts exactly your current practice — there is a moment's angle at which the body stays and the will defects: the head lowers politely and the heart re-arms for its own plan. Against this the verse uses the plain imperative *lā tawallū* — do not turn; and the classical ethicists (al-Muḥāsibī's tradition of psychology, al-Ghazālī's *Iḥyāʾ* after it) codified the discipline: the remedy for will-defection is *immediate enactment*, even tiny — "act upon the knowledge, and God will give you more," in the reported wisdom — because the heart that executes one small command is the heart that will be present for the next; and the one that "agrees, agrees, agrees" in the sermon and does nothing is learning, at speed, to hear as noise.

**The Obedience That Cannot Be Delegated**

And note whose obedience is demanded: *antum* — you yourselves. No believer in this community can inherit, borrow, or proxy his submission: the men of Badr who had argued over shares could not be absolved by the Prophet's ﷺ own satisfaction with the distribution (he was satisfied; the verse wanted theirs); nor can a later congregation shelter behind a scholar's compliance while the individual limb stays turned. Ibn Taymiyyah, arguing this point against the quietists of his age, stressed that *ṭāʿat* in these verses attaches the person to the command he himself heard — which is also why the very next verses (21–23) refuse every "we were only following the gathering." Obey *God and His Messenger* — the pair of names fixes the content (nothing in created orders contradicts the Creator, so the Messenger's commands are only as binding as their fidelity to the Book — the balance the tradition's ijmāʿ states: the Messenger is obeyed because God ordered his obedience; what contradicts the Messenger is not obedience to God). And at Badr, both halves of that balance had already been lived: the army that would not take the spoils except as God's and the Messenger's (v. 1) was the army that won; this verse now universalizes the experiment's result — the community that turns *while hearing* is already on the field of a lost line; the community that turns *toward* while hearing is accompanied by the very God who was with the three hundred and fourteen. *Wa-antum tasmaʿūn* — and you — while you hear — is the hinge on which every later Badr of this ummah turns.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:21

> **And be not like those who say, “We hear,” though they hear not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Divides Hearing from Claiming to Hear**

*Wa-lā takūnū ka'lladhīna qālū samiʿnā wa-hum lā yasmaʿūn* — and be not like those who *say* "we hear," while they are not hearing. The verse's entire art is in the two clauses' collision: the spoken report about the ear, and the ear's actual vacancy. The commentators divide the referents: al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī report the polytheists who attended the recitation and answered "we have heard the like of it from the soothed airs of the jinn" (34:8's phrase — *anīd al-shayāṭīn*, the "chanting of the devils" that the Quraysh had hired al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith to broadcast against the Qur'an's recitation, as the sīrah records); and they report the hypocrites whose creed-utterance was a courtesy to the community, not a message to God — *"they say: we hear, and they do not listen"* (2:93's echo at the Sinai), and most appositely 24:48's phrase for the hypocrites when the call goes out to what God and His Messenger judge: *"samiʿnā wa-ʿaṣaynā"* — we hear, and we disobey. Against the verse's backdrop sits a third group: those of 47:21 whose instant pious reflex — *"samiʿnā wa-ṭāʿatun"*: we hear and obey — evaporates the moment battle is written. So three varieties of the same verbal act: hear-claim as evasion, as cover, as intentionless reflex; and the verse's command is to the believers: *be not like them* — a warning that the same words in a believer's mouth, unaccompanied by the ear's work, replicate the disease exactly.

**"Samiʿnā" Is a Performative, Not a Report**

In Arabic usage — and the jurists codified this in the law of replies — *samīʿa* often means *heeded*, not merely heard: a man "hears" his lord when he obeys, and "is deaf" when he disobeys, though his ears are physically intact; hence the proverb of the Arabs that the best hearing is *al-taʿa* — compliance. The Qur'an uses the idiom itself: *"We hear and we obey (samiʿnā wa-aṭaʿnā)"* is the praised answer of the bearers of the Throne's carried message (2:285's creed and 5:83's "our Lord, we heard... we believe"), while the rebuked ones answer with *samiʿnā wa-ʿaṣaynā* (24:48). The grammar of the verse — *"they say samiʿnā while they do not hear"* — thus translates literally: *they say "we heed," while they do not heed*; the claim is a performative (an act of submission in words), and the performative failed because the will behind it was elsewhere. This is why, in this sūrah's moral order, mere attendance at revelation is a neutral-to-negative state: *"and of them are those who listen to you — but can you make the deaf to hear, even though they reason not?"* (10:42); *"if they had intended to go out, they would have prepared for it some equipment"* (9:46 — the ear's absence precedes the feet's). The tradition's sharpest line on hearing-and-acting is the Prophet's ﷺ parable of the two soils — the earth that takes the rain and yields abundant grass, and the flat rock that holds no water and grows nothing (al-Bukhārī 71; Muslim 2282) — and the rock, note, *received* the rain; it simply never let it enter. To say "we hear" while not hearing is to be the rock that glistens.

**The Anatomy of the Un-hearing Ear**

Al-Rāzī presses the verse on its logic — how can a person who says "we hear" *not* hear? His answer maps the stations between the eardrum and the heart: sound is received (which even the dead have), attended to (requires presence), understood (requires reflection), accepted (requires the will), and enacted (requires the same will against the appetites). The verse's subjects stop at any of the first three stations and still issue the verbal certificate at the fifth; and the sūrah, which has already legislated *tadabbur* — the thinking through — of revelation (v. 2: "when its signs are recited to them, it increases them in faith"), and *dhikr* — the remembrance — as its fruit, defines here the negative space: hearing that does not increase, does not alter, does not move the body, is, by the Qur'an's criteria, hearing in name only — noise with a religious accent. That the *worst* such name is about to follow (v. 22's "deaf and dumb beasts") shows how high this sūrah sets the stakes of the ear: not information, but life (v. 24 — the call is *to what gives you life*; the ear is the doorway of being).

**The Believer's Daily Test of the Verse**

Practically, v. 21 is the verse the Qur'an keeps in the worshipper's ear for exactly two seconds each time — the interval between the reciter's words and the listener's intention — and the devotional tradition built that interval into ritual: the *āmīn* said aloud after the Fātiḥah, the standing up for the iqāmah *before* the feet arrange themselves, the command "say: we hear and we obey" in the khutbah's closing (the Sunnah's *samiʿnā wa-aṭaʿnā*, reported in the ḥadīths of the two ʿĪds and of obedience in Aḥmad) — all of them are technologies of the *joined* ear-and-will the verse demands. And its modern application needs no translation: the believer who hears the prohibition of interest and keeps the account, hears "establish prayer" and keeps the schedule that swallows it, hears the call to forgiveness and rehearses the grievance — is the verse's subject, whether or not he ever missed a sermon. The cure is not more hearing; it is one act: the Qur'an's own counsel for the ear, at 49:6, is to *test* the report before it sits — and, once it has been recognized as true, to let it *move*. A man who moves one command at the first hearing trains the heart to hear the next; the one who never moves, never finally hears at all, and will stand on the Day — the sūrah will say (v. 24) — gathered, with his hearing as its only witness against him.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:22

> **Truly the worst of beasts in the sight of God are the deaf and the dumb who understand not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Insult Drawn from the Pasture**

*Inna shiyra'd-dawābbi ʿinda Allāhi'ṣ-ṣummu'l-bukmu'lladhīna lā yaʿqilūn* — "the worst of what crawls and moves, in God's sight, are the deaf-mutes who do not understand." *Dābbah* is from *dabba*, to move on earth — every creeping, grazing thing; *shirr* is the superlative of evil quality, the worst of the worst. To call a human being a beast is idiom; to call him the *worst* beast, an insult; and the sequence of the three defects explains why the insult is earned and not merely hurled. The animals the *dawābb* idiom evokes — the flock, the beast of burden — were prized precisely for the one faculty the verse's subjects lack: responsiveness. The camel hears its name and comes; the sheep follows the crook because it *attends*. A creature equipped with hearing, voice, and mind — and which answers none of the three when the Owner calls — is, in the functional order of creation, below the ox: the ox fulfills the contract of its nature. This is the logic of Qur'anic zoomorphism elsewhere: *"the parable of those who carry the Torah then do not carry it is that of the ass carrying books"* (62:5 — the donkey is not condemned for being a donkey, but for *wasting its carrying*); *"like the dog: if you attack him, he lolls his tongue, and if you leave him, he lolls his tongue"* (7:176); *"they are like livestock — nay, further astray"* (25:44). The verse's superlative therefore is not species-prejudice; it is a verdict on equipment: shame attaches to wasted faculties, and no waste is vaster than the waste of the *only* faculties that make a soul.

**Ṣummun, Bukmun, and the Missing Mind: the Total Blackout**

The three defects are one human being's failure in order, and the sūrah's context (vv. 20–21: *hear* and *do not turn away*) names why each is listed. First *ṣumm* — deaf: the receiving channel is dead; the call arrives at the ear and finds no one home (v. 21's ones who "say we hear and hear not"). Second *bukm* — dumb: the returning channel is dead; they cannot speak the truth even when it presses on the tongue, for a voice in submission would require a prior consent of the will — and the hypocrite's tongue is fluent only in self-interest. Third *lā yaʿqilūn* — no intellect in between: the processing center is offline; hence the first two are not accidents of the organs but consequences of the governing faculty's abdication. The order is a diagnosis in miniature: un-understanding makes a man's hearing and speech voluntary amputations. Al-Rāzī notes that the Qur'an repeatedly pairs the loss of these faculties with punishment-as-medical-fact: *"and We have made for Hell many of the jinn and mankind — they have hearts with which they understand not, eyes with which they see not, ears with which they hear not"* (7:179) — so that "deaf" is at once the crime and the sentence: the ear that will not hear *becomes* the ear that cannot, which is precisely the mechanism 8:23 will name (the withholding of hearing as response to refused hearing) and 2:7 memorialized (*"God set a seal on their hearts and their hearing, and over their vision a cover"*). The "blindness of the heart is not the blindness of the eyes, but it is the blindness of the hearts in the breasts" (22:46), as the source commentary cross-references.

**"In the Sight of God" — the Courtroom Where the Ranking Is Done**

The superlative is dated and located: *ʿinda Allāh* — in God's reckoning, not in the saloon's. In the world's ranking, these men are not beasts at all; they are connoisseurs, critics, committee members — the man with a dead heart and a working bank account ranks *high* in most ledgers. The verse exists to move the believer's eye to the other register, where the only sense-organ counted is the one that perceives truth and the only limb the one that obeys — which is the same reversal this sūrah began with, over the pile of spoils (v. 1: what the clans rank first, God assigns to His Messenger; v. 4: ranks are "in the sight of your Lord"). Ibn Kathīr ties the verse to Badr's audience with the reports that it names the Quraysh who sat in the Prophet's ﷺ gatherings, heard the recitation, and went out to their poets unchanged — hearing as entertainment, speech as mockery. But the believers are also inside the sentence, for the verse's opening was "O you who believe" (v. 20) and it has not yet changed address: every worshipper who lets the Name pass through him without the heart's tremor (which v. 2 defined as the first mark of true faith) is being offered this mirror; the tradition's own fear — the salaf saying, on this verse's kind, *"we used to consider that hearing was given to us only so that we would understand"* (in al-Bukhārī's chapter of knowledge, via the companions' reports) — treats the verses as permanent, not historical.

**Where the Verse Leaves Hope**

Finally, weigh what the verse does *not* say. It does not curse these men to the beasts' eternal state; it ranks them at the moment of their refusal — and the very next verse turns from their condition to God's knowledge of their hearts (v. 23: "had God known good in them, He would have made them hear"), which keeps the door of cure technically open: the faculties that were buried are *God's* instruments, and He who withholds hearing can restore it — the sūrah has just seen a whole army's fear become sleep, its doubt become steadiness, by His hand (v. 11). The worst of beasts, in this sūrah's own story, is not a final species: it is a rank that repents. What closes it is not a verdict but a warning — because the deaf-and-dumb man who *understands that he is the subject of the verse* is, by that self-knowledge, not yet fully ṣumm; and the only sin the verse makes incurable is the one it will describe next: the hearing that goes out and finds its owner already gone.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:23

> **Had God known of any good in them, He would have caused them to hear; yet had He caused them to hear, they would have turned away in rejection.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Puts the Hardest Question on Paper**

*Wa-law ʿalima Allāhu fīhim khayran la-asmaʿahum; wa-law asmaʿahum la-tawallaw wa-hum muʿriḍūn* — "If God had known in them any good, He would have made them hear; and if He had made them hear, they would have turned away, averse." The source commentary names this squarely: the verse "makes explicit a seeming tension between God's allowing understanding to enter people's hearts and people's responsibility for their own actions," and it was used by the predestinationists as support for the fixedness of the damned. To see why the verse is neither, we must fix what its two conditionals assert. The first: hearing — the true hearing of v. 21, the ear that heeds — is *sent* by God, not seized by man; good must be *known in* the recipient before the gift is given. The second: even the sending would not have worked with *these* recipients — their turning is so settled that a miracle of hearing would meet a will already made of back (v. 15's *adbāb* again). The verse's point is not that God withholds hearing arbitrarily; it is that He withholds a treasure whose receiver would spend it only on his own ruin.

**"He Would Have Made Them Hear": Foreknowledge, Not Compulsion**

Al-Rāzī, whose great commentary (*Mafātīḥ al-Ghayb*) engages this verse against the Qadarīyya, presents the classical reconciliation: God's eternal knowledge *follows* the servant's own will, it does not *produce* it; He knows what they would do, and what they would do is theirs. Thus "had He known good, He would have made them hear" states the divine custom — grace meets disposition — while "they would have turned away" states the divine knowledge of their disposition; neither clause makes the turning *caused* by the foreknowing. The Ashʿarite reading transmitted in al-Qurṭubī sharpens it the other way for a reason the verse itself gives: some people's *ʿadab*-logic is that even an omniscient test would fail them, so giving them the test is giving them more condemnation — which is why the Qur'an elsewhere calls withheld revelation itself a mercy: *"O you who believe, ask not about things which, if disclosed to you, would grieve you"* (5:101). What was known about these men — the Meccan aristocrats who demanded resurrections of the dead as proof (the asbāb al-nuzūl the source cites from al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī) — was not neutral data; it was a settled style of soul that turns any evidence into another arena for its pride. The tradition's own courtroom drama for this logic is the ḥadīth of Ādam and Mūsā, in Muslim: *"Are you blaming me for a thing that God decreed upon me forty years before my creation?"* — and the Prophet's ﷺ resolution of the two claims: *"You both contend regarding a decree of God: Mūsā erred not, for he was given the Torah and if God had willed, they would not transgressed (and he knew that Ādam transgressed and forgot)..."* (Muslim 2652) — divine foreknowledge and human responsibility pleaded together and both kept. 8:23 is that ḥadīth's surah-level twin: the foreknowing does not license the fault; it simply means God's gifts are not dispensed blind to the receiver's intent.

**The Double Meaning of Withheld Hearing: Mercy and Punishment as One Act**

The deepest note in the verse is that the same fact — *they were not made to hear* — reads two ways, and the source commentary's gloss holds both open without forcing one. From God's side it is *mercy*: a truth presented to a man who will mock it converts his unbelief into sacrilege and hardens what was soft (so God, as it were, keeps the dangerous medicine away from the patient who would overdose on it, and sends it instead — in the verse's larger economy — to the people of v. 24, who *can* hear). From their side it is *punishment*: to be shut out of the ear's blessing is the penal meaning of *"God set a seal upon their hearts and upon their hearing"* (2:7) and *"So God sealed over their hearts, thus they understand not"* (7:101) — the deprivation is just because it is the *wage* of their refusal, not its cause. Al-Qurṭubī's *wa-law asmaʿahum* note cites the salaf's dread of exactly this: that persistent hearing without obedience *matures* into inability to hear — a law any teacher recognizes in the boy who first ignores the lesson, then cannot follow it, then will not sit for it. Sin, in this sūrah's psychology, is not a debt alone; it is an excavation: it digs the channel that will carry the next refusal, until the man who once could not be bothered *cannot* be moved — and God "knows" that channel, and sends accordingly.

**The Verse Turned to the Believer: Despair Closed, Presumption Closed**

Read as the sūrah's last word on the deaf-and-dumb rank (v. 22), v. 23 closes two doors and opens the middle corridor. It closes *despair* for the repentant, on any past: if hearing is God's gift and the heart's good is His condition, then no sinner has ever "missed" the call in a fatal sense — the call is always being re-sent to whoever the Sender has known good in, and the man who flinched at Badr (v. 5), argued at v. 6, heard-not at v. 21, can be *made to hear* tomorrow, since "had He known good... He would have made him hear" implies: let Him find good, and the ear is granted. It closes *presumption* for the audience: a hardening now is not a neutral habit; the verse's second conditional says the hardening can become destiny-shaped — "they would have turned away" — so the heart that toys with the call is writing its own seal's ink (v. 24's middle clause, "God intervenes between a man and his heart," is the same lesson at full width). The corridor between: respond — at once, with the whole will, at the level the sūrah began to define in v. 2 (trembling) — and do not hand any soul, even the worst Meccan mocker, over to the foreknown turning, for that judgment is the One whose "knowledge of the unseen" is named in this sūrah's last verse (v. 75), and no reader of 8:23 stands on the Judge's side of it.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:24

> **O you who believe! Respond to God and the Messenger when he calls you unto that which will give you life. And know that God comes between a man and his heart, and that unto Him shall you be gathered.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Call, the Life, and the Hour of the Answer**

*Yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū-stajībū li'llāhi wa-li'r-Rasūli idhā daʿākum li-mā yuḥyīkum* — respond — *istijābah*, the full answering-response — to God and the Messenger *when he calls you to that which gives you life*. Three notes fix the verse's scope. What gives life, the commentators say: al-Ṭabarī transmits the salaf's "the Qur'an" and "the truth"; al-Qurṭubī adds that it is whatever carries God's commands and prohibitions — the *Sharīʿah* as the science of living, and a life beyond dying; and a third reading — "the call to jihad, for death in God's way is everlasting felicity" — fits the sūrah's field: Badr's dead are the verse's first exhibits, men called to what *looked like* death and was life. The verse thus welds the two horizons: the here-and-now vitality of a heart obeying (v. 2's living tissue, v. 21's true hearing) and the resurrection-life the whole sūrah is training the community for — *"Is one who was dead, then We gave him life and made for him a light by which to walk among the people, like one who is in the darkness, never to emerge from it?"* (6:122). And the *istijābah* is demanded *idhā daʿā* — at the moment of the call: this verse exists to make the interval between hearing and doing as short as the interval between lightning and thunder was for the Companions, for whom a command falling on the tongue found the feet already moving.

**"God Comes Between a Man and His Heart": the Verse of Holy Fear and Holy Hope**

*Wa'-ʿlamū anna Allāha yaḥūlu bayna'l-marʾi wa-qalbahu* — know that He interposes between a man and his own heart. The source commentary lays out the layers, and each is load-bearing. (1) *Omniscience*: He is nearer than the jugular vein (50:16); your heart's weather is known before the man reads his own sky. (2) *Sovereignty over change*: "He comes between the disbeliever and his faith, and between the believer and his unbelief" — the heart is *His*, and He can flip it from aversion to serenity in the night between two camps at Badr (v. 11 — fear became sleep), as He flips it the other way; hence the Prophet's ﷺ most frequent supplication, in the ḥadīth of Anas:

> *"Yā muqalliba'l-qulūb, thabbit qalbī ʿalā dīnik — O Turner of hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion."* (al-Tirmidhī 2140, graded ḥasan; Aḥmad)

and Muslim's wording that the hearts of the sons of Adam lie "between two fingers of the All-Merciful, turning them however He wills" (Muslim 2654). (3) *Practical psychology*: you do not own the seat of your own intentions — so do not trust your heart's present fervor as a permanent possession, and do not despair of any heart's distance from God. The clause is the sūrah's antidote to both the hypocrite's security (v. 21's "we hear") and the sinner's hopelessness (v. 23's foreknown turning): between a man and the will to turn or not to turn, stands — God. Fear Him, therefore: He can seal. Hope in Him, therefore: He can open.

**"And to Him You Shall Be Gathered" — the Last Clause as the Weight on the Scale**

*Wa-anna-hu ilayhi tuḥsharūn* — and to Him you are gathered. The gathering (*ḥashr*) is this sūrah's recurring horizon (vv. 6, 24, 37, 39, 50), and its placement here completes the verse's triad: the call (v. 24a), the heart's Owner (24b), the Assembly (24c). The logic is simple and unescapable: to respond to a call is to acknowledge a return — the one who says "I'll answer later" is, in the verse's structure, the one who denies the gathering; the one who believes he is headed to a standing before God answers *now*, because "now" is the only tense the gathering leaves him. Ibn Kathīr notes the salaf's use of the clause: ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib is reported in the tafsīr corpus to have recited this verse when asked about a matter of communal urgency, meaning: the one who knows he is being driven to the account answers the Caller while there is still a question to answer. And the tradition's image for the un-answering is the same verse's rebuke: the man gathered with his call unanswered — *"and on that Day the hypocrites, men and women, will say to those who believed: 'Look at us, let us borrow from your light'"* (57:13) — the borrowing being exactly what the *stijābah* at the moment of call made unnecessary.

**The Verse as the Community's Constitution of Response**

Widen the lens: vv. 20–24 have now built, in five short verses, the complete Qur'anic theory of communication between Heaven and the community: obey and don't turn while hearing (20); don't claim the hearing (21); don't waste the faculties (22); don't force the seal (23); answer the life-giving call, and hold nothing — not even your own heart — as private property (24). Every station of Badr's failure was one of these: the averse group (20's turning), the disputers (21's false claim), Quraysh's court (22's beasts), the sealed mockers (23), and — the verse's cure — the men of v. 2 whose hearts quaked and grew and trusted, who are here told: *keep the reflex you are known for*. That is why "O you who believe" stands twice in five verses: this community's greatness, the sūrah keeps saying, is not in its arms but in the speed and honesty of its *yes* — and the Day of Gathering is the audit of every yes that was only a maybe.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:25

> **And be mindful of a trial that will not befall only those among you who do wrong; and know that God is severe in retribution.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Physics of Communal Sin**

*Wa-ttaqū fitnatan lā tuṣībanna'lladhīna ẓalamū minkum khāṣṣah* — "and guard against a *fitnah* that will not strike exclusively the wrongdoers among you." The grammar of the verb *tuṣībanna* with the exclusive *khāṣṣah* and the oath-like emphasis makes the clause a statement of social physics: a wrong permitted in a community behaves like water entering a shared hull — it does not stop at the guilty compartment. *Fitnah* here covers, as the source notes, both senses the word carries in the Qur'an — trial/chastisement and sedition/strife (cf. 2:190–194's *al-fitnatu ashaddu min al-qatl*) — and the verse fuses them into a single warning: civil strife is the punishment, and the punishment begins as strife. The wrongdoers set the fuse; the whole population pays at the till; and everyone who could have cut the wire and didn't is *among* the till-payees. Al-Ṭabarī transmits under this verse the companions' line that it names a collective contagion whose carriers include the silent.

**The Hadith of the Ship: al-Qurṭubī's Parable Made Law**

The commentary tradition anchors the verse in a parable of the Prophet ﷺ that al-Qurṭubī cites here and that stands complete in the two Ṣaḥīḥs, from al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr:

> *"The parable of those who uphold God's limits and those who transgress them is like a people who drew lots over a ship: some settled on its deck and some below. When those below needed water, they passed through those above, and said: 'If we made a hole in our portion and did not harm our brothers above us?' — if they leave them to their intent, all will drown, and if they take hold of their hands, all will be saved, and all will live."* (al-Bukhārī 2493)

The image is the verse in one drawing: the *below-deck* men are not villains; they want water — the small, real appetites of sinners; they have "a right" to their own compartment — the rhetoric every evader uses; the harm they plan is invisible from the deck — and the Qur'an says the deck's indifference is the crew's grave. Hence the verse's command is not merely "do no wrong" but *be mindful* (ittaqū) of the wrong's *spread*: the believer is deputized into vigilance, and silence is a leak. The companion-ḥadīth of Hudhayfah seals it with the penalty of abdication:

> *"By Him in whose hand is my soul, you will command the good and forbid the evil — or God will soon send a punishment upon you from Himself, and you will call upon Him and He will not answer you."* (al-Tirmidhī 2169, graded ṣaḥīḥ)

**The Sword of al-Jamal: the Verse as Prophecy Fulfilled Among Friends**

The most unsettling classical report on this verse — transmitted by al-Qurṭubī and others — comes from the generation after Badr's children: at the Battle of the Camel (36/656), the first Muslim civil war, a companion who recognized the verse recited: *"I did not know what was meant by that verse until today."* The horror of the sentence is its arithmetic: the men who met each other's steel at al-Jamal were not Quraysh pagans; they were the armies of ʿAlī and of Ṭalḥah, al-Zubayr, and ʿĀʾishah — the very People of Badr, whose validity of faith the Qur'an itself certified (vv. 3–4, 72–75). If wrong done at the top — even wrong born of sincere ijtihād — can make the whole ship take water, then "the fitnah that does not strike only the evildoers" is not the description of a future pagan persecution; it is the description of the believers' own house. That is why the Sunni creedal tradition drew from verses like this its settled ruling on the Civil Wars: withhold judgment on the individuals (whose rank the Qur'an protects), and take from the event the law forever — that the community's peace is a trust (v. 27's *amānāt*) that the "silent" violate as much as the swordsmen; ʿAlī's own reported words when the Khawarij cried "no judgment but God's": *"a word of truth by which falsehood is intended"* (al-Bukhārī 71; Muslim 1066) — the precision of the slogan is exactly the precision the fitnah lacked, and that v. 25 demands.

**"Severe in Retribution": Why the Threat Is Not the Whole Verse**

Note that the verse's second half — *wa-ʿlamū anna Allāha shadīd al-ʿiqāb* — matches the refrain of vv. 13 and 52, and its function here is proportion, not menace. The punishment of communal decay is "severe" because its *scale* is communal: the punishment that falls on the whole ship cannot be small. But the same two attributes that seal the Qur'an's warnings of severity appear in the sūrah's promises of rescue (*ʿazūb* beside *ʿafuww, raḥīm* — v. 29, v. 69: "God is Forgiving, Merciful"), and the tradition's ethic of *amr bi'l-maʿrūf* is thus never an ethic of the denouncer: hands must be taken, the verse says — and taking hands is saving hands, "if they take hold of their hands, all will be saved, and all will live." The severity clause's work is to destroy the illusion of the bystander: there is no bystander; there are only crew — and the first mercy available to the drowning is the one who, at the hole, starts patching.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:26

> **And remember when you were few, deemed weak in the land, fearing that the people would snatch you away. Then He sheltered you, and strengthened you with His help, and provided you with good things, that haply you may give thanks.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Memory Cure for a Community with New Money**

*Wa-dhkurū idh kuntum qalīlan mustaḍʿafūna fi'l-arḍi takhāfūna an yatakhaṭṭafakumu'n-nās* — remember when you were few, ground down, seized about by men's fear.
