The sūrah's rhythm throughout is memory as medicine: it will not let Badr's victors forget the hour before Badr, because gratitude is the organ that keeps prosperity from mutating into the spoils-syndrome of v. 1. The three adjectives of the Makkah years — *qalīl* (few in number), *mustaḍʿaf* (deemed weightless, *ḍ-ʿ-f*: the passive root the Qur'an uses for the oppressed, as in 4:75 and 4:98: "the oppressed men, women, and children"), and *takhāfūna* (watching for the snatch) — re-create the exact texture: the boy eaten by his own family's boycott, the slave stretched on the sand under hot iron, Khabbāb's memory of the saw: *"a woman used to be taken among those before you and a saw placed on her head and she was sawn in two, and a man combed with iron combs beneath his flesh and between his bones — yet God will complete this affair"* (al-Bukhārī 3612). The community of the spoils was that community, thirteen years earlier; the verse keeps the ledger of *then* open so that the ledger of *now* cannot read itself as self-made.

**Three Acts of Grace, in Ascending Order**

*Thumma āwākum fa-ayyadakum bi-naṣrihi wa razaqaka min al-ṭayyibāti laʿallakum tashkurūn* — He gave you a refuge; then strengthened you with His help; then provided you of the good things; *so that you might thank*. The sequence is a theology of history compressed into three verbs, and the verbs run shelter → support → provision. *Āwā* — to grant an *maʾwā*, a home, a place to which one returns: the *Madīnah* itself is the word's fruit, the Anṣār's houses its mechanism (ṭ-ṭ: 59:9's "they prefer them before themselves, even though they are in privation" is the verse's human face); *fa-ayyadakum bi-naṣrihi* — the angelic ranks of vv. 9–12, the sleep, the rain: the help is *ayd* (hand-power), attributed to Him, exactly as the killing and the throwing were (v. 17); *wa razaqaka min al-ṭayyibāt* — the spoils, the harvests of the Wādī, the camels — named not as loot but as *ṭayyibāt*, good and clean and tasty: provision with the moral hygiene of *ṭayyib* still on it (4:4's *ṭayyibāt* of women's wealth; 2:172's command to "eat of the good things" that ties provision to gratitude). And the purpose-clause lands at the end like the keystone: *laʿallakum tashkurūn* — so that you would thank. Not: so that you would be wealthy, secure, or avenged. The *purpose* of the three gifts is a state of the recipients' souls. This is the whole Qur'anic doctrine of blessing at one verse: *"[God] gave you from everything you asked Him; and if you count the favor of God, you cannot enumerate it"* (14:34); *"If you are thankful, I will surely increase you"* (14:7); and the counter-verse that shadows it: *"No, man is transgressive when he sees himself self-sufficient"* (96:6–7). The thankfulness is not flattery paid to the Benefactor — He is enriched by nothing — but the creature's own return to correct proportion: to *know* (the verse's first verb, "remember") that the shelter, the hand, and the bread all arrived, and to live accordingly: to share the shelter, to lean on the hand, to spread the bread.

**Istidʿāf Inverted: the Promise of the Oppressed**

Behind v. 26 stands the Mosaic template the Qur'an supplies for all history's weak: *"And We wished to bestow favor upon those who were deemed weak in the land, and to make them leaders and heirs, and to establish them in the land"* (28:5–6); and Pharaoh's land is exactly the place "the people snatched them away." Moses said to his people in 7:128, the sūrah's parallel: *"Seek help in God and be patient — indeed the earth is God's; He inherits it to whom He wills of His servants; and the outcome is for the reverent."* The verse's *mustaḍʿafūna* is the same word as those passages, and this sūrah's *fa-ayyadakum bi-naṣrihi* the same *naṣr* as 22:40's promise ("and if God did not repel some people by others, monasteries... would have been destroyed"). Read as legislation, then, 8:26 is the community's charter of *reverse triumphalism*: your rank in history's ledger comes from having *been* the crushed; the hour of power is the test of whether you remember the weight, and every future *mustaḍʿaf* in the world is, by the verse's own pattern, the next candidate of 28:5. The spoils that opened the sūrah must therefore be divided with that memory attached: the khums' four recipients — kin, orphans, the indigent, the traveler (v. 41) — are the *mustaḍʿafūn* of the camp, and the verse before them (v. 27's *amānāt*) is their guard.

**"That Haply You May Give Thanks": the End the Verse Still Awaits**

A final reading note: the *laʿalla* of hope — "so that *perhaps* you would thank" — is the Qur'an's tense of freedom: the purpose is real, its achievement is not automatic, and the whole sūrah after this verse will prove the *perhaps* unresolved — the ransom debate (67–69), the ratios (65–66), the inheritance (75). A community can be given shelter, help, and bread, and still fail the purpose, as Israel in the desert was given and was ungrateful; and this is why the verse's form is a *dhikr*-command, an act of remembering, not a claim that the remembering is done. For the individual reader the instruction is the same as the sūrah's: at every rise — the raise, the recovery, the birth, the victory — run the three-step audit the verse performs: what shelter was given when you had none; what help came when the cause was lost; what provision arrived that no effort earned — and then thank: with the tongue's *al-ḥamdu lillāh* the Prophet ﷺ praised — *"God is pleased with a servant who says 'al-ḥamdu lillāh' after a meal"* (Muslim 2734) — and, as the tradition never tires of adding, with the hand: *shukr* in the Qur'an is never said by lips alone, which is why this verse, in a sūrah of spoils, was always going to end where money is the organ of the heart.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:27

> **O you who believe! Betray not God and the Messenger, and betray not your trusts knowingly.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**When the Enemy Is Inside the Walls**

*Yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū lā takhūnū amānātikum… wa-lā takhūnū Allāha wa'r-Rasūla wa-takhūnū amānātikum* — betray not God, and the Messenger, and your trusts. The triple object is the verse's design: treason against God, treason against His Messenger, and treason in the ledger of ordinary deposits — and the three are welded into one imperative because, in this sūrah's world, they are one organ failure. Treason against God is the violation of what He has made *amānah*: the prayer, the covenant, the khums of verse 41, the "criterion" of v. 29; treason against the Messenger is breaking the terms of the community he built, from his commands in the camp to the pledges made in his name; treason in *amānāt* is the neighbor's safe kept with a smile and emptied by hand. That the verse stands *after* Badr and *before* the laws of prisoners and treaties shows its function: a community that has learned not to loot the field with swords is being tested on whether it will loot it with hands — and *khiyānah* (treachery) is the sūrah's name for the smaller, quieter version of the same appetite. Al-Ṭabarī transmits the occasions of revelation reported for it: a garment, or a portion of the spoils, kept back before the distribution (the incident of a cloak hidden among the camp's property), and — in the related report preserved in al-Bukhārī — the affair of Tuʿma ibn Abīraq, who stole an armor and hid it in flour, while his clan swore to his innocence (al-Bukhārī 4334, 6774; and 4:105–107 was revealed for the same crime). The Prophet ﷺ's line in the ḥadīth of the banners stands behind all of it:

> *"Give the one to whom you gave your word your word, and betray not the one who gave you his word."* (Abū Dāwūd 2640, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd — and cf. 4:58's "if you call between people, call with justice")

**Amānah: the Load-Bearing Word of the Qur'an**

*Amānah* grows from *amn*, security — a thing deposited where the owner can rest. The word's range in the Qur'an is the verse's syllabus: God's trusts "and the Messenger's trusts" — for the Qur'an itself is *amānah* (2:283's deposits, 33:72's cosmic offer: *"We proposed the trust to the heavens and earth and mountains — they refused and feared it; man carried it"* — the classical commentators, following ʿAlī as transmitted in al-Ṭabarī, read that *amānah* as the load of command and obedience, of which vv. 20's *ṭāʿat* is the rehearsal); the ruler's office, which the Prophet ﷺ named *amānah* and *dhull* (humiliation) — *"the work you seek is a trust, and on the Day of Resurrection it is regret, unless one takes it by its right and discharges what it requires"* (Muslim 1832); the witness's word, the employee's hour, the spouse's privacy, the secret shared over coffee; and, for the mystics, the body itself — the Prophet ﷺ read the trust's weight at ʿArafa in the great sermon: *"your blood, your wealth, your honor are sacred to one another as this day of yours"* (al-Bukhārī 67; Muslim 1218), and at home he summed the whole in one line: *"the best of you is the best to his family, and I am the best of you to my family"* (al-Tirmidhī 3895). Against this architecture stands the ḥadīth that defines betrayal's social face with clinical exactitude — *"the signs of the hypocrite are three: when he speaks he lies, when he promises he breaks, and when he is entrusted, he betrays"* (al-Bukhārī 33; Muslim 59). The verse and the ḥadīth together teach the community its deepest civic law: *amānah* is the truest single test of faith — higher than public piety, because a man's dīwān can be clean while his desk is not; and the sūrah's answer at vv. 2–4 (hearts that quake, hands that give, trusts that rely) is what the dīwān of *tark al-khiyānah* actually is.

**"Knowingly": the Clause That Draws the Line**

*Wa-antum taʿlamūn* — while you know. The participle is the whole of moral theology at the point where error becomes betrayal: a trust failed through weakness, forgetfulness, or ignorance is a *lapse* — the community repairs it, the law of deposits compensates it, and the verse's other clause, "God does not burden a soul beyond its capacity" (2:286), covers it; a trust failed *while knowing* — with the ledger of the arrangement clear in the mind and the heart choosing the other hand's gain — is *khiyānah* in the verse's sense: a partitioning-off of religion from appetite, the mouth saying *āmannā* while the pocket works against it. That is the sūrah's own diagnosis of what v. 1's quarrel was *in danger of becoming*: men who knew the spoils were God's and the Messenger's, and yet… The exegetes (al-Rāzī in particular) note the grave balance the clause keeps: the *knowledge* that makes betrayal culpable is the same knowledge that was *given by God* — "and God knew that you would betray, and He pardoned you before" is the echo of 8:67–69's "had not a decree preceded from God, a severe punishment would have touched you for what you took" — the sūrah itself confessing how near its heroes walked to the treason this verse legislates. And the mercy inside the wording is that the verse commands what it knows them capable of: *lā takhūnū* — the prohibition assumes the conscience intact, the heart of v. 2 still pulsing; a people who must be told "do not betray" are a people who can be trusted with the trusts: *khayrukum khayrukum li-ahl — the best of you is the best to his family* (al-Tirmidhī 3895), the smallest *amānah* the test of the largest.

**The Trust and the Treaty: the Verse's Outer Edge**

The jurists, reading *amānātikum* in this sūrah of war, did not stop at private deposits: the treaty with the enemy, the pledge of the imam, the safe-conduct (*amān*) granted to one who came in trust — all fall under the verse, and the next verses (56–58) are its foreign policy: *do not betray a covenant even with traitors; if you fear a people's treachery, throw their treaty back to them on equal terms.* That is the sūrah's ultimate demonstration that *khiyānah* is not a sin one commits "against the system" — the system *is* the trust; and the Prophet's ﷺ line on the day of conquest stands as the verse's crown: *"Whoever kills a person under covenant will not smell Paradise, whose fragrance extends from the distance of four hundred years"* (al-Bukhārī 3160). A community that betrays even the betrayer has betrayed, at that moment, its God and Messenger — for the law it broke is not a favor to the enemy; it is a trust from the One who commanded fidelity *even when the other side has none.*

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:28

> **And know that your property and your children are only a trial, and that God—with Him is a great reward.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Idols Everyone Kisses**

*Wa'-ʿlamū annamā amwālukum wa-awlādukum fitnatun* — know that your wealth and your children are nothing but a *fitnah*. The verse names the two attachments that rule human history with the one word, *fitnah*, that Arabic borrows from the goldsmith's craft: *faṭana*, to put ore in the fire to see what it is (hence the fire of the grave's questioning, the *munāfaqūn* whose un-belief is detected like dross). Wealth and children are not called evil — the Qur'an calls wealth "good" (*khayr*, 2:180) and children "the adornment of life" (18:46), and this very sūrah has just narrated *God* "providing you of good things" (v. 26) — they are called the *test*, and the test is whether the blessing will be *used* or *worshipped*. The verse's placement after v. 27's law of trusts is the logic made social: *amānah* is broken by men not in the abstract but under the two specific pressures named here — what the wallet demands and what the nursery demands — the two most honorable pretexts for betrayal. The twin verse of Taghābun, which the source cites, says the same in its hardest voice: *"Among your spouses and your children there is an enemy to you, so beware of them"* (64:14) — "enemy" not of your person, but of your hereafter, in the name of love.

**How Wealth Tries: the Ledger of the Heart**

The Prophet ﷺ gave the ummah its wealth-psychology in sentences that the whole sūrah preaches: *"How wretched is the slave of the dinar, the slave of the dirham, the slave of the fine garment — if given, he is pleased; if not given, he is angry"* (al-Bukhārī 2887) — the *ʿabd* (slave) idiom chosen deliberately: to be owned by what you own is the exact inversion of the creature's rank, and v. 1's men clutching their shares of armor were one degree short of that slavery. He taught his companions the fear that inverts the ordinary one: *"By God, it is not poverty I fear for you — but I fear that the world is spread out for you as it was spread out for those before you, and you compete in it as they competed, and it destroys you as it destroyed them"* (al-Bukhārī 4015; Muslim 2971). And he gave the definition of the world's mode of deception: *"This world is sweet and green, and God has made you inheritors in it, to see how you act"* (Muslim 2742) — sweet, so you eat it; green, so you admire it; a lease, so you must use it *while* holding it: trial precisely *through* enjoyment. The sūrah's own economy (v. 1: spoils to God and the Messenger; v. 41: the fifth; v. 3: spending of what He provided) is the *method* of passing: money liquidated into justice, grace, and the poor, never solidified into a self.

**How Children Try: the Two Failures, Fear and Greed**

The children's trial runs in the opposite direction from wealth's. For them a man will *acquire* unlawfully ("my children after me shall not be left poor while men get shares" — the reported anxiety of the pre-Islamic Arab and of the early Muslims alike, in which the inheritance-verses of 4:11–12 were revealed, says Ibn Kathīr, precisely against hoarding for heirs beyond what God assigned), and for them he will *fail to act*: the hadith's line that the child makes a man *miserly and cowardly* (Aḥmad; classed ṣaḥīḥ) is v. 5's aversion at Badr with a family name attached — the father's legs do not go out to the battle his child needs not to lose. Yet the same children are, in other texts of the same Prophet, the door of mercy: *"Whoever is tested with something of these daughters and treats them well, they will be a shield for him from the Fire"* (al-Bukhārī 1418; Muslim 2629), and the tradition's consoling promise that the parent of children taken young is given a house of praise in Paradise (Aḥmad, the ḥadīth of Abū al-ʿĀṣ). The Qur'an's verdict in v. 28 resolves both directions: the children are trial — whether as cause of the parent's sins, or as his trial through loss. Either way, they are not the *reward*; the verse's last clause exists to stop them being treated as the end. The *fitnah* of the child is, at its root, the soul's love of continuity — legacy, name, genealogy — and the sūrah has just re-drawn genealogy for the community: the *walāʾ* bonds (v. 72) and "kinship ties have the greater right in God's Book" (v. 75) are kinship re-calibrated under the one Lord whose "with Him is a great reward" is the only inheritance that is not trial.

**"…and with God is a Great Reward": the Second Option**

The *wa-* that joins the two clauses — and with God is a great reward — is the verse's escape hatch, and its function is comparative rather than dismissive: a trial with a limit and a reward without one, a fire that purifies and a gold that "no eye has seen" (al-Bukhārī 7518; Muslim 2860, in the famous *ḥadīth qudsī* of the prepared Garden). Ibn Kathīr glosses: as if to say — do not let what is trial divert you from what is reward; take from the wealth what is *spent* and leave what is *inherited*, and from the children raise what is *dutiful* and do not commit what is *damning*. That is the *mīzān* — the criterion (v. 29!) — applied to the household: a wealth held at the measure of spending and a love of children at the measure of taqwa. And the verse's own word — *mā* in *annamā* — is the restrictive construction: *nothing but* a trial, i.e., they have no other office in the final court: they are not proof of favor (94:5's comfort cuts the other way), not security (4:9's threat against those who leave weak heirs behind is the same), not redemption. The Day the whole sūrah gathers toward (v. 16's *maṣīr*, v. 24's *tuḥsharūn*) will admit only what was carried *through* these two trials — which is why, of all Qur'anic verses on wealth and family, this one ends by pointing at what is *beyond* the house and the purse, and names it "great," so that the house and purse stay in proportion.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:29

> **O you who believe! If you reverence God, He will make for you a criterion, and absolve you of your evil deeds, and forgive you. And God is Possessed of Tremendous Bounty.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Master-Promise**

*Yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū in tattaqū Allāha yajʿal lakum furqānan wa-yukaffir ʿankum sayyiʾātikum wa-yaghfir lakum, wa-Allāhu dhū'l-faḍli'l-ʿaẓīm* — if you are reverent toward God, He will grant you a *furqān*, expunge your evils, and forgive you. This is the sūrah's central covenant — the thesis that explains every detail around it. For the whole of 8:1–28 was the *problem*: a community at war with its own appetites (loot, fear, faction), and the cure cannot be more rules alone, because rules do not change the eye that chooses to break them. What the cure needs is a *faculty of discernment*, a light by which the real is seen as real and the false as false — and the verse promises exactly that, for the price of the one quality already defined in vv. 2–4: *taqwā*. Al-Rāzī's gloss, quoted by the source, is the metaphysics of the promise: "when the heart is illuminated by obedience to God, darkness falls away from it — for the knowledge of God is light, and faults are kinds of darkness, and where light appears, darkness necessarily disappears." *Taqwā* is thus not the bribe for the *furqān*; it *is* the *furqān* in its moral shape — the guarding (root *w-q-y*) that keeps the eye clean.

**Furqān: the Name the Qur'an Gave to Its Own Day**

The word is the sūrah's own architecture. *Furqān* — from *f-r-q*, to split, separate, discriminate — names first the Qur'an itself ("Blessed is He who sent down the Furqān upon His servant," 25:1; and 2:2, 2:185, 3:3–4, 21:48 as the source notes), and second the *Battle of Badr*, which this sūrah calls "the Day of the Furqān, the day the two companies met" (v. 41) — the day the two ways of being human (revelation's community and Quraysh's honor-culture) were separated in the open, with swords for punctuation. Verse 29 puts the same word in the believer's *chest*: the inner Badr, the daily separation of the true from the false in the heart of the one who is reverent. The promise is therefore threefold in its object: the *Book* as criterion, the *history* as criterion, the *conscience* as criterion — and the tradition treats the inner one as the fruit of the other two: 39:22's "one whose breast God expands to submission, upon a light from his Lord" is the verse's parallel in the source's notes, and the famous prophetic ethic of conscience is its practical law: *"Leave that which puts you in doubt for that which does not put you in doubt, for truth is tranquility and falsehood is agitation"* (al-Tirmidhī 2518, from al-Nawawī's first collection). The *muṭtaqī* — the reverent man of v. 1 and v. 29 — is the one in whom that inner furqān has been installed: not a new information-service, but the old truth *seen from the inside*, a taste for the real (v. 14's *dhawq* inverted for the believer) that no appetite can overrule.

**Why Taqwā Buys What No Cunning Can Buy**

The logic of the verse's first clause is worth stating plainly, for it answers a permanent objection: isn't discernment a matter of intelligence, not piety? The Qur'an's epistemology says otherwise, because the decisive obscurity is not ignorance but *interest* — the lover of a thing sees its reasons as its merits (28:88's "he who is blind to it in this world will be blinder on the Day of Journey"). Taqwā is the *dis-interest* that clears the lens: the man who guards himself against God has, by that very guarding, nothing to gain from a falsehood being true, and the universe's testimony therefore reaches him unblocked. This is why the sūrah's entire Badr story is the *proof* of the promise: the community that feared God more than the caravan (v. 7 — "you wished the un-armed; God wished the truth") saw the real issue of the day while Quraysh, brilliant in trade and politics, mis-saw everything — the truest *furqān* moment is v. 44, where God *made each army few in the other's eyes*: the one people given inner sight was able to see through the mirage; the other, self-possessed in every other faculty, was given over to the mirage they made for themselves. Ibn Kathīr, adducing 2:282's "and let not one whose right is upon him refuse to write it" and the like, draws out the general principle: God grants each taqwa-bearing heart a *light by which it walks among people* (6:122's idiom), and this is the sense of the salaf's line preserved in al-Ṭabarī: "the furqān here is deliverance (makhraj) in the matter and clarity for the heart" — the promise covers both: the *eye* that distinguishes and the *exit* through the situation, which the reverent man always finds ("and whoever is reverent toward God, He will make a way out for him and provide for him from whence he reckons not," 65:2–3).

**"And Expunges Your Evils and Forgives You": the Two Cleansings, and Why Both**

The second and third promises are one act described twice, and the twice-telling is the mercy: *yukaffir* — to lay a cover over, from *kafūr*, the roof that hides what is under — and *yaghfir* — to wrap up, from *ghifr*, the helmet's lining and the sand that hides a footprint. First, the *erasing of the record*: evils expunged as if they had weight that is lifted; then, the *reconciliation*: sins forgiven as a wound healed, not merely hidden. The pairing teaches what the tradition insists upon: God's forgiveness with the Qur'an is never one verb — He *covers* the act and *transforms* the agent; and the sequence keeps the sūrah's first generation inside the verse: the men who disputed over spoils (v. 1), turned in fear (v. 5), argued after clarity (v. 6) — all of it is the "evils" to be *kaffara*, and *taqwā*, the quality that drove them to leave the claims, is what bought the cover. And the final seal, *wa-Allāhu dhū'l-faḍli'l-ʿaẓīm* — God is the Lord of tremendous bounty — states the verse's whole economy in four words: nothing here was a wage. The furqān was a gift; the cleansing was grace; *faḍl* is what the debtor receives beyond his contract, and the contract of the reverent is only that he tried — hence the salaf's etiquette with this verse, to hope from bounty rather than plead from effort, exactly as v. 17's "you did not kill them" taught the victors of Badr, in whose camp it was spoken, never to sign their names to the deed.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:30

> **And [remember] when those who disbelieve plotted against thee, to capture thee, or to slay thee, or to expel thee. They plotted, and God plotted. And God is the best of plotters.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Night of the Council: What "Plotted" Names**

*Wa-idh yamkuru biyka'lladhīna kafarū li-yuthbitūka aw yaqtulūka aw yukhrijūk* — when the disbelievers schemed against you to pin you down, or kill you, or drive you out. The sūrah flashes back to Makkah before the Hijrah — the night the chiefs met at the *Dār al-Nadwah* and, on Iblīs's advice in the shape of a hoary elder, took the three-options vote the verse recites verbatim: shut him in his house and wait for the worst of deaths to visit him there; kill him with a sword shared by one youth from every clan so the blood is untraceable; or exile him and be rid of him forever. The verb *makara* means to bend a plan toward its target through hidden joints — a trap laid in the dark, and the sūrah's point of history is that the Prophet's ﷺ deliverance from that night (sleeping Ali in his bed to confuse the watchers, dust cast over their heads as he stepped out — "and We have placed before them a yoke and behind them a yoke, and have covered them so they see not," 36:9, which the sīrah reports him reciting over his pursuers) was the very axis on which Madinah, Badr, and this whole sura exist. The "capture — kill — expel" is the three punishments the world reserves for the messenger, and the verse names them in order of their failure: he could not be pinned, could not be killed, and *was* expelled — in the technical sense: he left before they could, which is the verse's entire doctrine of divine *makr* in one sentence: God let the third option happen *on his terms, not theirs*, and made the expulsion the *victory* (for the flight was the founding of the state whose army now held the field at Badr). Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī preserve Ibn ʿAbbās's epigram on the verse's symmetry: God dealt with each option on the Day of Badr — *capture* (the seventy prisoners), *killing* (the seventy slain), and *expulsion* (the flight of the remnant from the field); some transmitted additions even say that within a decade every one of the council's conspirators had been executed, exiled, or captured at Badr.

**"And God plotted" — How the Word of Deception Is Said of God**

*Wa-makarū wa-makara Allāh* — the same verb, both sides, and the sentence that follows — *wa-Allāhu khayru'l-mākirīn*, God is the best of planners — has been the battlefield of the sūrah's whole theology. The Muʿtazilīs, wary of attributing *deception* (and *khudāʿ*, which they held *makr* to imply, is a sin) to God, re-read every such verse as "the requital of their plot with their own kind": God "plots" only in that He executes against them the consequences of the trap they dug, as the source commentary notes for 3:54 and the cluster 10:21; 13:42; 14:46; 16:26; 27:50; and 68:45's "My plot is firm." The Ashʿarīs and the traditionalists reply that *makr* is a name for *a plan that reaches its target through hidden joints* — and what is blameworthy is not the hiddenness but the *injustice* of the end; God's plans are just by definition, since no one's right is infringed in the dominion He owns — "He is not asked about what He does, but they will be asked" (21:23); the Qur'an swears, at 86:15–17, that the disbelievers' *kayd* and God's *makr* are both real, and that the second is the *firm* one. What the verse therefore forbids is the naïveté that reads history as brute force: there is a hidden joint in events, and it is *occupied* — the plot against the plot is not paranoia but an article of the creed: *wa-Allāhu ghālibun ʿalā amrihi* — "God is dominant over His affair, but most people know not" (12:21). At Badr's dawn this is already proven: the "defender of the caravan" had lost the caravan, and the caravan's defenders had lost their chiefs.

**The Cave: God's Plot Seen from the Inside**

The sūrah's memory of that night is not the escape alone but the hiding — the three days in the cave of *Thawr* — whose detail the sīrah fixes with the image of the pigeon and the spider at the cave's mouth, and whose moment the Qur'an narrates in the verse the commentators set beside this one: *"...when they were in the cave, when He said to His companion: 'Do not grieve — indeed God is with us'"* (9:40); and Abū Bakr's terror was answered by the Prophet ﷺ with the line preserved in the Ṣaḥīḥayn: *"What do you think of two, whose third is God?"* (al-Bukhārī 3653; Muslim 2381). The verse's *makr* of God is thus shown at its exact proportion: no army, no thunder — a spider's thread, a pigeon's nest, the dust in the eyes of the watchers, a cousin in a bed. This is a whole epistemology of deliverance the sūrah keeps teaching: God's counter-plot runs through the smallest and least contestable materials, and its "best-ness" lies precisely in its economy: *"you did not slay them, but God slew them"* (v. 17) is the same law on the field as here at the door, and the same comfort to the *mustaḍʿafūn* of v. 26 — the pinned-down, the hunted, the man "in the cave" of his hour: the best of planners has already begun; His threads are invisible by design.

**For the Believer Who Is Neither Prophet Nor Persecuted**

The verse's *li-yuthbitūka* — "to pin you down" — is the danger it teaches the community to recognize: the plot's aim is paralysis, the trapping of the initiative, and the counter is neither panic nor matching cunning but *movement under trust*: the Prophet ﷺ left the bed to Ali, and went to the cave, and the plan proceeded — *makr* answered by *tawakkul* and *hijrah*: the two verbs the sūrah's end (vv. 72–75) will make the constitution of the community. The ethic for later hours follows exactly: where the enemy's work is to pin, the believer's work is to *depart toward the lawful*; where the enemy's strength is the hidden joint, the believer's is the secret He owns: "they plan, and God plans, and the decision belongs to God" (12:42, Joseph's line, the source's parallel). And the last clause — *khayru'l-mākirīn* — is read in the great sermons not as a threat but as a *definition of the field*: no trap closes on the one God is un-trapping; and if the pinning succeeds for an hour (the cave, the chains, the exile), the hour is inside the longer plot — *"and We made the most weak of the people inheritors of the east of the earth and its west"* (7:137), the verse of inheritance, which is what v. 26 of this sūrah applied to the first community's own experience of the plot against their Leader in the House.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:31

> **And when Our signs are recited unto them they say, "We have heard already. Had we willed, we could have said the like of this. This is naught but fables of those of old."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Sentences That Say One Thing: "It Is Nothing"**

*Wa-idh tutlā ʿalayhim āyātunā qālū qad samiʿnā law nashā'u la-qulnā mithla hādhā in hādhā illā asāṭīru'l-awwalīn* — the verse compresses Quraysh's counter-recitation into three claims, and their order is the anatomy of every dismissal of revelation. First: *qad samiʿnā* — "we have heard this before" (the reduction of the unprecedented to the precedent: the orphans' inheritance verses are "the tales of the Persians," the promises of Paradise are "what Muhammad promised his companion," the Qur'an is "borrowed from a man" — 16:103 — the tradition names the Roman carpenter, or the freed slaves Jubayr and Abū Fakiha, whose scriptures the Quraysh claimed the Prophet overheard). Second: *law nashāʾu la-qulnā mithlah* — "had we wished, we could have said the like" (the claim of competence without the act — a boast that is *unfalsifiable by design*, since "wishing" is the only condition and no one can verify the counterfactual). Third: *in hādhā illā asāṭīr* — "this is nothing but the fables of the ancients" (the verdict that names the genre as fraud: *asāṭīr* plural of *usṭūrah*, the round tale, the story told in circles to entertain; and *saṭr*, the written line, in 25:5's twin charge: "fables of the ancients which he has had them written down — they are dictated to him morning and evening"). The three are one disease: the refusal to let the text be *new*, because newness would claim them.

**The Claim Tested by the Challenge**

The Qur'an's answer is not polemic but an open experiment — the challenge the source commentary cross-references in full (2:23; 10:38; 11:13; 17:88): *produce a sūrah like it; produce ten; produce the like of the whole* — and the challenge is addressed, in some of its verses, to "whatever you call besides God" (10:38), that is, to the very *nashā'u* ("had we wished") of v. 31: the verse's second claim is the one the whole Meccan decade was set to refute. Al-Rāzī presses the logic in the chapter of *iʿjāz* in his *Mafātīḥ*: a claim of ability that is never exercised, while the claimant has every motive to exercise it (honor, the defeat of his enemy, the vindication of his people's gods), is evidence not of ability but of its absence — the way a physician reads a man who says "I could run, if I wished" while unable to stand. The Meccans kept the boast and never the production, and the sūrah — this sūrah — was recited in the same mosque where they had answered "we have heard before," and the *asāṭīr* charge died with the generation that uttered it while the recitation spread over three continents. Al-Qurṭubī notes the tradition's detail: al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith traveled to Ḥīrah, bought the legends of Rustam and Isfandiyār, and recited them against the Qur'an — "Muhammad tells fables of the ancients, and I tell the tales of the heroes of old" — an answer that shows what the *asāṭīr* theory was really for: not literary criticism, but the supply of a rival pleasure to a people being pricked by the text. The sūrah's verdict on the whole exchange came in 17:88: *"Say: if mankind and jinn gathered to produce the like of this Qur'an, they could not produce its like, even if they backed one another."*

**"We Have Heard Already" — the Psychology of Desacralization**

The first claim, *qad samiʿnā*, deserves the longest pause, because it is the permanent temptation of every audience with a book before it: to file the living word under the dead category it can handle. "We have heard this" is the sentence that closes the door on the *address*: what is familiar cannot command, cannot wound, cannot save. The Qur'an's own diagnosis (53:59) is aimed at exactly this habit — *"you marvel at this discourse and laugh and do not weep"* — and the tradition's science of recitation is built against it: the salaf would not sit to the Qur'an as to poetry, and ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd's line — "the one who wants to hear the Qur'an fresh (*jadīdan*) as it was revealed, let him recite it in the recitation of the blind shepherd" (i.e., his own, in Ibn Mājah and the sīrah reports) — is an attempt to keep *samiʿnā* from becoming *qad samiʿnā*. The believer of v. 2 is defined against this man: *his* hearts quake when God is *mentioned* — the word "mentioned," not "explained": the same Name, every time, heard as the first time. The sūrah's structure now says what the Qur'an says everywhere: the one who has heard, and hears, is the one who trembles; the one who has heard, and concludes he has heard, is the one in this verse — and the two sat in the same mosque, at the same recitation.

**Fables of the Old: What the Charge Actually Concedes**

And the third claim, finally, has an oddity the commentators enjoy pointing out: to call the Qur'an *asāṭīr* is to admit its *power* while denying its *source*. Fables do not overturn orders, do not re-divide inheritances, do not end female infanticide in a generation, do not make armed men weep — the Quraysh said of it what the poet of 68:14 said of the one who recites it: *"a soothsayer, a madman,"* and the verse answers "and what a soothsayer he is" (69:40–42). The accusation is, in fact, the confession the text keeps extracting from its enemies: in 11:13, God "stabilizes" against them, "each of the fables" of 25:5 was *written for their entertainment* and their commerce — and the miracle of 8:31's three claims is that they were *never* matched, not by the trade-caravan poets, not by the markets of ʿUkāẓ's memory, not at the court of the Negus, not by Musaylima whose imitation was the joke of the decade. The verse therefore stands in this sūrah as the monument of what the Qur'an's *first audience* thought of it — recorded, unrefuted, and, by its own terms, unrepeatable — and as the warning to its last: the hour the recitation is only "heard already" is the hour its miracle has, for that ear, become a fable; and the cure is not more hearing but the tremor, which alone makes every *qad samiʿnā* into v. 2's *zādat-hum īmānā* — a growth.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:32

> **And [remember] when they said, "O God, if this be the truth from Thee, rain down stones upon us from the sky, or bring us a painful punishment."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Prayer for Annihilation**

*Wa-idh qālū Allāhumma in kāna hādhihu'l-ḥaqqa min ʿindika fa-amṭir ʿalaynā ḥijāran mina's-samāʾi aw ti'tinā bi-ʿadhābin alīm* — the verse sets down one of the most extraordinary "supplications" in the Qur'an: the Quraysh's *duʿāʾ* at the Kaʿba, in which they ask God — in the name of God — to stone them if Muhammad's book is true. Its form is the full liturgy (the vocative, the condition, the demand for *either* of two terrors), and its content is the pure opposite of prayer: not a petition for guidance, but a wager of destruction laid against one's own neck. Ibn Kathīr reports from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr and others that it was al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith who said it in the assembly, and al-Ṭabarī transmits Ibn ʿAbbās's gloss: the *ḥijārah* — the rain of stones — was answered for them on the Day of Badr by the dust the Prophet ﷺ threw ("blighted be the faces," v. 17), and the *ʿadhāb alīm* by the sword. On this reading, 8:32 belongs to the sūrah's Badr-memories the way v. 30 belongs to its Hijrah-memories: the battle is narrated backwards, from its causes, as if to say: you are standing on the field your fathers prayed to be buried in — and the prayer was granted.

**The Grammar of the Blasphemy: "If This Be the Truth"**

Two features of the Arabic mark the depth of the presumption. First, the condition itself: *in kāna... min ʿindika* — "if it *is* from You" — puts the truth of revelation on trial with God as judge *and* executioner in one breath: the logic is that of 11:20's "they used to deny the meeting with God" and of the people of the Table's warning, *"whoever disbelieves after this among you, I will punish him with a punishment I punish none of the worlds with"* (5:115) — to ask for the proof is to accept the stakes of the proof. They *wanted* the stones, which means they had already decided to prefer death over doubt — the psychology of pride that would rather perish than submit. Second, the demand's form: *rain* (from *muṭr*, used deliberately against their own economy: to the caravan-people, the *rain* of stones echoes the rain — the *muṭayram* of the desert's hope; the verse's verb *amṭir* is the very idiom of blessing turned into a threat). The Qur'an elsewhere holds up this kind of "prayer" as the signature of the warn'd people: *"and their prayer in My presence is nothing but whistling and clapping"* (34:8's echo); *"and when they were told, 'what God has sent down,' they said, 'the like of what you bring'"* (2:170 cluster); and most exactly 25:21, the sentence this verse is paired with in the tafsīr books: *"those who do not expect Our meeting say: 'Why are not angels sent down upon us, or [why] do we not see our Lord?' — they have indeed made themselves great in their own souls."* Ibn Kathīr notes that the two — the unseen demanded and the punishment demanded — are the same disease: faith by spectacle, the refusal to believe at the price the Book offers (vv. 20–24 above have legislated the answer: *respond to the caller, for the heart is on loan*).

**The Answered and Unanswered Prayers of History**

What v. 32 teaches by its position — between the Quraysh's *asāṭīr* claim (v. 31) and the two stayings of grace (v. 33) — is the law of God's answers to hostile prayers. They asked for rain of stones and a painful punishment, and the verse says neither came *then*, while He gave them the longer reprieve: the punishment "is delayed to a day" (20:134's logic: *"if it were a swift punishment, the matter would have been settled among them"*), while at Badr a *taste* arrived — the "awful" (*alīm*, painful) being first the sword, whose sharpest edge fell on the very men of the Dār al-Nadwah: Abū Jahl, Umayyah, al-ʿĀṣ, ʿUtba — the council that chose the plot (v. 30) also chose the prayer (v. 32), and both were answered in one field. The doctrine for later listeners is double: first, the *danger of the conditional imprecation* — the Qur'an records that the Prophet ﷺ was forbidden to answer his persecutors' "then judge between us" beyond what was revealed (cf. 30:53's "there is no deferring of God's affair"; and the sīrah's line that the *samiʿnā*-generation was "in the Fire, whatever they did"); and second, the *mercy in silence*: many an "if this be the truth, then destroy us" has gone unanswered for a whole lifetime, not because it was false, but because the One invoked is the Forbearing — which is v. 33's very subject. The verse thus does what the sūrah does everywhere: Badr becomes the readable page of God's manner with those who challenge Him, and every generation's arrogant wager — "strike us down if we are wrong" — is a replay, with its own stones in store, of the day this prayer was filed in the Qur'an as its own epitaph: the only thing they got from their duʿāʾ was the duʿāʾ, repeated forever, in the book they asked to be stoned over.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:33

> **But God will not punish them while thou art among them. And God will not punish them while they seek forgiveness.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Anchors That Hold Back the Storm**

*Wa-mā kāna Allāhu li-yuʿadhdhibahum wa-anta fīhim, wa-mā kāna Allāhu muʿadhdhibahum wa-hum yastaghfirūn* — God would not punish them while you are among them; and God would not punish them while they seek forgiveness. The verse is the reply to v. 32's wager — *no, the stones will not rain; and here is why* — and it installs in the community's creed a permanent physics of mercy: the punishment of the whole is held back by two things, a *person* and a *practice*. The person is the Prophet ﷺ — his presence in the land is an anchor (*the very root* of which is "restraint," as al-Aṣmaʿī's lexicography notes), and the tradition reads ʿAlī's line into it: when he was asked about the verse at his own hour: "the Prophet ﷺ was the anchor that has been lifted; now look to the second anchor" — the istighfār, meaning that the two guarantees are sequential, not simultaneous (the report runs in the tafsīr collections of ʿAbd al-Razzāq and Ibn Abī Ḥātim). The practice is *asking forgiveness* — even from those who ask nothing else, even with the lips only — and the verse counts it: God will not destroy a people while, inside them, the words "we seek forgiveness" are still being said.

**"While You Are Among Them": the Human Asymptote of Mercy**

The first clause legislates a law the Qur'an repeats through its prophets' biographies: the destruction of a people is bound to the departure of its warners — Lot's people were destroyed *after* he and his family left the city (29:31–32, cited by the source as the verse's twin), and it is narrated of every prophet that his nation was not annihilated in his lifetime (the sūrah's own argument at 11:58's rescue of Hūd). Al-Rāzī asks the obvious question — why should one man's presence hold the punishment of a whole city? — and answers, as he does throughout, from the sūrah's own grammar: the Prophet ﷺ was the *ʿilla* of their forbearance because in his presence the *door* of return stood open; punishment descends where the call has been finally closed, and the Caller, while alive among them, was the open door. The community that later lost him learned the verse's second half the hard way: ʿĀʾisha reports the ḥadīth in Muslim of the people who saw the abomination and did not forbid it, and al-Bukhārī's parallel, from Ṣafiyya bint Shayba:

> *"God will not punish the common people because of the specially chosen, until he sees the specially chosen committing abominations openly; when they see it done openly, the common and the chosen are destroyed together."* (al-Bukhārī 3477, from Safiyya bint Shayba, from the Prophet's ﷺ wife's report)

The *aman* of the righteous presence is thus not a magic talisman; it is the *social fact* of a conscience still visible in public — and conscience can be lifted.

**"While They Seek Forgiveness": the Minimum Threshold of Salvation**

What is the second anchor, and who holds it? The source commentary preserves the three readings: (1) conditional — "while they seek, i.e. *if* they seek" — istighfār as the cure always available (Ibn Kathīr's and al-Ṭabarī's *in* sense); (2) the *believers resident in Makkah* — the small community of the weak (al-ḍuʿafāʾ of 4:75 and 4:98, ʿAyyāsh ibn Abī Rabīʿa and the like) whose presence among the idolaters *for them* held back the punishment on all of the town (al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī carry both this and the first); and (3) the idolaters' own fragmentary piety — the *talbiyah* they cried at the Kaʿba ("*labbayk, and the good is all in Your hand, and evil is not attributed to You*," as Ibn Mājah and Aḥmad transmit the Meccans' pre-Islamic formula), their *ruqya* for the slain of Badr, their tawaf of repentance-seasons: whatever *honest* residue remained in them counted, and God "would not punish" while the residue remained, because the residue was the seed of the *many* who would come after — three years of Meccan istighfār produced the whole of Quraysh's conversion, and the verse, read with history, looks almost like its planning document. The salaf's line belongs here, transmitted by al-Ḥasan and quoted across the tafsīr books: *"How many of the people of disaster are held back by the people of istighfār!"* — the intercession of the penitent is one of the sūrah's standing lessons (its own "and what is there for them that God would not punish them, *while they turn others from the Sacred Mosque*" in v. 34 is the corollary: when the prayer *and* the House are both abused, what anchor remains?).

**The Verse as the Community's Self-Examination**

Finally, the sūrah's purpose in placing this in the *victors'* ears at the site of the dead: the punishment of Badr fell the moment the second anchor broke — the day Makkah's istighfār hardened into defiance — and the lesson for the *strong* is that a community's destruction is always announced by the same inventory: is the *salih* still visible, and are the *words of return* still being said? The hadith of Thawbān in Aḥmad gives the sūrah's later shape of the law: *"God will not take the punishment from a people among whom the weak are given the wages of the strong"* — i.e., mercy travels on the *dhikr* of the *dhuafa*, and the sūrah's khums (v. 41) and its *amānāt* (v. 27) are the legislation of the very anchor: keep the weak forgiven and the prayer alive in the camp, and the *mā kāna Allāhu li-yuʿadhdhibahum* clause holds. And the verse's last mercy is its tense: *while they are seeking* — not while they are pure; the second anchor is not innocence, it is the *verb of return*, the weakest and most indefensible of all devotions, the groan of the conscience — and the entire storm of divine justice is made to stand off at its sound. It is the most consoling threat in the Qur'an: punishment never comes to a place while men are still, even falsely, saying "forgive us" — and the only way the anchor breaks is if the words themselves stop being said, which is the true meaning of a society's damnation.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:34

> **What have they to keep God from punishing them, while they turn [others] from the Sacred Mosque though they are not its protectors? Its protectors are none but the reverent, but most of them know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Removes the Excuse**

*Wa-mā lahum allā yuʿadhdhibahumu'llāhu wa-hum yaṣuddūna ʿani'l-Masjidi'l-Ḥarāmi wa-mā kānū awliyāʾahu* — what should prevent God from punishing them, while they bar the way to the Sacred Mosque, and they were never its guardians? The verse completes v. 33's logic as its mirror: there, the anchors that *held* the punishment off (the Prophet's presence, the istighfār); here, the question of what could possibly *keep it off now* — and the answer is the spectacle of the bar: Quraysh, who had just asked for stones (v. 32), now *stand between God's House and God's worshippers*. The participle *yaṣuddūna*, active and habitual, names their defining act in the sūrah's eyes: not idolatry alone — they would have been punished sooner for that — but *obstruction*: the turning of others off the path (v. 36's *yunfiḍūna amwālahum li-yaṣuddū ʿan sabīli'llāh* — spending their wealth to bar God's way — is the same verb's economics). To bar the way to the Mosque is, in the verse's theology, to interfere with the one mechanism by which a people could still be saved — the istighfār of v. 33, which the House was made to host. They were *cutting their own anchor*. The rhetorical *mā lahum* — "what is there for them?" — is thus the calmest of verdicts: the excuse is not merely absent, it is inconceivable; the protectors of the Sanctuary were its prisoners' jailers.

**"They Are Not Its Protectors": the Expropriation of the Sacred**

The key word is *awliyāʾ* — guardians, protectors, the plural of *walī* — and the verse's clause *wa-mā kānū awliyāʾahu* is one of the Qur'an's great dispossessions. The Quraysh derived their legitimacy from the House itself — the *siqāyah* and *rifāda*, the water and the provision of the pilgrims, the custody of the key (the tradition's office of *ḥijābah*, held by Shayba ibn ʿUthmān's family, which the Prophet ﷺ restored on the day of the conquest with "take it, O sons of Ṭalḥah, eternal and perpetual — touched only by the pure" per the sīrah reports); and the Qur'an says here: none of that *constitutes* guardianship — *"its guardians are none but the reverent"* — *taqwā* being the only title-deed, and the reverent, by definition, unknown and uncountable, as in v. 22's courtroom ("in the sight of God"). The principle is universal and is cited as the Qur'an's charter against religious gatekeeping: bloodline, office, tenure, endowment, and seminary-credential — all the *awliyāʾ*-claims the world trades on — are, in this verse's register, the *keys of the jailers* unless the *reverence* is theirs. The same logic ran through the House's own founding prayer (2:125–129: "make it a place of security," *balad* — the verse now inverts *amn* into *ḥarb*, security into siege) and in 3:92 — *"you will not attain righteousness until you spend of what you love"*; the guardianship of the sacred is paid for with the *loved* wealth and the *loved* ego, and the Quraysh paid only with their exclusion of others. Ibn Kathīr's note is apposite: the *Most* reverent was in the Mosque as its prisoner, barred, while the *un-reverent* sat on its pulpit of idols — this is the *ʿujb* (the "wonder" of the affair) the verse exposes: the House of God in the hands of those who have no *wilāyah* is the sign that its *wilāyah* has, in fact, been lifted.

**Two Meanings in One Verb: Barred, and Barred from**

The source commentary's note is worth keeping: *ṣadda* here carries its double Arabic pull — they turn *others* from the way, and, in the very doing, they are turned *away* themselves. The two meanings are the verse's whole moral physics: whoever stands in the road of grace finds the grace passing on detoured roads — the Quraysh barred the Muslims from the ʿUmrah of al-Ḥudaybiyah (48:25: *"they prevent you from the Sacred Mosque and the driven offering"* — and the treaty's first fruit was the ʿUmrah al-qaḍāʾ the next year, and the Mosque itself two years after, in the hands of the barred); they held the Sanctuary from the *pilgrims*, and in the same gesture lost the Sanctuary forever (the conquest's decree "no idolater after this year" — 9:28 — was the verse's completion). The individual case is not gentler: the heart that bars another's path to God's remembrance finds its own remembrance *barring*: the salaf's law — *"whoever turns a servant from the path of God, God turns him from his own path"* — is 8:34 read as psychology; and the tradition's most quoted line on the inner Kaʿba stands at the end of this verse's meaning: *"the believer is the kāʿbah of the believer — his blood, his property, his honor are sacred to him"* (the idiom of the Farewell Sermon's sacrality, cited in Ibn Mājah and Aḥmad with variations) — i.e., the "Sacred Mosque" of this verse is, in its widest sense, the *person of the worshipper*, and the guardianship the verse returns to the reverent includes, first, the reverence *of the people*.

**"But Most of Them Know Not" — the Epistemic Wound Beneath the Crime**

The closing *wa-lākin aktharahum lā yaʿlamūn* keeps the verse from being a mere war-cry: their guardianship-fraud and their bar are charged to *ignorance* — not of the facts, but of the order: that the sacred is *owned*, not *held*; that access to God's House runs through God's law and not through their veto; that the punishment they asked for (v. 32) does not obey their schedule. The phrase is the same that closed v. 22's mirror of this House's people, *lā yaʿqilūn* — and the pair frames the sanctuary's politics inside the sura's epistemology: men who "do not understand" (v. 22) will "know not" (v. 34) that their own exclusion of the reverent is the measure of their own forfeiture. And the sentence's *most* — *aktharahum* — preserves the minority who know: the hidden believers of v. 33's second anchor, Mecca's secret istighfār, whose existence the verse has just credited as the anchor, and whose eventual emergence — the town of the bar converted in a single day — was the day the Sacred Mosque's real *awliyāʾ* took its key, saying the words of its Owner's best gift to those who bar and to those who bind both: *"no reproach upon you today — go, you are free"* (12:92, in the conquest's reported form, 8:80's sūrah-kin). The verse, set here between the punishment's delay and the punishment's reality, is thus the sūrah's own explanation of Badr's verdict: the guardianship passed, in this life's sight, from the *bar* to the *reverent* — and the passing is the *furqān* (v. 41) with which the day is named forever.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:35

> **Their prayer at the House is naught but whistling and clapping. So taste the punishment for having disbelieved!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**What the Highest Room and the Lowest Noise Have in Common**

*Wa-mā kāna ṣalātuhum ʿinda'l-Bayti illā mukhāʾan wa-taṣdīyah fa-dhūqū'l-ʿadhāba bimā kuntum takfurūn* — their prayer, *at the House*, was nothing but *mukhāʾ* and *taṣdīyah*. The verse's art is the collision of two vocabularies: *ṣalāh* — the name for the soul's standing before God, the first mark of the believer in this very sūrah (v. 3) — and *mukhāʾ* (the pursed-lip whistle; the lexicographers, from Ibn ʿAbbās and Mujāhid's transmitted glosses, define it as the whistle drawn through pursed lips) and *taṣdīyah* (clapping, from *ṣadā* or the whistling of the fingers; *al-ṣafīr*). Location amplifies the contrast: *ʿinda'l-Bayt* — at the Kaʿba, the first House raised for mankind (3:96), the house of Ibrāhīm's prayer ("make this a city of peace," 2:126) — where every other nation's pilgrims at least *venerated*. Two accounts of the occasion run in parallel in the sources (al-Rāzī carries both): the practical obscenity — Quraysh's custom of circling the House naked, blowing whistles and clapping to drown the prayer of any believer who dared to pray there, so that the Prophet ﷺ's prayer beside the Kaʿba in Makkah was disrupted by the performance beside him; and the liturgical reality — *this was* their rite, the parody they kept in place of prayer. Both readings converge on the same indictment: at the House, where humanity should be most audible to heaven, they produced only *sound effects* — the whistle that means nothing and the hands that clap for nothing, the applause a theater pays a performer and the cavalcade a crowd pays itself.

**The Definition of Prayer in Reverse**

The sūrah has already told its readers what ṣalāh *is*: vv. 2–3 — hearts that quake when God is mentioned, and the establishing of prayer. V. 35 shows the same organs empty: where the *mention of God* produces in the believer a tremor, in the people of the House it produced the *mukhāʾ* — a whistle is what sound looks like when there is no reverence to fill it. And where the believer's hands are *raised* in duʿāʾ or *placed* on the chest in ṣalāh, theirs are *clapped* — the gesture of the audience, of the mocking crowd, of the child at play: hands emptied of submission become hands of performance. The tradition reads the verse as the standing proof of the rule the fuqahā later worded exactly: ritual form without inner presence (*ḥuḍūr al-qalb*) is not prayer but its effigy — the Qur'anic term for that emptying is *lahw* and *laʿib*, "diversion and play": *"and the life of this world is nothing but diversion and play"* (6:32; 29:64), and 47:14's *"so they enjoyed what they had and it was the right thing for them"* belongs to the same family of verdicts. The *mukhāʾ* is, in fact, the natural final state of every liturgy that stops meaning: the congregation that has forgotten the *tremor* ends in the *whistle*, because sound is what is left when the silence of awe is gone — and this is the verse's permanent warning to the houses of God in every law: the *House* can be right and the *prayer* in it wrong, for God accepts what the heart stood in, not what the architecture hosted.

**"So Taste" — The Third Taste in the Sūrah's Ledger**

*Fa-dhūqū* — the imperative returns for the third time in the passage (v. 14's "taste it," the Fire; v. 32's wager, "rain stones on us"; here, the punishment *for your disbelief*, the taste at Badr). The repetition is a meter of the sūrah's theodicy: every performance has a price on the Day of Invoice, and the Qur'an keeps *sampling* that Day inside this world — the defeat of the proud at Badr is the taste of what the mocked prayer of vv. 33–34 deserved. Note the verse's own logic of liability: the punishment is attached to *kufr* ("for what you used to disbelieve"), not to the mockery alone — for the mockery was kufr's *smell*, the whistling that lets you know the House has no one praying in it. And the whole sequence vv. 30–35 has now run the full arc of Quraysh's history with the Revelation in five stations: the *plot* (30), the *dismissal* (31: "fables of the old"), the *wager* (32: "rain stones"), the *bar* (34: turning others from the House), and the *counterfeit* (35: whistle and clap in the House) — and at the end of the ladder stands Badr, the day the taste arrived, and the verse's *dhūqū* is addressed, in the sūrah's double-register, to both the slain and the living: the mockers in the ground, and every later worshipper whose prayer, at its House, has started to sound like applause for itself.

**The House's Future — and Ours**

The tradition does not let the verse close without its consolation: the same House whose guardians "were not its protectors" (v. 34) was, within a lifetime, the place where the *whistle was broken*: at the conquest, the Prophet ﷺ entered, the idols fell at 17:81's verse — *"Truth has come and falsehood has vanished; indeed falsehood is ever bound to vanish"* — the *mukhāʾ* and *taṣdīyah* ended, and the Bilāl's adhān rose over the House of God as the call to *ṣalāh*. That day is the sūrah's proof that vv. 34–35 are a sentence with an appeal, not a final verdict on a people: Quraysh who had whistled at the prayer were, within years, the people who stood in the rows, and the sūrah (in the reports of Ibn ʿAbbās preserved by al-Ṭabarī) says so in the words of v. 38: "if they desist, what is past is forgiven." For every House of God since, the verse is the standing alarm: the *location* guarantees nothing; the *mukhāʾ* is always one generation's familiarity away; and the *dhawq* — the taste — is the one syllable that the House's stones can never absorb.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:36

> **Truly those who disbelieve spend their wealth to turn [others] from the way of God. They will spend it; then it will be a source of regret for them, and then they will be overcome. And the disbelievers will be gathered unto Hell,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The War Chest and the Four-Act Sentence**

*Inna'lladhīna kafarū yunfiḍūna amwālahum li-yaṣuddū ʿan sabīli'llāh, fa-sayunfiḍūna thumma tuḥṣar ʿalayhim thumma yughlabūn* — they spend, *and they will spend*, then it will be confined upon them (turned to regret), then they will be overcome. The sūrah lifts its camera from the field of Badr to the *bank account*: the battle was financed — the provision and arms of the thousand were carried out of Makkah on the fortunes of its nobles, and the source commentary cites the reports that the verse names, beyond its general sense, the Quraysh's funding of the campaign and the personal stake of men like Abū Sufyān in the caravan's defense. The grammar is the sermon: the imperfect *yunfiḍūna* (they are spending) rolls into the emphatic future *fa-sa-yunfiḍūna* (they *will* spend it all) — and the doubling tells that the fire will keep eating the wood: no one's treasury is emptied halfway through a war against heaven; it is emptied *completely*, and that is why the sentence's third stage, *thumma tuḥṣar ʿalayhim* — "then it will be *closed in* upon them" (*ḥuṣr*, the same root as regret, *ḥasra*, the breathlessness of the man whose chest is shut around what he can no longer recover) — follows the spending *necessarily*: the money is spent; the cause loses; the estate and the loss sit together in the chest, forever, at the moment of accounting. And the fourth stage — *thumma yughlabūn* — then *they* will be the ones overcome, the passive verb (they are *made* defeated) closing the arc that began in their active spending: they began as agents of their capital, they end as its patients.

**Two Spends, One Verb: The Sūrah's Economics of Direction**

The verse is deliberately placed where the sūrah's *other* spendings can be seen from it. *Infaq* in the way of God was the second mark of the believer (v. 3, "they spend from what We provided them"), the object of the angelic duʿāʾ ("O God, give compensation to the spender," the ḥadīth's morning pair), and the law of v. 60 to come ("whatever you spend in God's way is repaid in full and you will not be wronged"). V. 36 completes the ledger with the *counter-spend*: the same verb, the same money, the opposite pole — spending *against* the way instead of *on* it, and the sūrah's four-stage sentence is the answer to the four-stage promise: what is spent on God's way is repaid here and there; what is spent against it is *lost* here (the estate), regretted *there* (the chest), and collected in the Fire (the gathering of v. 37). The tradition's parables stand at both columns and the Qur'an itself supplies the one for v. 36: *"The parable of what they spend in this worldly life is like the parable of a wind containing frost which strikes the harvest of a people who have wronged themselves and destroys it"* (3:117) — cold wind, harvest, *their own* hands: the spend is *real labor* and the ruin is *real*, and nothing is stored. And the ḥadīth of the three estates in Muslim says the balance sheet of every spender plainly:

> *"A man says: 'my wealth, my wealth!' — and your wealth is only what you ate and used up, what you wore and wore out, what you gave and so stored up; the rest goes to others while you leave it."* (Muslim 2957, from Ibn al-Ḥaḍramī)

The men of v. 36 had, by this reckoning, stored nothing and spent everything on the *frost* — which is what "regret" means in the verse's idiom.

**"Then They Will Be Overcome": The History the Verse Foretold**

The third stage was, to the hearers of the sūrah, a fact being verified in the same decade: the Quraysh's war chests fed Badr's defeat, Uḥud's *draw* (whose cost — the Hamza's death, the seventy, the wounds — was counted by their financiers as "the day for the day"), the Trench's bankruptcy (the source notes: the ten-thousand confederacy spent its alliance at Madinah's ditch and came away unpaid; Abū Sufyān's later line is preserved in the sīrah that the campaign that never was had cost them more than the battle that was), and finally the field of the conquest, when the same men who had financed the extermination rode out to buy terms — and the Prophet ﷺ's *you are all free* spent their fear instead of their gold. *Yughlabūn* is, then, an eschatological verb with a historical down-payment — as the sūrah's own refrain insists ("and God is not unaware of what you do," v. 47's *muḥīṭ*; "God's plot is the best of plots," v. 30). Ibn Kathīr's gloss records the consensus that the *ʿibrah* of the clause is for all who *fund opposition*: the enmity of the tongue (v. 31) and the mockery of the liturgy (v. 35) must, if they are to strike at the community, become *capital* — and the moment a cause must buy its swords it has already lost the account, because what God is being fought with is *created*, and the created cannot outlast its Owner.

**"And the Disbelievers Will Gather to the Fire" — the Spending That Ends in a Gathering**

The verse's terminal clause turns the ledger's page to the Barzakh, and the image is chosen to finish the lesson on *money as organization*: these men spent their wealth to *scatter* believers from the way (v. 35's *yaṣuddūn* — the bar at the House); their end is the *gathering* they will not escape — *yujmaʿūna ilā jahannam* — the passive verb of the Owner of the summons who gathers what He made, the same idiom as v. 24's *tuḥsharūn*. They financed a *company* (v. 19's *fiʾah*, the army with its own roll); they will be gathered into a *mass* (v. 37's heaped pile) with no roll but the Book, and their capital's last deed is to be *counted against them*. That is why the clause's *thumma* — "then" — is not sequence but *finality*: the regret (3rd stage) is the taste (v. 14, v. 35, and soon v. 50's "taste" again) and the gathering is the fire's own account; the spender who spent against God will find, at the end of the ledger, that his money bought *two* losses — the world's, and the loss of the world's chance — and v. 37's verb for the losers' verdict (*ulāʾika humu'l-khāsirūn*, the same word as 2:16's "they bought error at the price of guidance") will name exactly the trade he thought was strategy: *sharā bi'th-thamani al-ḍalālah* — in the Qur'an's economics, every spending against the way is a *purchase*, and the price, this verse says, is regret — *then* defeat — *then* the fire: three stages, no stage of recovery; and the sūrah's next breath (v. 38) offers what the spenders' own money could not — the forgiveness of the whole, past, account — if only they would stop spending against the One who can cancel it.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:37

> **that God may separate the bad from the good, and place the bad one upon the other and heap them all together, and place them in Hell. It is they who are the losers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Purpose Clause: Why History Is a Sieve**

*Li-yamīza Allāhu'l-khabītha mina'l-ṭayyibi wa-yajʿala ba'ḍa'l-khabīthi ba'ḍan fawqa ba'ḍin fa-yakbithahū jamaʿan thumma yajʿalahū fī jahannam, ulāʾika humu'l-khāsirūn* — the verse is built entirely of one verb-family's work: *mamāz* — separating the *khabīth* (the foul, the dross, the impure) from the *ṭayyib* (the pure, the sweet, the good). Grammatically a purpose-clause attached to v. 36's spending and defeat, it reframes the whole event: the fighting and the gathering to the Fire are, *from God's side*, an act of *sorting*. The Qur'an's theodicy repeats this everywhere — the Day is named *yawm al-faṣl*, the Day of Separation (37:21; 77:14, 38, 46); the Qur'an's own name includes it (*furqān*, "the Day the two hosts met," v. 41; v. 29's inner criterion); and every trial in the sūrah's world has been a sieve: Badr's ranks sorted the averse from the resolute, the ransom-debate (vv. 67–69) sorted appetite from obedience, Uḥud, in the source's note, was the day "the sincere followers of the Prophet were separated from the hypocrites" (cf. 3:179, cited there — the verse the commentators pair with this one as its twin). The universe, in this reading, is not a court of raw force but a refinery, and *khabīth* and *ṭayyib* are not social classes but states of the soul that only fire can distinguish.

**Place the Foul upon the Foul: the Physics of Accumulation**

The verse's central image is physical: God *sets the foul part on the foul part* and *khabithahū* — heaps them — as a date-harvest is piled (the root *k-b-th*, in the Arab's idiom, is the piling of dates on the mat or the heaping of wood for the fire — the verb recurs at 20:97 for the calf's filings "piled on the water" and at 27:17's "piled up with their [heaps of] dates"). The logic is a whole lesson in divine patience: evil is *self-collecting*. No separate warrant is needed for each sinner's portion; the foul adheres to the foul by nature — like attracts, and the heap makes itself — which is why the Qur'an can speak of the Fire's fuel as *"men and stones"* (2:24): the khabā'ith of two worlds, gathered by their own gravity, and God only *places* where their inclination has already heaped. Al-Rāzī sees in the four verbs' crescendo (separate → set upon → heap → place in the Fire) the exact anatomy of *istidraj*, the "drawing on" by which a sin is left to find its partners, its bulk, and its place: the punishment is not an ambush, it is a *funnel*. And the tradition's ḥadīth of the respite reads under this verse like its commentary:

> *"God grants the wrongdoer respite — until, when He seizes him, He does not let him escape."* Then the Prophet ﷺ recited: *"And thus is the seizing of your Lord when He seizes the towns while they are wronging — indeed His seizing is painful, severe."* (al-Bukhārī 4686; Muslim 2583, from Abū Mūsā)

**Why the Sorting Requires Spending, Sword, and Time — the Verse against Optimism and Fatalism**

The verse's placement answers the believer's oldest complaint: if God is sorting souls, why does the sorting take *history* — why not a verdict at the first lie? Because the *khabīth* and *ṭayyib* are, in this world, not yet themselves: they are *becoming*, through choices, and the sieve must wait for the material to finish hardening. The sūrah has demonstrated it on the living case: the same army contained the willing (v. 5's "averse group" who became Badr's heroes) and the wavering; the same Mecca contained Abū Jahl who asked for stones and the hidden believers of v. 33 who would yet hold its anchor. The *fighting* of vv. 39–40, the *waiting* of vv. 33, the *spending* of v. 36 — these are the *means by which the sorting is performed*: history is the assay, and its weight is that every participant is *assayed* as he participates. Ibn Kathīr records the double reference of the verse's plain sense — "the separation may occur *in this world* (the sieve of battle, as at Uḥud) as well as *the next* (the Fire)" — which keeps the verse free of both errors: not the triumphalism that says "the victory proves our purity" (the sorting continues *through* victory into the spoils, as v. 1 proved), and not the fatalism that says "nothing decides anything until the Last Day" (Badr decided, and the verse's verbs are *past-shaped in purpose*: God *did* sort that day).

**"It is They Who Are the Losers" — the Final Ledger**

The verdict-word closes the passage: *khāsirūn* — loss, *khusr*, the merchant's term for a bargain where the price exceeded the goods, used by the Qur'an from its very first human story: *"they bought error at the price of guidance"* (2:16). The heap of v. 37 is therefore also a *stock*: the khabā'ith heaped are the inventory they purchased with their *ṭayyib* — every good thing a soul had (its wealth spent in v. 36, its hearing offered in v. 23, its fear that could have been sleep and steadiness like v. 11) — sold to buy the pile. The verse, thus read, is the sūrah's own accounting of Badr's losers in the idiom of the bazaar its leaders ran: they came to the valley with capital, they spent it buying the heap, and the Day they discovered the price-list: *ulāʾika humu'l-khāsirūn* — those *are* the loss-takers, the definitive *hum* placing them, not their deeds, in the class. And the clause's mercy, which the next verse will open in full, is that the *mīzān* can still be re-bought — "if they desist, what is past is forgiven them" (v. 38): the sorting is final only at the *yahna*, not before; the heap that *can* be thrown into the Fire is, while the breath lasts, still a pile that its maker can set down.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:38

> **Say to the disbelievers that, if they desist, what is past will be forgiven them, but if they relapse, then the wont of those of old has already passed.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Amnesty Clause at the End of the Sword-Verse**

*Qul li'lladhīna kafarū in yantahū yughfar lahum mā qad salaf, wa-in yaʿūdū fa-qad mathala sunnatu'l-awwalīn* — say to the disbelievers: if they stop, what has gone before is forgiven them. The command is addressed, in the sūrah's structure, to the Prophet ﷺ at the very point of his community's maximum advantage — the day after the heap of v. 37 was sorted, the chiefs killed, the treasury spent to nothing — and its content is the strangest of ultimatums: *the door is open, and the price of entering it is simply to stop*. No indemnity, no humiliation-tax, no demand for the heads of the surviving plotters; the past crimes — the boycott, the torture, the expulsion, the murder-attempt of v. 30, the war itself — are placed in one phrase, *mā qad salafa*, and swept by a *yughfar*. The exegetes weigh the scope: Ibn Kathīr cites the reading "they should cease hostility *and embrace Islam*" (and the verse of 2:192–193, cited in the sources, is its twin: *"if they desist — then God is Forgiving, Merciful"*); the Study-Qur'an note preserves the other reading — that the verse "says nothing explicitly about becoming Muslim" and that *forgiveness* here names the *cessation of grievances*: the state pardons what the state could prosecute. Both readings agree on what matters: the sūrah teaches that the war's *telos* is not punishment but *desistance* — and that the one who desists, by heaven's own economics, is forgiven what he "desisted from."

**"The Wont of the Ancients Has Already Passed" — the Sunnah That Is a Precedent**

*Wa-in yaʿūdū fa-qad mathala sunnatu'l-awwalīn* — if you return, the precedent of the ancients has already gone by. *Sunnah* in the Qur'an's idiom is the *course* God runs with a people when a condition is met — a law, not a caprice: *"you will find no change in God's way (sunnah), and you will find no shifting in God's way"* (35:43; and 48:23); and its dark column is what befell Noah's, Hūd's, Ṣāliḥ's, Lot's, Pharaoh's peoples in sequence (the sūrah cites the very pair in v. 52–54). The verse's warning is therefore *legal* in tone, not rhetorical: if the hostility returns, no new argument, no new miracle, no new grace-period — the precedent applies, as it applied to the nations, and this is why the sūrah will not let the community "return" to fighting for anything less than the *fitnah*'s end (v. 39). The phrase also, in the salaf's reading, consoles: the *sunnat Allāh* cuts both ways — as it destroyed the returning oppressor, it *already* destroyed the *oppression's claim*; the defeat of the ancients who warred against heaven is *mathalat* — gone by, done, closed — and Badr's men are being told: you have become, for the next transgressor, what Pharaoh is for the next tyrant. History's files are now *precedent*, and the sūrah is its law-report.

**Islam Cuts Off What Came Before — the Verse as Charter of the Convert**

The fiqh of this clause became enormous, for the jurists read *mā qad salaf* as the charter of the *muhtadi*: what the sword of v. 39 cannot do — punish a converted people — forgiveness does instantly. The tradition's evidence is the ḥadīth of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, when he came to pledge:

> *"Do you not know that Islam demolishes what was before it, that migration demolishes what was before it, and that ḥajj demolishes what was before it?"* (Muslim 121)

which the commentators (al-Ṭabarī in his tafsīr, Ibn Kathīr here) align with this verse as its prophetic *sharḥ*: even crimes against the community itself are *mahṭūṭ* — wiped — at the threshold; and the sūrah's later generalization is 5:28's law of Cain's lesson ("if you stretch your hand to kill me, I will not stretch mine — I fear God, Lord of the worlds" ending in the verse that "whoever kills a soul... it is as if he killed all mankind, and whoever gives life to it, it is as if he gave life to all") read beside the *yughfar* of v. 38 forms the whole penal theology of the frontier. The lesson of the verse's order should be marked with care: the *promise of forgiveness* is placed *before* the threat — the sūrah's diplomacy is that the enemy's way out is easier than his way in, and the "wont of the ancients" clause stands only as the *conditional* shadow of the open door. The conquest of Makkah itself would later act the verse line-for-line: the men of v. 30's council and v. 32's wager, standing with necks bared, heard *mā qad salaf* made policy — *"Go, you are all free"* (Ibn Isḥāq's wording in the sīrah's reports) — which the fuqahā cite as the verse's own practical *taʾwīl*: the *yughfar* not of hearts alone, but of *cases*.

**The Still-Open Test: Why the Verse Is Not a Truce with Truth**

One danger completes the commentary. The promise of amnesty attaches to *nistihālah* — desisting from the attack — and the sūrah has placed it between verses about *the spending* (36) and *the fighting-until-fitnah-ends* (39), so that its sense is fenced against the lazy reading: it is not that truth and falsehood are equally valid once the fighting stops (the heap of v. 37 was *sorted* — the khabīth is named khabīth forever); it is that the *man* who desists is forgiven the *crime*, and the community that offers him this is obeying the same Book that condemned his cause.
