The *muṣālaḥah* of v. 38 and the *furqān* of v. 41 are not contradictions in the sūrah; they are the two hands of the one criterion: *distinguish the claim, release the man; end the aggression, not the invitation.* Which is also why the clause that will end the passage (v. 39's *"if they desist — then God sees what they do"*) makes forgiveness a live relation: the door stays open as long as the *yanzur* (He sees) keeps watching new deeds — and the "wont of the ancients" stands behind it like the sea behind the shore: not God's wish, but the shore's own choice, measured.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:39

> **And fight them until there is no strife, and religion is wholly for God. But if they desist, then truly God sees whatsoever they do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse of the Limit, Not of the Unlimited**

*Wa-qātilūhum ḥattā lā takūna fitnatun wa-yakūna'd-dīnu kulluhu li'llāh, fa-in intahaw fa-inna Allāha bimā yaʿmalūna baṣīr* — the verse is one of the Qur'an's three *qitāl*-clauses built on *ḥattā* ("until," 2:191, 2:193, and here) — and the *ḥattā* is the whole point: this is a command with a terminus, issued against an *event* (*fitnah*), not a war against a *category*. The sūrah's own context, which the source commentary states at length, fixes the terms: the fitnah the verse means was the persecution Quraysh maintained against the Muslims *before and after* the Hijrah — the torture in Makkah, the expulsion, the funding of extermination (v. 36), the barring of the House (v. 34) — and al-Rāzī's reading, which the sources note twice, understands "that religion be *wholly* for God" as the condition that God's worship be *unhindered* in the sanctuary's region: "the fighting is for the cessation of *fitnah*, and that is only possible when the forces of persecution disappear." The verse therefore commands the *end of a system*, not the beginning of a census of converts: when the note adds that some gloss fitnah as *shirk* itself, the gloss still does not license belief-raid — because the verse's own *fa-in intahaw* treats even the desistance of the *fighting* (not the "conversion") as the condition of peace, and because the Book's law of belief on this very subject is fixed elsewhere in its plain voice: *"There is no compulsion in religion"* (2:256), and *"would you make men believe till they were believers?"* (10:99), and *"to you your religion, to me mine"* (109:6). The *ḥujjah* for why *jihād* is here permitted is the *furqān*'s own answer: where religion's protection is a *necessity of human life* (the source commentary's note on the classical *ḍarūriyyāt*, citing 22:40's monasteries, churches, synagogues and mosques), un-fought persecution becomes the *de facto* religion of the strong.

**"That Religion Is Wholly for God" — the Positive End**

The *wa-yakūna'd-dīnu kulluhu li'llāh* names not a theocracy of the sword but the state the whole sūrah has been assembling: a world in which the allegiance that matters is *to God*, and the only *authority* over the human conscience is the One who does not need it. "Religion is for God" is the end of every man's *claim* to be the gate of grace — which is precisely what Quraysh had practiced at the House (v. 34: "they are not its guardians; its guardians are none but the reverent"). The word *kulluhu* (wholly) does the radical work: *religion, all of it* — not a public sector of piety and a private sector of power, but the whole of human allegiance under God alone: in the market (vv. 1, 27, 41), in war (vv. 15–16), in the home (v. 28), in the treaty (vv. 58, 61). The verse's ideal is thus not the forced *entry* of men into faith but the *removal* of every barrier — including the believers' own appetite for dominion, which the sūrah has policed from its first verse — between man and the *option* of God; and history's reading agrees: the *fitnah* did not cease by the sword alone, it ceased when the sanctuary was re-opened at Ḥudaybiyah (48:25's *fa-ʿakharahum*) and its gate's guardianship passed, by the verse's own title-deed (v. 34), to the reverent.

**"If They Desist — God Sees What They Do": the Ending and the Witness**

The verse's last clause is its most quoted and least weighed. *Fa-in intahaw fa-inna Allāha bimā yaʿmalūna baṣīr* — the sentence does *not* complete "then fight them no more" in the expected grammar (2:192 supplies that plainly: *"fa-in intahaw fa-inna'llāha ghafūrur raḥīm"*); it ends in *observation*. The exegetes note the force: the one who desists is handed, at once, to God's eye, not to the Muslims' ledger — the *baṣīr* clause is an amnesty in the sūrah's own idiom, and its logic is that which the Battle of Badr's Leader had taught at his most triumphant: the man who stops is beyond your jurisdiction in the one thing that matters (his heart's end), and under God's in everything else: *"to God belongs the affair, past and future"* (30:4). Ibn Kathīr adduces the *sīrah's* law here: the Prophet's line "I have not been ordered to search men's hearts or to cut open their bellies" (the wording runs through Bukhāriyyayn in the Usāma affair — where the man who said "lā ilāha illa'llāh" was killed by a companion's haste, and the Prophet repeated "la ilāha illa'llāh — how will you meet 'lā ilāha illa'llāh' on the Day of Judgment?" until the man wished he had believed then, al-Bukhārī 4300? Muslim 96). And the sūrah's other limit-verse — *"and if they incline to peace, incline to it"* (v. 61) — is this verse's twin in foreign policy: *desistance* and *inclination* are treated as the same thing: the end of fighting is not the beginning of punishment; it is the *transfer of the case* to the *Baṣīr*.

**How the Verse Has Been Read, and How It Reads the Reader**

No commentary should pass this text while hiding that its *ḥattā* became the battlefield of the abrogation debates and of later politics: the jurists of the classical *siyar* read v. 39 with the "sword-verse" (9:5) as a sequence of tightening commands; the objectors — from Abū Muslim al-Iṣfahānī (cited by al-Rāzī) to the modern reformers — show that *ḥattā* and *in* make the limit, not the abrogation, the point, and that 9:5 itself contains its own *fa-in tābu wa-aqāmū's-ṣalāh* (9:11's re-twin). What the *sūrah itself* does with v. 39 is the instruction the careful reader of v. 1–38 already has: it *never* lets the community finish the job with an accounting of spoils — the fight ends, the *seeing* begins, and the men of vv. 36–37 are left with the open door of v. 38 and the closed file of v. 14. To fight "until there is no fitnah" is, in the sūrah's mirror, first a command *against the self*: a community that has become the *persecutor* has, by this verse, ended its own title to the fight, and its *fitna*-ending war becomes the *fitna* of v. 25 for the ship below decks. The verse is read in every generation by the men it convicts, and that, the sūrah's structure suggests, is precisely why the *baṣīr* clause exists: so that no generation of the sword ever mistakes its campaign for the end of history.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:40

> **And if they turn away, know that God is your Master—an excellent Master, an excellent Helper!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Last Clause of the War-Passage: a Single Sentence of Custody**

*Wa-in tawallaw fa'-ʿlamū anna Allāha mawlākum, niʿma'l-mawlā wa-niʿma'n-naṣīr* — and if they turn away, know that God is your *mawlā*: an excellent master, an excellent helper. The sūrah's entire war-and-peace sequence (36–40) ends not with the enemy's fate but with the believers' *custody*, and the choice is the whole of the Book's psychology of conflict: when the fighting stops *or* the enemy persists, the community's knowledge-position is one and the same — *mawlā* first, then *naṣīr*. *Wilāyah* is the densest relational word in Arabic — it carries, in its root (w-l-y, proximity and swing of authority), ownership and guardianship, friendship and protection, alliance and clientage, succession and care: the Qur'an uses it of God as *the* name of the believer's entire security-estate ("God is the *walī* of those who believe," 3:68; "your *walī* is God and His Messenger," 5:55; "God is my *mawlā*, and He is the guardian of every believer," 42:31, and the whole alliance-law of the sūrah's end, vv. 72–75, turns on *awliyāʾ* in its precise, contractual sense of protecting kin). The verse thus answers the unasked anxiety of v. 39's *fa-in intahaw*: if they do *not* desist — if the war re-forms (and Uḥud and the Trench re-formed it within the Prophet's own lifetime) — the community's case is not "abandoned to the enemy's choice"; it is *transferred to a Master*.

**"Know That..." — The Epistemic Command**

The imperative is *fa'-ʿlamū* — *know* — not *feel*, not *hope*: the verse legislates a certainty, and the reason it can is that it states the certainty as a fact already in force: *anna Allāha mawlākum* — He *is* your Master — not that He *will be*, if the fighting goes well. The sūrah's whole Badr-narrative is the evidence for the tense: He was their *mawlā* at v. 9 (the succor answered), at v. 11 (the sleep, the water), at v. 12 (the firmness sent), at v. 17 (the killing that was not their killing); the verse simply extends the known fact past the end of the known battle. Al-Rāzī's observation is apt in this sūrah's courtroom: the *maʿrifah*-command aims at the heart's *anchoring*, because the one thing that breaks a community after war is not the enemy's return but the *forgetting of the wilāyah* — the moment men conclude the previous victory was their own (which is v. 1's loot, v. 65's numbers, v. 67's ransoms, all being, in this sūrah, cases of the same amnesia). To *know* the *mawlā* is the institutional memory this sūrah is being, verse by verse, to the ummah.

**"An Excellent Master, an Excellent Helper" — the Praise That Calibrates Expectation**

The superlative formula *niʿma... wa-biʾsa...* ("excellent is He / wretched is the...") is the Qur'an's idiom for closing a verdict — and here it does a subtle piece of customer-care: it praises *the relation itself*, so that the believer knows the *terms* of his contract with the Master. A *mawlā* of the Arabs' world was expected to defend his clients and absorb their losses; a *naṣīr* was expected to come at the cry (v. 9's *istighāthah* answered, v. 7's "one of the two companies is yours"). The two titles, doubled with the praise, say: the contract is *excellent* — and the sūrah's history has just shown *why* — with a Master whose *wilāyah* includes the sleep that no man can command, the rain that no general can schedule, and the terror that no army can manufacture (v. 12's rubʿ), and with a *Helper* whose aid arrived as *ranks* behind a bare-handed 314 (v. 9). The verse's other twin is 3:173 — the ḥudaybiyyah-decades' hardest day, when, the sīrah reports, the Quraysh cavalry came after the wounded at Ḥamrāʾ al-Asad and the verse ran down the camp: *"God is your Master, so trust in Him — the very ones to whom the people said: 'an army has gathered against you, so fear them' — and it increased them in faith, and they said: 'ḥasbuna'llāhu wa-niʿma'l-wakīl'"* (3:171–173, as the tafsīr books record of Badr's morrow: the pursuit never came, for the *mawlā* was enough — which is 8:40's own sentence, proven in the field where v. 39's "desist or turn" had just been answered: the army that turned *away* (v. 40) met, not a pursued enemy, but the *niʿma'l-mawlā* of the believers).

**The Verse as the End of Fighting-Theology**

To close the war-passage with *wilāyah* is to teach the community what its security is *made of*, and the sūrah spells the lesson across the verses that follow (41–46) in legal details: the khums (41), the ground and the timing (42–44), firmness and dhikr and obedience and no-quarrel (45–46) — the entire architecture of *being* a people whose case is held by another. And its mercy is at once its discipline: the *mawlā* who is "excellent" is the Master whose distribution of the loot (v. 1) and whose timing of victory (v. 10) are trusted precisely because He is *niʿma'l-mawlā* — a master who does not sell his clients, whose shares are pure *ṭayyib* (v. 3's rizq, v. 69's *lawāhu ṭayyib*!), and whose "help" was, at Badr, *given before the cry*. When the *mawlā*-promise is kept by the men of the camp (as in the khums' honest division and the treaty's honest return, v. 58), the verse's peace clause (v. 38) always remains; when it is broken, the *niʿma* stands in the place of the lost protection — because, in the sūrah's closing eschatology, the true opposition is not between two armies but between two *wilāyāts*: "the *awliyāʾ* of those who disbelieve is Ṭāghūt" (2:257), and *they* are the ones to whom v. 59 will say: their "outstripping" (v. 59's *sābbū*) avails nothing — for the *mawlā* of the believers cannot be outrun by a clientage that ends in the heap of v. 37. The verse's "know" is therefore the whole sūrah in a single possession: whoever *knows* God is his *mawlā* can fight with limits, win without spoiling, lose without despairing, and make peace without shame — and that, the Battle of Badr was, above everything else, the school of.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:41

> **And know that whatsoever you take as spoils, a fifth is for God and the Messenger, and for kinsfolk, orphans, the indigent, and the traveler, if you believe in God and what We sent down upon Our servant on the Day of Discrimination, the day the two hosts met—and God is Powerful over all things—**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Unresolved Pile to the Stated Fraction**

*Wa'-ʿlamū annamā ghanimtum min shayʾ fa-anna lillāhi khumsahu wa-li'r-Rasūli wa-li'dhī'l-qurbā wa'l-yatāmā wa'l-masākīni wa'bni's-sabīl* — the verse resolves, by arithmetic, what v. 1 had resolved by jurisdiction. There, the *anfāl* were declared God's and the Messenger's; here the community is told the standing rule for *ghanāʾim* — every spoils-take, whatever its size, is one-fifth Heaven's and four-fifths the army's. The word itself teaches: *ghanīmah* is from *gh-n-m*, to gain — in Arabic idiom, profit got *through* trouble (the jurists' definition: "what is acquired through exertion," as the source states), which is why the same root names the small mercies of trade and the great spoil of war alike. *Khumus* is a fifth — the only share of property that revelation *quantifies* at the moment of acquisition, and the quantification is the doctrine: before the army counts anything, one-fifth is already *gone* from them, so that even the four parts they receive are received with a receipt showing who cleared the ground first. The classical debate over whether v. 1 or v. 41 abrogated the other dissolves, as al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī note, into a specification: v. 1 gave the *title* (distribution belongs to God and His Messenger); v. 41 gives the *rule* by which that title is now to be exercised — and, some add, the fifth itself was the Prophet's *anfāl* in its first hour, which the men of Badr discovered when the Messenger distributed the whole four-fifths, "equally between the townspeople and the bedouin, the free and the slave" (the report runs through al-Ṭabarī), leaving the quarrel of v. 1 not arbitrated but *invalidated* by fairness they had not claimed.

**The Five Shares, and the Debates They Carry**

The fifth is assigned to five names — God, the Messenger, the *dhū'l-qurbā*, the orphans, the indigent, the traveler — and the distribution of the list's *head* over the list's *body* is the sūrah's quiet theology: God's name stands first over a share He does not "use," because the whole fifth is *His* and the other names are the *destinations He chooses* — hence the schools' readings. The majority (Shāfiʿīs and most) divide the khums into five: the "God-and-Messenger" share passing, after the Prophet ﷺ, to the imām for the community's general good; the kinsfolk — Banū Hāshim (and, on the Shāfiʿī and Ḥanafī readings, Banū al-Muṭṭalib as well, the kin who never split with Hāshim in the boycott of the Ṭālibiyyūn); the orphans, the indigent, the wayfarer. The Mālikī school (per al-Qurṭubī) folds the first two shares into the poor of the Muslims — the "designation" for God and His Messenger being, on this reading, *the imām's discretion*, since God needs no portion; the Ḥanafīs let God's share run to the mosques and public works. The Shīʿī jurisprudence, the source notes, reads the *khums* far wider — a fifth of *surplus income*, mines, sea-haul, and treasure — with the Imam's share, during the occultation, devolving to the *mujāhidūn* of knowledge who act as the Hidden Imam's agents — the same verse thus carrying, across the ummah's schools, the entire law of "who holds the public purse, and for whom" — and the disagreement itself, rooted in this verse's list, is a proof of the Qur'an's design: the wealth of conquest was *never* to become a tribe's spoil; it was to become an argument that keeps the poor's name in the deed forever.

**"The Day of the Furqān": the Date That Seals the Law**

*— idh āmantum bi'llāhi wa-mā anzalnā ʿalā ʿabdinā yawma'l-furqāni yawma laqi'a'l-jamʿān —* the clause is the verse's keystone: belief in "what We sent down upon *Our servant*" — the Prophet ﷺ, the sūrah calling him here by the rank that fits the distribution: the servant *receives* (of God's share) and *gives* (of his own hand), and the khums' Messenger-share is his *because* he is ʿabd, the trustee, not the owner (the Prophet's own law of the office would later be his line at the Ḥunayn distribution — the ʿUyayna-and-Aqrāʿ scene of al-Bukhārī — when a companion said "this division was not meant for God's face," and the Prophet ﷺ said, in the ḥadīth of the two Ṣaḥīḥs: *"May God have mercy on Mūsā — he was accused of more than that, and he was patient"* (al-Bukhārī 3405; Muslim 1062), and his warning of the day when the honest hand will "carry its own strap and cord" against the khāʾin, per the ḥadīth of Abū Humayd: *"Return the thread and the needle — do not hide what is less than that"* (Muslim 1833, with al-Bukhārī's parallel of the camel-strap)). And the day that *grounds* the belief-clause is named twice, in the sūrah's two great terms: *yawma'l-furqān* — the Day of Differentiation (v. 29's criterion, now the day's own title) — *yawma laqi'a'l-jamʿān* — the day the two companies met: the two names say that Badr is the reason the law can be *trusted*: the One who divided the truth from the false on that field with three hundred and fourteen is the One who now divides the fifth, and the community obeys the fraction because it has seen the Fractioner's hand at the wells. God's closing seal completes the reason: *wa-Allāhu ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr* — over all things powerful: the fifth of a pile of ʿArīsh-camels is no arithmetic to a God whose *qadīr* could have taken the whole field without the fifth — the fraction is thus education, and the khums' public purpose is written on the list of its recipients: the fifth runs, in four of its five destinations, to the *weak* (kin without patrons, orphans, the indigent, the wayfarer — the whole "below-deck" of v. 25's ship), so that the first law of the *ghazw*-economy is that war's profit must first touch the people who did not fight in it — and never, under the Book's own counter-principle, circulate "between the rich alone" (59:7, the Fayʾ-verse's famous *kay lā yakūna dūlatan bayna'l-aghniyāʾi minkum*, whose title for "circulation-wealth" the sūrah's whole spoils-law was written to prevent.

**The "Clear Proof" of the Distribution**

One last feature of the verse's placement: its list mirrors, in miniature, the sūrah's own program from v. 1 forward. The orphans and the traveler — the two weakest claims at Badr's camp, the men who had *no hands in the pile* — sit beside the Messenger as shareholders: which is the *fiqh* of v. 1 (the spoils are God's to place, not the victor's to keep) turned from a verdict into a constitution; and the kinsfolk-share is the sūrah's *ṣilat al-arḥām* (the kin's claim, vv. 75's final "the womb has the greater right in God's Book") funded at the first hour of the community's wealth. The verse therefore ends, as the sūrah will, on *ʿilm* and *qudra*: God knows the quarrel's roots and has power over every hand — and men "believe in what was sent down upon His servant" by the same act with which they hand over the fifth, which, in the sūrah's psychology (vv. 2–4), was always the real test of the same īmān. The khums is, in the end, the *anfāl* verse with its arithmetic done: the pile that nearly tore the ummah apart at its first victory becomes, by the fifth, the ummah's permanent proof that its wealth is *criterion* (furqān), not fortune — divided where the Day of Differentiation divided it, at the wells of Badr, where God's servant threw a handful of dust and God made the throwing.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:42

> **when you were on the near slope and they were on the far slope, and the caravan was below you. And had you made a tryst with each other, you would have failed the tryst. But [it came to pass] so that God may conclude a matter that was to be done, so that whosoever should perish, perishes according to a clear proof, and whosoever should live, lives according to a clear proof. And truly God is Hearing, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Map That Proves the Hand**

*Wa-antum bi'l-ʿudwati'ldunyā wa-hum bi'l-ʿudwati'l-quṣwā wa'r-rakbu asfala minkum* — you at the near bank, they at the far, the caravan *below you* toward the sea. The sūrah stops the narrative to draw the valley: the Muslims on the near slope of Badr's wādī (the *ʿudwah*, the sandy river-bank, soft ground the rain of v. 11 had packed for them), Quraysh on the far, and — the detail that re-writes the whole battle in one stroke — the caravan they had marched to fight *for*, already past them, downhill toward the coast, saved, out of reach: the war's *cause* was not in the valley, and no one present could have arranged it so. The map's theology is exact: if the battle had been a raid's accident, the caravan would have been *in* it; instead the three bodies sat in a triangle that no commander of either side chose — the near edge, the far edge, the sea-edge below — and the verse's purpose-clause names what filled the triangle: *li-qaḍiya'llāhu amran kāna mafʿūlā* — that a matter which *had to be done* might be done. The passive *mafʿūl* (a thing *made*, not a thing *happening*) is the Qur'an's technical hint: Badr was *fabricated*, and its fabric was not in any hand on the slopes.

**"You Would Have Missed the Appointment": the Failure of Human Scheduling**

*Wa-law tāʿad-tum la-ikh-talaftumu'l-mīʿād* — had you arranged to meet, you would have missed the appointment. The *mīʿād* (the appointed tryst — the word of duels, markets, and oaths, the idiom of Arab honor-dueling: the two sides were even inviting each other to name the day, and Abū Jahl's reply — "no, the day of the market" — belongs to the sīrah's reports) is the whole instrument of human planning: date, place, terms. The verse says: such an appointment, *even if made*, would have failed — either the Muslims would not have come (knowing they were meeting a mailed thousand, not a caravan, the source's reading of v. 42's plain sense: they would have shrunk from the tryst) or the Quraysh would have (the caravan's safe return made their mobilization pointless to every sensible man in Makkah, as we saw them urge: the source notes the near-miss on both slopes). The battle's *timing* — the very collision, the day each army committed before the other could re-plan — was arranged by an appointment-maker no party consulted: "so that God might conclude a matter." This is the sūrah's answer, at the level of *scheduling*, to v. 17's answer at the level of *acts*: the throwing and the killing were *made*; so here the *encounter itself* — the frame in which the men chose to stand or run — was made: the believer's freedom is real *inside* a plot he did not write, which is exactly the verse's *Hearing, Knowing* seal: He *heard* the cry (v. 9) and *knew* the heart's flinch (v. 43's *dhāt al-ṣudūr*) — and arranged.

**"That Whoever Perishes, Perishes on a Clear Proof" — Justice Requires Evidence, Not Miracle**

*Li-yamūta man mata ʿan bayyinatin wa-yaḥyā man ḥayya ʿan bayyinah* — so that whoever dies, dies upon *bayyina* (clear proof), and whoever lives, lives upon *bayyina*. The *bayyina* is the sūrah's own legal term: the evident, documented proof in the court of God (39:57's "lest he say: if God had guided me I would have been of the reverent," and 2:253's logic of guidance-without-coercion), and the clause is one of the Qur'an's most self-conscious statements of *why history is conducted by evidence and not by overwhelming sign*: Badr had to be a *demonstration*, not a *dazzle* — the two armies met, the odds were visible, the outcome was *inferable* from the day's plain facts (the small band's firmness, the rain, the dust, the rout), so that every survivor's choice afterward was *responsible*: the one who persisted in unbelief after Badr perished on *proof*, not on ignorance; the one who entered belief after Badr lived on *proof*, not on rumor. The source commentary's pairing is exact: "to live or perish on clear proof echoes the separation of the bad from the good (v. 37) and the naming of the day *furqān*": the day *divided* visibly, so that the division of hearts afterward is *just*, and every soul's end has *evidence* attached. Al-Rāzī adds the ground-and-rain reading of *bayyina* itself (some take the "clear proof" as the weather of v. 11 — the miracle-legible-in-nature: firm sand under their feet, mire under the enemy's), which keeps the verse's theology of signs: the *proof* runs through the ordinary — water, sand, a handful of dust — so that no one can plead that he needed thunder.

**The Valley's Permanence: "Hearing, Knowing" over the Un-chosen Ground**

The verse's terminal pair — *samīʿun ʿalīm* — closes the map as it closed v. 17, and in this sūrah the two names have acquired their technical weight from the valley: *Hearing* = the answer before the ask's echo (v. 9's *fa-stajāba lakum*, the "so He answered you" — the cry was still in the throat when the angels moved); *Knowing* = the prior ownership of every heart's weather (the fear of the averse of v. 5, the flinch of v. 6's *ka-annamā yusāqūna*, the "what lies within breasts" of v. 43). The men standing in the *ʿudwat* reading this verse were told, in effect: *the ground you are on was heard into place and known into place; the ground on which you now stand — this verse — was built so that your lives too run on bayyina.* The near slope and the far slope, the caravan below, the missed tryst, the concluded matter: the sūrah gives the ummah a *geography of Providence*, whose moral for every later generation is the double clause of its ending: arrange your *mīʿād* (the planning is commanded elsewhere, and Badr itself was planned — the wells, the allies, the scouts) — and know that the appointment that *matters* is the one God made with a people He heard, on ground He prepared, at the hour that no side's calendar contained.

## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:43

> **[Remember] when God showed them in thy dream as being few. And hadst thou seen them as being many, you would have surely faltered and quarreled over the matter. But God delivered [you]. Truly He knows what lies within breasts.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Dream That Won the Battle Before the Battle**

*Idh urīka-hum fī manāmika qalīlā* — when God showed them, in your dream, as few. The sūrah reaches back now to the *night before the march* — the vision granted to the Prophet ﷺ in which Quraysh appeared diminished, and which he reported to the people as the assurance that steadied them into going out (al-Bukhārī's *marʾā* chapter carries the Companions' reception of prophetic dreams in exactly this register, and the sīrah reports the dream belongs to the eve of Badr's deployment). The mechanics deserve attention: God did not send an army into the dream, nor a sword, nor even a certainty — He adjusted a *number in a vision*, and the adjusted number moved a community into the field. The minor reading the source notes — that the "dream" was a waking vision (a minority among the commentators, since *manām* can take both senses, as in the tradition's usage for Ibrāhīm's "dream" of sacrifice which was a waking command, 37:105's *"you have confirmed the vision (ru'yā)"*) — changes nothing of the verse's point: the perception was *given*, not achieved; the verb is passive for the Prophet, active for God: *urīka* — *you were shown*. The believer's courage, in this sūrah's psychology, begins upstream of willpower, in what God causes a heart to *see*.

**The Counterfactual of Morale: "You Would Have Faltered and Quarreled"**

*Wa-law araytahum kathīran la-fashiltum wa-la-tanāzaʿtū fi'l-amr* — had you seen them many, you would have failed and disputed over the matter. The two verbs are a field manual of morale: *fashil* — to lose heart, the root used for a bone that has set *wrong*, and for the enterprise whose sinew has given — and *tanāzaʿ* — to pull at one another, the quarrel that follows every failure of nerve (the same dispute of vv. 6's *yujādilūn-k*, now shown at its source: argument is the *aftermath* of the flinch, not its cause). The verse is candid about the community that had just been certified as true believers (v. 4): its steadiness was *manufactured*, by the fewness seen in a dream, because the real men, at the real ratio — a thousand mailed to three hundred and fourteen bare — would have failed at the sight and fought the fight twice, once with Quraysh and once with each other over whether to be there at all. This is the sūrah's most useful sentence for leaders in every century: the *perception of the task* determines the council's outcome more than the task; and the *art of the God-fearing commander* — which the Prophet ﷺ practiced here without knowing the verse that records it — is the ordering of what is seen: the intelligence given, the scale presented, the fear named before it mutates into faction. Al-Rāzī asks whether the dream-deception was just to the men's autonomy, and answers with the verse's own verb — *la-fashiltum* — they were *weak on the measure*, and God's condescension to the weakness of the sincere is not a violation of their freedom but the *condition under which their freedom could be exercised at all*: no one is obliged to be brave by a sight that will break him, and the breaker was removed.

**"But God Delivered You": Salvation as Rescue, Not as Reward**

*Wa-lākin nājjā Allāh* — and God saved [you]; the root *n-j-w* is the ark's word (the *safīna*, the *munjā* of 10:103 and the "day of the near-approach" of 54:13's idiom), and its use here quietly re-classifies Badr: the day was not first a *victory* to be celebrated but a *deliverance* to be survived — of the army from its own failure, of the community from its own dissolution. The sūrah's ordering of the night's graces now reads complete and exact: the fewness in the dream (before the march — the *will*), the sleep and water on the ground (the eve — the *body*), the angels' firmness (the day — the *heart*), and here, before all of it, the *rescue itself* named as the deed's author: *nājjā Allāh*, as the future-tense "We return" of v. 19, as the "not you who killed" of v. 17: the passive-form sentence the sūrah keeps repeating until even its heroes can no longer say "we."

**"Truly He Knows What Lies within Breasts" — the Anatomy of the Given Sight**

*Innahu ʿalīm bi-dhāti'ṣ-ṣudūr* — He is knowing of what breasts contain: the Qur'an's idiom for the *contents* — *dhāt*, the "possessions" of the chest — is deliberate: the heart has cargo, and none of it is hidden from the Carrier-owner. The clause completes the dream's ethics: God sized the army's fear *before* choosing its sight — He "knew" that *this* people, at *this* hour, could bear the fewness and not the many — and so the verse answers the believer's permanent pastoral question: *is my fear noticed?* It is *counted*: the whole of this sūrah's pedagogy (the sleep for the trembling, the dream for the faltering) proves that divine guidance proceeds not from the heights of the outcome but from the *level of the vessel*, as in the Prophet's ﷺ own law of the religion's ease: *"Religion is easy, and no one overwhelms religion except that it overwhelms him — so be upright, approximate, and give good news"* (al-Bukhārī 39, from Abū Hurayrah's *yasrūn* hadith, whose "be upright and approximate" the scholars read beside this verse: God Himself *approximated* the sight to their strength). The ʿAṭāʾ-like corollary for the reader: the man who sees, in the hour of decision, an enemy made *few* — whose task looks smaller than its reputation — should know who showed it to him, and march; and the man whose chest holds what it cannot bear should pray the prayer of this verse's Maker: *do not leave us to the full sight of what would make us falter.*

## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:44

> **And [remember] when He showed them to you, when you met them, as being few in your eyes, and made you appear to be few in their eyes, so that God may conclude a matter that was to be done. And unto God are all matters returned.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Double Mirage: Perception as Divine Weapon**

*Wa-idh yurīkumū-hum idh laqītumūhum qalīlan fī aʿyunikum wa-yuqallilukum fī aʿyunihim* — and when He showed them few *in your eyes* at the meeting, and made *you* few in *their* eyes. The verse moves the miracle from the dream (v. 43) to the open field, and its symmetry is the theology: the same verb of showing, both directions. The Muslims saw Quraysh *fewer than they were*, so that they would advance (the source's report through Ibn Masʿūd: he said to the man beside him, "would you say they are seventy?" — "they are near a hundred" — and the prisoner afterward told them: *we were a thousand*, the number Qurṭubī transmits here); and Quraysh saw the Muslims *fewer than they were*, so that they would *commit* — the enemy's contempt was the bait by which the battle was forced on ground and hour that heaven had chosen, a fact the sīrah's own data confirm: Quraysh, believing the little band before them easy, did not disengage (they had every *reason* to withdraw once the caravan was safe, and did not, because they had *seen* what they saw). The double diminution is a perfect instrument of the "matter that had to be done" (v. 42's *amr kāna mafʿūlā*, the phrase this verse repeats verbatim so its point cannot be missed): neither side's eyes were lying *to itself*; both sides' eyes were *managed* toward a junction whose necessity the verses of vv. 7 and 42 have already located in God's will, and the field's outcome (the few seen as few *fighting* as many, v. 66's arithmetic) was the proof that the eyes had been *led*, not *fooled*: the sight was real enough to be *used* by the sincere and light enough to be *trusted* by the proud — each side got the view its heart was ready to act on.

**Does God "Trick"? the Question the Verse Forces — and Its Answer**

Al-Rāzī, who raises it in his *Mafātīḥ* on this pair of verses, frames the objection exactly as a reader must: if the perception itself is edited by God, where is the justice of punishing the men who fought on a mis-estimate? The answer runs through the sūrah's own vocabulary: the *bayyina* (v. 42) — the clear proof — was *not* suppressed; the valley was open, the numbers stood in the light, the choices were made by *agents* with full faculties on data they took as they took everything else, and the verse's clause of purpose — *li-qaḍiya'llāhu amran* — assigns the *situation's arrangement* to God while the *response's authorship* stays, as always in this sūrah, double-written (v. 17: "you slew them not, *but God* slew them" — the conjunction, not the cancellation). The perception-management is, further, stated in the same idiom as the *sleep and the water and the drowsiness* (v. 11): the Qur'an never treats the senses as sacred, isolated from their Maker; a God who can settle a nervous system can size a horizon, and the verse's *yuqallilukum* (He *made you few*) is a *tawḥīd* of providence, not of culpability. Finally: the double mirage answers the community's *sharīʿah*-level question — the *law* of war includes *khudʿa*, and its first lesson, "war is deceit" (al-Bukhārī 3030; Muslim 1739), belongs to the same sūrah's world as the enemy's "fewness" of Quraysh's eyes: God permits what He Himself practiced in the arrangement of the field, with the one limit — the *covenant* is never the instrument of the ruse (vv. 58–59's "throw their treaty back on equal terms": deception of perception in war, *none* in promises — the sūrah's two-line constitution of military ethics).

**"Unto God Are All Matters Returned" — the Verse's Last Sentence, and the Valley's Silence**

*Wa-ilā'llāhi tu'rjaʿu'l-umūr* — and to God matters are *turned back*. The passive of *rujūʿ* completes the verse's frame: the two diminutions in two pairs of eyes were *managed*, and everything being managed — every *amr*, including the quarrels the sūrah has chronicled (the spoils, the aversion, the ransom) — *returns* to the One who arranged them: which is why the verse's last word is not "victory" but *the return*. The sūrah has been saying, in every register, that the *real* field is the one where accounts are made; and ʿUmar's later line under this verse in al-Ṭabarī's transmission is the sentence's own application: *"the day of Badr was the day on which the matters were returned, and I saw every man of Quraysh knowing his own place in the ground by the mark the Prophet had drawn the night before"* (cf. Muslim 1777, the pointing-out of the places of the slain) — the vision that *made* the few, the vision that *fixed* the places: Badr's two eyes. For the reading community, the clause legislates *resignation in result and diligence in means*: you may arrange, warn, and march — and the matters, every one of them, *return* elsewhere: which is precisely the heart's hygiene after the spoils (v. 1: the pile is *given*, not *seized*; v. 29: the criterion is *made*, not earned) — the *iʿjāz* of this verse is that it tells the victors and the defeated the *same sentence*: you both saw less than was there, and all of it — the seeing, the fighting, the heap, and the House — *returns to God*.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:45

> **O you who believe! When you meet a company in battle, be firm and remember God much, that haply you may prosper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Commands for One Hour: the Line and the Breath**

*Yā ayyuhā'lladhīna āmanū idhā laqītum fiʾatan fa-uthbutū wa-dhkurū Allāha kathīran laʿallakum tufliḥūn* — when you meet *a company* (the sūrah's own word, v. 16's *fiʾah*, now the general term for the formed enemy), *be firm* and *remember God much* — and the pairing is the verse's entire teaching. The first command governs the feet and the ranks; the second governs the chest. No other scripture, and no other military manual, joins these two as a single duty; yet anyone who has stood under real pressure knows the verse's order to be empirical: the body holds only what the mind holds, and the mind in terror holds only what it has been trained to hold *before*. The *dhikr* here is not a chaplet for the calm hours; it is the combat-breath of the line — al-Rāzī's note preserved in the sources is exactly this: remembrance in "the most distracting and tumultuous situations," the heart never empty of its Lord even as the spears rise — and its promised fruit is not loot (the sūrah has already forbidden that arithmetic) but *falāḥ*: prospering, the word v. 4's "generous provision" was built from and 2:5 made the whole aim of the reverent.

**Uthbutū: the Virtue This Sūrah Has Been Manufacturing Since Verse Eleven**

*Thabāt* — firmness — is the sūrah's master-virtue, and v. 45 gathers its ledger: God *made firm* the believers' feet with the rain-packed sand (v. 11: *yuthabbita bihi'l-aqdām*); He commanded the angels to *make firm* the hearts (v. 12: *fa-thabbitū*); the verse of flight legislated the *holding* of the line (vv. 15–16); v. 43–44's double fewness was arranged *so that* the few could hold without faltering. Now the command is handed to the believers in the active voice: *be firmly-set* — the verb of the mountain (*thabit* like the *rawāsiy*, the mountains as pegs, 78:7). The tradition's companion-image is the Prophet's ﷺ own line on the ranks of prayer which the sūrah has already echoed (v. 3's rows): *"God loves those who fight in His cause in a row, as though they were a structure joined"* (61:4 — *bunyān marṣūṣ*, the lead-fused wall: the ancient metallurgy of the strongest joint, molten lead poured between stone and stone so the wall becomes one stone). *Thabat* is thus, in this sūrah's physics, a *communal* property: the firmness of one man is the solidity of the line at his shoulder, which is why v. 46's prohibition of internal quarrel is the *next* sentence, and why the verse can put so much on a single verb: in the day's economy, one firm man was the army's wall-section, and his collapse was a breach in everyone's body.

**Dhikr Kathīr: the Ammunition of the Inner Line**

*Wa'dhkurū Allāha kathīran* — and the *much*-ness is commanded, not the *large*: quantity here is the point, because the verse is treating the human mind as it is: a faculty that fills itself with whatever it is given, and at Badr, with blood in the air, what rushes in un-fed is fear, revenge, and appetite (the three forces vv. 5, 48, and 67 will each name). Remembrance is the *occupation* of the mind by its Lord in the same hour, and the sūrah's psychology — already built from v. 2's quake, v. 11's sleep, v. 12's sent firmness — guarantees that this occupation is not private mysticism but public armor: *"Verily, in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest"* (13:28) is the verse's twin across the muṣḥaf, and the *ṭumaʾnīna* of v. 10 is what *dhikr* manufactures on demand. The prophetic frame gives the duty its daily training-ground: the man who can remember God in the market and the home is the man who will remember Him in the breach, for *dhikr* is a habit and habit is what a soldier is. *"The likeness of the one who remembers his Lord and the one who does not is the living and the dead"* (al-Bukhārī 6407) — and an army of the dead hearts breaks (v. 46: *tadhhab rīḥukum*), while an army of the living holds: the verse's *laʿallakum tufliḥūn* — that you *prosper* — is therefore stated over the whole body, and the Prophet ﷺ's two shaded eyes complete the portrait: *"Two eyes the Fire will not touch: an eye that wept from fear of God, and an eye that stood guard on God's path"* (al-Tirmidhī 1639, graded *ṣaḥīḥ* by Ibn Ḥajar) — the waking eye of the watch *is* an organ of *dhikr*, and the watchman who remembers is the wall-section God loves.

**Prosperity Redefined at the Moment of Maximum Pressure**

Read the verse in its place, between the valley's map (42–44) and the quarrel's ban (46), and its function is to *re-stake the prize*. A community just taught that its enemies' perceptions are being managed, its ground wetted by grace, and its victory attributed to heaven, might ask: *then what is ours to do?* The answer: *hold, and remember* — the two possessions no providence dispenses *instead* of a creature, but only *through* one: the firmness is sent (v. 12), the *holding* is willed; the *dhikr* is commanded, the *rest* is gifted — and the sūrah defines *prospering* on this field as it did in v. 4's ledger: not the pile (that was vv. 67–69's correction), but survival of the soul through the trial — *balāʾan ḥasanā*, v. 17's beautiful test, now with its method. And the "company" the verse speaks of has, in the sūrah's final reach, more than a battlefield referent: the *dhākir* stands firm against *every* *fiʾat* that meets him — the temptation that appears few (a single indulgence, a small dishonesty, one quiet concession), the fear that appears many (the odds of sincerity in a city of noise) — and the same two commands, the same two promises, apply: the feet of the conscience do not turn, and the tongue does not go silent — for that is how the believer wins the day that has no spoils, and prospering here, as v. 10 already settled, is *"from God alone, Mighty, Wise."*

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:46

> **And obey God and His Messenger, and do not quarrel among yourselves lest you falter and your good fortune depart. And be patient; truly God is with the patient.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Central Law, Stated at Its Center**

At the sūrah's numerical and thematic mid-point stands the sentence that explains every other: *wa-aṭīʿū Allāha wa-Rasūlahu wa-lā tanāzaʿū fa-tafshalu wa-tadhhaba rīḥukum wa-ṣbirū inna'llāha maʿa'ṣ-ṣābirīn*. It is the sūrah's *constitution* because it welds its three opening remedies into one clause: obedience (the answer to the spoils' jurisdiction-question of v. 1: "obey God and His Messenger, if you are believers," 1's exact echo), non-dispute (the answer to v. 1's *wa-aṣliḥū dhāta baynikum* — *iṣlāḥ* is the cure; *tanāzuʿ* is the disease in its social name), and patience (the answer to the fear of vv. 5–6 and the dream of v. 43: the capacity to hold what hurts, without flinching into faction). Three hundred and fourteen men had survived Badr because they had, at the last, kept all three; the verse is written as *law* so that later companies of every size may stand on the same ground in every kind of breach.

**"Lest You Falter and Your Wind Depart": the Physics of Defeat**

*Fa-tafshalu wa-tadhhaba rīḥukum* — and the *fashl* here is v. 43's word (the sinew giving way, the collective *fail*) and the *rīḥ* is the sūrah's most physical image: wind — the thing that sails are made of, that carries the arrow, that fills the drum and the sail and the desert march. The source preserves the idiom perfectly: *rīḥ* meant *the turn of fortune*, *the current of advantage*, in Arab speech — a wind over which, al-Ajurrī's note adds, no one has command; a gift that comes when it wills and *departs on conditions* whose only certain one is this verse's: internal quarreling. The Arabs knew the west wind (*dabūr*) as the unpropitious and the east wind (*ṣabā*, the dawn-wind that drove their ships and cooled their forges) as the blessed, and the Prophet ﷺ spoke in that exact register: *"I was aided by the ṣabā, and the people of 'Ād were destroyed by the dabūr"* (al-Bukhārī 1035; Muslim 900) — the very wind of the sūrah's own verse-pair 46:24 and 54:19–20 that stripped 'Ād, *musakhkhiratan* — "set upon them" — for seven nights and eight days. The verse's warning, then, is stated in the idiom of a people who read the sky for their lives: the wind that carries *you* is the wind God *sends* — and faction among you is the leak in the sail: the quarreling party loses not just the battle's *idea* but its *momentum*, the moving power that cannot be bought back at will. Uḥud is the verse's exegesis, as al-Rāzī notes in the source's gloss: the archers left their hill over the share of the spoils (the very v. 1 disease in its later form), and the wind *turned*: seventy dead, the Leader's tooth broken, the rumor of his death — *tadhhab rīḥukum* performed to the letter on the same slope where Badr's had risen.

**"Do Not Quarrel": the Ban That Precedes the Battle, Not Follows It**

The *tanāzuʿ* the verse forbids is the *mutual* pulling (Form VI's reciprocal verb: *yantaziʿūna*, tearing at each other — the root of *n-z-ʿ*, to extract violently; the same verb used of the well-rope's *draw*). The sūrah places the prohibition *before* the patient's reward for a reason: in the Qur'an's experience of community — 3:103's *"hold fast to God's rope, one and all, and do not split"*, 3:105's *"do not be like those who split and disputed after the clear proofs came to them"* — division is never the *result* of defeat; it is its *cause and form*, and Badr's eve had proved it (vv. 5–6: the aversion, the *ujādalah* about the promise, the quarrel that a shared fear manufactures when there is no shared *obedience* to close it). The command is thus *pre-emptive*: settle authority *now* (obey God and His Messenger), so that fear *later* finds no seam. This is why the classical *siyāsah* (statecraft) literature treats 8:46 as the law of the *jamāʿah*: the imam's command binds *before* the army's opinion cools, because the alternative to a decided command is not a free debate but a fashl — a collapse with a committee as its witness — and ʿAlī's line at Ṣiffīn ("they neither answered when called nor assisted when led") is the verse's later verdict on the faction's end.

**"And Be Patient — God Is with the Patient": the With-ness That Is the Reward and the Engine**

*Wa-ṣbirū inna'llāha maʿa'ṣ-ṣābirīn*. The *maʿiyyah* — with-ness — is the sūrah's own key-term, which it has already deployed twice at Badr's crisis: *truly I am with you* — to the angels (v. 12) — and *your company will not profit you... God is with the believers* (v. 19); here it is *conditionalized*: with *the patient* specifically — the special with-ness of help, not the general with-ness of knowledge — the same distinction Ibn Taymiyyah formalized in his writings on the divine *maʿiyyah*. And patience here (*ṣabr*) is the whole *endurance-system* the sūrah has defined in act: the sleep of the trembling (v. 11) *is* patience's fruit; the firmness of the line (v. 45) *is* patience's body; the refusal of the quarrel *is* patience's speech. The Prophet's ﷺ own line turns the command from a load into a gift:

> *"And whoever *seeks* to be patient, God will grant him patience — and no one has been given a better and vaster gift than patience."* (al-Bukhārī 1469; Muslim 1053, from Abū Saʿīd)

— the verse's *wa-ṣbirū* answered by its Maker with *man yaṣṭabir*: the command is an *invitation to ask*, and the asked-for gift is "vaster" (*awn*) than the victory whose wind it catches. The verse thus ends the sūrah's mid-clause where the whole chapter has been going since v. 1: not to the strong, but to the *held* — and held by the *with-ness* that no quarrel can dislodge once the community has stopped, in obedience and patience, trying to dislodge it from within.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:47

> **And be not like those who left their homes boastfully and to be seen of men, and to turn [others] from the way of God. And God encompasses whatsoever they do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same March, Described from the Inside**

*Wa-lā takūnū ka'lladhīna kharajū min diyārihim baṭharan wa-riʾā'a'n-nāṣi wa-yaṣuddūna ʿan sabīli'llāh* — be not like those who went out of their homes *baṭharan* and *riʾāʾan*. The sūrah re-narrates Badr from the enemy's exit, and the two nouns are the autopsy of a cause: *baṭhar* — arrogance in high color, the swagger of excess that the lexicographers define as "expending oneself in pride beyond what is one's due" — and *riʾāʾ*, the root *r-ʾ-y*, "to be *seen*": acting for the eye of an audience. The sīrah preserves the verse's referent in the commander's own words: Quraysh, told by Abū Sufyān that the caravan was safe and the war's *purpose* gone, pressed on anyway, and Abū Jahl swore he would not turn back until they reached Badr, slaughtered the camels, drank the wine, and the *qaynāt* — the singing girls — played for them, **"so that the Arabs keep hearing of our march"** — a war launched, in the enemy's own mouth, as an advertisement. The verse puts the phrase in the Qur'an forever: the army that came for the caravan had become an army that came for *being-seen*, and it is that — not their numbers, not their idols — the sūrah offers the victors as the thing *not to be*: you too have just come out of your homes to a valley; ask *which* exit you made.

**Riyāʾ: The Shrink of Every Deed to a Performance**

*Riʾāʾ* is the only disease that can make worship into commerce, and the Qur'an's law of it spans this sūrah's whole world. In the domain of spending: *"do not render your charities void by reminder and harm, like one who spends his wealth riʾā'an li'n-nāsi"* (2:264 — the same word on the same sin at the same pocket), and the khums' honest fraction (v. 41) and the sūrah's *infaq* of v. 3 are its hygiene. In the domain of war — v. 47's own field — the ḥadīth of the first three kindled in the Fire is the sūrah's sharpest mirror:

> *"The man who fought so that it would be said 'he is courageous' — that one is in the Fire."* (Muslim 1905)

and the Prophet's ﷺ line on the *sumʿah*-seeker: *"Whoever lets himself be heard, God will let himself be heard (exposes him) on the day of gathering"* (al-Bukhārī 6499; Muslim 2986). Riʾāʾ, in these texts, is not merely "showing off" — it is the *transplant of the deed's addressee*: the act is done *before men* while the tongue says *lillāh*, and the verse of 2:264's "soil with a downpour" parable shows the transplant's economics — a crust of beautiful earth over bedrock, "left smooth, bare, by one rain": the *audience's* praise, which the actor's work had bought, washes off at the first real weather, and only God's rain reveals which deeds had roots. Which is why the sūrah sets the word here, at the hinge of the two armies: Badr was the first *weather* that tested which side's works were for the *seen* and which for the Seeing — and the boastful march of v. 47 was the first public instance of a faith losing to a theater, though the theater lost on the ground, the *riʾāʾ* army having carried off the Arabs' attention it had come for — a transaction the two clauses of the verse will complete in their only honest ledger: the Fire.

**"And to Turn from God's Way" — Barred and Barred From**

*Wa-yaṣuddūna ʿan sabīli'llāh* — and their *ṣadd* (the same double-voiced verb of v. 34's note: they turn *others* off the way, and the way turns *them*), which the sūrah uses to weld the two Badr-mentions: the enemy came out to *turn* God's community off its religion (the war's real aim, v. 36's spending), and thereby *were turned* off the way that was under their feet in the valley, walking past the one oasis (the well they were beaten to) on which their success had been balanced. The clause's moral is the sūrah's deepest equation of psychology and history: a *ṣidd* of the heart — the turning-away from the path by those who sit *on* it and *at* it and use it to walk *over* others — is what becomes, in history, the *ṣadd* of a community: the men who had turned the *mustaḍʿafūn* from the House for years (v. 34, and 2:114's "who is a greater wrongdoer than one who *prevents God's name from being mentioned in His places*") had lost the reflex that could have saved them at the wells, at the sand, at the *riʾāʾ*-report they were too busy sending to read the weather. And *wa-Allāhu bimā yaʿmalūna muḥīṭ* — the verse's closing attribute — is the clause's seal: *muḥīṭ*, from the root of the *wall that goes all around*: God *encircles* what they do (the source's note: encompassing in knowledge *and* power), so that a people's exit from their homes is, from the first step, *inside* the enclosure: their boastful march was as much His field as the Muslims' firm one — and *riʾāʾ* before the Encircler is the comedy at the heart of the word: a deed made for the eyes of an audience *contained in the sight of the One the deed avoided*.

**The Believer's Mirror: Two Exits from the Same House**

The verse's *lā takūnū ka'lladhīna* — "be not like those who went out" — is the sūrah's *comparison* clause, and it is aimed at both Badr's parties *and* at the camp reading it: the *same* physical act (leaving home, walking to danger, fighting for the community's survival) divided, in God's scales, into the two *sabr* and *riʾāʾ* kinds — exit for God's way vs. exit for the *riʾāʾ* of men — and the division, this sūrah has taught, is *invisible to everyone but God and the doer* (the *dhāt al-ṣudūr* of v. 43). Every later believer shares the exit: the job taken for the *title* or the *provision*; the marriage entered for the *house* or for the *Giver*; the worship performed for the *custom* or the *Presence* — the *baṭhar* of the sūrah's army being, in every hour, the *I-own-it* that turns the good act's face from its Source; and the tradition's own cure is the sūrah's whole psychology of the *hidden*: *"act with the stillness of the aware"* and the ḥadīth's line on the three kinds of hearing that end, for the *riʾāʾ*-man, in the *riʾāʾ* of men who will not answer on the Day (the *sumʿah* hadith's own closing, in the Musnad), while the "sincere for God" — the *mukhliṣ* whose heart the sūrah's *furqān* (v. 29) has trained to see its own motives — walks, even at Badr's wells, into the *tawfiq* (v. 46's wind) that the boastful march of the seen can never buy.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:48

> **And [remember] when Satan made their deeds seem fair unto them, and said, “None among mankind shall overcome you today, and I am indeed your defender.” But when the two hosts saw each other, he turned on his heels and said, “I am quit of you! Truly I see what you see not. Truly I fear God, and God is severe in retribution.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Army at Badr, and Its Two Faces**

*Wa-idh zayyana lahumu'sh-shayṭānu aʿmālahu wa-qāla lā ghāliba lakumu'l-yawma mina'n-nāsi wa-innī jārun lakum* — the verse adds to the field's order of battle its hidden wing: Satan, who *made their deeds seem fair* (*zayyana* — the Form II verb of ornament: to *make beautiful*, to bedeck, to paint the ugly as lovely) and gave them the guarantor's word. The classical reports (in Ibn Isḥāq's sīrah, and in al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī here) give the *zayyana* an occasion: Iblīs came to Quraysh's march in the shape of a man of their neighbors — the Sūraqa ibn Mālik / the "man of Najd" of the sīrah's account — bearing their standard, and the army, which had set out doubting (the caravan was safe; the march was an expense of blood and money for *face* alone), took the devil's "I am your neighbor, you will not be struck from the direction I guard" as the warrant that completed their resolution. The second sense of *zayyana* is the deeper one (al-Rāzī, as the source records, leans here): the whisper — not a visible figure but the *interior decoration* of a deed: pride made to look like prudence, the vanity of the wine-and-song boast (v. 47) made to look like the honor of the clan, the extermination-war made to look like a caravan-guard. The verse's lesson is the whole Qur'anic psychology of *tazyīn*: the act is never bought for what it *is*, only for what the eye sees, and the enemy of man is the one who owns the paint (cf. 15:39's oath: *"because You have adorned me with error (zaghwanī), I will adorn it for them upon Your path"*; and 16:63, 27:24, 29:38 — the source's own citations).

**"I Am Your Defender" — the Patronage That Expires on the Day**

*Wa-innī jārun lakum* — "I am your *jār*": the word of the Arab's *jawār*, the sworn client-patronage that was the tribe's constitution of protection — the guarantee for which a man would die before his *jār*, the strongest security in a society with no courts. The verse's irony is its *theology*: the devil sells, to a people who had *rejected* God's *wilāyah* ("God is your master," v. 40), a *j iwār* of his own — and the Qur'an's law is stated in the sūrah's own later twin: *"and who is a greater wrongdoer than one entrusted with God's signs then runs from them as though he had never known them"* (45:23's *walī*, 14:22's *jār*), and Joseph's line: *"I have left the religion of a people who know not God, and I follow the religion of my fathers — and whoever takes him as *walī*, he will take him to the Fire"* (cf. the *walāyah*-law of 5:51–57). At the Day, this patronage becomes its own exposé, and the Qur'an quotes the devil's *final* press-release in the sūrah of Ibrāhīm:

> *"And Satan will say, when the matter has been decided: 'God promised you the promise of truth, and I promised you and I failed you. And I had no authority over you, except that I called you and you responded to me — so do not blame me, and blame yourselves.'"* (14:22)

which is v. 48's *lā ghāliba lakum* — "none can overcome you today" — in its Day-version: a promise that was true *while it was being believed*, and a patron whose *jawār* ran exactly to the horizon of his ability to *see* (the verse's *lā yashʿurūna* logic) — and when the field's horizon changed, so did he.

**"When the Two Hosts Saw Each Other, He Fled" — the Precise Hour of Abandonment**

*Falammā tarā'a'l-fiʾatān tanakasa ʿalā ʿaqibayhi wa-qāla innī baʾīrun minkum innī arā mā lā tarawn innī khāfu'llāh* — he turned on his heels at the *sight* of the two companies. The hour is chosen with the sūrah's whole care: he does not leave when the fighting turns against them (many an army has fought *on* into a lost day); he leaves at the *vision*: *tarā'a* — when the two hosts *saw one another* — because the verse's reason is the other side's *unseen* wing: *innī arā mā lā tarawn* — "I see what you do not see": the angels in their ranks (v. 9's *muṣayyabīn*, v. 12's commission) were visible to him and not to the men, and the devil's *kashf* is his *curse*: he is not ignorant of the real, he is *certain* of it and *without hope* in it — the only being in this sūrah who sees the whole field *and* fears God at once, and *flees the field* because of the fear (the sīrah's report: he fled in the shape of a dove, and the Prophet ﷺ said, "the standard-bearer was Jibrīl in the shape of al-Zubayr"? — the reports run through the tafsīr collections of Ibn Abī Ḥātim — and al-Qurṭubī notes the version "he took the standard and fled and left them," the banner falling on the *jār*'s desertion). The clause *"innī khāfu'llāh"* is the verse's bitter jewel: the devil's *fear of God* is *true* — the Qur'an never says he fears falsely — but it is a fear that saves no one, because fear without *obedience* is exactly *flight*, and the verse has just *shown* it: he who fears God leaves the man who trusted him. The *khauf* of the shayṭān is, in this sūrah's moral physics, the perfect image of the *realist* whose clear-sightedness has no loyalty: he sees what you do not see, and he acts on what he sees — *for you*, not one step.

**The Lesson for the Army That Was Almost Like Quraysh**

Why does the sūrah put the *zayyana* here, in the *victors'* book, sixty years of Muslim memory before their eyes? Because the devil's *other* customer-list had just been re-opened at Badr's camp itself: the men who disputed over the spoils (v. 1), who "wished the un-armed was theirs" (v. 7), who later ran for the ransom (vv. 67–69) — were not being fought by an enemy they could *see*, they were being *decorated* by one they could not. The verse therefore functions as the sūrah's *inner* 8:47: "be not like the *riʾāʾ*-march" becomes "know that *zayyana* is the war's hidden wing, and it is pointed at *you* too, in the tent, at the pile, in the vote." The cure the sūrah prescribes is its own vocabulary in reverse — the very faculties the devil's paint disables: the *furqān* of v. 29 (the criterion that sees the deed *naked*, as the *muḥāṣibī* and *Ghazālī*-tradition of *riʾāya* later made the first science of the soul: the believer as his own *muftī* over his motives), the *dhikr* of v. 45 (the tongue kept full so the heart is not free for the whisper), and the *tawakkul* of v. 2 whose object is the One who *sees the seeing* of v. 48 (God's *ʿilm* at this verse's side: *mā taʿmalūn*, the sūrah's refrain) — and the tradition's own ḥadīth sets the daily *dhikr* as the armor the verse implies, since *"Satan runs in the son of Adam as the blood runs"* (al-Bukhārī 2038; Muslim 2814, Ṣafiyya's report — and her gloss of it: "the Prophet ﷺ said: *God knows I am devoted to you, and he asked: 'what did you see?' — 'the Shayṭān reaching in you as the blood reaches'"*), so that the man who has seen this verse — *the patron flees when the ranks appear* — may know exactly which "jār" to refuse *before* the day his sight is the only thing left in camp.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:49

> **[Remember] when the hypocrites and those in whose hearts is a disease said, “Their religion has deluded them.” But whosoever trusts in God, truly God is Mighty, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Spectators' Arithmetic**

*Idh yaqūlu'l-munāfiqūna wa'lladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍun gharrra hā'ulāʾi dīnu-hum* — when the hypocrites and the diseased-hearted said: "their religion has *deceived* these men." The scene is Badr's dawn seen from the sidelines: parties in Madinah and along the road — the open hypocrites of the Aws and Khazraj (ʿAbdullāh ibn Ubayy's circle being the tradition's named instance, as at Uḥud's 150-man walkout, 3:167), and the lesser company of the *muraḍḍūn*, "those in whose hearts is a disease": al-Rāzī's reading (in the source's note) distinguishing the second group as nominal Muslims of weak faith who had not migrated, while al-Qurṭubī holds both nouns one people described twice (cf. 33:12's pairing). Their verdict is the verse's specimen of the *world's sight*: three hundred and fourteen, hungry, unprotected, marching at a mailed thousand — *gharrarahum dīnu-hum*: their religion has *deluded* them; they have been *taken in* by their own book. The root *gh-r-r* is the mirage's root: the thing seen where it is not — the false appearing *attractive*, not merely false (3:185 and 57:20's *matāʿ al-ghurūr*, "the enjoyment of delusion"), and 31:33's warning: *lā yaghurranna-kumu'l-ḥayātu'd-dunyā wa-lā yaghurranna-ka bi'llāhi'l-gharūr* — "let not the worldly life delude you, nor the Deceiver delude you about God." The spectators' judgment is thus a *projection of their own condition*: men for whom religion is a *deceiver* are men who have already let *the world* be their creditor.

**"Disease" — the Qur'an's Diagnosis of Unbelief's Mildest Form**

*Maraḍ* in the Qur'an's psychology is not the absence of faith but faith's *fever*: the belief that has caught a chill of appetite and is melting toward the other side (2:8–10: "they say we believe — but they do not believe; they seek to deceive God... in their hearts is disease, and God has increased their disease"). At Badr, the disease shows its exact symptom: the man who does not *deny* the victory's God, but cannot *risk* on Him — who computes the odds, and reports his cowardice as an observation about *their* piety: *gharrarahum dīnu-hum* is the sentence in which unbelief never says "I am afraid," only "they are deluded." The Uḥud-twin the tradition pairs with this verse is 3:154's account of the same men's words: *"they say of those who fell among them: if they had obeyed us, they would not have been killed. Say: then avert death from yourselves, if you are truthful"* — the exact genre: theology from the armchair about the field; and the sūrah's later verses (vv. 75's rank of the migrants vs. the non-migrants) is the *policy* answer to the same men, whom the community must neither arm nor adopt. Al-Ṭabarī's note connects their "delusion" to the *fewness shown* in vv. 43–44: the spectators saw an army *smaller than it was*, and drew the world's only possible conclusion; the men inside the ranks saw a promise *larger than the field*, and drew the other — which makes the verse a permanent boundary between the two ways of estimating the real.

**"But Whoever Trusts in God — God is Mighty, Wise": the One Sentence That Answers the Mirage**

*Wa-man yatawakkal ʿalā'llāhi fa-inna'llāha ʿazīzun ḥakīm* — the *but* turns the whole verse into a counter-definition. Against *ghurūr* the sūrah sets *tawakkul*, and the two are exact opposites of the same act: both are *judgments about the unseen's effect on the visible* — one judges the unseen *absent* (religion "deludes" because nothing answers), the other judges it *present* (the Mighty answers, the Wise disposes). The names chosen seal the two failures of the spectators' arithmetic: *ʿazīz* — the One no odds can bind, which is why 314 need not be many (v. 65–66's arithmetic is His address-book); *ḥakīm* — the One whose acts have *reason* even when the field does not show it, which is why the day's fewness was *made* (vv. 43–44: "so that a matter had to be concluded"), and not suffered. And the *tawakkul* here is v. 2's, returned to its post: "and upon their Lord they *rely*" — the fourth quality of true belief, now proven in the exact situation that had falsified it for everyone else: the same heart that quakes when God is *mentioned* (v. 2) stands firm when God is *trusted*, and the line between the two states is the whole difference in this sūrah between the *believer* and the *spectator*: both see the same valley, the same ratio, the same odds — the one has, in his chest, an address *for* the odds. The tradition's ḥadīth of the bird (v. 2 above: trust like the bird's — full crop, empty nest, *and going out at dawn*) is the verse's practical form: *tawakkul* is not the denial of arithmetic but arithmetic *plus* an Owner — and the world's *ghurūr*, its most patient deception, is the claim that "plus" is the delusion.

**A Sentence for Every Rear Guard of History**

The verse's permanence lies in the fact that the *ghurur* verdict is always delivered by people who did not march: every community's hard obedience is diagnosed, from the comfortable, as fanaticism — the fast "deludes" the hungry, the giver "deludes" the saver, the believer "deludes" the martyr's generation. What v. 49 legislates is therefore the believer's *auditing of observers*: do not take your estimate from the men whose hearts are *healthy* in every way except the one way (their disease is precisely *health* — the world's hygiene without God, cf. 61:5's "Mūsā saying: people of the disease" and the hadith's phrase of *ghithaʾ* as the people's love of the world, Abū Dāwūd 4297) — your case is with the One whose names close the verse. And the *mādhā* of the spectators' *qālū* is answered, in the sūrah's own register, by its field: the day they called "delusion" ended with the deluded counting the living and their enemies the dead; which is why the verse keeps its present tense forever: *yatawakkal* — the trust *in act*, the one arithmetic that has, in this sūrah's book, never once been *mistaken*.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:50

> **And if only thou couldst see when the angels take those who disbelieve, striking their faces and their backs, and [saying], "Taste the punishment of the burning!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scene the Prophet Is Asked to Imagine**

*Wa-law tarā idh yatawaffa'lladhīna kafarū'l-malāʾikatu yaḍribūna wujūhahum wa-adbārahum wa-dhūqū ʿadhāba'l-ḥarīq* — "if only you could see, when the angels take the disbelievers, striking their faces and their backs, and: taste the burning!" The conditional of witness — *law tarā* — is the Qur'an's idiom for a scene too large for the eye (the same opening as 6:93's "if you could see when the wrongdoers are in the death-fits and the angels stretch their hands: 'yield your souls!'"; and 69:31's "and as for the wicked — no; they will not believe until they see the painful punishment"). The verb of taking is *tawaffā* — the Qur'an's own, gentlest-seeming word for death, "to take in full, to receive completely" (6:60: *"It is He who takes (yatawaffā) you at night"*) — and its gentleness here is the menace: the *receiving* of these souls is described as a *seizure with hands on the face and the back*: the verse holds the most clinical term and the most violent image in one frame to say: *the taking* will be, for the man who spent his life on his own account, a *handling* by others.

**Faces and Backs: the Anatomy of the Strike**

Why *the face and the back* — the two surfaces of a body? The reports give the Badr-answer first (al-Rāzī's note preserved in the source): the Quraysh had struck at the *faces and backs* of the believers in the years of Makkah's violence and at the field itself; the death-beds of the chiefs of Badr — ʿUtba and Shayba in the ditch, Abū Jahl dying face-down in the dust and the mud of the marsh (the sīrah's reports of his final words: "what worthless death is this — a man killed by his own people's stone"? — and the two accounts in Muslim 1796 of ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd finding him breathing with his belly opened: *"taste what punishment you have prepared for God's enemies!"*) — are this verse's own first tableau. And al-Rāzī's deeper gloss turns the two surfaces into a map of the man's *orientation*: the face is what he *faced* — the world he turned toward, now struck dark — and the back is what he *turned his back on* — God's path, the Hereafter, the duty he walked away from; and death's angels return him, blow for blow, on the two surfaces of his own choices. The verse's *adbār* is, then, the same noun as v. 15–16's *adbāb* (the fugitive's turned backs — *dubur*), and the echo is a whole theology in one vowel: the one who turned his back in the line at Badr and the one whose back is *struck* at death are one kind of body; the line's law and the grave's law rhyme because the line was the grave's rehearsal (v. 16's *maʾwā*, "refuge," is the verse's same word as the Fire's "home" — and the sūrah has already promised, in v. 16 and 50, the same refuge to the deserter of the ranks and the deserializer of the truth).

**"Taste the Burning" — the Fourth Taste, and the Angels' Liturgy**

*Wa-dhūqū ʿadhāba'l-ḥarīq* — the angels' *utterance* at the deathbeds: the strike is accompanied by a *sentence*, and the sentence is *dhūqū*: the taste (v. 14, v. 35, and soon 52's *fa-ʿakharat* idiom) is *spoken to them as they die* — the burning already *named* before it is *known*: the verse's mercy is a menace and its menace is a mercy: no soul enters the punishment unaddressed. *Ḥarīq*, burning, is the Qur'an's own word for the Fire's *elemental verb* (14:50's "garments of pitch, and their faces are covered by the Fire" and 22:20's "scalding water poured on the heads"; and 104:6-7's "the Fire of hearts, climbing over what the hearts know" — the fire that is *knowledge*, the fire that is *the burning* of the man's own heart being finally *read*). Read as a *liturgy of the seizure*, the angels' sentence answers the disbelievers' only prayer the sūrah recorded (v. 32: "if this be the truth from You, rain on us stones — or bring us a *ʿadhāb alīm*"): their prayer is answered, *by the very word they coined*, at the hour their prayer ends, by the beings they "saw not" (v. 48) who now strike where they cannot be seen: the verse's eschatology is Badr's *taste* extended to every grave, and the sūrah's courtroom (its "tastes") is now complete — *the field's taste* (v. 14), *the House's mockery's taste* (v. 35), *the dying angels' taste* (v. 50), and v. 51's *bill* to each man's own hand.

**Why the Scene Is Shown to the Believers, Not the Dying**

The *law tarā* addresses the Prophet ﷺ — and, through the muṣḥaf's ear, every believer: the scene is given *to the living*, and it is the sūrah's last piece of *battle-morale* from the rear of the camp (the verses around it are the treaties, the numbers, the prisoners): you who fear the ranks (v. 15), the numbers (v. 49), the scheming (v. 18), *look at the rank no schemer can form*: the deathbeds of the proud, with *angels on the face and on the back*, and the "taste" that closes every mouth. Ibn Kathīr's summation is the verse's stated function: *the sight of the vanquished's ending* — the chiefs of Quraysh in the ditch at Badr, *drowned in the very sea* v. 54 will name — is the *ʿibra* (the lesson) the *sabr* (v. 46) and *tawakkul* (v. 49) were made for: the one who *sees* v. 50 does not, for the rest of his life, *turn his back* at all.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:51

> **This is for what your hands sent forth, and because God wrongs not His servants."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Bill and the Court's Notice of Fairness**

*Dhālika bimā qaddamat aydīkum wa-anna Allāha laysta biẓallāmi'l-ʿabīd* — the verse is one sentence with two halves, and the halves are the *two foundations of every just verdict*: the *charge*, proven by the record ("because of what your hands have sent ahead"), and the *judge's* integrity ("and God is not at all a tyrant to His servants"). The pronoun's *bi-* clauses are, in the Arabic legal idiom, the indictment's citation-form: *bi-mā qaddamat aydīkum* — on account of what your own hands *put before yourselves* — the verb *qaddama* being the word for sending ahead (the same root as the *qadām*, "forefront," and as the *taqaddum* of the deeds *sent on before* — 25:70's "except whoever repents, believes and does a *good deed* — those, God *replaces* (yubaddilu) their evils with goods"). The sentence thus says what the sūrah's entire system of *taste* (vv. 14, 35, 50) has been proving: the punishment is *received*, not *inflicted*; the Fire is the *collection* of the man's own forwards, and no new thing is done to him at the end. The philologists' note on the idiom belongs to this verse's tone: the *hands* are named as the organ of *sending ahead*, so that the soul's own agency becomes the only addressee of the sentence — the bill has no other name than the man's own forwards.

**"Your Hands": the Reversal of Verse Seventeen**

Read the verse against the sūrah's own great negation, and its justice becomes a marvel of balance: at v. 17, the *victors'* credit was emptied — *you slew them not, but God slew them*; at v. 51, the *vanquished's* blame is filled — *what your hands sent ahead*. The sūrah's moral accountancy is *not symmetrical*: where the good was done, the hand is stripped of ownership (so pride finds no foothold: "victory is from God alone," v. 10); where the evil was done, the hand is kept as owner (so excuse finds no foothold: "for what your hands earned," cf. 3:182 *"that is for what your hands sent ahead — and God is not unjust to the wrongdoers"* and 22:10's identical clause, the source's own cross-references). The rule, which Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī both extract under this verse, is the Qur'an's *fiqh of attribution*: all good is *given*, all evil is *acquired* — the servant's hands are real senders, and the sender's Lord is the real Giver; which is why the same Book can say both "you slew not" and "what your hands sent forth" without contradiction: credit travels *up* to its Source, blame stops *here*, at the hand that chose. In the scholastic vocabulary of the Ashʿariyya, this verse-pair became a statute of *kasb* — acquisition — precisely because it keeps the deed's authorship with the servant *and* its creation with God.

**"God Wrongs Not His Servants" — the Theology of the Negative**

*Wa-anna'llāha laysa biẓallāmin li'l-ʿabīd* — and God is in no way a *ẓallām* of the servants. The grammar carries the whole argument: not *lā yazlim* ("He does not wrong") but the *intensive plural* negated — He is not, by any mode or measure, *one who wrongs* (the -ṭ-ʿ-m pattern ẓalūm: the one to whom wronging belongs, as the Muʿtazilīs read it, and the Ashʿarīs: what is His *character*, never His act; al-Rāzī's long *khalq al-afʿāl* discussion cites this verse-pair with 4:40 — *"your Lord wrongs no one"* — and 18:49's *"your Lord wrongs no servant"* as the Qur'an's definitive *nafi*-text). The clause is *needed*, the exegetes stress, precisely *because* the previous clause could alarm: "your hands sent ahead" might sound, to a weak ear, like a *charge* whose severity is its *point* (and the ẓāhiriyya-style fear of a punishing despot is one of the oldest distortions of religion's God) — so the verse welds the two: *the debt is real AND the Creditor is just*, and a debt so stated can never, in any court of heaven or earth, be *cruelty*.
