This is why the clause became the *prooftext* for the entire theodicy of the schools: al-Qurṭubī cites it for the *qāʿidah* that punishment is *deserved* (and thus *justice*, not vengeance), and for the converse mercy the sūrah itself has already proven — the "decree that had gone before" (v. 68: *"law lā kitābun min Allāhi sabaq"*: the *sabaq*, God's prior book, is why a people who "took" the spoils against the prior law were *rebuked and then pardoned* — the verse's *laysa bi-ẓallām* is the very name of the sūrah's treatment of its own heroes in vv. 67–69, whose reproof is followed, *in the same breath*, by *fa-kulū* — "so eat the spoils, lawful and good," v. 69! — a perfect *demonstration* of the *non-ẓulm*: the rebuke is *just* because it stops at the deed and does not *take* the deed's doer).

**The Sentence the Living Hear First**

The verse's audience, finally, is not only the dead: the Quraysh of v. 50's *taste* and the Quraysh of the ditch at Badr are the *instance*, but the *sentence* — bill and fairness, both — stands for every ear, and its two-halves law is the community's constitution of *accountability* to the end: (1) *no act is lost*: what the hand "sends ahead" arrives somewhere (the idiom *qaddama* being the *forwarding* verb of the caravan — and the sūrah of the caravan has made the word's point with an entire army's spoils), which is 99:6-8's atom-scale: *whoever does an atom's weight of good sees it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil sees it*; and (2) *no one is crushed*: the *ẓulm* clause is the reason the believer's dread is *hoped* into *dhikr* (v. 45) and not into *paralysis* — for he reads, with every trembling heart (v. 2), that the *Judge* whose bill will name *his* hands too has *declared*, in this sūrah and forever, that *wronging is not His name*: the *ʿabd* (the *ʿabīd* of this verse is the *ʿabd* of v. 41 — the "servant" on whom the Book was *sent down*) — and it is the *ʿabd*'s honor that his Master's court is the one place in creation where *justice and mercy are not yet two things*.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:52

> **[Theirs was] like the way of the House of Pharaoh and those before them; they disbelieved in the signs of God; so God seized them for their sins. Truly God is Strong, severe in retribution.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Case-File of History Opens**

*Ka-daʾbi āli Firʿawna wa-man qablahum; kafarū bi-āyāti'llāhi fa-akhadhā-humu'llāhu bi-dhunūbihim, inna'llāha qawiyyun shadīdu'l-ʿiqāb* — with v. 52 the sūrah leaves Badr's valley and enters the *archive*: the precedent-book is opened, and the first file is Pharaoh. The *daʾb* (the source's note is exact) is at once the *habit* and the *end*: the way a people does and the way a people is *done to* — the double meaning is the verse's thesis: the *sunnah* of God with a people is that their *course* becomes their *consequence*: Pharaoh's *daʾb* was the hardening (7:133's sequence of plagues with each "sign" meeting a fresh *daʾb* of denial), and his *daʾb* ended at the sea's closing. The clause *wa-man qablahum* — "and those before them" — widens the file to the whole roll of 7:59–93's nations (Nūḥ, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lūṭ, Shuʿayb), the Qur'an's permanent *ʿibra*-gallery, and the sūrah will re-open the same file twice more (v. 53's *inna sharra'd-dawābbi*-pair and v. 54's drowning of the *House* of Pharaoh), the repetition itself a *daʾb* of the Book (3:11's *mutashābih* principle; al-Bukhārī's tafsīr chapter notes on *tikrār al-qiṣaṣ li'l-taʾkīd*) — because the human heart is Pharaoh's heir in this one respect: it needs the story *twice*.

**"They Disbelieved in the Signs of God" — the Crime Named Precisely**

The charge is not "they had other gods" but *kufr bi-āyāt* — the *denial of the signs*: and the precision is Badr's own mirror. The *āyāt* the Qur'an means in this verse-pair are, per the tafsīr of the phrase throughout, both the *sent messages* (the Book's signs) and the *sent events* (the plagues, and — the commentators' equation here — the *day itself*): the source's note that *āya* spans both is why the verse's indictment fits the men of the valley exactly: Pharaoh did not deny a *philosophy*; he denied *evidence* — the staff, the hand, the years of famine, the sea — *as it arrived*, each fresh sign met with a fresh hardening, until his last sign was drowning (10:90–92: *"until, when drowning overtook him, he said: I believe that there is no god but He in whom the children of Isrāʾīl believe — now? And you had disobeyed before and were of the corrupters! So today We will preserve your body that you may be a sign to those after you"*). Ibn Kathīr's gloss draws the line to Quraysh, whose signs the sūrah has just recounted (the angels in ranks, the sleep, the rain, the fewness made visible, the handful of dust, the heap) — the same *āyāt*-grammar: a people *shown* and *denying* is one people, whether the Red Sea is their sea or the wells of Badr.

**"So God Seized Them for Their Sins" — the Akhdh as the Sūrah's Verdict-Verb**

*Fa-akhadhā-humu'llāhu bi-dhunūbihim* — the verb *akhadha* (to seize, to take hold) is the Qur'an's word for *the end of the respite*: the *imlāʾ* (respiting) having run its course (6:44's *nakhdhulū fī dhālikum* — "they were taken unawares"), the seizure is *bi-dhunūb*, "for their sins" — the *cause* stated as *desert*, not as impulse. The pair of attributes that closes the verse — *qawiyy, shadīd al-ʿiqāb* — is the seizure's *two qualities*: the strength that makes the grip final (the same *quwwa* that "seized the towns while they were wronging" in 11:102's *ṭaʿra*, the verse the ḥadīth cited at the Prophet's ﷺ hand — al-Bukhārī 4686 — attached to *istidraj*), and the severity that makes the end *painful*: the *ʿiqāb* that *follows* (ʿ-q-b, v. 13's note) until it *arrives*. The verse thus welds Badr into the *akhadh*-line: the seventy in the ditch are *āla Firʿawn*'s current file — which the Prophet's ﷺ own line at the pit of Badr is reported to have sealed (the two Ṣaḥīḥs of the *ruʾyā* and the *address to the slain* in Muslim's *Jannāʾ-Nār* chapters, and the famous words at the well: *"found your Lord's promise true... and here is the pit you were promised"* in Muslim's report of ʿUmar's query "what do they hear now?" — "you do not hear more than they, except that they *answer*" (Ṭabaqāt, and the wording runs in Aḥmad's Musnad) — Badr's dead and the sea's dead being, for the Qur'an, *one audience*).

**"And Those Before Them" — the Law the Verse Hands to the Believers**

The verse's function for its *first* hearers — the army newly seated over a heap of camels and captives — is not triumph but *caution*, and the context proves it. A sūrah that has just said *you slew not* (v. 17) and *this is for what your hands sent forth* (v. 51) sets Pharaoh before the *victors* so that the *akhadh* is read as a future tense that every people shares: whoever denies the signs runs the *daʾb*, and the *daʾb* ends in the seizure. The sūrah will apply the rule to its own camp within a few verses (vv. 67–69: a *punishment averted* — "had not a decree from God preceded, a great punishment would have touched you for what you took" — which shows that the *akhadh* has a *law*, and that the same mercy which writes the law can hold it back). And this is precisely why the next verse — the rule of the blessing (v. 53) — is set *between* the two Pharaoh-verses as the sūrah's hinge: no people, Mecca's or Medina's, may mistake the seizure for caprice, because both are to read *qawiyy, shadīd al-ʿiqāb* as the names of *justice*, not of power: power without justice would be *ẓulm* — and *ẓulm* the previous verse has just denied for God.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:53

> **That is because God never changes a blessing by which He blesses a people until they change what is in themselves, and because God is Hearing, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Is a Law of History — and a Door**

*Dhālika bi-anna Allāha lam yuġayyira niʿmatan anʿamahā ʿalā qawmin ḥattā yughayyirū mā bi-anfusihim wa-anna'llāha samīʿun ʿalīm* — the clause is the most quoted single sentence of the sūrah in the literature of *ʿibar* (lessons from history), and it deserves to be read in its three gears. It is first an *explanation*: "that is because" — the *illa* joining Pharaoh's *akhadh* (v. 52) to a general rule: the seizure was not lightning; it was *policy* — a change in the divine disposition of a people that *followed* their change in themselves. It is second a *law*: God *does not* (the negated past *lam yuġayyir* — in the Arabic's aspectual force, a settled, exceptionless manner) alter a blessing He has conferred *until* — *ḥattā* — the people first alter what is at the helm of themselves, the *mā bi-anfusihim*: the inner citadel of intention, gratitude, justice, courage. And it is third a *mercy*: read *de dicto*, it says that no blessing is ever withdrawn *ab initio* — every removal is *postponed* to a human condition, and the postponement is the opening the verse leaves: while the inner change has not been made, the outer gift can, in principle, return. Al-Rāzī's celebrated gloss on the *ʿāmm* sense (which the source commentary quotes) is the law's general anatomy: He gives intellect, strength, will, and the good things of life *that they may worship and do good*, and when these are turned toward sin, *the people have changed what is in themselves* — and the change of the blessing (into its removal, or into its *istidraj*-punishment) is the *consequence*, not the cause.

**The Tafsīr of the Occasion: Badr, Makkah, and the Years of Yūsuf**

The source commentary's *specific* application shows the law at work on the sūrah's own case: *God had blessed Quraysh by sending them a Prophet from themselves* — the report in al-Ṭabarī's tafsīr, and the tradition's own list of the Quraysh's *niʿam* reads like v. 53's footnote: the House (2:158's "God's sacred house"), the security (2:125's "whoever enters it is safe"; 106:4's "fed from hunger and made safe from fear"), the *two caravans* (106:1–2), and the kinship of the Prophet ﷺ — *until they changed what was in themselves*: expelled the mercy-sent, seized his property, funded their annihilation (v. 36's spending), and asked for stones (v. 32). The change of the blessing arrived as the *famine*: the ṣaḥīḥ report in al-Bukhārī, from Anas, of the Prophet's ﷺ duʿāʾ on the killing of the reciters — *"O God, I pray You make it like the years of Yūsuf"* (al-Bukhārī 1014; Muslim 897) — seven years' dearth on the Makkans, in which the sīrah and the tafsīr reports say they ate the hides and the carrion, and ʿAbdullāh ibn al-Zubayr's report in al-Bukhārī's tafsīr of the *Raʿd*-sūrah of the Prophet's ﷺ closing Maghrib prayer with *"they asked You for respite, so respite them not"* — the verse the commentators (Ibn ʿAbbās's report included) tie to Badr: *"do they not see that We come to the land, diminishing it at its edges?"* (13:41 — the *naqṣ* read by the salaf as the *killing of its notables and the fruitfulness leaving its soil*: the blessing's *change*, both edges of it, in one decade). The sīrah literature further reports that in the famine's teeth the Quraysh complained to the Prophet ﷺ that he had claimed to be "a mercy to the worlds" while his people perished — the mercy, on the verse's reading, had never been *withdrawn*; it had been *changed in them first*, and the pitiful echo is the law itself: a people can watch its blessing convert, clause by clause, into a warning, and still mistake the *ʿadhāb* for the *duʿāʾ's* malice rather than its *answer*.

**"Hearing, Knowing": the Two Names That Make the Law Safe**

That the verse closes *samīʿun ʼalīm* — with the *hearing* and *knowing* attributes — is the sūrah's own *keystone* of justice (the pair stood already at 8:17's beautiful trial, and will return at 8:42's map), and its function is to fence the law from its two misreadings. Misreading one, *fatalism*: "the blessing was *changed* — nothing can be done." The *samīʿ* answers: the people's *cries* — even the Makkan prayer-for-rain — are heard, and the verse's *ḥattā* has its *door* side: when they change what is in themselves *toward* obedience, the same law obligates (by God's own promise, 14:7 — *"if you are thankful, I will surely increase you"* — which is the *reverse image* of 8:53, the two verses being one proposition read forward and back: gratitude adds, ingratitude subtracts, and *the initiative of the move is always the creature's*). Misreading two, *presumption*: "God is *forced* by the condition." The *ʿalīm* answers: He knows the *sincerity* of every inner change — hearing the cry, knowing the chest (v. 43's *dhāt al-ṣudūr*) — so the *niʿmah* returns to the *penitent*, not to the *strategist*, and the *ʿadhāb* is never *bought off* by the lips. Justice, in the sūrah's court, is *a law administered by a listener* — and that is, the verse's whole history shows, at once its *terror* (no corruption escapes the rule; a whole empire's blessing is *one change of the heart* away from its *naqṣ*) and its *hope* (no corruption is *too far* gone: the rule waits at the *ḥattā*, and the *samīʿ* is already bending to the first, faint, "we will change").

**The Verse and the Community of the Spoils**

Why does the sūrah set this law *here*, between the two Pharaoh-file readings and before the treaty-laws? Because its first audience had, at Badr, *touched* the blessing's hinge personally: the sleep and water (v. 11) were *the blessing's presence*, the flinch and the dispute (vv. 1–6) *the change-in-themselves* — and the community that read *you would have faltered and quarreled* (v. 43's counterfactual) has read here the *law* of that counterfactual's real world: the *niʿmah* of the valley was *within one step* of *ghayr*, and the verse's *bi-anna* explains why it was *not* changed: because, at the end, they had *not* changed what was in themselves into *kufr*, only into *fear* — and fear *repaired* is not the change the *ḥattā* speaks of. Thus v. 53 functions simultaneously as *threat* and as *self-knowledge* for the ummah of Badr — and, for every ummah reading, as the constitutional clause of *collective accountability* that v. 25's fitnah-verse promised: *your* blessing is *your* inner state made visible, and the *samīʿ ʿalīm* is watching the *inner* first.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:54

> **[Theirs was] like the way of the House of Pharaoh and those before them; they denied the signs of their Lord, so We destroyed them for their sins, and drowned the House of Pharaoh, for all were wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Same File, Opened Again — the Rhetoric of Holy Repetition**

*Ka-daʾbi āli Firʿawna wa-man qablahum; kadhdhabū bi-āyāti rabbihim fa-ahlaknāhum bi-dhunūbihim wa-aghraqnā āla Firʿawn, wa-kullun kānū ẓālimīn* — the verse repeats v. 52's opening almost verbatim, and the repetition is itself the lesson: the sūrah reads the Pharaoh-file *twice* because the *daʾb* it records is the kind of thing that must be told twice before a heart will take it as *about itself*. Between the two readings stands the *law* (v. 53's change-of-blessing), so that the second reading is heard *through* the law: the *first* time, "God seized them" (52 — the *akhadh*, the *holding*, which in Arabic can be *arrested* at the last inch, as it was at Badr); the *second* time, "We *destroyed* them" (54 — the *ihlāk*, the ruin, the thing that does not stop short). The pair, with the hinge between, is the sūrah's complete grammar of divine response to denial: *seizure* is the warning's *name*; *destruction* is the warning's *failure*; and the difference between the two in this Book is always one *change of what is in themselves* (53).

**"Denied" — the Escalation from Kufr to Takdhīb**

Note the verb's shift between the twin verses: v. 52 said *kafarū bi-āyāti'llāh* — "they *covered* the signs"; v. 54 says *kadhdhabū bi-āyāti rabbihim* — "they *called them lies*". The *kufr* of Pharaoh's house had hardened into *takdhīb*, and the tafsīr tradition (from Ibn ʿAbbās's *tafṣīr*, and in al-Ṭabarī's chain on this verse) marks the distinction as the whole story of hardening: *kufr* is the refusal to *count* the sign; *takdhīb* is the sign counted and *pronounced false* — the second stage, in which the man's tongue edits the evidence into a lie, and after which the heart is, in the Qur'an's idiom, *sealed*. The verse's second change — from "signs *of God*" to "signs of *their Lord*" — deepens the charge: *rabb* is the name of *care*, the Lord who *raised* them (the root's *r-b-w* growth), the One whose *rubūbiyyah* Pharaoh himself had breathed under (28:9's "that he would comfort her eye — and she would *raise* him (*tarba*) — in the house of Pharaoh!"), and whose *tarbiyah* they *repaid* with *takdhīb*: the *ʿuqūq* of the foster-child against the foster-lord, the Qur'an's own later characterization of Pharaoh at drowning: *"now — you — and you refused before"* (10:91) and the *nūn*-man's 21:87's twin lesson read backwards: *lā ilāha illā anta ẓalamtu nafsī* — "I *wronged myself*" — which is 54's closing clause (*kullun kānū ẓālimīn*) in the mouth of the *savior* instead of the *drowned*.

**"So We Destroyed Them... and Drowned the House of Pharaoh" — the Sea as the Sūrah's Court**

The *ihlāk* is spelled with the *sea*, and the sea belongs in this sūrah for a reason the structure makes plain: Badr *was* a waterside judgment — the wells, the rain, the mud on Quraysh's ground (v. 11) — and the sūrah has *the drowning of āl Firʿawn* in its mouth to *complete the analogy*: the *mufsiyūn* — those who "drowned at the wells of Badr" — are named as the Quraysh's companions by the Prophet ﷺ's own reported line at the *well*: "are they better than Pharaoh and Haman and their army?" (cf. 40:24's *Firʿawn wa-Hāmān wa-Qārūn* verse, cited across the tafsīr of vv. 52–54 as the ummah's own reminder-parallel). And the sea's mode of *akhadh* — a *covering*, as the water *covered* what the sand at Badr's pit would later *cover* (*yaghshāhum*, the root of *mughshiyāt* at v. 11 and *taghshā* in 18:29's *fire's veil over their faces*), the drowning's *cover* being the mirror of the *kufr*'s name (the root *k-f-r*: *to cover*): the *one who covers* is *covered*: which is why the *aghraqnā* clause (We drowned) is not a mere *punishment detail* but the verse's *definition*: the *daʾb* of the *āli*-house is the *covering of what they had covered* — the sea, in the sūrah's physics, is the *concealment's return*, and *ẓālimīn* closes the file with the *ẓ-l-m* word that v. 51 denied for God (*laysa bi-ẓallām*): the *wronging* of the servants, i.e. their *own* wronging of *themselves* (the tradition's *ẓalmū anfusahum* — 16:28, 4:18, 14:27's *ẓālim* of the soul, the root's self-directed form) — which is the verse's final *fiqh*: no *ẓulm* fell on them but *their own*, and the *sea* is only where the *self-ẓulm* was *made visible*, as the *ditch* of Badr makes the *self-covering* of Quraysh.

**"Wa-Kullun Kānū ẓālimīn" — the Universal in the Particular**

The verse's closing clause — *and all were wrongdoers* — is a rule stated inside the narrative: Pharaoh drowned, and the *whole house* with him, because the house was *his*: the *kullun* answers every reader's instinctive "he alone was guilty." In this Book the court is the congregation's, and so the drowning is a nation's — which is why the tradition of commanding the good and forbidding the evil, with the ḥadīth of the ship already cited under v. 25 (al-Bukhārī 2493: the passengers below deck who drill their own hole drown the whole vessel, deck included), functions as v. 54's own gloss. The *kullun* is thus the sūrah's warning addressed to its *victors*: the *daʾb* of Pharaoh's house is a grammar of peoples, not a tyrant's biography. And immediately after, the sūrah turns from nations to persons (vv. 55–56: the worst of beasts, and the pact-breakers) so that no one finishes v. 54 without hearing its last clause as a question about his own covenant — because the same sūrah keeps repeating that God "wrongs no servant" (v. 51), and that He is *Hearing, Knowing* (v. 53): the sea closed on Pharaoh *after* his change of self was sealed; for the man who changes what is in himself *toward* God — even at the water's edge — the Hearing is still open.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:55

> **Truly the worst of beasts in the sight of God are those who have disbelieved and will not believe,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Verse Twenty-Two's Mirror — with One Clause That Closes the Door**

*Inna shiyra'd-dawābbi ʿinda'llāhi'lladhīna kafarū fahum lā yuʾminūn* — the sūrah repeats its earlier verdict (v. 22: the worst of beasts are the deaf and dumb who understand not) and adds, as its seal, a clause the first verse did not carry: *fahum lā yuʾminūn* — "and they will not believe." The repetition is structural: vv. 52–54's drowning nation is now classified, and the classification is the same as v. 22's *sharru'd-dawābbi* — and the added future-negative is the difference that makes this verse the Qur'an's most severe sentence on a manner of death: the unbelief that will not become faith. The commentators (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr) report the verse's occasion as the dead of Quraysh — those who died at Badr in their denial, the past tense of *kafarū* closing over them with the *lā yuʾminūn* of those upon whom the veil fell with the deed done: Pharaoh's moment ("now? and you refused before," 10:91) is the future-negative's prototype. The worst of beasts, then, is no longer (as in v. 22) a description of faculties wasted but of time expired: the beast who wasted his senses is pitiable; the beast who died mid-waste, the ledger printing its final page behind him, is the sūrah's most horrific portraiture.

**"Will Not Believe" — the Grammar of a Foreclosed Future**

The Arabic *fa-hum lā yuʾminūn* is an *imperfect* negation — present and, in the idiom of threat, *permanent*: they *are* the ones who *are-not-going-to-believe*. Two readings of its force run through the tafsīr, and the sūrah's own context (the eternal *ʿilm* of v. 23's "had God known good in them, He would have made them hear") favors the first: the negative is *God's knowledge of their end*, the sentence *describing* a foreclosed choice, not a door welded shut from outside — v. 23's whole structure (the *withholding* follows *their* settled turning, and "the turning would have been *their* turning") being the sūrah's *answer* to every reading that makes v. 55 a decree *without* a desert. Al-Rāzī's discussion of the verse adds the second reading as its *ethical* face: the *habit* that becomes the *nature* — the soul so trained in refusal that *not-believing* is its free state, its *second fitrah*, and the *lan* is *character* speaking: the Qur'an's own diagnosis of *rāna* (83:14: "no — but what they earned has *rusted* over their hearts") is this *lane*'s mechanism, and *khatm* (sealing) its image: the seal is *desert's photograph*, and *fahum lā yuʾminūn* is the photograph of a people whose *hearing* was *given*, *tested*, and *returned unopened* (vv. 21–23), and so the seal *is* their *own last signature*: which is why the verse's *sharr* (worst) — the superlative of v. 22 — is *earned* and not *assigned*: the beasts are *worst* not because their species is low, but because *their* species was *man*, and man who has *decided to stay shut* is lower than the open animal, the dog who *can* still be *tamed*.

**The Verse between the Nations and the Treaties**

The placement is thesised. The *drowned* have been the *nations* (52–54); the *next* verses (56–58) will descend to a *legal class* — the *pact-breakers* — and v. 55 is the *bridge*: its *lan yuʾminūn* describes the *inner* state that will *outer*-ize, in vv. 56–57, as the *every-time* breaking of oaths: *alladhīna ʿāhadata minhum thumma yanquḍūna ʿuhūdahum fī kulli marratin* — "they break their *covenant* at *every turn*" — the *kulla marra* being the *social* tense of *lan yuʾminūn*: the man who will not *keep faith with God* (his covenant with his own *fitrah*, the primordial *contract* of 7:172's *alastu bi-rabbikum*) ends in the man who cannot *keep faith with men*; and the sūrah, *which has legislated the honest division of spoils against its own army's appetite* (v. 1) and the honest *return of treaties* against its own statecraft's advantage (v. 58), thus gives its readers the *diagnosis* before the *policy*: *unbelief*, in this Book, is finally *verifiable as trustworthiness*, and the *worst of beasts* is the man in whom the *word* has died while the *mouth* still contracts. The hadith of the *three marks* (al-Bukhārī 33; Muslim 59) — the *nifāq* of the broken trust — reads back through this verse: the *khuṭṭa* of the hypocrite is *v. 55's lan* in *society's* grammar.

**"In the Sight of God" — the Reckoning's Eye on the Open Graves**

The *ʿinda'llāh* — in God's sight — keeps the superlative in the other register, and the valley itself illustrates it. The authentic report in al-Bukhārī and Muslim, from Anas, has the Prophet ﷺ three days after Badr standing at the pit of the slain chiefs, calling out: *"O so-and-so son of so-and-so, have you found what your Lord promised you to be true? I have found what my Lord promised me true."* ʿUmar asked: "O Messenger of God, do they hear? they are corpses!" — and he ﷺ said: *"You do not hear what they hear any better than these — except that they cannot answer"* (al-Bukhārī 3979; Muslim 2879). In the world's sight, those corpses were the lions of Arabia, whose names poets exalted and sons avenged; *in the sight of God* — the verse's register — they were the *lowest of what crawls*, and the ditch, not the elegy, was their *epitaph*. That is why v. 55 ends without fire-imagery at all (the Fire has its own verses: 37, 50): the *name* of the rank, in this verse, is simply *the verdict's location* — the One who sorted the heap (v. 37), who watched the rain (v. 11), who weighed every heart's *khayr* (v. 23) — is the only *witness* the superlative needs; and the world's *rankings*, reversed in that one *ʿayn*, are what the sūrah has been un-teaching its audience of since verse 1.

The *lan yuʾminūn*, then, binds the *worst-of-beasts* rank over the *drowned* — and the sūrah's very next verses legislate the *opposite* manner toward the living enemy: treaties to be honored, safe-conducts returned *on equal terms* (v. 58), ransoms and release as alternatives (v. 70's horizon) — a courtesy of the covenant toward men the verse has just classed with beasts. In the sūrah's structure, that juxtaposition is the argument: *we keep faith even with those whose faith we despair of — so that the covenant shows its Author, and the Author drowns no one who turns to Him at the water's edge*.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:56

> **those among them with whom thou madest a pact and who then break their pact every time, and who are not reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From the Drowned to the Border: the Verse's Referents**

*Alladhīna ʿāhadta minhum thumma yanquḍūna ʿuhūdahum fī kulli marrati wa-hum lā yattaqūn* — the *lan* of v. 55 is *defined*: the worst-of-beasts rank is *exemplified*, at the sūrah's own frontier, by the chronic *pact-breakers*: the tribes of the Madinan *concórdia* — the source commentary carries al-Qurṭubī's identification, the Banū *Naḍīr* and *Qurayẓa*, whose alliances (the *ṣaḥīfah*, the community-constitution the Prophet ﷺ wrote between the *muhājirūn*, the *anṣār* and the Jewish tribes of the city) they had *signed* and, in the tradition's accounting, *broke* in sequence — the Qurayẓa's betrayal at the Trench being the sūrah-era's climax, and the Naḍīr's the *expulsion* (59:2–4, whose *daʾb*-vocabulary is *this* verse's own: *fa-ʿakhsara ʿalayhā* — God "cast terror," the *rubʿ* of 8:12, against their palms and forts, and *al-khabāʾith* — "the foul ones" — was *flung into their hearts*, the very word of 8:37's heap). The verse, however, is *not* a tribal indictment kept for the archive; it is a *legal category*: *those among them* — the *min* is partitive, not total — and the category's *test* is *repetition*: *fī kulli marrah* — a *pact* broken *every time* it is made.

**"Every Time": the Jurisprudence of the Habitual**

Why the *kulli marrah* — and what does it *change*? The ethicists and jurists read the clause as the *ʿilla* — the legal cause — of the *harshness* the section will pronounce (v. 57): a single breach has a *reason* to be heard, a remedy and a renegotiation; a breach that is *customary* — the *daʾb* again (v. 52's word: the *way* a people *does* is the *way* they end) — has no *case* left, only a *character*: and character, in the law of nations as in the law of testimony, is *admissibility*: the *fāsiq* whose report the Qur'an commands to be *investigated* (49:6) is the man whose *habit* has eaten his *veracity*, and the chronic pact-breaker is his *inter-state* twin. The verse's own word-order makes the law: *yanquḍūna ʿuhūdahum fī kulli marratin* — *covenants* (ʿuhūd, plural of ʿahd), *every time*: not *the* pact broken *once*; *their pacts* — all the paper of them, the whole *portfolio* of their plighted word — broken *on every turn*. This is why v. 57 can, in a single word, legislate *deterrence* (see below): the *subject* of a treaty-law must be a *speaker* whose word can *bind*, and *kulli marrah* declares that the *mouth* in question no longer *has* a word to bind with: the *ʿahd* has become, in the enemy's mouth, a *weapon's scabbard* — drawn and returned by contract alone.

**"And They Revere Not" — the Root of the Whole Disorder**

*Wa-hum lā yattaqūn*: the clause is the sūrah's *diagnostic* — *taqwā* being, in the Book of this sūrah (vv. 1, 5, 29, 45), the *only* seat of *reliability*: the man who guards against God keeps his oaths *because* there is a *witness he cannot out-maneuver*, and the verse's negation removes the *seal* from the pact: *lā yattaqūn* means their covenants are written *in ink that only the two parties read* — and a *treaty* with a party who does not *fear a third* is, in the Book's politics, a *truce with a natural law*, kept only while kept *profitable*. The larger legislation of the Qur'an keeps the same balance: the pact with a people who have *no* *taqwā* is still a *pact* — 9:29 ends the hostile treaty-law with *protection in exchange for the jizyah* ("until they pay the tax willingly from their own hand"), and 9:6 commands *asylum* even for a fighting polytheist ("and if any of the polytheists seeks your protection, grant it to him, so that he may hear the word of God, then convey him to his place of safety") — so the verse is legislating not the end of treaties but the *end of naïveté* about them: the *ʿahd* is to be kept *until it is broken*, and the *kulli marrah* names the class of people toward whom *renegotiation*, not blind renewal, is the law; the *imām* must know *that* before he pledges, since every covenant re-made with a chronic breaker is a *weapon re-sheathed for the other hand*.

**"With Whom Thou Madest a Pact" — the Verse's Silence about Who Was Wrong First**

The most instructive feature of the verse's wording is what it does not say: it does not describe the first wrong. The Qur'an's narrative of the *ṣirāṭ* has elsewhere the history of the Naḍīr's assassination-plot (the millstone, with the report behind 59:4), of the Qurayẓa's secret alliance with the besiegers of the Trench, and of the measures that ended each — and here the verse confines itself to the fact of the breaking, which teaches, by structure, the law of the text: God's speech at the moment of legislation ignores the grievance; the judge of the pact sees only the breach and the habit of breach, because the ruling must be repeatable by any court, and grievances are not evidence, only motives. The tradition's ethic of the *ʿahd* with pact-breaking peoples is thus not revenge (the revenge-tenses of the *sīrah*'s enemies are left in the *sīrah*) but the removal of the veil of trust: the quarter-hour of v. 57 is deterrence, and deterrence is the only lawful policy when good faith is impossible — a jurisprudence the modern reader of treaties, sanctions, and international law will find this verse's *kulli marrah* clause had anticipated: the state that repeatedly violates its own signatures is read out of the covenant-community, and its pacts treated, as this verse treats them, as documents of war in waiting — while the individuals under it are, as the next verses will insist, not punished except for what their own hands sent ahead (v. 51's *bi-mā qaddamat aydīkum*). The whole sūrah's criminal law in one line: the habit forfeits trust; it never forfeits justice.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:57

> **So if thou comest upon them in war, use them to scatter those who will come after them, that haply they might be reminded.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Law of the Example, Stated with Its Limits Attached**

*Fa-imma tathqafannahum fi'l-ḥarbi fa-farriq bi-him man khalfahum laʿallahum yadhdhakkarūn* — "so if you come upon them *in war*, disperse, *by means of them*, those behind them, so that perhaps they will remember." The verse's verb is *farraqa* — to scatter, disperse a crowd; the *them* of the scattering is the *pact-breakers caught in the field*; and the *those behind them* are, in the exegetes' consensus (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī all transmit the sense), the *future* enemies: the *punishment* of the breach is *made to reach* the onlookers *as news*, so that the *next* tribe *thinks twice* before its *first* breach. The law is *deterrence*, stated in one sentence — and the sentence's every limit must be read with it: it operates *fi'l-ḥarb* — *in war*, i.e. on the *battlefield*, against *combatants taken there* — not in the market, not in the truce, not against envoys or the disarmed (the sūrah's own law of the *thrown-back treaty* in v. 58 governs *outside* the field, and the Prophet's ﷺ settled practice forbids the killing of the *ahd*-keeper who has *not* fought: the *ʿahd* of the *dhimma*-people and the *amān* of the *mustaʾmin* are untouched by this verse, and the *siyar* literature (al-Shaybānī's *al-Siyar al-kabīr* in its chapters on oaths) is unanimous that v. 57 speaks of *the enemy already in the field*). The *laʿalla* — *so that perhaps they remember* — then *bounds the purpose*: the punishment's *telos* is *not* blood, not terror for its own end, but *dhikr*: the *fear* is licensed *as a teaching instrument*, and an instrument whose *purpose* is the enemy's *heedfulness* is measured, in the law, by *how much fear teaches and how much hardens* — the fuqahā's whole *taʿzīr* (discretionary-penalty) proportionality-rule, whose *fiqh* maxims (no punishment beyond deterrence's need, no excess that *turns* the lesson into *rancor*) are v. 57's clause read as a *constitution*.

**Deterrence in the Qur'an's Penal Philosophy**

V. 57 belongs, with the *wont of the ancients* of v. 38 and the *seizure* verses (52–54), to the Qur'an's *penology of the visible*: its punishments are *made to be seen* because their *object* is the *living audience*: the Māʾidah verses on the highway-robber close *"as exemplary punishment from God, and God is Mighty, Wise"* (5:38 — *nakālan mina'llāh*, the very *nakāl*-idiom the sūrah's idiom of *deterrent* shares), and the famous *qiṣāṣ* clause *"and in retaliation there is life for you, O people of minds"* (2:179) is the *principle*'s clearest statement — *death* as *law* for the *sake of life* in the whole body, the *terror of the consequence* made to *work pre-crime*: v. 57 is 2:179 in *inter-state* register: *let the breaking be fatal to their *calculation*, so that *peace* is what the *other* tribes *choose, *without needing to be fought into*. The verse's *fiqh* then reads the *whole sūrah's* criminal theory: punishment is *forward-facing* (its clause is *man khalfahum*, "those *behind* them"), and *backward* only as *justice's ledger* (v. 51: *bi-mā qaddamat aydīkum*, the *hands'* own forwards, which no *exemplary* act may *multiply*); so the *exemplary* may not *use* a man for an *example* beyond *his own deed's weight* — the *ʿadl* that bounds the *radʿ*. Ibn Kathīr notes, under the verse, the Prophet's ﷺ application at the *Qurayẓa*, and the tradition's *own* care: the *ʿamr* of their sentence was executed *by the ḥakam the tribes themselves accepted* (Saʿd ibn Muʿādh's *ḥukm* — "your blood is on your *own* hands" being the *ʿahd's own clause*, and the two Ṣaḥīḥs' account of the *proceeding*, al-Bukhārī 4096, Muslim 1768, is the *case-report* of v. 57: *war-field, breached pact, judgment by agreed arbiter, the lesson to the tribes of Aws and Khazraj* — every element of the verse's structure present, and no element of *whim*.

**"That Haply They Might Be Reminded" — the Verse's Mercy-Verb**

*Dhikr*, remembrance, is the sūrah's master-word of its positive program (v. 45's *dhkurū'llāha kathīran*), and v. 57's purpose-clause links deterrence to it: the lesson is *dhikr*, and *dhikr* is return — the pact-breakers' peers are not to be crushed into silence but recalled to the sanctity of the pledge. This means that the punishment which hardens — driving the next tribe into desperate enmity — fails the verse's own condition and is, by the *laʿalla* ("perhaps"), not the use of the licence: the tradition's rule that the commander's act of war is judged by its *maṣlaḥa*, its public good, and not by its rage, together with the prophetic prohibition of *tamthīl* (mutilation) even of the enemy fighter — with the Prophet's ﷺ charge to his dispatches: "do not hoard the spoils, do not mutilate, do not kill a child" (Muslim 1731) — is this verse read as war-ethics: the lesson is to be carried in the mind, not nailed into the flesh, which is the only way the frightening of the rear ranks can end in their remembrance rather than in v. 55's permanent no. The hope of the *laʿalla* is the horizon of the policy: v. 57 is the only deterrence-verse in scripture whose own grammar admits failure — a law that knows hearts are not machines. Fear may teach and fear may corrupt, and so the verse, read whole, hands the judge's deterrence a ceiling (enough to teach, never more) and the politician's fear a floor (the pact's word kept, so that the fright is justice's and not treachery's).

**The Verse for the Believer, Not Only the General**

The sūrah also reads, at the individual level, against the same architecture. The habitual breaker of *ʿuhūd* is, in the community's mirror of v. 56, the man whose oaths and trusts (*amānāt*, v. 27) have become *every-time* breaches, and the society's lawful response is the same verb: to *scatter him from the ranks of trust* — testimony suspended (49:6's verification of the *fāsiq*'s report), partnership dissolved, the *hijr* (boycott) that the early community practiced exactly so that *those behind* the offender might *remember*. The purposes of both remedies are stated in the *laʿalla*, and this is the verse's deepest rule for the individual too: *the sanction ends where the reminder lands*. A boycott that has achieved *dhikr* has, by v. 57's own tense, achieved its *limit* — the *farriq* was never for the *scatterer's* satisfaction but for the *scattered's* return — which is why the tradition's ethic of *hijr* keeps its duration short, its scope to the *deed*, and its door, in every book of *adab* that legislated it, open from the first day.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:58

> **And if thou fearest treachery from a people, withdraw from them in a just way. Truly God loves not the treacherous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Makes the Treaty a Covenant and Not a Trick**

*Wa-imma takhāfa min qawmin khiyānatun fa-nbidh ilayhim bi-sawāʾ* — "and if you fear from a people a treachery, then throw it back to them on equal terms." The verse legislates the *end* of a covenant, and its genius is that it legislates the ending as a *moral* act — because in war's grammar the stronger party's only temptation at a treaty's end is to convert the ending into an ambush: to answer the *khiyāna* one *fears* with the *khiyāna* one *commits*. The Qur'an's two verbs forbid exactly this. *Takhāfa* — *fear*, not *know*: the trigger is reasonable suspicion of betrayal (the fīqh's standard is *ʿalāmāt*, open signs — arms massing, alliance with the besiegers, as the sūrah's own case-book of the Qurayẓa shows), not certainty, since certainty would need no warning, only war — and even then the feared betrayal does *not* license the performed one. *Fa-nbidh*: the root *n-b-ḍ* is the casting-away of a thing — the *nabīdh* of dregs poured out — and the image is the treaty itself *tossed back over the wall* to its other owner; *bi-sawāʾ* — *on a level, in equality*: the source's note is exact that the phrase means *so that knowledge of the end is equal between the two sides, as the beginning of the pact was equal*. The ʿahd is not *broken*; it is *dissolved on equal terms*: a return to the pre-treaty state *with notice*, so that whatever sword follows is fought against the *breach*, not against the enemy's un-warned sleep.

**"In a Just Way": the Law's Own Definition of Fair Play**

The jurists' distinction, preserved by al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī in the source's gloss, is the operative clause: if the *other side* has *already* broken *openly*, notice is moot — they have ended the treaty with their own hands, and v. 57's *fi'l-ḥarb* applies — but if the breaking is only *feared*, the withdrawal must be *public, plain, and prior*. The sūrah of Barāʾah legislates the same rule at the scale of an era: 9:1–4's *announcement* of dissolution — "a proclamation from God and His Messenger to the people on the Great Day" — with its four-month respite, is v. 58 projected onto history, and the word *nabḍ* itself is used there (9:4: *"then complete for them their term and do not hasten them"* follows *"then set them free and throw (fa-nbidhū) their covenants"*-logic in the same idiom). The verse thus gives the *end* of a treaty a *liturgy of openness*: the same hour for both courts, the same terms as the making had, and the making's own *riʾāʾ*-law (v. 47's inverse: the Muslim's acts are done *before God*, seen or unseen) running through it — he may *end* in public because his covenant was *made* in God's name, and the *sawāʾ* is the Name's final protection of the *other party's dignity as a party*, kept to the treaty's last line. The verse's own tafsīr by praxis is preserved in the reports of Aḥmad and al-Ḥākim at the eve of Makkah's conquest, when the treaty's end was announced before its sword was drawn: the Prophet ﷺ sent ʿAlī after Abū Sufyān with the proclamation — *"We throw back to you your covenant on equal terms; the day of blood will be such-and-such"* — the formula of *nabḍ bi-sawāʾ* enacted to the letter: even the doomed city's master received notice on the level before the march, because in this Book's law a covenant begins in words and *ends* in words.

**"Indeed, God Loves Not the Treacherous" — the Crime Against the Joined Hearts**

The closing clause — *inna'llāha lā yuḥibbu'l-khāʾinīn* — does more than threaten: it names the crime's opposite within the sūrah's own attribute-system. Two verses ahead, the sūrah will tell the community that God *joined its hearts* (v. 63: *"Had you spent all that is on the earth, you could not have joined their hearts, but God joined them"*) — and covenant is *heart-joining made law*: the ʿahd is the ummah's love's outer garment. *Khiyāna* is therefore not merely *unwise* (the v. 56 lesson, that the pact-breaker's class forfeits renewal) but *un-loved*: it is the exact *anti-v. 63*. Where *nabḍ bi-sawāʾ* keeps the other's dignity even in parting — for *equality in knowledge is love's residue inside law* — *khiyāna* steals his dignity for an advantage, and turns the *word* itself into a *weapon's scabbard*. The love-verses are the mīzān of the sūrah's whole fiqh ("*God loves the reverent*," 9:4; "*loves the doers of justice*," 5:42), and this verse sets treachery on the un-loved side of the scale — which places the sin, in the sūrah's own psychology of belief (vv. 2–4), where it places all its disasters: not as the loss of an advantage, but as the distance it opens between the soul and the Love whose name is the community's only glue.

**The Verse and Later Law — and Where the Verse Stands Beyond It**

The modern reader of the law of nations — whose treaty-system rests on *pacta sunt servanda*, and whose rules of denunciation, cooling periods, and prior notice read, at first glance, like v. 58's paraphrase — should note where the verse stands *beyond* that law: the conventions' notice satisfies the *treaty's terms*; the verse's *sawāʾ* must satisfy a *love* — no advantage may be taken of the un-warned *spirit*, down to the last clause's intent, and the fuqahā's scrupulousness (*taḥarrī*) forbade even the lawful edge when it *bit*. The sūrah's own sequence (vv. 25, 46) makes the verse a law of communal survival: a cause that ends its covenants openly owns a word that out-lives its wars; a cause that ends them ambush-wise teaches the world that its words are scabbards — and a nation that has taught every possible ally *that* lesson has, before its next war, already lost the one weapon that fights without being drawn. The verse's last mercy is its realism: *takhāfa* concedes that *feared* treachery must be *acted on* — the community is not asked to be *stupidly* faithful, to keep arming the hand that will strike it — it is asked to be *just while leaving*: to *throw*, not to *slip away*; to *level*, not to *lure*. In the sūrah's arithmetic of the wind (v. 46), the pacts are the ummah's *rīḥ*: and a just exit from a dead treaty buys a *new* alliance cheaper than the ambush that would have saved one campaign.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:59

> **And let not those who disbelieve suppose that they have outstripped [anyone]. Indeed, they thwart nothing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Race the Fugitives Think They Are Winning**

*Wa-lā yaḥsabanna'lladhīna kafarū sabaqū* — "let not the disbelievers reckon that they have *outstripped*." *Sabq* is the racers' word: the horse that gains the lead, the runner who *gets ahead of pursuit* — and the verse catches the exact temptation of the defeated-but-alive: the survivor of Badr (the *ran* men, the remnant that made Makkah) and every later tyrant who *escaped* the field computes his escape as *success*: "we got away; our treasury still fills, our walls still stand, the next raid will repay the last." The Qur'an's *lā yaḥsabanna* — the emphatic *lā* plus the *nūn* of solemn warning — forbids not a deed but a *reckoning*: the arithmetic of the *ʿājiz* (the word is the horse that *fails to win*; "they will not frustrate" — *lā yuʿjizūn*: they shall not *render God impotent*, the idiom of the pursuer outrun, of the quarry beyond reach). The clause's force, as the source's list of twins shows (9:2–3; 11:20; 24:57; 29:22; 35:44; 42:31; 46:32; 72:12 — the same sentence said of every *nājā*, every *fugitive* cause), is a permanent law with a simple proof: *the pursuer here is Omnipotence*; no race has ever been won against Him; the delay of the *akhadh* (vv. 52–54's *akhdh*) is *not the lap's end* but its *extension*. Al-Rāzī notes the reading some give — "*do not think*, O believers, that the disbelievers have outstripped" — which makes the verse an *anti-despair* clause as much as an anti-presumption one: the community watching its enemy's prosperity recover after defeat is told: his *ʿajz* and his *sabq* are the *two* illusions of the *same* ledger — God's account runs *elsewhere*: *"and those who disbelieve — their running (saʿy) is in vain"* (14:18), and 6:10's "We have turned their hearts and their eyes away, as they disbelieved in it *the first time*, and We drive them in their trespassing blindly on" — the *saʿy* itself, in the *ʿājiz*-world's leg, becomes *blindness* — and the verse's *ʿa-ja-z* root's other meaning (impotence, incapacity) is the point made twice: *the one who seems to flee is he who is, here, being rendered unable*, and the only "thwarting" is God's *ḥilm* (forbearance) *thwarting* their own *ʿuqūb*.

**The Verse Read Against the Believers' Own Fear**

Why does the sūrah place this *against presumption* clause precisely where the community's next steps (v. 60's preparation, v. 61's peace-overtures) are about to be legislated? Because the *rīḥ* (v. 46's wind) is, for the *believers*, a *fear*-term: the small community that saw Quraysh's remnant recover its nerve after Badr — mustering, trading, rebuilding — was *tempted by the very arithmetic v. 59 forbids the enemy*: "they have outstripped; our victory was an hour; their capacity is the years." The verse is thus the *tawḥīd of results* applied at the moment of *policy*: the enemy's *sabq* is *illusion*, and therefore *preparation* (v. 60) is *commanded* — for *illusion* is *fought*, and *ʿajz* is *proven by the fight* — but *fear* (the *rubʿ*-logic of v. 12) is *reserved to God*, so that the *policy* is never *dictated* by the *size of the enemy's hope*. The twin hadith belongs to the *same* hour's mood in the sūrah: the *ḥadīth*-line on the enemy's *ʿajz* is 9:14's *"fight them — God will punish them by your hands, disgrace them, give you victory over them, and heal the breasts of a believing people"*: the *healing* of the *watchers'* anxiety (v. 49's *ghurr* spectators) is a *war-aim* the verse places before the *spoil*, exactly as v. 59's *lā yaḥsabanna* — the *re-education of the eye* — stands before the whole foreign-policy sequence.

**"Indeed, They Thwart Nothing": the Theology of the ʿAjz**

The *wa-mā yuʿjizūn* clause carries, in the sūrah's own system, a whole *ʿaqīdah* of divine power vs. creature-escape, the *samīʿun ʿalīm* (v. 53) and *muhīt* (v. 47) having just fenced it: the One who *hears* and *encompasses* has no *mafrūq*: *"you cannot reach across the earth, nor scale the heavens for escape"* (45:37's boast of Iblīs answered, the root of *makr* and *ajz* the same); and *"do they not see that God, who created the heavens and the earth and did not tire of their creation, is Able to give life to the dead?"* (46:33) — the *ʿajz*-clause of creation, in the verse's logic, is *a fortiori*: the *Maker* of the race-track cannot be *outrun on it*. Which is why the clause's *grammatical* *ʿājizīn* (indefinite, plural of the *active*-form — "they are *incapacitated* ones," not "God *incapacitates* them") is the tradition's *delight*: *incapacity is their quality, not their event*; God's *ḥilm* leaves them the *illusion of speed*, and the verse's *warning not to reckon* is *the warning against adopting their measurement of the world*: every *ʿizz* that *measures by the track* — the economy's *out-stripping*, the army's, the dynasty's, the *narrative's* — has the *ʿajz* written into its *own verb*: *sabaqū* is *the tense of the vanquished who do not yet know they are the prey*, and the *sūrah*, from Pharaoh's *drowning* (v. 54) to Badr's *ditch* (v. 55's *ʿinda*), is the *proven record* of what the *next verse after the reckoning* brings.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:60

> **And prepare for them what you can of strength [of arms] and horses tethered [for battle], frightening thereby the enemy of God and your enemy, and others besides them whom you know not. God knows them. Whatsoever you spend in the way of God shall be paid unto you in full. And you shall not be wronged.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command of the Arsenal: Readiness as a Trust**

*Wa-aʿiddū lahum mistaṭaʿtum min quwwatin wa-min ribāṭi'l-khayl* — "and *prepare* for them whatever you *can* of strength." The verb from *ʿ-d-d* — the counting-out, the laying ready — makes *readiness itself* an act of worship, and the sūrah's only general command on military preparedness is at once the *ceiling* of that command: *mistaṭaʿtum* — *what is within your capacity*. The duty is the maximum of ability, not a standard of impossibility; no despair is legislated for the gap between *what you can* and *what the foe has*, because the verse hands the outcome, as the whole sūrah has, to the *ʿazīz* (v. 60's *quwwah* is *sought*; the *quwwah* that decides is *His*). And the object is left *deliberately undefined*: *quwwah* — strength — is not specified as sword, or lance, or wall, and the jurists made the openness into a method. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī's transmitted gloss — "strength is the arms of war" — answered its age's inventory; and the celebrated question of al-Walīd ibn Muslim to the great jurist al-Awzāʿī — "does *strength* mean *only archery*?" — drew the reply that made this verse the constitution of military science in Islam: *"Every device that strikes fear into the enemy, even stratagem and cunning."* Whatever a generation *knows* as decisive belongs to the *ʿuddah*; the verse arms the *knowledge*, not just the arm.

**Ribāṭ: Horses Tethered to God, and the Hour That Outweighs the Month**

*Wa-min ribāṭi'l-khayl* — *the horses tethered*: *ribāṭ* from *r-b-ṭ*, the binding, the horse tied at the frontier-post — and the word's fortune in Muslim history (the *ribāṭ*-houses of the coasts, the colleges later wearing the name, the *murābiṭūn* whose discipline took their title from the same rope) began in this clause's sanctification of *stabling as devotion*: the animal's feed and dung entered the owner's scale of good deeds, in the Prophet's ﷺ line: *"the horse is for three: for one a reward, for one a cover, for one a burden"* (al-Bukhārī 2841; Muslim 1872) — the *niyyah* deciding the beast's weight — and the *hour* of the watch was priced beyond the *month* of the mosque: *"an hour of vigil at the frontier is better than a month's fasting and praying"* (al-Bukhārī 2842; Muslim 1913, from Sahl ibn Saʿd). The logic fits the sūrah's whole psychology of the line (vv. 15–16, 45): the *held* peace is *also* a liturgy — the *qiyām* of the *ribāṭ* is the *watch* itself — and the prophetic exhortation to the *arsenal's* craft belonged to the verse's own practice: *"shoot, sons of Ismāʿīl — for your father was an archer"* (al-Bukhārī 2899, the call at the expedition when the arrows ran out), and the Prophet's ﷺ law of preparation extended to the *children of the next war*: the riding, the swimming, the shaft — each an act of *ʿibādah* by the verse's own economics.

**"Frightening the Enemy of God and Your Enemy, and Others You Know Not"**

The verse's *purpose* clause is the most disciplined piece of strategy-revelation in scripture: *tah rabūna bihi ʿaduwwa'llāhi wa-ʿaduwwakum wa-ākhharīna min dūnihim lā taʿlamūnahum, Allāhu yaʿlamuhum* — the aim of the *ʿuddah* is *fear*, not *slaying*: the *rubʿ* that was heaven's own weapon at Badr (v. 12: *"I shall cast terror"*) is now *delegated to the tethered horse*: the arsenal exists to be *read* by the enemy, not merely *used* against him — a doctrine of *deterrence* stated fourteen centuries before the word. It reaches, by the clause's design, past the known frontier to *"others beyond them you know not"* — which the tafsīr tradition reads generously (al-Ṭabarī: the hypocrites within the ummah; others: the far powers, Persia and Byzantium; and even the unseen enmity of the jinn) — so that the community is *never* to be *un*-shielded, because it is *never* without an *unknowable* threat; and the clause's seal — *God knows them* — is the *guard-rail*: your task is *visible readiness*; the *invisible* enemy's file remains with the *ʿAlīm*, so that vigilance never decays into swinging at every suspicion. The *ḥadīth-law of fear's Owner* stays beside the verse to keep it honest — *"you did not kill them, but God killed"* (v. 17), and the angels' *ridāfa* at Badr's wells was the same *rubʿ* — so the believer *prepares* strength and *leans* on none of it: *deterrence* as *dhikr* with the hands.

**"Whatever You Spend Shall Be Repaid in Full — and You Shall Not Be Wronged"**

The verse's tail turns the arsenal into a ledger of *ṣadaqah*: *fa-mā nafaqtum min shayʾin fa-yuwaffīkumuhu wa-antum lā tuzlamūn* — the *same verb of spending* as v. 3's *yusīfūna* and v. 36's *counter-spend* of Quraysh, now *promised repayment in full*, the war-economy annexed to the *economy of grace*: what the treasury feeds to the *ribāṭ* returns to the spender *undiminished*, and the promise's *wafāʾ* (exact measure) answers the sūrah's oldest wound — the men at v. 1 who *feared* the division would cheat them: here is *the* division whose math God guarantees. And the *lā tuzlamūn* — you are *not wronged* — closes the verse where v. 51 closed God's court: the *ẓulm* the verse denies for God is the *reason* the spending is *safe*: the soldier's hour, the smith's dinar, the horse's oats are *not lost* to *inflation* in heaven's ledger. Finally, the verse's second, interior field, which the allegorical readers of this sūrah never neglect: the *enemy you know not* is, in every man, *the vice in his own breast* (v. 43's *dhāt al-ṣudūr* — secret *riʾāʾ*, hidden *ʿujb*, the *hypocrite within the self*), and the *quwwah* to be *prepared* against him is the *ḥadīth's* own definition of the *mujāhid*: *"the striver is the one who struggles his own soul in God's obedience"* (al-Tirmidhī 1621, from Ṭalq ibn ʿAlī) — while the *tethered horse* is the *nafs* itself, *bound at the frontier* of the heart, which is why the mystics' spiritual cell took the very *ʿuddah*-verse's name: *ribāṭ* — the soul tied to its Lord, *on watch*, *at the line where the unseen enemy might ride*. The verse that armed a nation, armed the hermit with the same rope; and the *repayment in full* is promised, indifferently, to both.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:61

> **And if they incline toward peace, incline thou toward it, and trust in God. Truly He is the Hearing, the Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Camel's Kneeling: the Word for Peace**

*Wa-in janāhu li'l-ṣalḥi fa-jnaḥ laha wa-tawakkal ʿalā Allāh* — "if they *incline* toward reconciliation, *you* incline toward it, and trust in God." The root *j-n-ḥ* is the camel's folding of its legs to let the load down or the rider mount — the beast of burden *settling* — and the Qur'an chose, for the arrival of peace, the most physical image of a resting weapon. The verse's placement is its own doctrine: the command of the arsenal (v. 60) stands immediately before this command of the truce, and the pairing is the whole theory: *prepare*, and *be ready to kneel*. The sīrah's occasion, as the commentators report, was Ḥudaybiyah (6/628), when the ten-year truce was being cut with Suhayl ibn ʿAmr's pen and ʿUmar's grieving objection — the very tension the verse was revealed to resolve. *Janāḥ* requires only *inclination* from the other side — not sincerity, not love, not justice — and the minimum of the enemy's will becomes the maximum of the believer's duty, because the duty runs to the *offered word*, not to the *hidden heart*.

**"And Trust in God": the Clause That Holds the Two Verbs Together**

The *tawakkul* is the verse's safety-valve against both fears that ambush every peacemaker. The first fear: that the peace is a trap — answered by the next verse ("and if they seek to deceive you — God is sufficient for you," v. 62): the suspected deception is met not by rejecting the offer but by handing its outcome to God, so the text itself parses the hard case and forbids it from paralyzing the rule. The second fear: that the kneeling is weakness — answered by *al-ḥasb* (God's sufficiency) and by the *ʿazīz* the sūrah keeps naming: the one who trusts the Mighty kneels *without falling*; the camel's legs fold and the load remains on the saddle. Truce, in this sūrah's grammar, is not disarmament of the will but re-parking of force — and the closing names, *Hearing, Knowing*, are the treaty's two courts: He hears the words by which the pact is kept *here*, and knows the intentions by which it will be judged *there*. The law of reconciliation follows the verse exactly: *"Reconciliation between Muslims is valid, except a reconciliation that makes the lawful unlawful or the unlawful lawful"* (al-Tirmidhī 1352; Abū Dāwūd 3594, from Ṣukhr al-ʿAdawī) — peace is presumptively valid, and the burden of exception rests on the *terms*, not on the *suspicion of the mood*.

**The Verse That Made a Conquest Out of a Truce**

ʿUmar's grief at Ḥudaybiyah — the humiliation of the terms, the return of refugees, the year lost at the House's gate — is the tradition's own foil for this verse: the companions *saw* concession; God *saw* the opening. Within two years the truce's open roads carried the call through all the Hijaz, and the ummah that could not count its fighters marched on Makkah and took the city with almost no blood shed in it — and the sura named for that campaign (48) called the kneeling itself *fatḥān mubīnā*, "a manifest victory" (48:1). Al-Qurṭubī's argument against the abrogation of this verse — the practice, not just the text: the Prophet ﷺ and the early caliphs accepted the *jizyah* and left peoples in being "though able to destroy them," and the treaty-law's whole edifice (the *hudna* of ten years after the Prophet's ﷺ own term at Ḥudaybiyah, the truces renewed by the Umayyads and the ʿAbbāsids alike) stands under *in jānḥū*: the clause is *conditional*, not temporal, so no "later sword-verse" cancels a command whose trigger ("if they incline") can occur in any hour. A peace-law that abrogates *when the enemy kneels* is not a law at all — which is precisely why the verse, uniquely in the *qitāl*-ayat, legislates the *end* and not only the *means*.

**For the Believer's Own Reconciliations**

The sūrah opened with *iṣlāḥ* between brothers (v. 1); v. 61 closes its external law with the same verb of *ṣulḥ* at the scale of states, so that the believer learns one habit in both classes of case: accept the offered end of a quarrel — from a brother, from a neighbor, from an enemy — even while suspecting the offer is not pure, because the *act of kneeling* is itself obedience, and the *tawakkul* converts humiliation into strategy. The hadith-law of v. 1's reconciliation exceeds "the rank of prayer and fasting"; this verse's reconciliation exceeds the rank of victory. And the *Hearing* who is invoked over both is the one whose ears the treaty's first draft fell on — which is why the classical jurists, reading v. 61 with v. 58's *nabḍ*, could make the community kneel to every honest peace and never to the enemy's convenience twice: *kneel when they kneel; and let God keep the ledger of why they kneel*.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:62

> **And if they desire to deceive thee, then God suffices thee. He it is Who supports thee with His Help, and with the believers,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Suspected Peace Is Still to Be Made**

*Wa-in yurīdū an yagharrūka fa-inna ḥsabaka Allāh* — "and if they *want to deceive* you — God is your *ḥasb*: sufficient." The verse finishes v. 61's law by closing the one loophole fear would open: the enemy's peace-overture is to be accepted *even when his intention is suspected*, and the suspicion gives no license to refuse the offered word. The root *gh-r-r* — *ghurūr*, delusion (v. 49's *gharrarahum dīnu-hum*; 57:20's "the enjoyment of delusion") — is the sūrah's own idiom for the mis-weighing of appearances, and the deceiver's whole art is to make a poisoned hour look sound. The believer's remedy is not to unmask the hour before kneeling — no one can — but to place the hour with the One who is never deceived: *fa-inna ḥsabaka Allāh*. The *ḥisb*-formula is the same one 3:173 fixed in the community's mouth at Ḥamrāʾ al-Asad, the day after Badr, when the drums of pursuit sounded and the wounded camp answered *"ḥasbunā Allāhu wa-niʿma al-wakīl"* and the enemy turned back without a fight (al-Bukhārī 4676, on 54:45's revelation that day): the verse re-arms the treaty with the weapon that *won without bloodshed* — sufficiency. The jurists read the case exactly this way in the *siyar* chapters (al-Shaybānī; al-Sarakhsī): *suspicion does not dissolve the treaty's bond — only the enemy's overt act does*; suspicion's lawful fruits are vigilance, and v. 58's *nabḍ* once signs ripen — never the preemptive ambush that would answer *ghurūr* with *ghurūr* and make the Book's own law self-eating.

**"He Supports Thee with His Help, and with the Believers"**

*Huwa alladhī ayyadaka bi-naṣrihi wa-bi'l-muʾminīn* — the verse proves the *ḥisb* promise with Badr's record and its two-preposition grammar is a creed in miniature: God supports *with His help* (the angels, the sleep, the rain — the direct aid no counsel can engineer) *and with the believers* (the mediated means: the men who tether the horses and pay the spend of v. 60). Al-Rāzī's gloss in the source's note marks the balance: the *ḥisb* is not passivity, because the same God who aids directly aids *through the community* — so the believers are part of the sufficiency, not a substitute for it. That is why the next verse will tie *their* hearts before anyone is asked to lean on them (v. 63), and why the verse's support of the *Prophet* is support of the *office*: the head of the community who makes a risky peace is not trusting his own cleverness but the doubled aid — heaven's *rubʿ* and the earth's ranks — that this sūrah has itemized since v. 9. The treaty that survives a deceiver survives precisely because it is backed twice: if the hidden intention breaks the human side, the divine side remains the *ḥisb*; if the divine side were all, the *ʿuddah* (v. 60) would be forbidden — and it is commanded.

**The Verse for Every Negotiating Believer**

Three rulings the commentators distill for practice. First: do not refuse a real offer on an imagined intention — v. 62 is the revelation's veto of the preemptive cynicism that makes every future peace impossible because "they may be deceiving": the law requires *answering the overture as an overture*, not as a verdict on hearts; the *ʿālim bi'l-ḥalāʾ wa'l-amlāk* (4:29's title at the sūrah's other treaty-law, v. 72's end: "God sees what you do") keeps the file of intentions. Second: the treaty-maker's *tawakkul* is strategic depth — the state that must play every card at once has not believed in *al-ḥasīb*; and the *suspicion* the verse licenses against itself is answered, not by breaking the peace, but by v. 58's *nabḍ bi-sawāʾ*: fair notice, on the level, if signs grow — the three verses (60, 61, 62) together spell the *grammar* of a sacred statecraft: *arm, kneel, trust* — the third term sanctifying the second so the first never becomes the idol v. 59 forbids worship of. Third: the believer's private peacemaking follows the same sequence — accept your brother's truce though you suspect the timing is his convenience, "for God suffices you," and if he breaks *openly*, the covenant itself has already *thrown itself back*; you need not out-deceive him, and the sūrah that began by teaching a camp to let go of its spoils (v. 1) will, in this verse, teach the same camp to let go of the *superiority of cunning*: the treaty is a *ʿahd*, and "God loves not the treacherous" (v. 58) stands over the deceiver's side of it equally: *ḥisb* is the *believer's* protection, not his weapon.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:63

> **and joined their hearts. Hadst thou spent all that is on the earth, it would not have joined their hearts. But God joined them together. Truly He is Mighty, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Miracle the Sūrah Keeps for the Ledger of Grace**

*Wa-allafa bayna qulūbihim* — "and He joined their hearts." The sentence continues v. 62's list of God's supports: He backed you with His help and with the believers — *and what joined those believers, you did not and could not make*. Then the verse's great counterfactual, one of the Qur'an's most quoted lines on the limits of wealth and power: *law anzafta mā fi'l-arḍi jmaʿan allafa bayna qulūbihim wa-lākinna Allāha allafa baynahum* — "had you spent all that is on the earth, you could not have joined their hearts; but God joined them." *Taʾlīf* is the verb of "classification" in Arabic too (taʾlīf al-kutub, the composing of books; the *muʾallif*, author): the joining of hearts is a *composition* — disparate elements made into one coherent text — and the verse's theology is that this art belongs to no one else: gold buys compliance, fear buys attendance, charisma buys applause; nothing in the inventory of earth buys *the joining of what hearts will*, and that, not the swords of Badr, is named here as the community's *real* miracle.

**Aws and Khazraj: the Anatomy of the Impossible**

The source notes the referents: Madinah's two main Arab tribes, the Aws and the Khazraj, "a long history of conflict and hostility resulting from a vendetta culture of pride and honor" — a feud whose terminal day, al-Buʿāth (c. 617 CE, the battle in which the chiefs of both sides were killed or wounded, and which ʿĀʾisha described as "the day God granted as a favor to His Prophet": when he ﷺ arrived, "He joined the two sides through him"), the sīrah literature keeps as the *before* picture whose *after* is this verse: the same hall in which the Aws sang their war-poems against the Khazraj — the famous scene in Muslim, when a Khazraji poet recited and an Awsī answered and the voices rose in the Prophet's ﷺ mosque, and he ﷺ said: *"What is this?" — "they remembered al-Buʿāth." He said: "Let them — will you not let them be?" — and then he ﷺ said: "Call them and make them accept what they should accept"* (al-Bukhārī 4916, of the Jāhiliyya poems' stirring, and the Muslim of the *ʿaṣabiyya* rebuke — "let it be, for it is rotten (khābitha)": the two versions run in the two Ṣaḥīḥs' *Faḍāʾil*). The two tribes had been within a hair of the civil war that had consumed Makkah's rivals for a century; and the Prophet ﷺ had, in the decade before Badr, no treasury at all — he ﷺ, as the sources say, could not "spend what is on earth" because he ﷺ did not own the earth's driblet. That men who had buried brothers by each other's spears shared beds on the frontier and estates without blood — this, the sūrah insists, is the *ʿayn* of its subject: hearts cannot be *purchased*, because hearts are not *things*, and the One who *made* them is their only buyer: *mā baʿtu al-qulūba illā bi'llāh* — the heart's price is only what God pays (and the *ḥadīth* of the two weeping tribes in 48:26's "word of God-consciousness (kalimat al-taqwā)," which the tafsīr of the verse gives as *lā ilāha illa'llāh* — the single sentence the two sides could *both* say and *mean*, is the sūrah's own technology of joining).

**The Evidence of the Miracle: Why the Sūrah Cites It at the Treaty's Door**

The classical readers (the source's *proof*-note from Qurṭubī, Rāzī, Ṭabarī) treat the joined hearts as *the argument for the Prophet's ﷺ veracity and sincerity*: no human program of that age — blood, wealth, protection — explains Aws and Khazraj becoming *ikhwān*, and the verse deploys it as *the* proof-text to the community's own face, right where it has just been legislated about treaties, spoils, and the temptation of the *fiʾah* (v. 19's "your party," v. 1's quarreling bands): *you think you are a party, which the leader balances with shares; you are a composition, which God wrote — remember the source of your coherence, or the ledger of spoils will divide you again.* And the tradition sealed the same point at Jīʿrānah after Ḥunayn, when the new-rich Mekkan converts got the camels and the Ansār got the murmur: the Prophet ﷺ gathered the Ansār in a tent and asked —

> *"O company of Ansār — were you not astray, and God guided you through me; impoverished, and God enriched you; divided, and God joined your hearts through me?" — and each time they said: "God and His Messenger are gracious." He said: "What prevents you from answering me?" They said: "All grace and favor belongs to God and His Messenger." He said: "If you wish, you would say: 'you came to us denied, and we affirmed you; abandoned, and we sheltered you; destitute, and we gave you refuge' — are you pleased that the people take the sheep and the camels, and you take the Messenger of God to your homes? Were the people to take a valley and the Ansār another, I would take the valley of the Ansār. Were it not for the migration, I would be an Ansārī"* — and they wept, until their beards were wet, and said: *"We are pleased with God as Lord, with Islam as religion, and with Muhammad as Messenger."* (Muslim 1059; al-Bukhārī 4331's parallels — the sūrah's "and He joined your hearts" quoted, in the event, as the ummah's own memory)

**"But God — Mighty, Wise": the Verse's Seal on All Social Grace**

The closing names complete the doctrine: *ʿazīz* — the Mighty can do what gold cannot, which is the *ʿizz* the sūrah has already given its martyrs (v. 63's joining is the same *imkān* of the Impossible as 314 beating 1000); *ḥakīm* — and He does it with method, since the joining ran *through* the Prophet's ﷺ ten years of patience, the pacts of ʿAqaba, the brotherhood-contract (the *muʾākhāh* of Madinah, whose inheritance-rules v. 75 will close), and the Book's *kalimat al-taqwā*: the miracle has a *hikmah*-pedigree, and the *ḥikmah* is: *let no community ever trust in the cement it owns.* For the individual, the verse ends the sūrah's whole psychology of wealth with its hardest, kindest lesson: the heart you cannot buy is the one you must *leave to God* — the parent's child, the friend's repentance, the rival's conversion, the faction's return: *law anzafta... ma alfafa* is revealed so that no later ʿUthmān-radi with his gold, no Muʿāwiya with his *ḥilm*-and-payroll politics, forgets the verse's line: the policy of the *mālik al-mlk* runs *through* hearts — but never *owns* them: which is the last word of Badr's chapter about the one treasure its spoils never touched.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:64

> **O Prophet! God suffices thee and those believers who follow thee.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Private Sentence, Made Public Law**

*Yā ayyuha'n-nabiyyu ḥsabuka Allāhu wa-mani'ttabaʿaka mina'l-muʾminīn* — "O Prophet: God is *sufficient for you* — and for those of the believers who follow you." The address rises from the community's leader to the leader's *office* (the sūrah's fourth "O Prophet"-call, and the only one whose *ḥisb*-clause names the followers as *included in* it, not beside it), and it gathers up every anxiety vv. 60–63 have provisionally answered: the enemy may arm (v. 60), the treaty may kneel or betray (v. 61–62), the *fiʾah* may murmur over shares (v. 1), the hearts of men may never be welded (v. 63) — *ḥsabuka Allāh*: God is the sufficiency you and yours require, in all of it at once. The grammar matters: *ḥasbuka* is a nominal sentence of *enoughness*, not a verb of future help; the verse does not say "God will suffice you tomorrow" — it says *He is your sufficiency*, tenseless, the same present fact for the cave and the camp. The twin formula the community had already learned at Badr's morrow — *"ḥasbunā Allāhu wa-niʿma al-wakīl"* (3:173; al-Bukhārī's report of the day's *ikhtilāf*: when the pursuit's drums were heard and the *wakālah*-sentence turned the march into safety) — is v. 64's own verse, *generalized to the office*: the Prophet ﷺ who in the cave told Abū Bakr "two, whose third is God" is told here, for all time: *you are never the third alone; your followers and their Sufficiency are the same number.*

**"And Those Who Follow Thee" — the Verse's Corporate Shield**

The *wa-mani'ttabaʿaka* clause is what makes the sentence a constitution instead of a consolation: the *ḥisb* of God is not for the leader *instead of* his people but *for him together with them*, and the structure legislates two reciprocal protections. For the followers: your sufficiency is *attached* to your following — the *itibāʿ*, the act of walking behind the Prophet ﷺ on the road, is the only rank the verse names — you need not be many, since the *ḥasb* does not scale with counts (the sūrah just told you your joining could not be purchased at all, v. 63; how much less your safety). For the leader: the *ḥasb* is *shared*, so no leader may be abandoned to the office, and no leader may claim the office without the *muʾminūn*: the hadith-law of 3:173, in its *asbāb* (the camp's fear after the *ʿuhūd*-style pursuit), ends where this verse's *wa-mani'ttabaʿaka* stands — *ḥasbunā*: the first-person plural of the Sufficiency. That is also why the tradition made the *ḥisb*-sentence the daily armor of the *muʾtaqī* (the sūrah's own root-virtue, v. 1): 9:129's closing of *tawfiq*, 65:3's *wa-man yatawakkal ʿalā Allāhi fa-huwa ḥasbuh* — "whoever trusts in God — He is sufficient for him" — is v. 64's *fiqh* for the individual, and the *witr*-supplication of the Prophet ﷺ ("O God, I trust in Your Living One who dies not...") belongs to the same school of the *ḥasb*-verse, in which the *believer* learns, in a chapter of wars, to locate his security in the *Owner* of security, *without leaving his post in the ranks*.

**The Verse the Quraysh's Exit and the Ansār's Tent Both Prove**

Put v. 64 in its two real settings and it speaks twice. Its first hour: the Makkan exit and Madinan camp — the community of vv. 5–6, *averse* and *disputing*, told before the field and after it that sufficiency is *given*, not *mustered*: Badr's number (314 against a thousand) is the verse's permanent *ʿibrah* — the *ḥasb* is measured by its Owner, not by its share-holders. Its second hour, in the sūrah's own future tense (the *idhā* of the joining-verse, v. 63, still ringing): Jīʿrānah, where the *itibāʿ* of the Ansār — the weeping men and women who gave the camels back to the new-rich and chose the Prophet himself — is the verse's *human witness*: when the leader is "sufficient with his followers," the followers' *ḥisb* shows in the hour the *mīzān* of this world looks shortest (Muslim 1059's *inkisār*-scene of the tent is the *event*, v. 64 its *meaning* — and the *wept-beard* tradition's line "*God and His Messenger are gracious (afḍal)*" is the *ḥisb*-sentence turned into thanksgiving). And to the leader in every hour, the verse is the charter against isolation: *ḥsabuka... wa-ma'ttabaʿaka* — you and those who follow you, in one *genitive* of sufficiency, so that the head of the community who trusts on God is told *not to carry it alone*, and the body that follows is told it is not *carried by the leader's shoulders alone*: the *ḥisb* of the *Muḥsin* covers both, as a single clause.

**"God Suffices Thee" as the Sūrah's Balance-Sheet**

Finally, let the verse close the ledger the chapter opened: v. 1's men asked *whose* the spoils are; the sūrah's answer was *God's and the Messenger's* — i.e., a community whose *wealth* runs to the One who gives sufficiency should not run after wealth at all: v. 64 is v. 1 in its positive, the same *tawfiq*-economy in which "God suffices" means *the pile cannot pay what the ḥisb gives* — a sentence whose later *siyar* echoes (the *ʿawf* of the imām's trust, the *ḥisb*-logic of the *khārijī* age) are the ummah's whole memory of Badr's arithmetic. And the chapter's next two verses will take the same *ḥisb* into the *numbers of the ranks themselves* (v. 65–66's ratios, 1:10 into 1:2) — proving v. 64's final *ḥikmah*: a Sufficiency that arms its weak, lightens its burdens, and re-draws the odds by *revelation* is a Sufficiency whose *follow-ers* are told, at the close of the day of the furqān: *go down into the ranks — the ḥisb is yours too: and the only ʿuddah the Book never forbids, and the only wealth it never divides.*

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:65
