> **O Prophet! Rouse the believers to fight. If there be twenty steadfast among you, they shall overcome two hundred. And if there be one hundred of you, they shall overcome one thousand of those who disbelieve, because they are a people who understand not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Rouse" — the Verb that Whips the Sleeping Fire**

*Yā ayyuha'n-nabiyyu ḥarriḍi'l-muʾminīna ʿalā'l-ḥarbi* — "urge on, stir up, rouse the believers to fight." The verb *ḥarraḍa* is Form II of *ḥ-r-ḍ*, to incite, to whip up, to provoke to action — the root the lexicographers link to *tarḥīḍ*, the sharpening of a spear-point, and to the *ḥarīḍ*, the eager horse straining its tether. The address is not *command them* but *stir them*: the men already believe (the sūrah's whole vv. 2–4 has certified them), and what their limbs require is not a new law but an ignition of what their hearts already hold — a distinction that governs all Qur'anic *taḥrīḍ*: the community's malaise is never the absence of the command; it is the command's sleep inside it. which is the tradition's *own memory*: the sīrah preserves his ﷺ *taḥrīḍ* at the very line of Badr — as the ranks drew up he ﷺ straightened the rows with an arrow-shaft, passing shoulder to shoulder, and when Sawwād ibn Qārib's chest protruded he ﷺ touched it and said *"Be aligned, O Sawwād — be aligned"* (Aḥmad; classed sound by its editors); and he ﷺ *roused* them not with promises of odds but with the law of the moment: *"Do not wish to encounter the enemy — but when you meet them, be patient (iṣbirū)"* (al-Bukhārī 2966; Muslim 1741): the *taḥrīḍ* is the *ṣabr*-command, and the verse's ratio is the *ṣabr*-command's *arithmetic*.

**The Law of the Ratios, and What the Ratios Are Made Of**

*In yakun minkum ʿishrūna ṣābirūna yaghlibū miʾatayn, wa-in yakun minkum miʾatun yaghlibū alfan bi-annahum qawmun lā yafqahūn* — the verse lays down a ratio — twenty steadfast overcome two hundred; a hundred overcome a thousand — ten to one. Read the terms with care, and the verse is not arithmetical *promise* but a *quality-equation*: the multiplier is *ṣabr* (the active participle *ṣābirūna* is the sentence's operative noun, "steadfast ones"), and the divisor of the enemy is *fuqh* (the final clause, *bi-annahum lā yafqahūn*, "because they are a people who *do not understand*"): what makes one believer worth ten of the other is not physique but the *two* qualities this sūrah has been manufacturing since verse 2 — the *ṣabr* of the firm and *fiqh* of the sighted, against the *faḍāḥah* of men who see only the surface of odds. The ʿaqli is the *mīzān*: the polytheist fights for the *seen* — loot, honor, revenge — and his courage is *exhausted* by its object when the object vanishes; the believer fights for the *unseen* — "the religion is wholly God's" (v. 39) — and his courage *replenishes* as long as the unseen endures, so ten of the latter *outlast* a hundred of the former as the *well* outlasts the *pail*: this, the classical *ʿuqalāʾ* read, is what the ratio *means*, and why it is stated as a *law of natures*, not a *lottery of numbers*: a hundred with the two qualities, and a thousand without them, *are*, on the field's real scale, ten-to-one. The tradition's own reading confirms it: Ibn ʿAbbās on the verse — *"the steadfastness (ṣabr) of twenty defeats a double a hundred, because the one fights for what the other cannot even see."*

**"Because They Do Not Understand" — the Fiqh the Polytheist Lacks**

What *fiqh* do the thousand lack? The tafsīr tradition (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī) glosses *lā yafqahūn* here as *not understanding the nature of what is being fought for*: they see the *cost* of dying and cannot see its *price*; they understand the *loss* of a chief and cannot understand the *gain* of a *martīr* (the hadith of Mus'ab ibn ʿUmayr, Badr's commander, slain at Uḥud with his body shrouded in a *namira*-cloak: "when we covered his head his feet showed, and when we covered his feet his head showed, until the Prophet ﷺ said: cover above, and put *iḍhir* grass on the feet" — al-Bukhārī 1279, Muslim 2128: the sighted man's death that *enriches* the earth of a *single cloak*), and in the loss of *their* dead they see only the pit. Which is why the sūrah's *fiqh*-term recurs at its every hinge: the *furqān* of discernment (vv. 29, 41) is exactly the faculty the thousand *do not have*, and the *tafaqquh* of the *tāʾifa* (9:122's *li-yunḍhū qawmahum idh rajaʿū ilayhim laʿallahuhum yaḥdharūn*: the *tafaqquh* that *warns and steadies*) is what makes one Muslim's steadfastness a *force-multiplying* public property and not a private endurance. The ten-to-one is thus a *knowledge-ratio* before it is a *courage-ratio*: men who *understand* what is *real* (the heap of v. 37, the *sabaq*-illusion of v. 59, the *ḥisb* of v. 64) *cannot* be outnumbered, because the field on which they fight is not the one on which their opponents are counted.

**The Command and the Promise — How the Verse Loads Its Double Duty**

Read the opening imperative with the conditional law that follows, and the verse's structure is *taḥrīḍ + burhān*: *stir them, and here is why stirring works*. The *ʿilla* of the *ḥarriḍ* is that the believers' own *mathematics* is not what despair computes: the general who reads his *strength* off the enemy's numbers has already lost the *fiqh* that makes his men the ratio's numerator; the general who rouses the *ṣabr* and *fiqh* his men possess is invoking a divine *sunnah* of exchange-rates that Badr itself proved (v. 7's *taḥrīḍ*-promise honored). And the tradition read the verse as *conditional obligation*: the twenty-to-hundred ratio is the *farḍ* of the *ṣābirūn* (hence v. 15–16's flight-prohibition, whose legal *ʿilla* is precisely this: *if* you possess the two qualities, *then* standing is your *capacity*, and flight from what you *can* bear is the *kabīra* of v. 16), while the *ʿazm* of the ratio is what vv. 65–66's lightening will *re-quote* as the *ḥisb*-God adjusts to *ḍuʿf* (v. 66) — the pairing to which we come next: the sūrah's *real* lesson of the *taḥrīḍ*-verse is that the *rousing* and the *ratio* are one, since the only force the general may *count* is the force God has already *put in the camp* by the Book's *own* arithmetic, and the only *command* to rouse is: *see what you are — and do not surrender your estimate to the enemy's parade-ground.*

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:66

> **Now God has lightened your burden, for He knows that there is weakness in you. And if there be one hundred steadfast among you, they shall overcome two hundred. And if there be one thousand, they shall overcome two thousand by God's Leave. And God is with the steadfast.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Reduced the Tax on Courage**

*Al-āna khaffafa Allāhu ʿankum wa-ʿalima anna fiykum ḍuʿfan* — "now God has lightened your burden, knowing that there is *weakness* in you." The *al-āna* is startling — a *now* inside the revelation — and the commentators explain: the 1:10 ratio of v. 65 pressed on the camp with the weight of a *farḍ*, and some said, in the reports the sources preserve, "we are *commanded* to one-against-ten?" — God answered by *reducing the rate* to 1:2: a hundred steadfast now overcome two hundred, a thousand, two thousand — *by God's leave*. The most famous reading of the pair is *nāsikh and mansūkh* in the same moment of revelation (al-Ṭabarī's reports, Ibn Kathīr's discussion: v. 65 was enjoined, it *grieved* the believers, and v. 66 came as *rukhaṣah* — a concession-lightening — before it could crush them), which Abū Muslim al-Iṣfahānī and al-Rāzī resist on *ʿaql*: God changes not, so the two verses state *two capacities* — the ideal of ten-to-one (v. 65, when *ṣabr* is at its full) and the obligation of two-to-one (v. 66, "for He knows weakness in you") — and the law tracks *capacity*, not the other way about. Either way the *fiqh* is fixed and the *fiqh* is *kind*: the *farḍ* of standing is *measured by what the community can actually bear* — and no *thābit* is demanded beyond *ṭāqah*, for the God of this sūrah "imposes on no soul except its capacity" (2:286), and the *ḥisb* of v. 64 is *adjusted*, by the Book's own hand, to the men who own it.

**Three Words That Hold the Verse Together**

*Ḍuʿf*, *khaffafa*, *bi-idhni'llāh*: read the three and the verse's realism and its *tawḥīd* are one. *Ḍuʿf* — weakness, the *ḍ-ʿ-f* root the sūrah used for the *mustaḍʿafūn* of Makkah's crushing (v. 26) — is now *in* the believers' *own camp*: the very men whose hearts vv. 2–3 certified carry *ḍuʿf* as a *created fact*, and the verse *names* it without *shame*, which is the sūrah's most consensual lesson to the *ṣābir*: the *taqwā* of vv. 2 and 29 is *not* the absence of weakness — it is the obedience that *proceeds inside* it, since God "knew the weakness in you" *before* He lightened the rate, and the *lightening* proves the *knowledge* is *care*, not *contempt*. *Khaffafa* — He made it *light*, Form II, the verb of God's *active condescension*, the same *takhfīf* as the "light" Book (54:17's *wa-laqaṣṣadnā'l-Qurʾān li'dh-dhikri fa-hal min muddakir*) and of *al-yusr* after *al-ʿusr* (94:5-6, twice): the divine *policy* in this sūrah is *re-scaling the burden to the heart*, not *breaking the heart to the burden* — the *nashīz* of v. 65's *taḥrīḍ* is followed, in the same breath, by the *taysīr* of the *khāfiḍ*. And *bi-idhni'llāh* — "by God's leave" — is welded to the new ratio to *re-open the door* the *rate* might seem to have closed: the 1:2 is *not* a *mechanical* guarantee that now frees the men to calculate; the victory *remains* *from God alone* (v. 10), and the *idhniyyah* (leave) is the *ʿillah*-clause of *every* ratio that ever was: the *numbers* are God's *instrument*, not His *ceiling* — so the reduced law *keeps* the *ḥisb* and only *moves* the *count*, which is the *ḥikmah* the *alāna* alone would not teach.

**"God Is with the Steadfast" — the With-ness That the Ratio Was Only the Surface Of**

*Wa-Allāhu maʿa'ṣ-ṣābirīn* — and God is *with* the patient. The clause *de-links* the verse's whole arithmetic from *odds-thinking*: the *real* subject of vv. 65–66 is not *the number one can defeat* but *the with-ness one stands inside*, which is *the same sentence* (v. 46) that closed the *ḥisb*-promise of patience in the middle of the *qitāl* sequence: the *maʿiyyah* is the true multiplier — v. 9's *angels in ranks* (the with-ness made visible) and v. 66's *God with the steadfast* (the with-ness *made permanent for the ṣābirīn*, whoever they are and however few they stand) are the *same aid* *re-taught* without its *angels-detail* so the *lesson* cannot be *pinned to Badr alone*: *whoever* is *ṣābir*, God *is with him*, and the *with-ness* is what made 314 twenty-to-one *in act* at the wells. Which is why the tradition read *ṣabr* here as the sūrah's *summa*: not the *stoic* passivity but the *active holding of the line for God's sake* — *ṣabrun jamīl* (12:18, 12:83, the *Yaʿqūb*-patience that *makes no complaint of the beloved* and *keeps walking to Egypt*), and the *ṣabr* the hadith of *al-Iṣṭabār* legislates as *ʿaṭāʾ*: *"whoever seeks to be patient, God will make him patient"* (al-Bukhārī 1469; Muslim 1053) — the *ṣābirūn* are *made* steadfast, *by the very God who is with them*: the *with-ness* and the *ṣabr* are *one economy*, and the *ratio* is its *shadow*.

**Why the Two Verses Must Be Read as a Single Lesson**

The sūrah, in vv. 65–66, hands the *general* and the *believer* one rule in two numbers: *command to your capacity, and measure your enemy's defeat by God's leave, not by your count*. The *taḥrīḍ* (65) is *obligation*; the *takhfīf* (66) is *mercy*; and the *with-ness* (66's end) is *the reality* both were *about*: the men who would *not* have *stood* at ten-to-one in *their own strength* were *given* the *two-to-one* so that *their* standing became a *fit* — the *fiqh* the *ʿulamāʾ* distilled into the *ijmāʿ* of *al-ṭāqah* (the *rākūn* of the *rukhaṣ*: the *farḍ* of the *jamaʿah* in *ḥarb* is *the ratio it can bear*), and the *mufassirūn* into the *sūfī* line — that *God lightens the burden before the back breaks* — a *knowledge*-truth (*ʿalima anna fīkum ḍuʿfan*: He *knew* it, and so the *knowledge* *preceded* the *command*) that makes *every* *mukallaf* in the *line* a man *measured by mercy* before he is measured by the *law*. Read thus, the *alāna* is the *sūrah's own voice* — *now*, *for you*, *with your weakness* — and the *with-ness of the steadfast* is its *ever*, and the *bi-idhn* its *open door*: the *chapter of the Day of the Furqān* ends where every *furqān*-hour will begin, in the *ratio* a *heart* can *hold* when God *is with it* — and the next three verses (67–69) will carry that *same realism* from the *field* to the *pile of prisoners*, where the *ḍuʿf* of the *nafs* is *stronger* than any *khaṭar* of the *enemy*.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:67

> **It is not for a prophet to take captives until he overwhelms [his enemy] in the land. You desire the ephemeralities of this world, while God desires the Hereafter. And God is Mighty, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Rebuke — and Its Different Object**

*Mā kāna li-nabiyyin an yakuna lahu asrun ḥattā yuthkhina fi'l-arḍ* — "it is not for a prophet to have captives until he makes himself dominant in the land." The sūrah's second reproof of its own camp (the first was the spoils of v. 1) falls on the hour *after* the victory, and its object is not greed this time but *sentiment and calculation*: the seventy prisoners of Badr sat in the camp, and the Prophet ﷺ consulted — Abū Bakr: "they are cousins and sons of uncles; take the ransom, it will strengthen us and perhaps God will guide them"; ʿUmar: "give me so-and-so — let me behead him; these are the leaders of unbelief, and they are sincere in their enmity" (Muslim 1763, from Ibn ʿAbbās's first-hand account of the council); and the *law of prophets* came down over both opinions, because *both* wanted something from the prisoners — money or security or revenge — while the hour's *weight* required that the prisoners' fate be the *last* thing the community wanted. The verse's verb — *yuthkin* — is, as the source stresses, *from* "weight/heaviness" (*thakhāna al-qawm*: they became heavy, established, numerous), *not* "slaughter": the standard translation "till he has made great slaughter" is an old gloss (al-Ṭabarī records it *because* the construction with *qatl* is frequent) but the *literal* sense is *until he has sapped their power and made manifest his dominance* (the source's philological note). The law is therefore structural: *a war is not a police action and a prisoner-market is not its sequel*: until the enemy's power is *broken in the land*, captive-taking converts war into *raid*, and ransom into *motive* — the sword begins to *count heads* instead of *cutting the remnant* (v. 7's *dābir*).

**"You Desire the Ephemeral — God Desires the Hereafter"**

*Turīdūna ʿaraḍa'l-ḥayāti'd-dunyā wa-Allāhu yurīdu'l-ākhira* — *ʿaraḍ* is the passing *caravan-goods*, the *stuff that crosses* (from the root *ʿ-r-ḍ*, to pass by; the same word for the world as *ʿāriḍ* of 46:24's cloud) — and the clause cuts the sūrah's theme to its bone a second time: the men of v. 1 *disputed the pile*; the men of vv. 67–69 *wanted to monetize the mercy*: the ransom of kinsmen, the gold that would have *finished* the war by making it *pay*. The divine *irādah* "the Hereafter" does not mean God is indifferent to the community's survival; it means the *frame* within which survival is asked for must be the *long* field — because, as al-Rāzī's political reading in the sources unfolds, the *short* field corrupts the war's *purpose*: ransom-softness after a single defeat tells Arabia that a Muslim army can be *fed by what it captures and enriched by what it releases*, and the enemy learns that defeat is *recoverable at cost*, renewing the war (*"they would not rise up... unless"* — Rāzī's *ʿilla*: the ransomed return with heavier arms and lighter respect); the *ʿākhira*-frame, by contrast, makes the war's *currency* deterrence and *ends*, not cash. The verse therefore legislates at the level the sūrah always works at — the *niyya*'s direction: the same prisoners will be *held*, *ranomed*, and *released* by the very revelation that ends this passage (vv. 68–70), and the correction is not to the *act* but to the *desire*: *you wanted the passing; He wanted the permanence — choose whose want you are feeding.*

**"And God Is Mighty, Wise" — the Seal on the Unfinished War**

The closing names (*ʿazīz ḥakīm* — the sūrah's signature pair, vv. 1, 5, 8, 10, 19, 41) state the two perfections the camp needed to hear against its two temptations: *Mighty* — the community's security does not depend on the *ransom-deal or the revenge*: God can *establish His people in the land* without selling His captives to sentiment, and He did: within a decade the whole peninsula lay within the *dominance* the verse set as the *prior* condition — *thakhāna fi'l-arḍ* — Quraysh's power cut, its chiefs dead or *made believers* (v. 38's open door kept its promise: "if they desist, what is past is forgiven"), and the very ransomed — ʿAbbās among them (v. 70) — returned *without price*, as the promise of that verse shows; *Wise* — and the law given (no prisoner-trade before dominance; prisoners as *favor or ransom* after it — 47:4's *fa-imnān baʿdu aw fidāʾ* till "the war has laid down its burdens") is *wisdom's* own shape, a rule *timed to the community's phase*, in which *kindness* and *severity* are *both* instruments of the *same long policy*. The tradition adds the grief of the hour to the law's cost: the report of the Prophet's ﷺ and Abū Bakr's weeping over the counsel taken — "the punishment was displayed to me nearer than the tree" the two Ṣaḥīḥs and the Musnad preserve — is the sūrah's witness that a *rebuke* of this weight falls *on love* precisely because the *error itself* was love's over-reach: and the next verse (v. 68) will explain, at once, why the *displayed* punishment did not land.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:68

> **Were it not for a decree that had already gone forth from God, a great punishment would have befallen you for what you took.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Shows the Edge of the Cliff**

*Law lā kitābun mina'llāhi sabaqa la-amasnakum fīma akhadhtum ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm* — "had not a decree from God preceded, a great punishment would have touched you for what you took." The verse is the sūrah's single most chilling sentence, because it discloses the *distance* between the camp's safety and its ruin: the very ground they stood on — after a *rebuke* — was *one decree away* from punishment. The verb *masa* — touched — is the idiom of the Qur'an's afflictions: *"affliction (masṭ) has touched me, and You are the most merciful of the merciful"* (21:83, Ayyūb's cry), and the she-camel's warning: *"leave her not to harm, lest a painful punishment seize you"* (7:73): punishment, here, is conceived as a *contact* the camp had *almost* incurred, which sharpens the *bi-mā akhadhtum* — "for what you took": the *taking* of the ransom (the reading of Ibn ʿAbbās and most of the tafsīr) *before* the decree's permission, or the *taking* of the spoils-haste (the other reading carried by the sources' first interpretation of v. 67) — the *nearness* is the *lesson*, and the *averted* landing is the *mercy*: the *kitāb sabaq* (a decree *preceding*, written before the event and running beneath time) is the Qur'an's own word for grace's *prior claim*: the same phrase as *"sabaqat rahmatī ghaḍabī"* — "My mercy has *preceded* My wrath," the Prophet's ﷺ ḥadīth of the Book God wrote with His own hand before the heavens were hung (al-Bukhārī 7404; Muslim 2751, from Abū Hurayrah): vv. 67–68 are that *sibāq* (precedence) *demonstrated on a camp*, and the terror of the verse and its comfort are its two faces.

**The Decree That Preceded: What the Commentators Counted**

The source commentary lays out the *kitāb sabaq*'s three classical identifications. (1) *The prior legislation that spoils were lawful to this community* — the decree written into the ummah's constitution (cf. v. 1's *anfāl* and v. 41's *khums*: what was *almost* ʿadhab became, by decree, *ḥalāl* — the law itself *re-classified* the *taking*, and this reading ties the verse back to the *ʿadhab*-of-treachy-warning of v. 27: *what made the taking near-punishment was the pre-law hour; the law is the aversion*). (2) *A special dispensation for the people of Badr* — the rank of *the day of the furqān* (v. 41) as a *shield*: several of the salaf (in Ibn Kathīr's chain) hold the *sābiq* is the honor God gave the Badr-folk — the famous report of Jābir: *"no one who witnessed Badr will enter the Fire"* (Aḥmad; al-Ḥākim graded it ṣaḥīḥ, with corroborating chains in the Sunan): the *kitāb sabaq* of *the day* itself. (3) *The general decree that God will not punish until He has warned and legislated* — "and We never punish until We have sent a Messenger" (17:15) — the verse's own *ʿilla*-formulation: *ʿadam al-muʾākhadha qabla'l-bayān*, no taking-to-task before clarification (the *uṣūl*'s axiom from this very text, cited by al-Rāzī among the *aḥkām*-derivations of the verse); and its near-cousin the *salaf's* line on 2:286's *"lā tuʾakhidhnā in nasiyna aw akhṭaʾnā"* — "when He said *qad faʿaltu* (I have done it)," the angel's reply to the *ummah*'s first *sabaq* (Muslim 126) — the *taking* of vv. 67–68 being *khaṭaʾ* in the very form the *decree* had *pre-empted*. The three are one doctrine at three levels: the *law*, the *people*, and the *principle* — and together they turn the verse's *near-terror* into the sūrah's most complete *ʿaqīda of grace*: the *ʿadhab* is *possible* and the *kitāb* is *prior* — and *prior wins*.

**"For What You Took": the Sūrah's Third Lesson of the Hand**

Mark, in the passage's structure, the sequence of verbs: v. 1's *dispute* over what God gave; v. 67's *desire* for what the enemy held; v. 68's *taking* of what the hour had not yet released. The sūrah's hand-discipline is one lesson in three tenses: do not grasp at the gift; do not grasp at the foe; do not grasp before the decree — and the ḥadīth of the needle (v. 41 above: *"return the thread and the needle"*) stands at the end of the series as the law's small-scale echo of the *sabaq*: the ummah's decree made the pile lawful and shared, so that no one's hand may precede the hand of the Book. The near-miss of v. 68 remains, for the preachers, the sharpest lesson in the geography of safety: a rebuke that *could have been* a punishment is a decree's favor — and the verse keeps the believer from testing that distance twice. *Law lā kitābun mina'llāhi sabaqa*: the only reason the world has not ended in the next hour is a decree already written — and it was not written for our sakes alone.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:69

> **So consume the spoils you have taken, lawfully and in a good way, and reverence God. Truly God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Fa-Kulū": the Word That Ends the Reproach**

*Fa-kulū mimmā ghanimtum ḥalālan ṭayyiban wa-ttaqū Allāh* — "so *eat* of what you have taken as spoils, lawful and good; and reverence God." The *fāʾ* is the sūrah's most relieved conjunction in three verses: *so* — eat: the same imperative-form God used to admit Adam to the Garden ("*eat freely of it, wherever you wish*" — 2:35, and the prohibition's echo "*eat not*" 2:35b/7:19) and to repatriate the Prodigy-days of the camp's fear. The verse consummates the divine manner of this chapter: v. 1 *took* the pile from their hands and put it at God's disposal; vv. 67–68 *rebuked* their reaching for the prisoners' gold and showed the near-cliff; v. 69 *returns the whole pile to their hands* — *with a law upon it*: eat what is *yours*, now that *He has made it yours*: what the sūrah has been performing is not the confiscation of the world but its *re-legalization*: the spoils that became the *idol* (v. 1), then the *scandal* (vv. 67–68), become, by the decree's *fiqh* (khums out, four-fifths in — v. 41), *ḥalālan ṭayyiban* — food. This is the Book's *law of the hand* at its most complete: it never commands the renunciation of the good; it commands that the good *arrive through its doors* — and the verse's own word-pair fixes the doors: *ḥalāl* is the door of the *Sharīʿah* (from where it was *earned* and *distributed*: not the hoard of the strong), *ṭayyib* the door of the *soul* (how it is *used*: not as the *ʿaraḍ* of v. 67, the passing-caravan thing): *lawfulness of acquisition, goodness of consumption* — and the *ḥadīth*'s two-sided mirror of the same pair is its warning to the *mukallaf* in every transaction: *"this wealth is green and sweet, and whoever takes it by its generosity is blessed in it, and whoever takes it by its greed is not blessed in it"* (al-Bukhārī 1465; Muslim 1052, the *khayd* of the alms-claim).

**"And Reverence God in It" — Taqwā Inside the Enjoyment**

*Wa-ttaqū Allāh* — the sūrah's keynote (v. 1's *fa-t-taqū Allāh*) returns *inside* the permission, which is the verse's discipline against the *relaxed* reading the *kulū* could license: *now that it is lawful, fear God — in the lawful*: the *ḥalāl* needs *taqwā* *more* than the *ḥarām* does, because the *ḥarām* has a fence while the *ḥalāl* has an *appetite* (the *ʿulamāʾ*'s line: *"the *ḥalāl* is what your *nafs* passes over — and the *ḥarām* is what it *seizes*"*, in the *warāʿ*-reports of al-Ḥasan and the *Iḥyāʾ*'s *kitāb al-kasb*), and because a people newly enriched by heaven's *own distribution* are precisely the people to whom v. 28's *trial* clause will apply (*innamā amwālukum... fitnah*): the verse re-attaches the *taqwā* of v. 1 (the pile's fear) and v. 29 (the pile's criterion — *in tattaqū Allāha yajʿal lakum furqānan*), so that the camp's eating is *Badriyya*: eat as the people of the Day of the Furqān eat, with discernment inside the appetite. And the prophetic pair of *ʿazm* and *rukhaṣah* governs the same halal-economy from the other side: *"God loves that His concessions be taken, as He loves that His firm obligations be done"* (Aḥmad; Ibn Ḥibbān) — the *kulū* of the verse is itself such a *rukhaṣah* whose taking *is* obedience: the fear of God *inside* the concession is the *ʿazīmah* that completes it.

**"God Is Forgiving, Merciful" — the Two Names That Bury the Cliff**

*Inna Allāha ghafūrun raḥīm* — the *kaffār*-promise of v. 29 (evils expunged, the quarrel over the pile forgiven) is the verse's seal, and the occasion's tafsīr reads it as the absolution of Badr's council: the *taking* that v. 68 had shown at the edge of punishment is, under these two names, *past* — *Ghafūr*: the record of the near-cliff is covered; *Raḥīm*: the law of the eating is opened. The ummah learns, from the chapter's last financial clause, the whole fiqh of fault and favor: the law's rebuke is not resentment, and its permission is not forgetfulness. The past is buried under *ghufrān* so it can no longer accuse; the future is opened by *raḥma* so it can no longer be abused. Eat — and fear. The sūrah that began at a pile ends here, at the same pile: now *ḥalāl*, now *ṭayyib*, now a test again (vv. 28, 74's *rizq karīm*) — and the men who quarreled over it are the very ones whose chapter turns, in the next verse, to the *captive's* voice and his hope (v. 70).

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:70

> **O Prophet! Say to those captives in your custody, “If God knows there to be any good in your hearts, He will give you what is better than that which was taken from you, and will forgive you. And God is Forgiving, Merciful.”**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Victor's Mouth Put to a New Errand**

*Qul lahum* — say to them: the sūrah, after rebuking the camp for its desire for the captives' ransom (v. 67), now sets the camp's tongue to work in the captives' ear. The command is startling in the idiom of ancient warfare, where the prisoner's whole vocabulary was chains and price-lists: the victor is told to bring *good news* to the man in his power — *in yaʿlami'llāhu fī qulūbikum khayran yuʾtikum khayran mimmā ukhitha minkum wa-yaghfir lakum*. The echo is deliberate: the words "if He knows any good in your hearts" are the sūrah's own sentence about the obstinate Meccans at the height of their arrogance (v. 23: "had God known any good in them, He would have made them hear") — and where v. 23 closed a door, v. 70 opens it for the same city, now in fetters. The Book that *foreknew* their hardness now tells them that what the *foreknowledge* looks for — the *khayr*, the seed, whatever its size — may be found in *these* chests after all, and its finder pays rather than taxes: *better than what was taken from you*. And the jurisdiction note is exact: the court named in the verse is *God's knowledge of hearts*, not the camp's; the men who held their bodies are commanded not to hold *verdicts over their souls* (v. 43's *ʿalīm bi-dhāti'l-ṣudūr* belongs to Him alone), and so the message's true grammar is: *your loss is seen, your heart is searched, and your account is with the Forgiving*.

**The Ransom of al-ʿAbbās: the Verse Made Flesh in One Wallet**

The occasion preserved in the sources: among the captives was al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, the Prophet's ﷺ own uncle, and the demand of his ransom was pressed to the letter — greater than other men's, so that the faith should not be *bought* with kinship and the law should not bend for the Prophet's ﷺ family. When he pleaded poverty, the tradition reports the famous disclosure — the gold he had entrusted to his wife Umm al-Faḍl in Makkah, known to no eye in the camp — which struck from al-ʿAbbās the testimony that a man who had watched his nephew's life for sixty years could give no other way; the source commentary faithfully notes that *some doubt the authenticity of this account*, while all agree on the ruling: the ransom was paid, and its payment released al-ʿAbbās and others with him. On top of the legal line runs the verse's promise-line: the tafsīr reports transmit that compensation was given him from the spoils for what had been taken — and the *better* was doubled for the man of faith: the forgiveness of sixty years of the idol-feast announced in the same breath (*wa-yaghfir lakum*). So the verse legislates the whole *law of the defeated* in three sentences: ransomed *as the law demands*; compensated *as the grace promises*; addressed *as souls on the way* — no fourth category, the slave-market's old one, is left standing anywhere in the passage.

**"He Will Give You Better" — the Economics of the Wound**

Read in the chapter's own ledger, v. 70 mirrors v. 41: the law *takes* the fifth from the victor's pile, and the promise *replaces* the captive's loss with better — the same God distributing on both sides of the chain, so that in this economy *no one is merely a loser*: the *sabaq*-decree of v. 68 runs for camp and captives together, and the sunnah's general law of compensation is the echo of the verse's *yuʾtikum khayran*:

> *"No Muslim struck by a calamity who says what God commanded — 'we belong to God and to Him we return; O God, compensate me in my calamity and give me better than it' — but God gives him better."* When Abū Salama died, Umm Salama said it — and God gave her *the Messenger of God ﷺ* in compensation for her loss. (Muslim 918)

a promise the ummah has tried for fourteen centuries and never found false; and Badr's own records are its first pages: the ransom-paid captives who returned to become the city's *asset* — al-ʿAbbās ransomed and honored for his kinship and his later Islam, and Thumāma ibn Uthāl, chief of Banū Sulaym, whom the Prophet ﷺ released with the words "go forth to your valley" — who, when Makkah's trade died in the boycott he laid (not a grain reached Makkah except by Muhammad's leave, per the Bukhārī-report of Abū Hurayra), came back a believer to *feed* the city that had captured him: *khayran mimmā ukhitha minkum* — better than what was taken — in gold, in harvest, in *history*, all at once.

**"God is Forgiving, Merciful" — the Open Ending**

The seal names — *ghafūr raḥīm*, v. 69's pair, repeated — close the captive's file and the camp's file the same way, and this is the sūrah's final fiqh of forgiveness as rehabilitation: *Ghafūr* buries the kufr and its wounds for both parties; *Raḥīm* opens the future for both. Note to whom the forgiveness is promised: not to the faith they *profess* in chains (v. 71, next, exists precisely because the camp must not be naïve about professions), but to the *khayr* God's *knowledge* finds — the conscience's residue, the honest shame, the seed of a later Islam: the most exact evangelism in the Book, because it refers conversion to the *only* court that can see it and pays the convert in *God's* account before any human one. The captives were told: what was taken from you *here* will be *replaced* to you *there* — and the camp was told, in the same sentence's shadow: *you* are the ones who needed that verse three days ago (vv. 67–68), and the God who promises the captive a *better* is the God who *rebuked you and then fed you* (v. 69). The two hands of the sura's economics — the hand that takes by law and the hand that gives by promise — meet here, and the second out-runs the first: which is the last word the chapter speaks to the defeated, and the last caution it whispers to the strong.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:71

> **But if they desire treachery against thee, they have been treacherous with God before and He gave [thee] power over them. And God is Knowing, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Guard-Rail Set Beside the Open Door**

*Wa-in yurīdū an yakhkanūka fa-qad khānū Allāha min qablu fa-ammakana minhum, wa-Allāhu ʿalīmun ḥakīm* — "and if they intend to betray you — they betrayed God *before*, and He enabled you over them." The verse is the necessary pendant to v. 70, and the sūrah's balance of mercy and vigilance is complete only when the two are read together: there, the captive is offered hope — the good in his heart, replacement, forgiveness; here, the camp is warned — hope is not *naïveté*. The structure mirrors the treaty-law the sūrah already wrote for the *outside* enemy (vv. 61–62: "if they incline to peace, incline to it — *and if they seek to deceive you, God suffices you*"): the very same pairing, open hand and open eye, now legislated for the *believing-looking inside*: the community is forbidden both errors in one stroke — the error of the hoarder who will not forgive the captive, and the error of the sentimentalist who trusts the captive's *word* while ignoring his *pattern*. And the pattern the verse cites is itself the argument: *they have been treacherous with God before* — the oath of every creature with its Maker (7:172's *alastu bi-rabbikum*, which this Quraysh kept with the idols the day the covenants were sold), and, in the hour's history, the treaties of the *ṣaḥīfa* and the truces half-broken (v. 56's *yanquḍūna ʿuhūdahum fī kulli marrah*) — a people whose record with *God* is *khiyāna* may, at the least, have a record with *you* of the same coin, and the camp's mercy must not cost it its memory.

**"He Gave You Power Over Them": the Argument from Badr's Evidence**

*Fa-ammakana minhum* — "and He made you *possible* over them": the root *m-k-n*, to establish, to invest with capacity, is the same word the sūrah used for the *istimkān* it promises the oppressed (7:128's "and He made you successors in the land," and 28:5–6's "and We wished to *establish* the *mustaḍʿafūn* in the land and make them imams and heirs," the verse cited above at v. 26): the power the camp holds over the chained chiefs is named *as a grant*, not as an achievement — and the lesson follows in the mode of the whole chapter: *the same Hand that lifted you over them can do it again; your security was never in your ring of steel but in the ʿayn that watches the chests*. Al-Rāzī's gloss in the source — "if the captives are not true in their faith and return to their old enmity, remember: they were treacherous before, and God *still* gave you victory at Badr" — makes the verse a *proof of tawḥīd in policy*: the community that needs neither fear (the treachery is *known* beforehand and handled by the Knowing) nor cunning (the plot is *weakened* — v. 18's *taḍʿīf* again) needs only *obedience*: the past tense of *fa-ammakana* is the sūrah's *empirical* warrant, Badr itself the precedent filed for every future hour of doubt about the enemy's intentions: *you were not stronger then; you were enabled — and the Enabler has not changed*.

**"Knowing, Wise" — the Two Names That Close the Captives' Dossier**

*ʿAlīmun ḥakīm*: the attributes (which closed the chapter's first half at v. 10, "Mighty, Wise") return here in *knowing-first* order because the case is one of *hidden intentions*: *ʿAlīm* — He *knows* whose "faith" is flesh and whose is spirit (v. 70's "if God *knows* good in your hearts" and v. 23's foreknowledge — the sūrah never stops handing the hearts to their only expert, which is *also* a restraint on the camp: *you* are to act on *overt* conduct — the *nabḍ* of v. 58's *ʿalāmāt* and the sword of v. 15's *laqiyā* — never on the verdict about the unseen that is the *ʿAlīm*'s monopoly), and *Ḥakīm* — the Wise will *dispose* of every treason, so the policy of the community needs no panic: ransoms, releases, covenants, and the *ʿahd*-laws already given cover every case; the treason, if it comes, will meet a *wisdom* with a precedent — *they betrayed; He gave you power* — as the treason of the Trench met its *khandaq* and the treason of the Qurayẓa met its *ḥakam*. And for the individual believer the pair is his whole law of trust: *be soft-hearted as v. 70 and clear-eyed as v. 71* — forgive the sin, do not fuse the pattern; give the chance, keep the gate — and the Prophet ﷺ, in the tradition's portrait, is the verse *in a man*: the uncle ransomed to the last dirham and the enemy's daughter married to her own freedom, the same hour's same hand: *knowing, and wise*.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:72

> **Truly those who believe, and migrate, and strive with their wealth and themselves in the way of God, and those who sheltered and helped—they are protectors of one another. As for those who believe and did not migrate, you owe them no protection until they migrate. But if they ask your help for the sake of religion, then help is a duty upon you, except against a people with whom you have a covenant. And God sees whatsoever you do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Constitution of a People Written as Four Classes and One Bond**

*Inna'lladhīna āmanū wa-hājarū wa-jāhadū bi-amwālihim wa-anfusihim fī sabīli'llāhi wa-lladhīna āwaw wa-naṣarū ulāʾika ba'ḍuhum awliyā'u ba'ḍ* — the sūrah's closing act is its *statecraft*: the bond of the community defined as *mutual walāyah* between the two parties of the Hijrah — the Emigrants who believed, left, and spent their wealth and selves, and the Helpers who *sheltered* (*āwā* — gave roof and city) and *helped* (*nasarū* — gave arms and blood). The word *awliyā'* (plural of *walī*) is, as the source's note insists, the densest constitutional term in Arabic — friendship, protection, proximity, authority, *and* inheritance — and the sūrah's final act turns on which of its doors the word opens (see v. 75); what all readings agree on is the *fact* the clause establishes: this community is not a fellowship of sentiment but a *juridical body with reciprocal duties* — the Muhājirūn and Anṣār are *each other's guarantors*, and the guarantee is *total*: wealth for wealth, blood for blood, estate for estate (the *muʾākhāh*, the brotherhood-contract in which the Prophet ﷺ paired, man by man, the landless of Makkah with the householders of Madinah — Ibn ʿAbbās' report runs: the Prophet ﷺ joined the Emigrants and the Helpers as brothers, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf said to his brother in faith, "may God bless you in your family and your wealth — *guide me to the market*," and thrived on dried yoghurt and untilled land until he was among the wealthiest of the community — al-Bukhārī 2048).

**The Class in Between: Believers Without Papers**

*Wa-lladhīna āmanū wa-lam yuhājirūna mā lakum min wilāyatihim min shayʾ ḥatta yuhājirū* — "those who believe but have not migrated: you owe them no *wilāyah* until they migrate." The clause has always been the sūrah's hardest, and its *asbāb* gives it its exact face: Ḥudaybiyah's third condition — that a Meccan who came to Muhammad ﷺ without his guardian's leave be *returned* — had just made *emigration* the community's border-law (the scene of Abū Jandal, fetters-clinking, turned back, and the words the sīrah preserves: "O God, I leave you with *myself*" and the grief that nearly broke the truce's author); and the non-migrant believers left *inside* Makkah — the tested (4:75's "why do you not fight in God's cause for the *oppressed* — men, women, and children who cry: 'our Lord, take us out of this town of wrongdoers'") — are placed in a *legal parenthesis*: brothers in *dīn*, not yet kin in *dawlah*. The parenthesis is *not* a rejection — the next clause forbids that reading in one breath — but the *rule of the case*: the city's protection runs to those who built it and to the *law of it*; it does not, in the hour of the truce, override the city's *covenants* for the sake of those who can still (but do not) enter. On the harder reading the verse leans for those *able* to emigrate and *satisfied* with the compromise, 4:97 waits — "the angels take them, wronging their own souls" — and the tradition applies it: *mā lam yastatīʿū sabīlan*, the exempt (4:98) and the capable-but-comfortable (4:97), read as the two ends of 8:72's bracket. And the *ḥadīth* that closes the era's legal window — *"there is no hijrah after the conquest [of Makkah]; but there is jihad and intention, and when you are called for help, help [them]"* (Muslim 1353; likewise al-Bukhārī) — shows the clause as *timed*: it legislated a *border* only as long as the border *was* the ummah's life-line; once the *dār* was the whole peninsula, the *wilāyah* went to all who believed, and the parenthesis *closed into the door* (which is v. 75's "they are of you" — see below).

**The Duty Across the Border — and Its Treaty-Limit**

*Wa-stanṣarūkum fī'd-dīni fa-ʿalaykumu'n-naṣru illā ʿalā qawmin baynakum wa-baynahum mīthāq* — the clause is the most advanced piece of international-moral legislation in the ancient corpus: the non-citizen believer's *appeal for help* in religion is *legally binding* upon the state — *help is a duty upon you* (*ʿalaykumu*) — and the *single* limit named is the *mīthāq*, the covenant: you ride to free the oppressed *coreligionist*, but you do not ride *over the treaty's back*; the covenant is not a loophole but a *wall of conscience* — 9:4's four months and the *nabḍ bi-sawāʾ* of v. 58 govern even against the *breaker*, so how much more against the *keeper*. The logic is the sūrah's own equation: *covenants are the ummah's treasure* (its word is what makes its walāyah *credible*: "no faith for one who has no *amānah*," in the Musnad line) — the community does not buy its kinsmen's freedom with its *own name*, because its name is the instrument by which *more* kinsmen will be freed in *every future* hour. Al-Rāzī's synthesis (in the source's note) draws the practical ruling: *wilāyah* in the full political sense is withheld from the non-migrant — the state neither rules them nor covers them against their own government by force — but *nuṣra* for religion is owed as a *moral and legal* duty *short of the covenant-breach*: negotiate, ransom, shelter those who can arrive — and the later *fiqh* inherited exactly this architecture (the *mustaʾman* who visits, the *dā al-ʿahd*, the *fidā* ransoms owed from the *khums'* orphans' and indigent's shares).

**"And God Sees What You Do" — the Eye over the Whole Ledger**

The closing *baṣīra* is the verse's *guarantee of both its hard words*: the *withheld* wilāyah and the *mandated* nuṣra are alike *watched*: the state that hoards its power *from* its vulnerable kin is seen, and the state that spends its *honor* (the covenant) for a *campaign of sympathy* is seen. It is also the sūrah's final ring back to its first economics: v. 3's *mu'minūn* spend from what they are provided, v. 72's *awliyāʾ* owe protection to each other *of the same substance* — wealth and self (bi-amwālihim wa-anfusihim); and v. 1's *anfāl* to God and His Messenger is v. 72's *walāyah* in its source — *both* belong to the One who *sees*: which is why the constitution's last word is not *sovereignty* but *sight*: the community that knows it is *seen* can be *held together by law what the heart alone cannot*: covenants kept, appeals heard, borders honest, and the *ḥisāb* — the reckoning — left, as it was at Badr, to the *ʿAlīm*: the last of the sūrah's three *al-ʿalīm* seals (vv. 26's context, 53, 72, and 75's *bi-kulli shayʾin ʿalīm*) and the door into the treaty of inheritance that closes the chapter: the *Book of God* (v. 75) is written by the Eye that sees *what you do*.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:73

> **As for those who disbelieve, they are protectors of one another. Unless you do the same, there will be a strife in the land, and a great corruption.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Other Alliance, Stated Without Illusion**

*Wa-lladhīna kafarū ba'ḍuhum awliyā'u ba'ḍ* — "and as for those who disbelieve: they are *awliyā'* of one another." The verse is the sūrah's *realism*, and it is placed, with surgical intent, immediately after the believers' alliance-clause: the world is *not* a scatter of individuals but a structure of *camps*, and the Qur'an says of the unbelieving camp what Badr had just *proven* — the tribes rode as *one* bond, their trade-leagues and pacts moving to the valley as a single body, the same principle of blood and interest that binds any *awliyā'*. The clause is not a *command* to hate but a *description* to weigh: it warns the ummah that the other alliance is *real, organized, and self-referential*, and that a community which imagines itself *outside* the map of alliances will end up *under* someone else's. Ibn Kathīr reads it as one of the most comprehensive verses of *walāʾ wa-barāʾ* — the believer's loyalty is *owed first* where God placed it, to v. 72's Emigrants and Helpers — and the sūrah's own treaty-law fills in the rest: the verse governs *hearts and priorities*, not contracts; the covenants stand inviolate until the *nabḍ* of v. 58's rules, and the other camp's bond, however real, binds *them*, not the order of the world.

**"Unless You Do the Same": the Believers' Alliance as Public Work**

*Illā tafʿalūhu takun fitnatun fi'l-arḍi wa-fasādin kabīr* — "unless you *do the same* — that you are protectors of one another — there will be strife in the land and great corruption." The grammar of the warning is what makes the verse a *science of state*: the condition is *performance*, not sentiment — *do* the walāyah (help, defend, stand with, and legislate for one another), because an alliance is nothing but the sum of its *duties*; and the penalty is not a punishment *sent* but a disaster *unleashed*: a community of faith that fails to bind itself does not remain *neutral* — the vacuum is filled by the *other* walāyah, the first clause operating as the law that governs precisely in the second clause's absence. Then *fitna* — the sūrah's own word for the trial of vv. 25 and 28, now socialized: persecution, war, fear, mistrust, and the civilisational fitna of 2:191's "sedition is worse than killing" — and *fasādin kabīr*, the *corruption* whose root ran through the Qur'an's opening (2:11's "do not make mischief on the earth," its twin verses 7:56 and 11:116's "after its mending"), up to Pharaoh's political *fasād* in 28:4 — "Pharaoh *corrupted* in the land and made its people sects, *ḍuʿafāʾ*" — the very word, *ḍuʿafāʾ*, of v. 26's *mustaḍʿafūn*. The greatness clause (*kabīr*) marks the measure: the corruption foretold is not the unbelieving camp's *existence* but the believers' *unbondedness* — the collapse of a public order when its guardians abdicate into their private virtues. Which is why the classical exegetes — al-Ṭabarī reading *fitna* here as *shirk* and open war, Ibn Kathīr as the confusion of the ranks — treat the verse as a *farḍ al-kifāya* in constitutional form: the walāyah of the ummah, its courts, treaties, defence, and welfare, is a communal duty, and its neglect is recorded not as a private shortcoming but as the ruin of the land.

**The Verse against Both of Its Caricatures**

Read whole, v. 73 cuts against the community's two perennial errors. Against the *tribal* error — walāyah reduced to blood, party, and ethnicity, the *ʿaṣabiyya* the Prophet ﷺ buried on the very ground this sūrah's Madinah was made from: when two men reviled one another with boasts of lineage in his presence and a companion asked, "O Messenger of God, is this not one of the calls of the Jāhiliyyah?", the Prophet ﷺ said: *"Leave it aside — it is rotten"* (Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim, Kitāb al-Imāra, "the chapter of warning against the calls of the Jāhiliyyah") — against this, the verse's first clause de-sacralizes *every* camp's bond: the believer owes his *walā'* where God assigned it — the brotherhood of faith and the equity of covenants — and owes every other camp *justice*: 60:8's "God does not forbid you, touching those who did not fight you for your religion, to be kind and equitable to them" is this sūrah's twin, its treaty-law spelled out in al-Mumtaḥana. Against the *utopian* error — the dissolution of the ummah's particular duties into a humanity-without-address — the warning stands in full force: *unless you do the same*: the community that will not organize its own protection will find no one else doing it for it, and the land will pay. And the sūrah's own arc is the perfect commentary: from v. 1's *anfāl*, the distribution of *spoils* as the test whether the community will run on God's economics or each man's greed, to v. 73's *awliyāʾ*, the distribution of *loyalty* as the same test at the scale of the whole body: spoils and walāyah are alike "for God and His Messenger" first — and the *fitna* begins exactly where either is made one's own.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:74

> **As for those who believe, and migrate, and strive in the way of God, and those who sheltered and helped, it is they who truly are believers. Theirs is forgiveness and a generous provision.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Ring: Verse Four's Crown Restored**

*Wa-lladhīna āmanū wa-hājarū wa-jāhadū fī sabīli'llāhi wa-lladhīna āwaw wa-nasarū ulāʾika humu'l-mu'minūna ḥaqqan lahum maghfiratun wa-rizqun karīm* — the verse is the sūrah's ring-composition made audible: nearly word-for-word it restores v. 4, the sentence that crowned the opening portrait of the true believers ("*it is they who are the believers in truth; for them are degrees with their Lord, and forgiveness, and a generous provision*"). The chapter began by *defining* the community against the backdrop of its first disputed spoils and ends by *ennobling* the same community at the peak of its political maturity — and the echo is doctrine: the qualities that earned the crown at the sūrah's start (belief, migration, striving, sheltering, helping) are *the same* listed at its end; the rank did not grow with Badr's *victory* but with Badr's *obedience*; between the two verses stands the entire lesson of the chapter, from the *anfāl* dispute to the captives' ransom, and the crown fits the head that wore it through all of it. The *ḥaqqan* (in truth) answers, once more, the hypocrites' claim-grammar of v. 15's neighbourhood and vv. 41–43's "to make the false seem true": this faith is *ḥaqq* — real, verified, weight — because it *paid* (the Emigrants: home, wealth, self) and because it *gave* (the Helpers: roof, aid, share): *ṭāʾifatan* of v. 2's "a group of the believers" has become, in the chapter's closing roll-call, the *whole* of true belief.

**The Rank of the Shelterers — and Its Seal**

The tafsīr consensus, reported already at v. 63 and reinforced by the verse's own emphatic construction (*ulāʾika humu* — "it is *they* who *alone*"), exalts the Muhājirūn and the Anṣār above every category after them; the sūrah seals their rank by the twin grants — *maghfira* (the past cleared: the Jāhiliyya's debts of blood and idol, the Būʿāth wound of v. 63's pre-Islamic day) and *rizq karīm* (the future nobly provided: v. 74's provision being the *karīm* word of 2:261 and of v. 4's "generous provision," a sustenance with *dignity* in it, not mere amount). And the Prophet's ﷺ own seal upon the Helpers is the companion-text of this verse in every classical tafsīr of it:

> *"The Anṣār: none loves them but a believer, and none hates them but a hypocrite; so whoever loves them, God loves him, and whoever hates them, God hates him."* (al-Bukhārī, *Manāqib al-Anṣār*; Muslim 1051)
> *"Were it not for the hijrah, I would have been a man among the Anṣār; and if the people took one valley and the Anṣār another valley, I would take the Anṣār's valley."* (al-Bukhārī, *Manāqib al-Anṣār*; Muslim, *Kitāb al-Faḍā'il* — the same mercy to the Helpers that the Prophet ﷺ enacted at al-Jīʿrānah, quoted above at v. 63)

— and ʿAlī's charge to those given authority over the Helpers, from the Prophet's ﷺ dying counsel in the *ṣaḥīḥ* collections: *"Accept the good-doer among them and overlook the wrongdoer among them"* — the rank of the Anṣār legislated, in the end, as a *duty upon everyone else*: the verse's "they sheltered and helped" remains, in the ummah's fiqh, a *debt that converts to protection of the creditors*.

**"There Is No Hijrah After the Conquest" — and the Hijrah That Never Ends**

The source's note on v. 74 records the majority reading of the rank as *permanently restricted* to that first generation, on the strength of the famous ḥadīth: *"There is no hijrah after the conquest — but there is jihad and intention, and when you are called for help, then come"* (Muslim 1353; likewise al-Bukhārī) — the migration *to Madinah* as a legal obligation ended when Makkah itself became the *dār al-islām*, and most read the "conquest" as that of Makkah, while others (noted by the source) date the closing of the hijrah-window to Ḥudaybiyyah's open road. But the sūrah's own usage keeps a second door standing: the *moral* hijrah — *mahājara mā kariha'llāhu*, "to forsake what God has forbidden," the Prophet's ﷺ gloss of *al-muhājir man hadhara mā uṭqiqa min dhālik* in the *ṣaḥīḥ* line ("the emigrant is the one who abandons what God has forbidden" — al-Bukhārī 10) — is *never abrogated*, and the scholars the source names (al-Qurṭubī's and al-Rāzī's tradition, quoting the *jihād*-as-*hijrah* reading) extend the verse's reward to every believer who *leaves the abode of fear or sin for the sake of the deen*: Q, R's opinion, cited by the source, that "leaving one's homeland to campaign in God's way is a kind of hijrah," makes v. 74 the *open charter*: the rank of the *first* Emigrants and Helpers is closed forever, but the *species* of their deed — depart, and help — renews with every generation, which is exactly what the very next verse, v. 75, will legislate for "those who believe *afterwards*."

**Forgiveness First, Provision Last — the Order of the Gifts**

Observe the verse's economics one final time: *lahum maghfiratun wa-rizqun karīm* — forgiveness *before* provision. The sūrah that opened with the believers *asking* about the *spoils* closes with God's schedule for the same people's *wealth*: first the heart is *emptied of the past* (maghfira: the sin, the ransom, the feud of Būʿāth, the shame of the *anfāl* dispute), and only then is the hand *filled with the future* (rizq: the *karīm* provision, generous precisely because it no longer *matters* the way it did — v. 28's warning that "your wealth and your children are a *fitna*" is *cured*, not cancelled, by this order). The *tawba*-and-*rizq* pair is the Qur'an's own grammar elsewhere — 71:10–12's "seek His forgiveness, *He will send the sky upon you pouring, and give you wealth and children*," and 11:3's the same — and here, at the sūrah's end, it lands with the whole Battle behind it: the community that won its wealth *after* it was made to ask *whose* it was has finally learned, in one sentence, the two gifts the *anfāl* were ever meant to teach: *forgiveness for the hearts that disputed over provision, and a provision noble enough not to be disputed over*. The last class-name before the law of inheritance (v. 75) could not have been kinder nor better placed: the true believers are *named* — then the estate is *ordered*: the chapter's journey from *spoils* to *sharīʿa* is complete, its economics now ready for the *wombs* to which God will assign what the *walāyah* of brotherhood once held.

---
## Sūrah al-Anfāl 8:75

> **As for those who believe after you and migrate and strive with you, they are [to be counted] among you. But family relations have the strongest claim on one another in the Book of God. Truly God is Knower of all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**"They Are of You": the Last Generation Enrolled**

*Wa-lladhīna āmanū min baʿdu wa-hājarū wa-jāhadū maʿakum fa-ulāʾika minkum* — the final legislative sentence of the sūrah is an act of *enrolment*: whoever believes *after* that founding company, migrates and strives *with* the community, is "of you" — admitted into the same body, the same rights, the same covenant-law of vv. 72–74, without the first generation's *rank*. That is the whole balance of the verse, and the sūrah leaves it exact: *minkum* — of you — and *not* "better than you"; the rank of the Companions stands (v. 74's "it is *they* who truly are believers"), and the door stands open behind them; the hadith of the best-generations scale says the same thing in the Prophet's ﷺ voice:

> *"The best of people are my generation, then those who follow them, then those who follow them."* (al-Bukhārī 2652; Muslim 2533)

— and beside it stands the tenderness of the hadith of yearning, in which the *last* believers are called *brethren*:

> *"I long to see our brethren." They said: are we not your brethren, O Messenger of God? He said: you are my companions — our brethren are those who believe in me without having seen me.* (Aḥmad's Musnad; Ibn Ḥibbān, ṣaḥīḥ chain)

So the sūrah's *minkum* carries both: full membership for the late-comer — his spoils-law, his walāyah, his inheritance, his share of rizq karīm — and an abiding preference for those who bore the founding cost. The verb even preserves the *moral hijrah* that outlives the legal one (v. 74): *hājarū wa-jāhadū maʿakum* — "they migrate and strive *with you*": the late-comer's migration is defined by *companionship with the community* — the emigration of the heart from the abode of fear and sin into the ranks of the people of God is never closed, and no *futūḥ* ever abrogates it.

**"Womb-Kin Have Stronger Claim": the Law Returns to the Blood**

*Wa-ūlu'l-arḥāmi ba'ḍuhum awlā bi-ba'ḍin fī kitābi'llāhi* — "and the possessors of wombs: some of them have a stronger claim on others in the Book of God." The *arḥām* (the wombs — kinship named from the *raḥim*, the organ that carried the bond before any contract did) step into the place the *muʾākhāh* had held: the brotherhood-contract between Emigrant and Helper — with its extraordinary consequence that a brother in faith could inherit ahead of a father in unbelief (v. 72's note; and 4:33's "and for all We have appointed *mawālī*" of what parents and kinsmen leave, the verse the sources pair with this one) — gives way, in the settled law of inheritance, to the blood-lines of 4:11–12. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf's report, preserved by al-Bukhārī in the Book of Inheritance-Laws, is the sūrah's own witness to the sequence: the Muhājirūn and Anṣār inherited through the bond of brotherhood *until the verse of inheritance in al-Baqarah was revealed* — and Ibn ʿAbbās' companion-report in the same chapter records the moment the *walāyah*-bond was *decoupled* from the estate. The commentators' disagreement, which the source lays out carefully, is itself instructive: some hold this verse *abrogates* the brotherhood-inheritance outright; al-Rāzī demurs entirely — there is *no abrogation here at all*, he argues, because v. 72's *walāyah* never meant *mirāth*: the verse's own *fī kitābi'llāhi* — "in the Book of God" — points to the shares already *explicit* in the Qur'an, and the clause simply *clarifies the boundary*: protection and alliance in faith, estate and claim in blood. Q's juristic question (whether *ūlu'l-arḥām* here draws the agnatic line or reaches even through the female line, the *dhawū al-arḥām* heirs the later ḥanafi law systematised) and R's note (the verse ends the pre-Islamic and early *muʿāqada*, inheritance-by-declaration between chosen partners) both, in their different ways, record the same sūrah-wide principle: *distribution is God's, not the community's invention* — v. 1's "the anfāl are for God and His Messenger" arrives, after seventy-four verses, at its most domestic form: even the *family estate* is *fī kitābi'llāhi*.

**The Womb Honoured by the Same Book That Redrew the Map**

Yet the verse's philosophy is not a *retreat* from vv. 72–74's faith-bond but its completion, and the pairing is the sūrah's answer to the oldest of political questions — *can a community of choice out-legislate a community of blood?* The Qur'an's answer here: the *walāyah* of v. 72 *did* outrank blood — in protection, in alliance, even, for a season, in inheritance — and once that revolution had made the ummah a *body*, the estate returned to the wombs, so that the *choice* of faith would not *punish* the givenness of kin: religion binds the *will*, blood binds the *account*, and neither cancels the other. That is why the verse's honour of the *raḥim* rests, in the prophetic tradition, on the *name of God* itself:

> *"God says: I am the Rahman; I created the womb (raḥim) and drew for it a name from My Own; so whoever maintains it, I maintain him, and whoever severs it, I sever him."* (al-Bukhārī 5988)

— the same *raḥma*-thread that ran from v. 70's promise to the captive ("if God knows good in your hearts") to the ummah's final law: the *Most Merciful* is honoured *in* the womb-claim, and *severing* kin, which the sūrah's whole ethics of alliance forbids, is named in the tradition as barred from Paradise's entrance. The community that was built here — on the orphan's and the captive's and the kinsman's *shares* — is a body, not a party; and a body keeps *all* its limbs, the chosen and the given, each at its post.

**"God Is Knower of All Things" — the Sūrah's Final Seal**

*Inna'llāha bi-kulli shayʾin ʿalīm* — seventy-four verses of spoils and hearts, covenants and captivities, ranks and remedies, are sealed by the *all-knowing*: the God who at v. 1 was appealed to over the *anfāl* now holds the *whole* distribution of the ummah in His knowledge — every heir's claim, every partner's contract, every late-comer's sincerity, every severed tie and every kept one. The ring is complete and deliberate: *the sūrah opens with a dispute over property and closes with the property of the womb assigned by God; it opens "they ask you about the spoils — say: the spoils are for God and His Messenger" and closes "womb-kin are prior, by turns, in the Book of God."* Between these two sovereignty-claims the chapter fitted a battle, a treasury, a treaty-law, a psychology of greed and trust, and a ladder of ranks from the Prophet ﷺ's pebble-thrown handful of dust to the last believer's enrolment — and the closing *ʿalīm* says what the whole chapter has been teaching from v. 2 onward: *the distribution of the world only works among a people who believe they are watched.* The commentary of this sūrah, from Ibn ʿAbbās' reports in the early tafsīrs through al-Ṭabarī's histories, Ibn Kathīr's battle-chronicles, al-Qurṭubī's rulings and al-Rāzī's architecture of reasons, converges at one reading of the chapter as a whole: *Badr is not the sūrah's subject; Badr is its classroom* — the *anfāl*, the war, the captives, the alliances, the inheritance: one syllabus teaching a young community that *nothing that enters the love of this world may enter the command of the next*; and the God whose *kull shayʾ* ends the book is the God whose *ʿalīm* began it: *knower of what the hearts chose — at Badr, and in every ledger, until the last one opens.*

---
**[End of the commentary on Sūrah al-Anfāl]**
